# v0.1 schema and specification: review findings

- **Date:** 2026-11-04
- **Branch:** `v0.1-schema`, reviewed at `96c902a`
- **Brief:** `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-review-brief.md`
- **Method:** the brief's six lenses, run as 38 independent passes, then one round of eight gap probes chosen by a completeness critic (a second round was not run, to stay within the usage limit). The passes reported 435 candidate findings, which merged into 218 distinct ones. Each was reproduced by an independent reviewer and challenged by another against the RFCs, the design, the settled decisions and the known items; 16 were set aside and the rest confirmed. Confirmed findings that one fix resolves were then merged into one entry.
- **Tools:** Python `jsonschema` 4.26 (Draft 2019-09) with the harness's YAML 1.2 core-schema loader, and Ajv 8.20 (`ajv/dist/2019`, `allErrors`, `verbose`) with `yaml` 2.9.1, each mapping errors to codes through `rules.json`. Paths under `scratchpad/work/` in the evidence name the review's scratch files, which are not kept: each entry carries the documents and outputs it rests on.

## Summary

The generated contract mostly holds, but the spec doesn't pin down how YAML becomes a JSON instance, so the Python and TypeScript stacks can give one file different verdicts or codes. Most important findings are in the validator, check and runner layers, where terms such as driving, planned results, a result's name and `unchanged` are undefined or read two ways, and where some RFC rules were widened, narrowed or dropped without an entry in design §11. The tests also let many constraints loosen with `just test` green, because fixtures are required per code, not per constraint.

**163 findings:** 1 Critical, 72 Important, 90 Minor.

| Lens | Critical | Important | Minor |
|---|---|---|---|
| RFC fidelity | 1 | 31 | 16 |
| Implementer | 1 | 38 | 32 |
| Author | 1 | 13 | 10 |
| Internal consistency | 1 | 18 | 26 |
| Standards | 1 | 20 | 28 |
| Edge cases | 1 | 17 | 15 |

A finding that more than one lens found counts once under each.

**The three findings that matter most:**

1. **F001. Non-string map keys.** The only Critical finding, and the only place where the committed contract gives Python and Ajv opposite verdicts on an ordinary file: a decision with options `true` and `false`. Settling it (map keys are strings, checked on the YAML node before conversion, under one code) fixes how astra-tools and astra-typescript load YAML.
2. **F005. Reading an undriven analysis's output built once.** Two validator MUSTs, worded the same in §9 and `rules.json`, reject the unpinned read of a declared analysis's output built once that §7 and RFCs 0004 and 0007 call valid. A validator written from §9 rejects RFC 0007's main use case; one written from §7 accepts it.
3. **F041. What `unchanged` is judged against.** `STALE_RESULT` and the runner's `STALE_INPUT` both turn on it, and with the working tree and `HEAD` both left open, a local checker and a CI checker disagree on every dirty tree, and under the `HEAD` reading a runner can't build a chain of outputs in one run.

## Findings

Most severe first; within a severity, by the first lens that found them.

### F001. A map key that YAML reads as a boolean, number or null has no defined JSON form: Python rejects `true:` as WRONG_TYPE, Ajv accepts it, and §4/§9 say INVALID_ID

- **Lens:** RFC fidelity, Implementer, Author, Internal consistency, Standards, Edge cases · **Severity:** Critical · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:175-182`, `docs/specification.md:235-236`, `docs/specification.md:1099`, `docs/specification.md:1110`, `docs/specification.md:1233-1240`, `docs/specification.md:1248-1250`, `docs/specification.md:1268-1271`, `tools/jsonschema_generator.py:61-62`, `src/astra/schema/generated/rules.json:23,144,158,163,168,172,178,182,191`, `tests/test_rules.py:498-512`

**What is wrong.** Under the YAML 1.2 core schema, a plain key `true`, `false`, `~`, `1` or `1.5` is a boolean, a null or a number. JSON Schema's data model has only string property names (2019-09 core §4.2.1, §9.3.2.5). The specification never says how a YAML map with a non-string key becomes the JSON instance that §10's contract is applied to, and it never says that keys must be strings. §3 only advises quoting: keys are "quoted when meant as ids or strings, as in `"true": {}`".

So the verdict depends on the loader:

- The harness (ruamel, then `jsonschema`) keeps the key's type. `propertyNames/type` fails, and `rules.json` maps it to WRONG_TYPE.
- `yaml` 2.9.1's `toJS()`, then Ajv, turns every key into a string. `true` becomes the valid id `"true"`, so the file is VALID. `1` becomes `"1"` and `~` becomes `""`, which fail the pattern as INVALID_ID.
- A Python tool that round-trips through `json.dumps` accepts `true:`, `false:` and `~:` (`None` becomes the valid id `"null"`).

The code is also inconsistent within the spec. §4 and §9 give a bad map key INVALID_ID; §10's `type` default makes the nine `propertyNames/type` keywords WRONG_TYPE. Those nine entries can't fire on a JSON instance at all.

**Evidence.** A boolean decision written the natural way (repro-c023 `r01-bool-options.yaml`):

```yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
outputs:
  result:
    type: metric
    format: json
    decisions: [augment]
decisions:
  augment:
    options:
      true: {}
      false: {}
```

```
$ uv run python toolkit/validate.py Analysis r01-bool-options.yaml
/decisions/augment/options | …#/$defs/Decision__identifier_optional/properties/options/propertyNames/type | WRONG_TYPE | True is not of type 'string'
/decisions/augment/options | …same… | WRONG_TYPE | False is not of type 'string'
$ node toolkit/js/validate.mjs <repo> Analysis r01-bool-options.yaml
VALID
```

The same split holds for an output key `true:` and for a `true:` key in `Universe.decisions`. The quoted control is VALID in both.

| key | Python (harness) | Ajv + `yaml` 2.9.1 |
|---|---|---|
| `1:`, `2:` (options) | WRONG_TYPE at `…/propertyNames/type` | INVALID_ID at `…/propertyNames/pattern` and `…/propertyNames` |
| `~:` (outputs) | WRONG_TYPE (`None is not of type 'string'`) | INVALID_ID |
| `1.5:` | WRONG_TYPE | INVALID_ID |
| `? [a, b]` | WRONG_TYPE | INVALID_ID |

`yaml`'s AST keeps the key types (`true:boolean`, `~:null`, 0 errors, 0 warnings); `toJS()` drops them. With `"true": {label: quoted}` and `true: {label: unquoted}` in one map, Python reads two keys and reports WRONG_TYPE, while `toJS()` keeps only the later entry and Ajv says VALID. One option is lost with no DUPLICATE_KEY.

- spec:235-236: "a map key or an `id` as [`INVALID_ID`]".
- spec:1099 (INVALID_ID): "Every map key matches its key's type".
- spec:1110 (WRONG_TYPE): "Every value has the JSON type its field takes".
- spec:1268-1271: "`type` (`WRONG_TYPE`)".
- tools/jsonschema_generator.py:61: `prop["propertyNames"] = JsonSchema({"type": "string"})`.
- `rules.json` maps all nine `…/propertyNames/type` keywords to `"WRONG_TYPE"`.
- `test_every_map_key_is_an_id` filters on `/propertyNames` and `/propertyNames/pattern` only, so it never checks these nine entries.
- No fixture or example has a boolean, number or null key. `tests/data/valid/Analysis-003` covers `yes`/`no`/`on` only. The design doesn't record the case.

Minor, on the side: the harness raises DuplicateKeyError for `true:` beside `1:` (Python's `True == 1`), although YAML 1.2.2 §3.2.1.3 treats them as distinct keys. Both stacks reject that file anyway.

**Why it matters.** A decision with options `true`/`false` is common in multiverse analyses, and it is the case §3 warns about. astra-tools rejects the file, while astra-typescript and Ajv-based editors accept it. That breaks §10's "validators in either language agree". For number and null keys both reject, but with different codes, which breaks "every tool reports the same code for the same error". A JS tool can also drop an entry without a trace.

**Suggested fix.** In §3/§9, say that every map key MUST resolve to a string (`!!str`), checked on the YAML node before conversion (in JS, before `toJS()`), with one code (INVALID_ID fits §4 and §9). Recode or drop the nine `propertyNames/type` entries, widen `test_every_map_key_is_an_id`, and add invalid fixtures with `true:`, `1:` and `~:` keys.

### F002. A driven analysis whose decisions are all inactive in one of the driver's universes has no valid form (PARTIALLY_DRIVEN against INACTIVE_DECISION)

- **Lens:** RFC fidelity, Implementer · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:690-695`, `docs/specification.md:699-700`, `docs/specification.md:1140`, `docs/specification.md:558-560`, `docs/specification.md:573-574`, `docs/specification.md:1136`, `docs/specification.md:725-727`, `docs/specification.md:562-571`, `rfcs/0004-composition.md:137`, `rfcs/0004-composition.md:407-408`

**What is wrong.** The spec defines driving only by naming flat keys. In some driver universe, every decision of a driven analysis may be inactive, because each one's `when` names a decision of another analysis. That universe can't set those keys (INACTIVE_DECISION), and any key it inherits is dropped (step 3). So it names nothing through the declaration, and PARTIALLY_DRIVEN fires. If no universe drives the declaration, the driver's unpinned read of the stage's output fails UNDRIVEN_DECLARATION. Every way of writing the stack breaks a rule. The spec never says when "a universe drives a declaration", and nothing exempts a universe that has nothing active to set. RFC 0004 has the same gap: driving is "named in the universe" (:137), and the rows at :407-408 are not reconciled. Design §13 (`design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:777-783`) records the case of an analysis with no decisions. Its likely settlement does not cover this case, because here the stage has a decision. The two cases share a root cause.

A second, weaker gap: the list of what the effective selection is held to (562-571) leaves out PARTIALLY_DRIVEN and CONFLICTING_SELECTIONS. Read by own keys, RFC 0004's `u2` (`derived_from: u1`, setting only `subanalysis1.b`, rfcs/0004-composition.md:161-165, :460-463) would fail PARTIALLY_DRIVEN for `subanalysis2`. Line 562 makes the effective-selection reading the evident one, so this is a wording fix.

**Evidence.** The spec's §5 calibration example (spec:465-477), with the prior moved into a driven stage:

```yaml
# astra.yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
analyses: {stage: {path: stage}}
decisions: {correction: {options: {none: {}, calibrated: {}}}}
outputs:
  table: {type: table, format: csv, decisions: [correction]}
  calibrated_table: {type: table, format: csv, when: [correction.calibrated], inputs: [stage.fit]}
universes:
  cal: {decisions: {correction: calibrated, stage.prior: weak}}
  raw: {decisions: {correction: none}}
  raw_derived: {derived_from: cal, decisions: {correction: none}}
# stage/astra.yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
analyses: {root: {path: ..}}
decisions:
  prior: {when: [root.correction.calibrated], options: {weak: {}, informative: {}}}
outputs:
  fit: {type: metric, format: json, when: [root.correction.calibrated], decisions: [prior]}
```

Both files are VALID in the Python and Ajv validators. A condition may name a declared analysis's decision (spec:459-460). Walking it through §6–§7:

- As written: `raw` and `raw_derived` (inherited `stage.prior` dropped) name no `stage.*` key, by own keys or effective selection, so PARTIALLY_DRIVEN.
- `raw` sets `stage.prior: weak`: INACTIVE_DECISION ("A universe itself sets only decisions whose `when` holds in its effective selection", 1136).
- No universe sets `stage.*`: `cal` builds `calibrated_table`, which reads `stage.fit` through an undriven declaration, so UNDRIVEN_DECLARATION.
- Pinning the read: `stage` has no universes, so UNKNOWN_PIN_UNIVERSE.

The same holds for a condition on a sibling the root drives (`fit.mask` with `when: [data.release.dr2]`, universe `dr1: {data.release: dr1}`).

**Why it matters.** Analysis authors can't put a conditional decision in a driven stage unless the stage also has an unconditional one. RFC 0004:107 anticipates `when: [root.a.a1]`, and RFC 0003's drop rule produces this case whenever a derived universe switches a decision off. The only workaround is a dummy decision set to a meaningless option. Validator authors have to invent the predicate, and two implementations will disagree.

**Suggested fix.** Define "a universe drives a declaration" on its effective selection, holding also when the declared analysis has no active decision in that universe (or restrict PARTIALLY_DRIVEN to the universes where it has one). Add PARTIALLY_DRIVEN and CONFLICTING_SELECTIONS to the list at 563-571, and settle this together with the §13 no-decisions point.

### F003. A pin through a chain of declarations: the spec doesn't say which declaration decides whose universe it names

- **Lens:** RFC fidelity, Implementer · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:732-737`, `docs/specification.md:1146`, `src/astra/schema/generated/rules.json:581-585`, `docs/specification.md:716-718`, `rfcs/0004-composition.md:357`, `rfcs/0004-composition.md:413`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:586`

**What is wrong.** A pin's universe depends on whether "its reference goes through a driven declaration". With one declaration that is clear. With a chain, say `stage.lib.out#v1` where the root drives `stage` but nothing drives `lib`, it is not. Under reading A, any driven declaration on the path counts, so `#v1` names the root's `v1`. Under reading B, the last declaration decides (the one that reaches the output's analysis), so `#v1` names lib's own `v1`. The two readings give opposite verdicts. Under A, if the root happens to have a `v1`, the validator accepts a pin to a result the root's universes never build. "The driver" is undefined in the same way. The ambiguity comes from RFC 0004, which uses the same wording. The spec does not depart from the RFC, and design:586 matches both.

**Evidence.** The wording:

- `docs/specification.md:732-735`: "A pin `#u` names, when its reference goes through a driven declaration, the universe `u` of the driver, and otherwise the universe `u` of the analysis whose output it is".
- `docs/specification.md:1146` (the same at `rules.json:581-585`): "Every pin names a universe of the driver, through a driven declaration, or otherwise of the analysis the output belongs to".
- `docs/specification.md:716-718`: the universe being built "is pushed through every analysis it drives".
- `rfcs/0004-composition.md:357`: "names the driver's universe through a driven declaration or otherwise the analysis' own universe". `:413` says "of the declared analysis if not driven", which leans towards B but doesn't say which declared analysis, so it doesn't settle the chain case.

The second-declaration pattern (735-737) is shown at depth 1 only. No multi-hop pin appears anywhere in docs/, rfcs/, examples/, tests/data/ or design/.

A minimal stack, valid at the schema layer:

```yaml
# astra.yaml (root: drives stage, sets no stage.lib.* key)
spec_version: "0.1.0"
analyses: {stage: {path: stage}}
outputs:
  report: {type: report, format: md, inputs: [stage.s_out, stage.lib.out#v1]}
universes:
  u1: {decisions: {stage.s: x}}
  u2: {decisions: {stage.s: y}}
# stage/astra.yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
analyses: {lib: {path: lib}}
decisions: {s: {options: {x: {}, y: {}}}}
outputs: {s_out: {type: data, format: csv, inputs: [lib.out#v1], decisions: [s]}}
# stage/lib/astra.yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
decisions: {l: {options: {p: {}, q: {}}}}
outputs: {out: {type: data, format: csv, decisions: [l]}}
universes: {v1: {decisions: {l: p}}}
```

```
$ uv run python validate.py Analysis <the three files, and the variant with root v1>
VALID (all four)            # Python jsonschema; Ajv gives the same
```

- **A:** `#v1` is the root's. The root has none, so the check fails with `UNKNOWN_PIN_UNIVERSE`. If the root adds `v1: {decisions: {stage.s: x}}`, the pin is accepted. Its record would be the root's `.astra/records/v1/stage.lib.out.json`, which nothing builds. No other rule catches this: `NOT_BUILT_IN_UNIVERSE` (1142) covers only reads "without a pin", and `PIN_INACTIVE` (1147) checks only `when`.
- **B:** `#v1` is lib's own, and the stack is valid. It is the same result that stage's `lib.out#v1` reads.

The spec's own second-declaration pattern splits the same way one level down. The root drives `fit` and reads `fit.pre_own.clean#v1`, where `pre_own` is fit's undriven second declaration of `../prep`.

A third case shows that "the driver" is undefined too. The root declares `up` without driving it, `up` drives `lib`, and the root reads `up.lib.out#w1`. This is valid if the pin names up's `w1`, and gives `UNKNOWN_PIN_UNIVERSE` if "driven" means driven by the root.

**Why it matters.** Validator authors (astra-tools, astra-typescript) will give opposite verdicts on the same stack. Authors can't tell whether to write a root universe or a lib universe after `#`. RFC 0007 makes these stacks ordinary: a driven stage reads a pinned result of an analysis from a commit, and the root's evidence cites it through the stage.

**Suggested fix.** In §7 (732-735) and row 1146, say that the last declaration of the reference decides. If it is driven, the pin names a universe of the analysis that drives it. Otherwise it names one of the output's analysis's own universes. Add a depth-2 example beside the second-declaration pattern, in the tidy-up RFC.

### F004. A derived universe that changes a decision under its other spelling either conflicts or overrides: "written over" doesn't say whether it works by key or by decision

- **Lens:** RFC fidelity, Implementer · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:536-540`, `docs/specification.md:556-557`, `docs/specification.md:562-571`, `docs/specification.md:701-704`, `docs/specification.md:1141`, `src/astra/schema/analysis.yaml:291-297`, `rfcs/0003-cross-universe-artifact-references.md:77-79`, `rfcs/0004-composition.md:221`

**What is wrong.** §6 step 2 builds a derived universe's effective selection as its parent's "with the universe's own `decisions` written over it". A universe's `decisions` is keyed by flat keys, and §7 lets one decision have two spellings (`stage.inner.d` and `inner.d`). The spec doesn't say whether "written over" works key by key or decision by decision. It also doesn't say which map CONFLICTING_SELECTIONS is checked on. The rule says "in a universe", and it is not among the rules the effective selection "is held to" (562-571). §6 does separate own selections from inherited ones elsewhere, for INACTIVE_DECISION (573-574). Once two declarations reach one analysis and both are driven, PARTIALLY_DRIVEN makes every universe name both spellings. So any `derived_from` diff that changes such a decision meets this case.

None of D1-D13 settles it. Design §13 "Agreeing spellings" covers only whether agreeing spellings should warn.

**Evidence.** The base sets both spellings, as spec 701-703 and RFC 0004:221 allow, and the derived universe changes the decision under one of them:

```yaml
# astra.yaml
analyses: {stage: {path: stage}, inner: {path: stage/inner}}
outputs: {report: {type: report, format: md, inputs: [stage.summary, inner.result]}}
universes:
  u1: {decisions: {stage.m: x, stage.inner.d: d1, inner.d: d1}}
  u2: {derived_from: u1, decisions: {inner.d: d2}}
# stage/astra.yaml:       analyses {inner: {path: inner}}; decision m {x, y}; output summary, decisions [m]
# stage/inner/astra.yaml: decision d {d1, d2}; output result, decisions [d]
```

```
validate.py (jsonschema) and validate.mjs (Ajv): VALID / VALID / VALID
```

So the schema layer accepts the files, and the verdict rests on the validator layer. A resolver that maps each flat key to (analysis directory, decision) gives a different u2 under each reading:

- Merge by key, check the effective selection: `{stage.inner.d: d1, inner.d: d2}`, so CONFLICTING_SELECTIONS fires.
- Merge by decision, with every inherited key kept at the new option: `{stage.inner.d: d2, inner.d: d2}`, so u2 is valid. This is RFC 0003's "unspecified decisions inherit their selection" (0003:78-79).
- Merge by decision, with the own spelling replacing the inherited ones: `{inner.d: d2}`. u2 then no longer names `stage`'s declaration `inner`, so PARTIALLY_DRIVEN fires.
- Merge by key, check only own `decisions`: no error, but `d` has two options. That goes against spec 531 ("selects one option for every active decision"), but 562-571 leave the rule out.

`analysis.yaml:293-297` repeats step 2's wording, so the field reference has the same gap.

**Why it matters.** astra-tools and astra-typescript can reach different verdicts on one stack: an error, a valid universe with `d2`, or a universe whose option for `d` depends on the tool. Authors can't tell whether a diff must repeat every spelling of the decision it changes.

**Suggested fix.** In §6 step 2 and the Universe description, resolve each key to its decision (its analysis's directory and its id). An own selection then sets that decision under every inherited spelling, and the inherited keys are kept, so PARTIALLY_DRIVEN is unchanged. List CONFLICTING_SELECTIONS and PARTIALLY_DRIVEN among the rules at 562-571 and in the 1141 row's wording.

### F005. NOT_BUILT_IN_UNIVERSE and UNDRIVEN_DECLARATION, as worded, reject an unpinned read of an undriven analysis's output built once, which §7 says the rules allow

- **Lens:** RFC fidelity, Implementer, Author, Internal consistency · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:720-727`, `docs/specification.md:1142`, `docs/specification.md:1143`, `docs/specification.md:804-807`, `docs/specification.md:1146`, `docs/specification.md:1149`, `src/astra/schema/generated/rules.json:557`, `src/astra/schema/generated/rules.json:564`, `rfcs/0004-composition.md:410-411`, `rfcs/0007-analyses-in-other-repositories.md:104`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:635`

**What is wrong.** Both rules cover "every output of another analysis read without a pin". Neither makes an exception for outputs built once. Only driving pushes a universe through an analysis (spec:716-718). So when an output built per universe reads, unpinned, an output built once of an undriven declaration, the declared analysis is not built in the universe being built, and the declaration is not driven. Both MUSTs fail. Yet §7 "Use" says "the rules above" let an undriven analysis from a commit give "its outputs built once (`upstream.stage.report`)". The two normative passages contradict each other.

Pinning is no way out. A pin draws `REDUNDANT_PIN` (SHOULD). If the declared analysis has no universes, a pin also breaks `UNKNOWN_PIN_UNIVERSE` (MUST), and the read then has no valid spelling at all.

The source is in the accepted RFCs. §9's rows copy RFC 0004's validator table (rfcs/0004:410-411) almost word for word, and RFC 0007:104 has the same unqualified wording. Against them stand RFC 0004:336, RFC 0007:96-99 and RFC 0006:114/125. The design records the behaviour as landed: design:635 "An undriven analysis from a commit gives pinned outputs, outputs built once and inputs | spec 7 (follows from 0004's rules)". It does not follow from the rules as written.

This is not design §13's open point (design:777-783, a decision-less analysis reading a per-universe output). Here the reader has decisions and the output read is built once. That point's probable fix ("a declaration of an analysis with no decisions of its own counts as driven") does not cover an upstream with decisions, such as the spec's own `upstream`, which also gives `upstream.summary#u1`. The draft tidy-up RFC does not mention either rule.

**Evidence.** The spec's own §7 read, made from an output built per universe (`$W/a/astra.yaml`, `W=…/scratchpad/work/repro-c013`):

```yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
analyses:
  upstream: {url: https://github.com/owner/upstream, commit: "0123456789abcdef0123456789abcdef01234567"}
decisions: {model: {options: {rf: {}, svm: {}}}}
outputs:
  fit: {type: metric, format: json, inputs: [upstream.stage.report], decisions: [model]}
universes: {ua: {decisions: {model: rf}}, ub: {decisions: {model: svm}}}
```

This document and its variants (a path declaration whose upstream has decisions and universe `u1`; a decision-less `lib`; the pinned spellings `upstream.report#u1` and `lib.report#ua`; RFC 0006's before and after) are VALID in both the Python and the Ajv validator. The verdict falls to the validator rules.

`fit` lists `decisions`, so it is built per universe (spec:596). Built in `ua`, it reads `upstream.stage.report` unpinned. No universe sets an `upstream.*` key, so `upstream` is not driven.

- spec:1142 `NOT_BUILT_IN_UNIVERSE` | MUST | "Every output of another analysis read without a pin, and every decision of another analysis, is used only where that analysis is built in the universe being built". rules.json:557 says the same.
- spec:1143 `UNDRIVEN_DECLARATION` | MUST | "In the file of the analysis whose universe is being built, every such read goes through a declaration that analysis drives". rules.json:564 says the same.
- spec:804-807: "Unless it is driven, none of the recipes of an analysis from a commit run, so the rules above let it give only its outputs in its own universes, pinned (`upstream.summary#u1`), its outputs built once (`upstream.stage.report`), and its inputs (`upstream.data`)."
- spec:1149 `REDUNDANT_PIN` | SHOULD | "No reference to an output built once has a pin". spec:1146 `UNKNOWN_PIN_UNIVERSE`: a pin through no driven declaration names a universe "of the analysis the output belongs to".
- rfcs/0004:336: "external inputs and outputs that are built once are the same in every universe and so take no pin."
- rfcs/0007:99 lists "its outputs that are built once (`upstream.subanalysis3.output3`)" as usable through an undriven declaration.

RFC 0006's migration hits the same rules. In `$W/d`, `lib.data` is an input before the migration ("Inputs carry no universe", spec:599), so the rules do not apply. Afterwards it is an output built once, and the unchanged `d/astra.yaml` breaks both. rfcs/0006:114 says readers "don't change".

**Why it matters.** A validator written from §9 or rules.json rejects per-universe work on a declared analysis's single result: a shared data-prep stage, a reference dataset, a released analysis read from a commit, which is RFC 0007's main use. A validator written from §7 accepts it. astra-tools and astra-typescript can then give the same file different verdicts. Analysis authors get an error, or a warning if they pin, on a file the RFCs call correct. If they follow RFC 0006's migration, the error lands in readers they did not edit. The JSON Schema contract is not affected.

**Suggested fix.** In §7 (spec:720) and the §9 rows, write "every output built per universe of another analysis read without a pin, and every decision of another analysis", and say that an output built once is read unpinned from any universe. Record it as a numbered clarification in the tidy-up RFC, since it narrows RFC 0004's table, and correct design:635.

### F006. An empty `when: []` or `decisions: []` is valid, and "built per universe" doesn't say whether it counts

- **Lens:** RFC fidelity, Implementer, Author, Internal consistency, Edge cases · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:596-598`, `docs/specification.md:602`, `docs/specification.md:457-462`, `docs/specification.md:864-867`, `docs/specification.md:1103`, `src/astra/schema/analysis.yaml:201-212`, `src/astra/schema/generated/analysis.schema.json:343-351`, `src/astra/schema/generated/analysis.schema.json:390-398`, `rfcs/0003-cross-universe-artifact-references.md:195-197`

**What is wrong.** §6 decides an output's scope by whether it "lists `decisions`" or "has a `when`". The contract accepts both lists empty: `Output.decisions` and `Output.when` have no `minItems`, and `EMPTY_COLLECTION` covers only `outputs`, `Decision.options` and `Insight.evidence`. The spec doesn't say whether an empty list counts. The case is clearest for `when: []`. The text keys on the field being present: such an output "has a `when`" and is conditional (spec:457). Yet every one of its zero conditions holds, so it is active in every universe (spec:461-462). RFC 0003 gave the reason for the clause: "has a `when` (whether it exists depends on the universe)". By that reason, `when: []` is built once. §6 leaves the reason out. `decisions: []` is a weaker case, since spec:602 ("It lists no decisions") favours built once, but line 596 still tests the field.

**Evidence.** Both validators accept this document. The control, `outputs: {}`, fails with `EMPTY_COLLECTION` in both.

```yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
decisions:
  model:
    options: {rf: {}, svm: {}}
outputs:
  a: {type: metric, format: json, decisions: []}
  b: {type: metric, format: json, when: []}
  c: {type: table, format: csv, inputs: [a#u1, b#u1]}
universes:
  u1: {decisions: {model: rf}}
findings:
  f: {claim: A is high., evidence: [{artifact: a}]}
```

```
python jsonschema: empty-output-lists.yaml VALID
ajv:               empty-output-lists.yaml VALID
```

In the generated schema, Output `decisions` (343-351) and `when` (390-398) have only `items`, `type: array` and `uniqueItems`.

`docs/specification.md:596-598`: "An output is **built per universe** when it lists `decisions`, has a `when`, or has an unpinned entry of `inputs` that names an output built per universe."

`docs/specification.md:1103`: "`outputs`, `Decision.options` and `Insight.evidence` each have at least one entry".

The two readings differ for `a` and `b` in `u1`. The record is at `.astra/records/u1/a.json` or at `.astra/records/a.json` (spec:864-867). `c`'s pins are correct or `REDUNDANT_PIN`. `artifact: a` gets `UNPINNED_EVIDENCE` or no warning. The default idioms of the two implementation languages also split: `Boolean([])` is `true` in JavaScript, `bool([])` is `False` in Python.

**Why it matters.** A runner writes the record in one place and a checker of the other reading reports `MISSING_RECORD` or `MISPLACED_RECORD`. Their warnings differ too. astra-tools and astra-typescript can diverge if each uses its language's default test. Scaffolds, generators and an author removing the last decision all produce empty lists.

**Suggested fix.** Give `Output.when` and `Output.decisions` `minimum_cardinality: 1` (and widen `EMPTY_COLLECTION` at spec:1103, with an invalid fixture). Or word §6 as "lists at least one decision, or has a non-empty `when`".

### F007. Evidence that cites a per-universe output without a pin is valid, but the check gives it no status

- **Lens:** RFC fidelity, Implementer, Author, Internal consistency · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:519-521`, `docs/specification.md:856-858`, `docs/specification.md:864-869`, `docs/specification.md:1016-1019`, `docs/specification.md:1150`, `docs/specification.md:1163-1164`, `docs/specification.md:1185`, `rfcs/0003-cross-universe-artifact-references.md:219-221`, `rfcs/0004-composition.md:414`, `rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:203`

**What is wrong.** UNPINNED_EVIDENCE is a SHOULD. Evidence that cites an output built per universe without a pin is therefore valid, with a warning. The check layer gives evidence a status through "the record its address names", and assumes that address is pinned. An unpinned address names one result per universe, not one result. Neither §8 nor STALE_EVIDENCE's row says what status such evidence gets. The spec copies RFC 0005:203 word for word. RFC 0003 (219-221, 240) and RFC 0004 (414) make the missing pin a warning only. The gap lies between these RFCs, and design §11-13 does not list it.

**Evidence.** A minimal analysis:

```yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
outputs:
  accuracy: {type: metric, format: json, decisions: [model]}
decisions:
  model: {options: {rf: {}, svm: {}}}
universes:
  u1: {decisions: {model: rf}}
  u2: {decisions: {model: svm}}
findings:
  accurate:
    claim: The classifier reaches 0.93 accuracy.
    evidence:
      - artifact: accuracy
```

Both validators (Python `jsonschema` and Ajv) report it VALID. So does the §2 example (spec:101-151) with `- artifact: accuracy` in its finding. At the validator layer it breaks only UNPINNED_EVIDENCE: `| UNPINNED_EVIDENCE | SHOULD | Every evidence citing an output built per universe pins it | 0003, 0004 |` (spec:1150). An analysis "is valid when it breaks none of their `MUST`s" (spec:90-91).

The check text:

- spec:1016-1019: "Evidence citing a result gets the status of the result's record (STALE_EVIDENCE), through the record its address names: the address, pinned when the output is built per universe, names exactly one result."
- spec:1185 (and `rules.json`): `| STALE_EVIDENCE | MUST | Every evidence citing a result cites an unchanged one | the cited result's status | 0005 §5 |`.

The planned results are `accuracy#u1` and `accuracy#u2` (spec:856-858). Their records are at `.astra/records/u1/accuracy.json` and `.astra/records/u2/accuracy.json` (spec:864-866). The evidence's address `accuracy` is neither. Run through the table at spec:864-869, it gives `.astra/records/accuracy.json`, where no record exists. `grep -n -i 'unpinned\|without a pin' docs/specification.md` finds nothing on evidence beyond spec:519-520.

A checker can read this three ways: `missing`, from the built-once path; the worst status of `accuracy#u1` and `accuracy#u2`; or no status, because the evidence cites no single result.

**Why it matters.** Checker authors must invent an answer. astra-tools and astra-typescript can give the same finding in a valid analysis different statuses: `missing`, `stale`, or none. A reader can see a finding as fine while every result it rests on is stale.

**Suggested fix.** State the status in §8 and in STALE_EVIDENCE's row: for example, the worst status of the output's planned results. Making UNPINNED_EVIDENCE a MUST contradicts RFC 0003 §3 and RFC 0004:414, so it needs a change listed in the tidy-up RFC.

### F008. `Output.inputs`'s normative description says a reference to an input can be pinned, which `PIN_ON_INPUT` forbids

- **Lens:** RFC fidelity, Implementer, Author, Internal consistency · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `src/astra/schema/analysis.yaml:198-200`, `src/astra/schema/generated/analysis.schema.json:362`, `docs/specification.md:383-385`, `docs/specification.md:617-618`, `docs/specification.md:1145`, `rfcs/0003-cross-universe-artifact-references.md:181-182`

**What is wrong.** The description of `Output.inputs` says every entry, a reference to an input or to an output, "can be pinned to a universe with `#`". The specification and RFC 0003 allow a pin only on an output, as a MUST. Spec §1 and §10 make field descriptions the normative definitions of the fields, and nothing says which source wins when they disagree. So two normative sources disagree, and the description is the one that departs from the RFC.

The likely origin is RFC 0003 §2. It opens broadly ("Entries in `Output.inputs` may pin an artifact reference to a universe", line 166) and narrows the statement to outputs only at lines 181-182. The description kept the opening and dropped the restriction. The wording dates from 0aa0188. Design §11-§13 and the tidy-up RFC do not record it.

**Evidence.**

`src/astra/schema/analysis.yaml:198-200` (copied verbatim to `generated/analysis.schema.json:362`):

> What the output is made from, in order: references to inputs and outputs, each of which can be pinned to a universe with `#`.

The descriptions are normative:
- `docs/specification.md:29-31`: "whose descriptions define its fields"
- `docs/specification.md:1213-1214`: "Their descriptions are the normative definitions of the fields"

Against the description:
- §5, `docs/specification.md:384-385`: "A reference to an output can be pinned (§6)."
- §6, `docs/specification.md:617-618`: "Only an output takes a pin ([`PIN_ON_INPUT`](#validator))"
- §9, `docs/specification.md:1145`: "| `PIN_ON_INPUT` | MUST | Every pinned reference names an output | 0003 |"
- RFC 0003, lines 181-182: "A pin is only valid on references that resolve to an *Output*: an external `Input` does not vary by universe, so pinning it is an error."

The rule is in the validator layer, so the contract correctly accepts a pinned input. At the schema layer, the description is the only thing an author sees:

```yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
inputs:
  data:
    path: data.csv
outputs:
  table:
    type: table
    format: csv
    inputs: [data#u1]
    decisions: [d]
decisions:
  d:
    options:
      a: {}
universes:
  u1:
    decisions:
      d: a
```
```
$ uv run python toolkit/validate.py Analysis pinned-input.yaml   -> VALID
$ node toolkit/js/validate.mjs <repo> Analysis pinned-input.yaml -> VALID
```

**Why it matters.** Authors read this text in the field reference (`docs/elements/Output.md`) and as editor hover text from the JSON Schema. It tells them `data#u1` is fine, and every validator must then reject the file with `PIN_ON_INPUT`. The brief rates an inconsistency between two normative sources Important (brief:198). A careful reader can resolve it through §9, so the harm is to authors, not to validator implementers.

**Suggested fix.** Change the description to "What the output is made from, in order: references to inputs and outputs; a reference to an output can be pinned to a universe with `#`." (`Evidence.artifact`, `insight.yaml:77-80`, already uses this kind of wording.) Then run `just gen-schema`.

### F009. Every validator must report `PIN_LIKE_COMMENT`, but RFC 0003 made the check only recommended

- **Lens:** RFC fidelity, Standards · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:89-91`, `docs/specification.md:1152`, `rfcs/0003-cross-universe-artifact-references.md:239-242`, `rfcs/0003-cross-universe-artifact-references.md:418-419`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:551`

**What is wrong.** RFC 0003's validator table marks one row "warning (recommended)": the lint for a stray space that turns a pin into a comment. Every other warning in that table is a plain "warning". The RFC also calls the stray space a cost "mitigated by a validator lint". The table marks only this row, so the natural reading is that implementing the check is optional. The check needs the source text, and many loaders drop comments. The spec has no such qualifier. §9 makes `PIN_LIKE_COMMENT` an ordinary SHOULD rule, and §2 says a validator reports every parse, schema and validator rule the analysis breaks. A validator that skips this check therefore doesn't conform. Nobody decided this. The design's first commit (e26bef7) kept the RFC's "warning (recommended)" word for word. The catalogue rewrite (baf6e31, "Standardise the rule catalogue and firm up the design's rationale") turned it into a bare SHOULD, and its message doesn't mention the change. Design §11 (17 changes) doesn't list the departure, and neither does change 28 of the tidy-up RFC. Design §10:551 maps the RFC row with no qualifier and counts it as landed.

The §9 Level column can't express this. The level applies to the rule's sentence, so to the document, and SHOULD only makes the code a warning.

**Evidence.**

```
rfcs/0003:239  | A pin on an output built once | warning |
rfcs/0003:240  | Evidence citing an output built per universe without a pin | warning |
rfcs/0003:241  | Decisions declared but no universes | warning |
rfcs/0003:242  | An `inputs` entry whose line-comment looks like a pin | warning (recommended) |
rfcs/0003:418  Cost accepted: the YAML stray-space comment footgun, mitigated by a
rfcs/0003:419  validator lint.
```

`docs/specification.md:89-91`: "A **validator** reads an analysis, every `astra.yaml` of its stack and git, and reports every parse, schema and validator rule the analysis breaks. An analysis is valid when it breaks none of their `MUST`s."

`docs/specification.md:1152`: "| `PIN_LIKE_COMMENT` | SHOULD | No `inputs` entry has a line comment that looks like a pin (needs the source text) | 0003 |"

Nothing in the spec exempts a validator from any rule. The duties outside the catalogue (spec:22-27) don't include this one, and `rules.json` carries the rule as `"layer": "validator", "level": "SHOULD"` with no optional marker.

```
$ git show e26bef7:design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md | grep -n PIN_LIKE
335:| `PIN_LIKE_COMMENT` | warning (recommended) | An `inputs` entry's line comment doesn't look like a pin (needs the source text) | 0003 |
$ git show baf6e31 | grep -n PIN_LIKE
145:-| `PIN_LIKE_COMMENT` | warning (recommended) | ...
180:+| `PIN_LIKE_COMMENT` | SHOULD | No `inputs` entry has a line comment that looks like a pin ...
```

The two documents below differ only in this line. A plain loader can't tell the stray space from a plain reference:

```yaml
    inputs:
      - f1_score #rf     # stray-space.yaml
      - f1_score#rf      # pinned.yaml
```

```
Python validator, Ajv validator:  VALID, VALID
ruamel safe load / YAML.parse:    ["f1_score"]   vs ["f1_score#rf"]
yaml parseDocument:               "f1_score", comment: "rf"
```

**Why it matters.** It affects validator authors whose pipeline loads YAML to data (JSON plus Ajv, astra-typescript on pre-parsed documents). They conform under RFC 0003 without this check. Under the spec they don't, unless they add a second, comment-preserving parse (`parseDocument`, ruamel round-trip). Two validators will disagree on whether `- f1_score #rf` gets a warning, and a conformance suite can't say which one is right. Validity doesn't change: the rule is a SHOULD.

**Suggested fix.** In §2's validator bullet, say that reporting `PIN_LIKE_COMMENT` is RECOMMENDED, not required, because it needs the source text. Don't put this in the §9 row: the extractor rejects "OPTIONAL" or "MAY" in a SHOULD row. Or record the tightening as a numbered change in design §11 and the tidy-up RFC.

### F010. A Windows drive path (`C:/x`, `C:x`) passes as a relative input or declaration `path`

- **Lens:** RFC fidelity, Edge cases · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `src/astra/schema/analysis.yaml:156`, `src/astra/schema/analysis.yaml:365`, `src/astra/schema/analysis.yaml:375`, `docs/specification.md:344-348`, `docs/specification.md:633-636`, `docs/specification.md:648-653`, `docs/specification.md:1107`, `rfcs/0004-composition.md:402`, `rfcs/0006-data-inputs.md:83`

**What is wrong.** RFC 0004 and RFC 0006 require an input's or a declaration's `path` to be relative. The specification defines "relative" only as "no leading `/` or `~`", so both path patterns accept a drive prefix. The url pattern has a `(?![A-Za-z]:)` guard and the path patterns do not. As a result the spec rejects `C:/repo` as a `url` because it is "a local path" and "a Windows drive path", yet accepts the same string as a relative `path`. Python jsonschema and Ajv agree on every verdict, so the defect is in the contract and the spec, not a disagreement between validators. Design §11 item 15 records the separator and control-character rules as a departure. It says nothing about drive prefixes, and nothing in the spec says paths are resolved as POSIX paths.

**Evidence.** The patterns (`src/astra/schema/analysis.yaml`, HEAD 96c902a):

```
156  pattern: '^(?![/~])(?!(?:[^\x00-\x1f\x7f]*/)?\.\.(?:/|$))(?![^\x00-\x1f\x7f]*/$)[^\x00-\x1f\x7f\\]+$(?!\n)'   # Input.path
365  pattern: '^(?![/~])[^\x00-\x1f\x7f\\]+$(?!\n)'                                                                     # AnalysisDeclaration.path
375  pattern: '^(?:[A-Za-z][A-Za-z0-9+.-]*://[^\x00-\x20\x7f]+|(?![A-Za-z]:)(?:...'                                    # url: drive guard
```

Each document is `spec_version: "0.1.0"` and `outputs: {result: {type: metric, format: json}}`, plus one line:

```
inputs:   {data: {path: "C:/data.csv"}}                    VALID
inputs:   {data: {path: "C:data.csv"}}                     VALID
analyses: {up: {path: "C:/repo"}}                          VALID
analyses: {up: {path: "C:repo"}}                           VALID
analyses: {up: {url: "C:/repo", commit: "d1570f8..."}}     INVALID_VALUE at /analyses/up/url
```

The verdicts are the same under jsonschema and Ajv. Path libraries read the same strings differently:

```
PureWindowsPath('C:/data.csv').is_absolute()           -> True
PurePosixPath('/home/me/analysis') / 'C:/repo'         -> /home/me/analysis/C:/repo
path.win32.resolve('D:\\work\\analysis', 'C:/data.csv') -> C:\data.csv
path.win32.isAbsolute('C:/repo')                        -> true
```

`C:data.csv` is drive-relative on Windows (`is_absolute()` is False), but `win32.resolve` still sends it to drive C's current directory, outside the analysis.

`docs/specification.md:648-653`: "A bare word or a local path (`a`, `owner/repo`, `../repo`, `C:/repo`) is not a `url` … A host of one letter is not scp-like, so that a Windows drive path is never read as a remote". `docs/specification.md:1107` (INVALID_PATH): "A declaration's `path` is relative (no leading `/` or `~`) …". The tidy-up draft uses the same parenthetical in change 26 (l.191). Its Context (l.49) motivates the change with "`data\x.csv` is one file on Linux and a directory and a file on Windows", and change 24 (l.183) says "`C:/repo` is a Windows path". The only Windows fixtures, Analysis-128 and Analysis-156, use backslashes. No fixture, example or spec passage has a drive-prefixed `path`.

**Why it matters.** Change 26 exists so that a path means the same thing on every OS, and this case escapes it. A validator or runner on Windows resolves `C:/data.csv` outside the analysis. One on POSIX resolves it to `<dir>/C:/data.csv`. The same file then fails under different codes on different machines. On Linux it is a missing file. On Windows it is outside the repository or untracked. A Windows author can paste an absolute path into the analysis's own folder, and it passes locally but fails MISSING_INPUT_FILE on Linux CI. The schema layer never flags it. No §9 code says an input lies inside its own analysis's directory. INPUT_IN_ANOTHER_ANALYSIS (l.1132) covers only other analyses.

**Suggested fix.** Add `(?![A-Za-z]:)` after `^` in both path patterns. Say "no drive prefix such as `C:`" in §5, §7, the INVALID_PATH row and tidy-up change 26. Add invalid fixtures with `path: "C:/x"` on an input and on a declaration. The alternative is to state that paths are always resolved as POSIX paths.

### F011. The `url` pattern accepts a one-letter scp host after `user@`, though §7 and tidy-up 24 say a one-letter host is not scp-like

- **Lens:** RFC fidelity, Internal consistency, Edge cases · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:652-653`, `src/astra/schema/analysis.yaml:375`, `src/astra/schema/generated/analysis.schema.json:46`, `astra-rfcs/tidy-up/astra-spec/rfcs/NNNN-schema-tidy-up.md:183`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:238-239`

**What is wrong.** Spec §7, tidy-up change 24 and design §4 all state a rule about the host: a one-letter host is not scp-like. The pattern enforces something else. Its guard `(?![A-Za-z]:)` comes before the optional `user@`, so it tests only the first two characters of the whole value. `c:repo` and `C:/repo` fail, but `git@c:repo` and `x@C:/repo`, each with a one-letter host, pass. The justification "as git itself does" is also wrong as worded: only Git for Windows treats a leading letter and `:` as a drive. On Linux and macOS git reads `C:/repo` as ssh to host `C`. No git build excludes a one-letter host after `user@`. The pattern matches Git for Windows' start-of-string rule; the prose describes neither the pattern nor git.

**Evidence.** The texts:

> A host of one letter is not scp-like, so that a Windows drive path is never read as a remote, as git itself does. (`docs/specification.md:652-653`)

> `[user@]host:path` (`git@github.com:owner/repo`), with a host of more than one letter since `C:/repo` is a Windows path. (tidy-up `NNNN-schema-tidy-up.md:183`)

> The one-letter-host exclusion keeps Windows drive paths out of the scp-like form, as git itself does. (`design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:238-239`)

The contract (`analysis.yaml:375`):

```
'^(?:[A-Za-z][A-Za-z0-9+.-]*://[^\x00-\x20\x7f]+|(?![A-Za-z]:)(?:[^\x00-\x20\x7f/@:]+@)?[^\x00-\x20\x7f/@:]+:[^\x00-\x20\x7f]+)$(?!\n)'
```

Minimal document, varied by `url`:

```yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
outputs:
  o: {type: metric, format: json}
analyses:
  up:
    url: "git@c:repo"
    commit: "0dbb615a0fa1b1d6ecf3158e97e35b57c606beb3"
```

Python `jsonschema` and Ajv agree on every value:

```
git@c:repo        VALID
git@g:owner/repo  VALID
x@C:/repo         VALID
c:repo            /analyses/up/url | …#/$defs/AnalysisDeclaration__identifier_optional/properties/url/pattern | INVALID_VALUE
C:/repo           (same INVALID_VALUE)
```

An exhaustive comparison of the pattern with a predicate written from the prose (every string up to length 6 over `aC:/@1` and space, 137,256 strings) finds 360 disagreements, all of the form `user@<one letter>:path`.

Git 2.56.0 on Linux, with a logging `GIT_SSH_COMMAND`:

```
git ls-remote C:/repo     -> ssh-args: -o SendEnv=GIT_PROTOCOL C git-upload-pack '/repo'
git ls-remote git@c:repo  -> ssh-args: -o SendEnv=GIT_PROTOCOL git@c git-upload-pack 'repo'
git ls-remote x@C:/repo   -> ssh-args: -o SendEnv=GIT_PROTOCOL x@C git-upload-pack '/repo'
```

git's `url.c` `url_is_local_not_ssh` returns `!colon || (slash && slash < colon) || (has_dos_drive_prefix(url) && is_valid_path(url))`, a test on the start of the string; `git-compat-util.h` defines `has_dos_drive_prefix` to return 0 except under `compat/win32`.

No fixture covers `user@<one letter>:`. The only drive fixture is `tests/data/invalid/Analysis-180-url-drive-path.yaml` (`C:/repo`). The LinkML description (`analysis.yaml:378-386`) is silent on one-letter hosts and agrees with the pattern.

**Why it matters.** A validator or editor written from §7 or tidy-up 24 (astra-typescript, an editor plug-in) rejects `git@g:owner/repo`, where `g` is an ssh-config alias, while every JSON Schema validator accepts it. The tidy-up RFC would carry the wrong statement into the record. The contract's verdict agrees with RFC 0007:58 ("Where git can fetch another repository"), and one-letter ssh hosts are rare, so the harm is to implementers, not to real documents.

**Suggested fix.** Reword §7, tidy-up 24 and design §4 to say what is enforced: "a value that starts with one letter and `:` (a Windows drive path, as Git for Windows reads it) is not scp-like", and add a valid fixture with `git@c:owner/repo`. If one-letter hosts are meant to be excluded, move the guard after the optional `user@` and add an invalid fixture instead.

### F012. A `url` with a space is `INVALID_VALUE`, though git fetches it and no text excludes it

- **Lens:** RFC fidelity, Edge cases · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `src/astra/schema/analysis.yaml:375`, `src/astra/schema/analysis.yaml:378-386`, `docs/specification.md:644-656`, `docs/specification.md:1112`, `rfcs/0007-analyses-in-other-repositories.md:58`, tidy-up RFC change 24 (`NNNN-schema-tidy-up.md:183`), `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:700-701`

**What is wrong.** The `url` pattern uses `[^\x00-\x20\x7f]` for every part of the URL, so a `url` with an ASCII space fails in every form: `file://`, `ssh://`, `https://` and scp-like. Git fetches such a remote as written. RFC 0007 defines `url` as "Where git can fetch another repository (a remote) from". Tidy-up change 24, spec §7 and the LinkML description narrow it to "git's two remote forms", and say a bare word or a local path is not a `url`. None of them says a `url` has no space, or that a space must be percent-encoded. The design intended the restriction (its pattern at `design/...:235` has `\S+`), but section 11 item 12 doesn't list it as a departure. The contract is self-consistent: Ajv and jsonschema agree. It is stricter than all the normative prose.

**Evidence.** Each document has `spec_version: "0.1.0"`, one output, and `analyses: {up: {url: <U>, commit: "d1570f85f45af286e965bf70c451031763c5deee"}}`. Same result from jsonschema and Ajv:

```
"file:///home/me/My Repos/upstream"     /analyses/up/url | ...#/$defs/AnalysisDeclaration__identifier_optional/properties/url/pattern | INVALID_VALUE
"host:my repo"                          -> same error
"ssh://git@host/my repo.git"            -> same error
"https://example.org/my repo"           -> same error
"file:///home/me/My%20Repos/upstream"           -> VALID
"ssh://git@nas/~/shares/My%20Repos/upstream"    -> VALID
"git@nas:shares/My%20Repos/upstream"            -> VALID
```

Git 2.56 fetches the rejected `file://` URL, and decodes `%20` in the scheme form but not in the scp-like form:

```
$ git ls-remote "file://$G/My Repos/upstream"
5021e4845ee5d7053b9c1eb900104b6f6e06e3e6	HEAD
$ git ls-remote "file://$G/My%20Repos/upstream"
5021e4845ee5d7053b9c1eb900104b6f6e06e3e6	HEAD
$ GIT_SSH_COMMAND=./fakessh.sh git ls-remote 'host:my repo'
ARGS: [host] [git-upload-pack 'my repo']
$ GIT_SSH_COMMAND=./fakessh.sh git ls-remote 'ssh://git@nas/~/shares/My%20Repos/upstream'
ARGS: [git@nas] [git-upload-pack '~/shares/My Repos/upstream']
$ GIT_SSH_COMMAND=./fakessh.sh git ls-remote 'git@nas:shares/My%20Repos/upstream'
ARGS: [git@nas] [git-upload-pack 'shares/My%20Repos/upstream']
```

No fixture or text in `tests/`, `docs/specification.md`, the schema modules or `design/` covers a space in a `url`. The class excludes only ASCII space and controls: a `url` with U+00A0 is valid to both validators.

**Why it matters.** Paths with spaces are common (`C:/Users/John Smith`, macOS `Mobile Documents`). An author or tool that copies `git remote get-url` into `url:` gets `INVALID_VALUE` for a remote git fetches, and nothing says how to write it. Every such repository has an accepted spelling: percent-encode it in a `scheme://` URL. That spelling is found only by trial. There is a trap: `git@nas:shares/My%20Repos/upstream` is valid to the contract, but git asks for a different path.

**Suggested fix.** Say in the LinkML description, spec §7 and tidy-up change 24 that a `url` has no space or ASCII control character, and that a space is written `%20` in a `scheme://` URL (git doesn't decode the scp-like form). Add an invalid fixture. Or allow spaces in the path part.

### F013. `DECLARATION_OUTSIDE_REPOSITORY` judges every `path` against the root's repository, so it rejects the path declarations inside an analysis from a `url`

- **Lens:** RFC fidelity, Author · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:637-638`, `docs/specification.md:1124`, `src/astra/schema/analysis.yaml:369-371`, `docs/specification.md:673-675`, `docs/specification.md:639-642`, `docs/specification.md:763-766`, `docs/specification.md:650-651`, `src/astra/schema/analysis.yaml:383-384`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:569`

**What is wrong.** The rule, its §9 row (and so `rules.json`) and the normative description of `AnalysisDeclaration.path` say a declaration's `path` stays inside the repository of the root. The root is the analysis being validated. The spec applies the rule to declarations anywhere in the stack, not only the root's own: a submodule's `..` is judged against the root's repository. An analysis declared by `url` lives in another repository. Its own `path` declarations resolve inside that remote, never inside the root's repository. Read as written, every one of them breaks a MUST, including those in RFC 0007's own examples. RFC 0007 defines `path` by "the declaring analysis's repository". The spec and the LinkML description replaced that with RFC 0004's wording, which RFC 0004 marked as provisional until remotes arrived. Design §11 doesn't list this as a departure, and design:569 carries over only RFC 0004's row.

**Evidence.**

- spec:637-638: "It stays inside the repository of the root ([`DECLARATION_OUTSIDE_REPOSITORY`](#validator))"
- spec:1124: "A declaration's `path` stays inside the repository of the root"
- analysis.yaml:369-371: "relative to the folder of this `astra.yaml` and inside the repository of the root analysis."
- spec:673: "The **root** is the analysis being validated or built"
- spec:640-642: a submodule that declares outside its own repository "can be validated and built only from a root whose repository holds what it declares."
- spec:765-766: "Every rule of this specification applies to an analysis from a commit as it is, with its files read at its commit." Read literally, "root" still means the analysis being validated.
- rfcs/0007:57: "`path` ... The directory of an analysis in the declaring analysis's repository, as in RFC 0004"
- rfcs/0007:61: "If it has analyses of its own they are reached by address through it (`upstream.subanalysis3.output3`)". rfcs/0007:242 uses `iris_knn.feature_extraction.method`, and `feature_extraction` is a `path` declaration inside the iris-pipeline remote.
- rfcs/0004:63: "within the repository of the root analysis ... Anything outside the root's repository is left to the later RFC."

The following rewrite of RFC 0007 §1 on top of RFC 0004 Example 1 is valid at the schema layer, so the verdict depends on this validator rule:

```yaml
# followup/astra.yaml (the root)
spec_version: "0.1.0"
analyses:
  upstream:
    url: https://github.com/owner/analysis
    commit: "0f2ce11579a60f84e4dd6a06b8da589991bd078e"
outputs:
  report: {type: report, format: json, inputs: ["upstream.summary#u1", upstream.subanalysis3.output3]}
# top of github.com/owner/analysis:  analyses: {subanalysis2: {path: subanalysis2}, subanalysis3: {path: subanalysis3}}
# subanalysis3/astra.yaml:           analyses: {root: {path: ..}, subanalysis2: {path: ../subanalysis2}}
```
```
$ uv run python toolkit/validate.py Analysis <the four files>   # jsonschema
VALID (x4)
$ node toolkit/js/validate.mjs <repo> Analysis <the four files> # Ajv
VALID (x4)
```

With `followup` as the root, all four `path`s resolve inside github.com/owner/analysis, not inside followup's repository.

**Why it matters.** Validator authors reach opposite verdicts on RFC 0007's own examples. One applies §9 to every declaration in the stack against the root's repository and rejects any remote analysis that has path-declared sub-analyses, which is the normal case. Another measures each `path` against the repository it is read from and accepts them. astra-tools and astra-typescript can disagree, and editors that show the `path` description repeat the root-only wording.

**Suggested fix.** Word the rule and the `path` description against the repository the declaring `astra.yaml` is read from: the root's repository, submodules included, for the working tree, and the remote's repository at its commit for an analysis from a `url`. This keeps the submodule case at spec:639-642. Change the same sentence at spec:650-651 and analysis.yaml:383-384.

### F014. Nothing says how a `path` is resolved: lexically or through the file system (symlinks, `.git/`, case, Unicode normalisation)

- **Lens:** RFC fidelity, Implementer, Edge cases · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:344-345`, `docs/specification.md:350`, `docs/specification.md:352-359`, `docs/specification.md:633-639`, `docs/specification.md:664-665`, `docs/specification.md:765-766`, `docs/specification.md:768-770`, `docs/specification.md:774-775`, `docs/specification.md:1124-1125`, `docs/specification.md:1130-1132`, `docs/specification.md:1141`, `docs/specification.md:1196`, `src/astra/schema/analysis.yaml:154-163`, `src/astra/schema/analysis.yaml:364-373`, `rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:161`, `rfcs/0006-data-inputs.md:54-55`

**What is wrong.** The rules on input and declaration paths ("names one file", "is tracked in git", "holds an `astra.yaml`", "inside the analysis's directory", "inside the repository of the root", "the same directory is the same analysis") never say how a `path` reaches its file. It can be compared as written against git's index or tree, or opened through the file system. RFCs 0004, 0006 and 0007, the design (§11–§13) and the tidy-up RFC are silent too. Change 27, "Paths are written one way", covers separators, control characters, `..` and a trailing `/`, not this. The two readings disagree in four places:

| Facet | As written / against git | Through the file system |
|---|---|---|
| Symlinked declaration folder (`b: {path: stage_link}` beside `a: {path: stage}`) | two analyses; a universe choosing `a.d: d1, b.d: d2` is valid | one analysis; the universe breaks `CONFLICTING_SELECTIONS` |
| Declaration link out of the repository (`ext_link -> ../elsewhere/ext`) | passes `DECLARATION_OUTSIDE_REPOSITORY`; edits there escape `UNCOMMITTED_CHANGES` | fails it |
| Symlink at an input path (git-annex link; link into `other/`; link out of the repository) | all pass, so an analysis can take another analysis's or an outside file | git-annex inputs fail the location rule for any analysis below the repository top; the others fail `INPUT_IN_ANOTHER_ANALYSIS` / `INPUT_NOT_TRACKED` |
| `path: .git/x` | inside the repository | outside git's work tree, never tracked |
| `data/Iris.csv` with `data/iris.csv` tracked, NFD `café` with NFC tracked, on a case- or normalisation-insensitive file system | `MISSING_INPUT_FILE` / `INPUT_NOT_TRACKED` fail | the file opens; `INPUT_NOT_TRACKED` passes or fails depending on how git is asked |

**Evidence.** The only text on declaration paths: "**`path`** is the directory of the declared analysis's `astra.yaml`, relative to the folder of the declaring `astra.yaml`. … It stays inside the repository of the root" (`docs/specification.md:633-638`); "The same directory is the same analysis … it is read once, and built once per universe" (`:664-665`). `grep -rn -i -E "symlink|symbolic|realpath|normali[sz]|canonical|lexical"` over `docs/` (less `docs/elements`), `rfcs/`, `design/` and `src/` finds nothing on paths. Every document below is VALID under both Python `jsonschema` and Ajv, so only the validator layer decides.

*Symlinked declarations* (scratch repository, all committed, git 2.56.0):

```yaml
spec_version: 0.1.0
analyses:
  a: {path: stage}
  b: {path: stage_link}
  e: {path: ext_link}
outputs:
  r: {type: data, format: csv, inputs: [a.o, b.o]}
universes:
  u1:
    decisions: {a.d: d1, b.d: d2}
```

```
stage_link  lexical-inside=True  realpath-inside=True   realpath=stage
ext_link    lexical-inside=True  realpath-inside=False  realpath=../elsewhere/ext
same dir? lexical: False  realpath: True
```

After `echo '# changed' >> ../elsewhere/ext/astra.yaml`, `git status --porcelain` is empty. With `inner_link -> deep/inner`, `path: inner_link/../other` opens `deep/other/astra.yaml` from Python's `open()` and `other/astra.yaml` from Node's `path.join`. At a commit the git command decides: `git cat-file -p HEAD:stage_link/astra.yaml` gives `fatal: path 'stage_link/astra.yaml' exists on disk, but not in 'HEAD'`, while `git cat-file --batch-check --follow-symlinks` finds the blob.

*Symlinked inputs* (git-annex via `nix shell nixpkgs#git-annex`; §5 allows a git-annex link as a stand-in, spec:356-358):

```
120000 d5a013f7... 0	sub/data.csv
readlink: ../.git/annex/objects/QW/GM/SHA256E-s8--492d5ea4....csv/...
lexical path inside sub/: True
resolved inside sub/: False
analysis/data.csv: resolved inside analysis/: False
after git annex drop: islink True | isfile False | exists False
-- data.csv -> ../other/data.csv: tracked True | as written inside sub/: True | resolved inside other/: True
-- ext.csv (outside link):        tracked True | as written inside sub/: True | resolved inside the repository: False
```

Since `INVALID_PATH` already forbids a leading `/`, `~` and `..`, "inside the analysis's directory" (spec:352) can only fail when links are resolved. A dropped git-annex link dangles, so `MISSING_INPUT_FILE` can't be checked by stat alone. A path through a symlinked folder is already refused by `git add` ("beyond a symbolic link").

*`.git/`*: `analyses: {in_git: {path: .git/x}}` is schema-valid (pattern `^(?![/~])[^\x00-\x1f\x7f\\]+$(?!\n)`, analysis.yaml:365). With `.git/x/astra.yaml` written, `git status --porcelain --untracked-files=all` is empty, `git ls-files --error-unmatch` exits 1, and inside it `git rev-parse --is-inside-work-tree` is `false`.

*Case and normalisation* (clone into a `tmpfs -o casefold` with `chattr +F`, Linux 7.2.9, standing in for APFS and NTFS):

```
## core.ignorecase: true
data/Iris.csv       | test -f: exists | ls-files exact: [] | --error-unmatch exit=1 | :(icase): [data/iris.csv]    | HEAD:<path>: not found
Stage/astra.yaml    | test -f: exists | ls-files exact: [] | --error-unmatch exit=1 | :(icase): [stage/astra.yaml] | HEAD:<path>: not found
data/café.csv (NFD) | test -f: exists | ls-files exact: [] | --error-unmatch exit=1 | :(icase): []                 | HEAD:<path>: not found
```

`git status --porcelain --untracked-files=all -- data/Iris.csv` prints nothing, so an "exists and is not untracked" check passes. So `path: Stage` passes `MISSING_DECLARED_ANALYSIS` without a `commit` and fails with one. `stage` and `Stage` share an inode (`os.path.samefile` True) but have different realpaths, so "the same directory" splits again. Apple's APFS FAQ: case-insensitive by default and "normalization-insensitive in both the case-insensitive and case-sensitive variants". Nothing was run on macOS or Windows.

**Why it matters.** astra-tools and astra-typescript must each pick a lookup, and natural Python and Node code already read different files for the same `path`. The same stack is then valid in one tool and invalid in the other, passes on a Mac and fails in Linux CI, or passes as the root and fails when declared at a commit, against spec:796, "A commit's files never change, so neither does the answer". Results get different names. An outward link, or a path under `.git/`, puts an analysis's files beyond `UNCOMMITTED_CHANGES` (spec:1196, "no changes other than the results and records it writes"; RFC 0005:161, "Only committed code is run"), so the record's `commit` pins nothing of them. An as-written input check lets an analysis take another analysis's data, against RFC 0006:54, "The data belongs with the analysis that uses it".

**Suggested fix.** In §5, §7 and the §9 rows, state that a `path` is resolved lexically, as an entry of git's index (or its tree at `commit`) inside the work tree, by exact code points, never through the file system; "the same directory" uses the same comparison. Forbid a symlinked folder on a declaration path, and accept a symlink at an input path only as a recognised git-annex stand-in, with a code for any other.

### F015. §9 states INPUT_IN_ANOTHER_ANALYSIS more broadly than §5 and RFC 0006, and rejects every input of a nested analysis

- **Lens:** RFC fidelity, Implementer, Internal consistency · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:1132`, `src/astra/schema/generated/rules.json:483-487`, `docs/specification.md:352-355`, `docs/specification.md:159`, `docs/specification.md:300-303`, `rfcs/0006-data-inputs.md:54`, `rfcs/0006-data-inputs.md:84`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:382`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:618`

**What is wrong.** The specification states INPUT_IN_ANOTHER_ANALYSIS twice, and the two statements disagree. §9 says an input's file is "outside every other analysis's directory". §5 says it is "inside the analysis's directory and outside every analysis within it". An analysis is a directory with an `astra.yaml` at its top (§3), so a nested analysis's directory lies inside its parent's. Every input file of a nested analysis is therefore inside another analysis's directory, the enclosing one, and §9 read literally rejects it. §5 accepts it. The §9 row copies RFC 0006's validator-table shorthand (l.84) and drops the qualifier in RFC 0006 §1 (l.54). The design is inconsistent with itself in the same way: §7 (l.382) has the broad wording, and §10 (l.618) records the narrow wording as landed. Design §11 and the tidy-up RFC list no departure for this rule.

**Evidence.** The two statements, and the sources:

- `docs/specification.md:1132`: "| `INPUT_IN_ANOTHER_ANALYSIS` | MUST | An input's file is outside every other analysis's directory | 0006 |"
- `rules.json:487`: `"statement": "An input's file is outside every other analysis's directory"`
- `docs/specification.md:352-354`: "The file is inside the analysis's directory and outside every analysis within it, a directory with an `astra.yaml` of its own"
- `docs/specification.md:1025-1026`: "the only place this specification states a rule that a tool checks or keeps"
- `rfcs/0006-data-inputs.md:54`: "stays inside the analysis's own folder and outside any analysis within it (a folder with an `astra.yaml` of its own). The data belongs with the analysis that uses it."
- `rfcs/0006-data-inputs.md:84`: "| An input's file isn't inside another analysis's folder | error |"
- `design/...-schema-design.md:618`: "| Inside its analysis's directory, outside any analysis within it | `INPUT_IN_ANOTHER_ANALYSIS` |"

A minimal nested layout, valid under both validators at the schema layer (the rule is a validator-layer rule):

```yaml
# analysis/astra.yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
analyses:
  stage:
    path: stage
outputs:
  summary: {type: metric, format: json, inputs: [stage.features]}
```
```yaml
# analysis/stage/astra.yaml   (and the file analysis/stage/raw.csv)
spec_version: "0.1.0"
inputs:
  raw:
    path: raw.csv
outputs:
  features: {type: data, format: parquet, inputs: [raw]}
```

Applying each wording to the tree on disk:

```
analysis/stage/astra.yaml input raw -> analysis/stage/raw.csv
   §9 wording: BROKEN, inside analysis
   §5 wording: holds
```

The same split holds for `examples/iris_pipeline`: an input at `feature_extraction/raw.csv` owned by `feature_extraction` breaks §9 via the root `.` and holds under §5. No nested analysis under `examples/` has an `inputs:` map today, so the examples don't trigger it.

A secondary case: with `previous: {path: ., commit: X}`, §7 (l.768-770) makes `previous` a distinct analysis whose directory is the declaring one's own, so §9 read literally also rejects the root's inputs. §5's "within it" does not. This depends on whether an analysis read from a commit has a directory, so it is a sub-point.

**Why it matters.** D13 has validator authors (astra-tools, astra-typescript) build from §9 and `rules.json`. One built that way rejects every input of a nested analysis in RFC 0004's layout, which RFC 0004:45 explicitly allows ("optionally its own inputs"). One built from §5 accepts it. The `rules.json` statement is also the message tools print, so authors get a wrong account of the rule.

**Suggested fix.** Reword the §9 row to match §5, e.g. "An input's file is inside its analysis's directory and outside every directory below it that holds an `astra.yaml` of its own" ("below", so the analysis's own directory at another commit isn't caught). Run `just gen-schema`, and bring design §7:382 into line.

### F016. UNDECLARED_READ drops RFC 0005's "files inputs name", so the rule forbids a recipe reading its own code

- **Lens:** RFC fidelity, Implementer, Internal consistency · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:1198`, `docs/specification.md:423-425`, `docs/specification.md:970-973`, `src/astra/schema/generated/rules.json:706-712`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:448`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:607`, `rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:155`, `rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:162`

**What is wrong.** RFC 0005 §3 limits the MUST to two things: the files that inputs name, and other outputs' results. Everything else a recipe uses, its scripts among them, is pinned by the commit. The specification drops that limit in every place it states the rule: "A recipe reads files and results only through its output's inputs". That covers every file. Read as written, the specification's own §2 recipe breaks it, because `python src/train.py` reads `src/train.py`, which no input names. §8 adds "anything else it uses is pinned by the commit", but that "anything else" follows "files and results", so it still doesn't cover a script, which is a file. §9 calls itself the only place a rule is stated, and the §9 row and `rules.json` leave the clause out. Design §11 doesn't list this as a departure, and neither does the tidy-up draft. The broad wording came in with baf6e31 ("Standardise the rule catalogue").

**Evidence.**

```
rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:155
  A recipe MUST read the files inputs name and other outputs' results only through the
  inputs its output lists - ... Anything else a recipe uses is pinned by the commit or is
  part of the environment. A runner MAY enforce this.
rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:162
  | Recipes read the files inputs name and other outputs' results only through their output's inputs | runner (MAY, with a sandbox say) | ...
rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:190
  - changing a script in `analysis/subanalysis1/src/` leaves every result unchanged as far as a check can tell. ...

docs/specification.md:1198   | `UNDECLARED_READ` | MUST | A recipe reads files and results only through its output's inputs | 0005 §3 |
docs/specification.md:424-425  ... A recipe reads files and results only through its output's inputs ([`UNDECLARED_READ`](#runner)).
docs/specification.md:970-972  A recipe reads files and results only through its output's inputs ([`UNDECLARED_READ`](#runner)):
                               anything else it uses is pinned by the commit or is part of the environment.
docs/specification.md:1025-1026  This section is the rule catalogue: the only place this specification states
                                 a rule that a tool checks or keeps.
rules.json:707-711  "code": "UNDECLARED_READ", "layer": "runner", "level": "MUST", "source": "0005 §3",
                    "statement": "A recipe reads files and results only through its output's inputs"
design:448  (the same row, word for word)    design:607  | Recipes read only through their inputs; runners MAY enforce | `UNDECLARED_READ` |
```

The specification's own example, `docs/specification.md:124-128`:

```yaml
    inputs: [iris]
    ...
    recipe:
      command: >-
        python src/train.py --data {inputs.iris}
```

No input in the specification or in `examples/` names a `src/` file (`grep -rn -E "path:.*src/" examples docs/specification.md` finds none). Every recipe there runs a `src/*.py` script, for example `examples/iris/astra.yaml:32`. The specification elsewhere treats code as pinned by the commit: `docs/specification.md:901-902`, "It pins every file of the code and every `astra.yaml`" (the same description is at `provenance.yaml:75-76`).

**Why it matters.** The rule is runner-layer, so the JSON Schemas are not affected. A runner author who takes up the MAY and sandboxes from the §9 row or from `rules.json` would block every recipe's own scripts and libraries. Another would follow the RFC, so the two runners would disagree. A checker or auditor that reports the code would flag every recipe in the specification as breaking a MUST.

**Suggested fix.** Restore the RFC's scope at `docs/specification.md:1198`, `:424` and `:970`, and at design `:448`/`:607`: "A recipe reads the files inputs name, and other outputs' results, only through its output's inputs". Then regenerate `rules.json`.

### F017. `STALE_INPUT` widens RFC 0007's runner rule from results of an analysis from a commit to every result read, and still cites only 0007

- **Lens:** RFC fidelity · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:968-970`, `docs/specification.md:1201`, `rfcs/0007-analyses-in-other-repositories.md:208`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:451`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:642`

**What is wrong.** RFC 0007 says an output isn't built when it reads a missing or stale result *of an analysis from a commit*. The specification makes this a MUST for every result an output reads, the analysis's own results included. The Source column gives a bare `0007`. No other RFC has a runner rule against building over a stale or missing local result. RFC 0005 §4 says the opposite: a result that reads a stale one is itself stale until that one is rebuilt. Design §11 lists 17 departures from the RFCs (682-711), and none of them is this one. The tidy-up draft (`design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-changes.md`) never mentions `STALE_INPUT`. The scope was lost at the first design commit (e26bef7, `RUN_NOT_STALE`, renamed in baf6e31) and was never recorded.

**Evidence.**

```
$ sed -n 1201p docs/specification.md
| `STALE_INPUT` | MUST | A runner builds an output only when every result it reads is `unchanged` and its bytes can be got and are the recorded ones | 0007 |
$ sed -n 208p rfcs/0007-analyses-in-other-repositories.md
| An output that reads a result of an analysis from a commit when that result is missing or stale, or its bytes can't be got or aren't the recorded ones | runner | isn't built |
$ sed -n 182p rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md
- a result it reads is stale. Once that result is rebuilt, this one is unchanged again if the rebuilt bytes are the same
$ sed -n 642p design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md
| Doesn't build from a missing or stale result | `STALE_INPUT` |
$ grep -c STALE_INPUT design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-changes.md
0
```

`docs/specification.md:968-970` (§8) says the same for "every result the output reads". RFC 0007 §5 (`:130`) also says "an output that reads a result of the declared analysis". `rules.json` carries the widened rule with `"source": "0007"`.

**Why it matters.** Take the specification's own example (`docs/specification.md:1001-1002`): "`svm` makes `accuracy#svm` stale, and `comparison` with it". Suppose a runner rebuilds `comparison` alone while the local `accuracy#svm` is still stale. Under RFCs 0005 and 0007 that runner conforms, and `comparison` comes out stale. Under the specification it breaks a MUST. For local results the rule also requires the bytes to be the recorded ones, which no RFC asks of runners. A runner author working from the RFCs and one working from the specification would disagree on whether a partial build conforms. Anyone who follows the Source link to RFC 0007 finds a narrower rule there. A full build in order conforms under both readings.

**Suggested fix.** Either restrict `STALE_INPUT` to results of an analysis from a commit, as RFC 0007:208 does (spec 968-970 and 1201, design 451), or keep the general rule, add it to design §11 and the tidy-up RFC as a numbered change, and cite `0007, tidy-up`.

### F018. RFC 0005's MUST on what a result's default place holds has no rule; its breach, `modified`, is filed under ABSENT_RESULT, a SHOULD

- **Lens:** RFC fidelity, Internal consistency, Standards · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:46`, `rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:86-87`, `docs/specification.md:22-27`, `docs/specification.md:889-892`, `docs/specification.md:1025-1026`, `docs/specification.md:1173`, `docs/specification.md:1184`, `src/astra/schema/generated/rules.json:669-676`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:598-599`

**What is wrong.** RFC 0005 §1 has two rules about a result's default place. The recorded bytes SHOULD be there (`absent` when they aren't). Whatever is there MUST be the recorded bytes or a stand-in (`modified` when it isn't, reported by tooling that recognises stand-ins). The RFC's table gives each rule its own row. The specification keeps only the first as a rule. It states the MUST once, in §8, as a present-tense sentence with no keyword and no code.
