| Which declarations does a key `fit.pre.scaling` drive? | A: every declaration it passes through (RFC 0004:137, :334). B: only the declaration of the analysis whose decision is set (glossary and §7 "setting another analysis's decisions"; design §13 reasons this way). | Opposite verdicts on a valid-looking stack. |
| Does a universe drive through its own `decisions` or its effective selection? | §9's neighbouring rows each say (`MISSING_DECISION`: "effective selection"; `INACTIVE_DECISION`: "itself sets"); `PARTIALLY_DRIVEN` says neither, and §6's list at l.562-570 leaves it out. A close reading favours the effective selection. | The own-keys reading rejects ordinary derived universes, RFC 0004's example among them. |
| Is driving counted per declaration or per analysis? | §7 (l.699, l.742) and RFC 0004:334 make it per declaration. l.664-665, l.701-703 ("either spelling") and RFC 0004:221 (the spelling "doesn't matter") read as a free choice per universe. | A stack that picks one spelling per universe gets `PARTIALLY_DRIVEN` with no hint why. |

No example, fixture or spec snippet tells any of these readings apart.

**Evidence.**

> **Driving** is setting another analysis's decisions from one's own universes, by flat keys: the decision's address, `<declaration>.<decision>`, through as many declarations as lead to it (`classification.classifier`, `stage.inner.d`). The analysis whose universes set them is the **driver**, and the declared analysis is **driven** through that declaration. — `docs/specification.md:690-695`

> An analysis is considered to be *driving* the analysis stack if it has universes that set **all** of the required decisions of the whole stack of analyses which are named in the universe — `rfcs/0004-composition.md:137`

> only `subsubanalysis1` is named in the root's universes (`subsubanalysis1.d: d1`) and therefore only `subsubanalysis1` is driven — `rfcs/0004-composition.md:334`

```
| `MISSING_DECISION` | MUST | Every universe's effective selection sets every active decision of its analysis and of every analysis it drives | 0003, 0004 |
| `INACTIVE_DECISION` | MUST | A universe itself sets only decisions whose `when` holds in its effective selection | 0003 |
| `PARTIALLY_DRIVEN` | MUST | Every declaration is driven by all of the driver's universes or by none | 0004 |
```

Three minimal stacks, each schema-valid in the Python `jsonschema` validator and in Ajv.

1. *Through which declarations.* Root drives `fit.pre.scaling`; `fit` has its own decision `model` and universes `f1`, `f2`; root pins `fit.score#f1` and `fit.score#f2`.

   ```yaml
   # astra.yaml (root)
   analyses: {fit: {path: fit}}
   outputs:
     contrast: {type: metric, format: json, inputs: ["fit.score#f1", "fit.score#f2"]}
   universes:
     u1: {decisions: {fit.pre.scaling: none}}
     u2: {decisions: {fit.pre.scaling: standard}}
   # fit/astra.yaml
   analyses: {pre: {path: ../prep}}
   decisions: {model: {options: {svm: {}, rf: {}}}}
   outputs: {score: {type: metric, format: json, inputs: [pre.features], decisions: [model]}}
   universes:
     f1: {decisions: {model: svm, pre.scaling: none}}
     f2: {decisions: {model: rf, pre.scaling: none}}
   # prep/astra.yaml
   decisions: {scaling: {options: {none: {}, standard: {}}}}
   outputs: {features: {type: data, format: csv, decisions: [scaling]}}
   ```

   Reading A: `fit` is driven, so u1 and u2 break `MISSING_DECISION` (no `fit.model`) and both pins break `UNKNOWN_PIN_UNIVERSE` (the root has no `f1`, `f2`). Reading B: `fit` is undriven, the pins name fit's own universes, and the stack is valid.

2. *Own keys or effective selection.* Root drives `sub`; `svm: {derived_from: base, decisions: {model: svm}}` inherits `sub.method: pca` from `base`.

   ```
   $ uv run python repro-c002/partially_driven.py stack/astra.yaml
   --driven own
     PARTIALLY_DRIVEN(sub): not driven by ['svm']
   --driven effective
     sub: driven by all of ['base', 'svm']
   ```

   RFC 0004's running example (`rfcs/0004-composition.md:453-463`, where `u2: derived_from: u1` sets only `subanalysis1.b`) fails its own rule (:408) under the own-keys reading, with `PARTIALLY_DRIVEN` for `subanalysis2` and `subsubanalysis1`. `examples/iris_pipeline/astra.yaml` passes under both readings.

3. *Per declaration or per analysis.* The root declares C as `c` and through B as `b.c`, and each universe sets C's one decision under one spelling.

   ```yaml
   # astra.yaml (root)
   analyses: {b: {path: stages/b}, c: {path: stages/c}}
   outputs: {z: {type: metric, format: json, inputs: [b.y]}}
   universes:
     u1: {decisions: {b.x: x1, c.d: p}}
     u2: {decisions: {b.x: x2, b.c.d: q}}
   ```

   Per declaration, the root's `c` is named only by u1 and B's `c` only by u2: `PARTIALLY_DRIVEN`. Per analysis, C's `d` is set in every universe: valid. This is not design §13's "Agreeing spellings" (design:772-773).

**Why it matters.** These are validator-layer rules, so only the text decides them, and two validators built from the sources give opposite verdicts on each stack above. An author who writes `stage.inner.d`, the spec's own example, can't tell whether that obliges them to set every decision of `stage`, whether pins to stage's outputs still name stage's own universes, or whether they may switch spellings between universes.

**Suggested fix.** In §7, define it once: a universe drives a declaration when its effective selection sets a decision through it, saying whether that is every declaration the key passes through or only the last. Word `MISSING_DECISION`, `PARTIALLY_DRIVEN`, `UNDRIVEN_DECLARATION`, `UNKNOWN_PIN_UNIVERSE` and the "either spelling" bullet (l.701-703) to match, and add a three-analysis example.

### F034. No frame is given for a pin written in a driven analysis's file, so a built-once output can read a different result under each root but has one record

- **Lens:** Implementer · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:732-735`, `docs/specification.md:725-727`, `docs/specification.md:690-695`, `docs/specification.md:705-706`, `docs/specification.md:596-600`, `docs/specification.md:852-854`, `docs/specification.md:866`, `docs/specification.md:1146`

**What is wrong.** The pin rule turns on whether "its reference goes through a driven declaration" (732-735). Whether a declaration is driven depends on the driver, and so on the root, since "any analysis can be the root" (673-674). §7 states a frame for unpinned reads: "in the file of the analysis whose universe is being built" (725-727). It states none for pins. Take a pin written in a driven analysis's own file, through a declaration that the root drives from above (`stage.inner.d`, 690-695). Two readings follow:

- **Driver's frame.** `inner` is driven in the root's build, so `#u1` is the root's `u1`. The pin then means something different under each root.
- **File's frame.** In stage's file nothing drives `inner`, so `#u1` is inner's own `u1`. If inner has no universes, that is `UNKNOWN_PIN_UNIVERSE` under every root.

Neither reading is the safe default. A second case runs the other way: a driven `fit` that drives its own `pre` and pins `pre.clean#v1`. fit alone is valid. Under the driver's frame the root is the one driver (706), the root has no `v1`, and the pin fails.

The RFCs leave the same gap (`rfcs/0004-composition.md:357`). No example or fixture writes such a pin.

A related wording point: 1146 says "of the analysis the output belongs to", while 733-734 say "of the analysis whose output it is". §8 uses "belongs to" for results, and a driven analysis's results belong to its driver (853).

**Evidence.** Under `scratchpad/work/repro-c004/p/`:

```yaml
# p/astra.yaml (root R); p/alt/astra.yaml (root R2) is the same, with
# stage: {path: ../stage} and only u1: {stage.inner.d: q}
analyses: {stage: {path: stage}}
universes:
  u1: {decisions: {stage.inner.d: p}}
  u2: {decisions: {stage.inner.d: q}}
outputs: {summary: {type: metric, format: json, inputs: [stage.pick]}}
# p/stage/astra.yaml
analyses: {inner: {path: inner}}
outputs:
  pick: {type: metric, format: json, inputs: ["inner.deep#u1"]}
# p/stage/inner/astra.yaml: decision d {p, q}; output deep with decisions [d]
```

All four files, and the case-S files, are VALID in Python `jsonschema` and Ajv. The split is in the validator, check and runner layers.

`pick` is built once, because "the pin fixes the universe it reads" (599-600). Its result belongs to stage (853-854), at `stage/.astra/records/pick.json` (866). Under the driver's frame it reads d=p when R is the root and d=q when R2 is. Two records with address `pick`, entry `inner.deep#u1` and different input hashes (`rec/pick-1.json`, `rec/pick-2.json`) are both schema-valid. By "the results it reads" (992), whichever root runs second makes the other's `pick` stale. Within one root, `pick` is still the same in every universe. It differs across roots.

Silent variant (`p-silent/`): give inner its own `u1: {d: q}`. Both readings now pass under R, and they read different results.

**Why it matters.** Validator authors must pick a frame, and the two frames give opposite verdicts on case P and case S. Runner and checker authors get one record path that two roots overwrite with different inputs. The author of a reusable analysis can't write a pin that means the same under every driver.

**Suggested fix.** State the frame in §7 (and in the tidy-up RFC). For example: a pin resolves in the file it is written in, against that file's declarations and that analysis's own universes, whatever drives it. Or forbid such pins in a driven analysis's file. Reword 1146 to "of the analysis whose output it is".

### F035. UNDRIVEN_DECLARATION, as worded, rejects a driven analysis that reads its driver's own decision or output through `..`

- **Lens:** Implementer · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:720-727`, `docs/specification.md:1142-1143`, `docs/specification.md:683`, `docs/specification.md:708-711`, `rfcs/0004-composition.md:411`, `examples/iris_pipeline/feature_extraction/astra.yaml:11-12,47-48`, `examples/iris_pipeline/classification/astra.yaml:38`

**What is wrong.** A driven analysis can declare its driver with `..` and consume the driver's decision or unpinned output. The spec allows the cycle (666-667) and recommends this for "the seed of a whole stack" (710-711). The rule covers "every decision of another analysis", and "another" is measured from the file that uses the element (683, 729, 734-735). It then asks that, in the driver's file, the element be "reached through a declaration that analysis drives". In the driver's file the element is its own and is reached through no declaration. The only spelling that goes through a declaration is `stage.root.seed`, and `stage.root` is not driven. Read literally, the rule fails. The text doesn't say whether the driver's own elements are exempt, or whether "through a declaration" means every declaration on the path or at least one. Validators will split three ways.

**Evidence.** The rule (`docs/specification.md`):

```
720: universe selects. So, for every output of another analysis read without a
721: pin, and every decision of another analysis:
725: - in the file of the analysis whose universe is being built, it is reached
726:   through a declaration that analysis drives
1143: | `UNDRIVEN_DECLARATION` | MUST | In the file of the analysis whose universe is being built, every such read goes through a declaration that analysis drives | 0004 |
```

A minimal stack:

```yaml
# seed/astra.yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
analyses:
  stage: {path: stage}
decisions:
  seed: {options: {s1: {}, s2: {}}}
outputs:
  report: {type: report, format: md, inputs: [stage.fit]}
universes:
  u1: {decisions: {seed: s1, stage.method: a}}
  u2: {decisions: {seed: s2, stage.method: b}}
# seed/stage/astra.yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
analyses:
  root: {path: ..}
decisions:
  method: {options: {a: {}, b: {}}}
outputs:
  fit: {type: metric, format: json, decisions: [method, root.seed]}
```

The same layout with an output, `features: {..., decisions: [seed]}` in the root and `inputs: [root.features]` in the stage, behaves the same. All four files are VALID in the Python and Ajv validators. Building `stage.fit` in u1, NOT_BUILT_IN_UNIVERSE, MISSING_DECISION, PARTIALLY_DRIVEN and INPUT_NOT_BUILT all hold, and there is no OUTPUT_CYCLE. Only UNDRIVEN_DECLARATION fails.

The repository's own example has this layout. `examples/iris_pipeline/feature_extraction/astra.yaml:11-12` declares `iris: {path: ..}`, and line 48 has `decisions: [method, n_components, iris.random_seed]`. `classification/astra.yaml:38` reads `iris.split`, which the root builds per universe (`astra.yaml:59`). RFC 0004 treats the layout as valid: "consumes the root's seed by address" (581), and "Everything the stages build is driven" (651). Its rule row (411) has the same wording as the spec, so the RFC's rule and its examples disagree. Design §13's open point (a reader with no decisions) is a different case. Its suggested fix doesn't help here.

**Why it matters.** A validator written from row 1143 rejects `examples/iris_pipeline` and the stack-wide seed that §7 recommends. A validator written from the convention accepts both. Under the strict reading, the seed has to move into a separate, driven analysis.

**Suggested fix.** Word 720-727 and rows 1142-1143 from the builder's side: "every output or decision of an analysis other than the one whose universe is being built (the same directory being the same analysis)". Record the case next to the §13 open point.

### F036. `UNDECLARED_READ` binds the recipe but sits in the runner layer, so a runner that doesn't sandbox stops conforming, though enforcing the rule is only a MAY

- **Lens:** Implementer, Standards · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:83`, `docs/specification.md:91`, `docs/specification.md:94-95`, `docs/specification.md:970-973`, `docs/specification.md:1060`, `docs/specification.md:1198`, `docs/specification.md:1203-1206`, `src/astra/schema/generated/rules.json:706-712`, `rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:155`, `rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:162`

**What is wrong.** `UNDECLARED_READ` is a MUST in §9's runner table. It is the only rule there whose subject is "A recipe" rather than "A runner". The analysis's authors write the recipe. But the runner layer covers the runner's "own actions" (83). Breaking a runner rule means "The runner doesn't conform" (1060), and a runner conforms "when it keeps the runner rules" (94-95). No other role can carry the rule: an analysis is valid by its parse, schema and validator MUSTs only (91). The same section then says a runner only MAY enforce the rule (1203-1206). Read together, a runner without a sandbox stops conforming whenever a recipe it runs reads an undeclared file, which it is allowed not to detect. That turns the MAY into a de facto MUST.

RFC 0005 §3 puts the MUST on the recipe, makes enforcement a MAY, and says what happens when the rule is broken: "the recipe fails". The specification does not state that outcome. The rule also doesn't say the runner refuses, so 1060's "it reports the code" doesn't apply either. The design's §7 has the same text, and §11 records no departure.

Also, 1206 says §8 states the MAY, but §8 writes it in lowercase ("A runner may enforce this", 972-973). Under §1's BCP 14 clause, lowercase words are not keywords.

**Evidence.**

```
docs/specification.md:1198  | `UNDECLARED_READ` | MUST | A recipe reads files and results only through its output's inputs | 0005 §3 |
docs/specification.md:1060  | runner | The runner doesn't conform. Where a rule says the runner refuses, it reports the code |
docs/specification.md:94-95 A **runner** runs recipes and writes each result's bytes and record. It
                            conforms when it keeps the runner rules.
docs/specification.md:1203-1206  Two duties that are not rules of their own, because nothing can check them: a
                            runner SHOULD let someone see what an environment it recorded holds, and MAY
                            enforce `UNDECLARED_READ` (with a sandbox, say). The specification states them
                            in its §8.
rules.json:706-712          "code": "UNDECLARED_READ", "layer": "runner", "level": "MUST"
```

RFC 0005:

```
:155  A recipe MUST read the files inputs name and other outputs' results only through the
      inputs its output lists [...] A runner MAY enforce this.
:162  | Recipes read [...] only through their output's inputs | runner (MAY, with a sandbox say) | the recipe fails |
```

```
$ grep -n -i "recipe fails\|fails the recipe" docs/specification.md
(exit 1)
```

**Why it matters.** Runner implementers will disagree on whether a runner needs a sandbox to conform, so a conformance claim, such as astra-tools' runner's, says nothing definite. Nothing says what an enforcing runner does: fail the recipe, report the code, or still write the record. Until that is settled, the conformance suite planned in design §12 can't write a runner fixture for this code.

**Suggested fix.** Say that `UNDECLARED_READ` binds the recipe (the analysis), not the runner. A runner conforms whether or not it enforces the rule, and a runner that enforces it fails the recipe and reports the code, as RFC 0005 §3 says. Write §8's "may" as MAY.

### F037. Two results of one analysis can share one default place

- **Lens:** Implementer, Author, Edge cases · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:199`, `docs/specification.md:275-277`, `docs/specification.md:378-381`, `docs/specification.md:855-858`, `docs/specification.md:864-874`, `docs/specification.md:891-892`, `docs/specification.md:1123`, `docs/specification.md:1195`, `src/astra/schema/analysis.yaml:187`

**What is wrong.** A result's bytes go to `results/{universe}/{name}.{format}`. A driven result's name is dotted (`stage.summary`), and `format` may contain a `.` (`tar.gz`, `nii.gz`). §4 lets an output share its id with a declaration. So the root's output `stage` with format `summary.json` and the driven result `stage.summary` with format `json` have the same default place. Both validators accept the documents, and no rule in §9 forbids the collision. The two records stay distinct.

A collision needs both things at once. Ids contain no `.`, so `name1.f1` can equal `name2.f2` only if `name1` is a proper dotted prefix of `name2`. Some analysis on the chain must then have an output and a declaration with one id, and that output must have a dotted format. Only results built per universe can collide. The gap is inherited: RFC 0005 §1 (`rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:36`, `:46`) gives the same layout, and RFC 0004 (`rfcs/0004-composition.md:401`) has no rule against the shared id.

**Evidence.** Minimal documents:

```yaml
# astra.yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
analyses:
  stage: {path: stage}
outputs:
  stage:
    type: data
    format: summary.json
    inputs: [stage.summary]
    recipe: {command: python summarise.py {inputs} --out {output}}
universes:
  u1: {decisions: {stage.method: a}}
```

```yaml
# stage/astra.yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
decisions:
  method: {options: {a: {}, b: {}}}
outputs:
  summary:
    type: data
    format: json
    decisions: [method]
    recipe: {command: python fit.py --method {decisions.method} --out {output}}
```

Python `jsonschema` and Ajv both give `VALID` for both files. Walked by hand, none of `UNRESOLVED_ADDRESS`, `AMBIGUOUS_ID`, `MISSING_DECISION`, `PARTIALLY_DRIVEN`, `NOT_BUILT_IN_UNIVERSE`, `UNDRIVEN_DECLARATION` or `OUTPUT_CYCLE` fails. The root `stage` is built per universe, because its unpinned input is. The §8 table (spec.md:866-867) gives:

```
stage.summary#u1   record .astra/records/u1/stage.summary.json   bytes results/u1/stage.summary.json
stage#u1           record .astra/records/u1/stage.json           bytes results/u1/stage.summary.json
```

The `nii.gz` shape reproduces too: root output `scan` (`nii.gz`) and driven `scan.nii` (`gz`) both go to `results/u1/scan.nii.gz`.

- spec.md:275-277: "Ids of different kinds can be the same, except where one field could resolve to either: an input and an output never share an id".
- spec.md:1123: `AMBIGUOUS_ID` covers only "an input and an output".
- spec.md:1195: `UNRECORDED_RESULT` (MUST) "writes the bytes to their default place".
- spec.md:891-892: "Whatever is at a result's default place is its recorded bytes or something standing in for them."

**Why it matters.** A runner can't meet `UNRECORDED_RESULT` for both results and spec.md:891-892 at once. The second write overwrites the first. An ordinary checker then reports the overwritten result `absent`, though both records are correct. Tooling that recognises stand-ins may report it `modified` instead. Runners will also differ: one overwrites, another errors or renames. The trigger needs contrived ids, but the documents are valid.

**Suggested fix.** Add a validator rule (or extend `AMBIGUOUS_ID`) so that no output shares an id with a declaration of the same analysis; this keeps `tar.gz`. Alternatively, add a rule that no two planned results of an analysis share a default place.

### F038. No rule says "the runner refuses", so the codes a runner reports are undecided

- **Lens:** Implementer, Standards · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:1060`, `docs/specification.md:1032-1034`, `docs/specification.md:1196`, `docs/specification.md:1198`, `docs/specification.md:1201`, `docs/specification.md:1203-1205`, `docs/specification.md:963-970`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:328`

**What is wrong.** The layer table makes a runner report a code only "Where a rule says the runner refuses" (1060). No runner rule says that. The Rule column states what holds (1032-1034: a Rule "never says what happens when it is broken"), and none of the runner rows uses the word. Only §8's prose says "refuses", and only for `UNCOMMITTED_CHANGES` (965). `STALE_INPUT` has the same form ("A runner builds an output only when …", 1201) as `UNCOMMITTED_CHANGES` ("A runner runs only when …", 1196), but its prose (968-970) never says "refuses". `UNDECLARED_READ` is open too: when a runner enforces it with a sandbox (the MAY at 1203-1205), nothing says whether it reports the code. A rule *could* say "A runner refuses to run while …" without breaking the convention, since refusing is what a conforming runner does. None does.

The RFCs gave each runner rule its own outcome. The spec's runner table (Code | Level | Rule | Source) dropped that column and put this one generic row in its place. The design has the same row (design:328), and §11 does not list it as a departure.

**Evidence.**

```
$ grep -n -i 'refuse' docs/specification.md
965:refuses to run ([`UNCOMMITTED_CHANGES`](#runner)). It records each recipe's
1060:| runner | The runner doesn't conform. Where a rule says the runner refuses, it reports the code |
$ python3 -c "import json; r=json.load(open('src/astra/schema/generated/rules.json')); \
  print([x['code'] for x in r['rules'] if x['layer']=='runner' and 'refus' in x['statement']]); \
  print([x['code'] for x in r['rules'] if x['layer']=='runner' and 'only when' in x['statement']])"
[]
['UNCOMMITTED_CHANGES', 'STALE_INPUT']
```

The RFCs record one outcome per rule:

- `rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:161`: `| Only committed code is run | runner | refuses to run |`
- `rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:162`: `| Recipes read … only through their output's inputs | runner (MAY, with a sandbox say) | the recipe fails |`
- `rfcs/0007-analyses-in-other-repositories.md:208`: `| An output that reads a result … missing or stale, … | runner | isn't built |`

**Why it matters.** "It reports the code" is a conformance requirement on runners, and three readings give three answers. A literal reader matches the rule text and reports nothing. A reader of §8 reports `UNCOMMITTED_CHANGES` only. A reader who takes the two "only when" rules as refusals reports `STALE_INPUT` as well. A runner that skips a stale input without emitting `STALE_INPUT` conforms under one reading and not another. Runner authors and a future runner conformance suite cannot tell which codes must appear. File validity and result statuses are unaffected.

**Suggested fix.** Name the codes in row 1060, e.g. "A runner that doesn't run (`UNCOMMITTED_CHANGES`) or doesn't build an output (`STALE_INPUT`) reports the code", and say whether a sandboxed `UNDECLARED_READ` is reported. Or give the runner table a consequence column, as the RFCs had. Change design:328 to match.

### F039. "Planned results" doesn't say which results of driven and consumed analyses a check covers

- **Lens:** Implementer · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:849-850`, `docs/specification.md:852-854`, `docs/specification.md:92-93`, `docs/specification.md:716-718`, `docs/specification.md:961-962`, `docs/specification.md:1019-1021`, `docs/specification.md:1179`, `docs/specification.md:34-52`

**What is wrong.** Every check rule works on a root's planned results, and the spec defines the term in one sentence: "The results an analysis's outputs and universes call for are its **planned** results" (`docs/specification.md:849-850`). The Terms table (`:34-52`) has no entry for it. The sentence doesn't say:

- whether every active output of a driven analysis is planned in each of the driver's universes, or only the outputs that the root's outputs read;
- whether results that belong to another analysis are planned results of the root. This covers a driven analysis's built-once outputs, which "belong to the analysis whose output it is" (`:853-854`), and the results of consumed analyses.

The text around it pulls both ways. A checker "reads an analysis's records" (`:92`). `MISSING_RECORD` puts the record "in the analysis it belongs to" (`:1179`), which implies some planned results belong elsewhere. `:1019-1021` names one case only, the results of an analysis from a commit "that are read or cited". `:716-718` push the universe "through every analysis it drives", but don't say that building in it builds every active output there. `:961-962` say a runner records "every result it makes", but not what building the root makes.

**Evidence.** The repository's own `examples/iris_pipeline` gives a different planned set under each reading. In `feature_extraction/astra.yaml:56-61`, `feature_plot` is driven (`decisions: [method, n_components]`) and nothing in the stack reads it:

```
 6  root's outputs only: ['comparison', 'iris_data', 'split#baseline', 'split#mlp_svm', 'summary#baseline', 'summary#mlp_svm']
12  + what they read, transitively: [... + classification.accuracy#*, classification.predictions#*, feature_extraction.features#*]
14  + every output of the driven analyses: [... + feature_extraction.feature_plot#baseline, feature_extraction.feature_plot#mlp_svm]
```

RFC 0005 says this example "has 14 results" (`rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:215`), which only the widest reading gives. The layouts in RFC 0004 §5 (`rfcs/0004-composition.md:367-394`) and RFC 0005 §1 (`:50-77`) also list a built-once result the root never reads (`subanalysis3/.astra/records/output3.json`). The spec doesn't carry this over. RFC 0005 uses "planned" (`:199`) without defining it.

A minimal stack makes the split concrete. The root drives `stage`, consumes `common` and reads `other` pinned:

```yaml
# astra.yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
analyses: {stage: {path: stage}, common: {path: common}, other: {path: other}}
outputs:
  report: {type: report, format: md, inputs: [stage.fit], recipe: {command: ...}}   # per universe
  joined: {type: data, format: csv, inputs: [common.table, other.score#v1], recipe: {command: ...}}  # built once
universes: {u1: {decisions: {stage.model: linear}}, u2: {decisions: {stage.model: tree}}}
# stage/astra.yaml: input raw; decision model {linear, tree}; no universes; outputs
#   clean (inputs [raw], built once), fit (inputs [clean], decisions [model]),
#   diagnostics (inputs [clean], decisions [model], read by nothing)
# common/astra.yaml: input src; output table (inputs [src], built once)
# other/astra.yaml: decision k {a, b}; output score (decisions [k]); universes v1 {k: a}, v2 {k: b}
```

All four documents and all 10 records validate in both the Python and the Ajv validator. The check of the root has three defensible planned sets:

1. The root's outputs in its universes: 3 results (`report#u1`, `report#u2`, `joined`).
2. Plus what those read, transitively: 8 (adds `stage.fit#u1/#u2`, `stage.clean`, `common.table`, `other.score#v1`).
3. Plus every active output of the driven analysis in each universe: 10 (adds `stage.diagnostics#u1/#u2`).

Delete `.astra/records/u1/stage.diagnostics.json`, which sits in the root's own records directory. Reading 3 reports `MISSING_RECORD`; readings 1 and 2 report nothing. Checking `stage` alone gives `diagnostics` no planned result either, since `stage` has no universes. Delete `stage/.astra/records/clean.json` instead: readings 2 and 3 report it `missing`, and reading 1 reports nothing.

A narrower, related gap: `:992` says "A result made from a stale one is stale", but nothing says what status a result gets when a result it reads is `missing`.

**Why it matters.** astra-tools and astra-typescript, each written from the spec alone, report different `MISSING_RECORD` sets for the same repository. Under the narrow reading, a driver's missing driven results are never reported by any check, though their records live in the driver's own `.astra/records`. Runner authors can't tell whether building the root must build unread driven outputs, or another analysis's built-once results.

**Suggested fix.** Define planned results in §8 and add the term to the Terms table: each universe of the root calls for every active output of the root and of every analysis it drives, and an output built once is called for once, in the analysis it belongs to; then include, or explicitly exclude, the results of consumed analyses that these read or that the root's evidence cites. This matches RFC 0004 §5 and RFC 0005 §1. One clause can also say what a result reading a `missing` result gets.

### F040. A local result that reads a `missing` result has no defined status

- **Lens:** Implementer · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:984-995`, `docs/specification.md:1183`, `docs/specification.md:1166-1170`, `docs/specification.md:820-823`, `rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:182`

**What is wrong.** For a result of the local analysis, `STALE_RESULT` passes staleness on from a read result only when that result is stale. Nothing says what happens when a read result is `missing`: no record, an invalid record, a misplaced one, or missing record inputs. The reader still has a valid record of its own, so the status table gives it `unchanged` or `stale`. The text doesn't say which. For an analysis from a commit the spec does say: the read results must be unchanged (820-823). The two cases don't match, and the catalogue row has only the local wording.

The spec also says only for the commit case that read results are compared by their records' content hashes (822-823). For a local analysis that is clear from 887-892 and RFC 0005, but it is never stated. This is a wording point and belongs in the same fix.

**Evidence.** The definition, `docs/specification.md:992-993`:

> - the results it reads. A result made from a stale one is stale, and once that one is rebuilt, it is unchanged again if the rebuilt bytes are the same.

The catalogue row, `docs/specification.md:1183`: "… its inputs' content, or the results it reads". It follows RFC 0005 §4, line 182: "a result it reads is stale". The commit case, `docs/specification.md:820-822`: "The result is unchanged if nothing that `STALE_RESULT` compares changed between `A` and `B`, and the results it was made from are unchanged too." The status table, `docs/specification.md:1170`, gives `missing` only to a result with no valid record of its own.

Take the spec's §2 analysis. Commit A holds `astra.yaml`, data and src. Commit B adds the results and three records. `comparison`'s record lists `accuracy#svm` with `sha256:6b4c1402…`, which equals svm's own `content_hash`. On a branch from B, delete `size` from svm's record:

```
$ git diff --stat <A> invalid-svm-record -- astra.yaml data src     # empty
$ uv run python toolkit/validate.py ProvenanceRecord v1/svm-accuracy.json v1/comparison.json
(root) | https://w3id.org/astra/provenance.schema.json#/required | MISSING_FIELD | 'size' is a required property
comparison.json: VALID
$ node toolkit/js/validate.mjs <repo> ProvenanceRecord v1/svm-accuracy.json v1/comparison.json
(root) | https://w3id.org/astra/provenance.schema.json#/required | MISSING_FIELD | must have required property 'size'
comparison.json: VALID
```

`accuracy#svm` is `missing` (`INVALID_RECORD`; `git rm` of the record gives `MISSING_RECORD`). `comparison` has a valid record, nothing in 987-991 changed, and the bytes it read still hash to what it recorded. Read literally, 992-993 give `unchanged`. A checker that treats "the results it reads" as changed, or that generalises 820-822, gives `stale`.

**Why it matters.** Two checkers give `comparison` different statuses. The finding `svm_scores_higher` cites `comparison`, so `STALE_EVIDENCE` passes in one checker and fails in the other. A more realistic case gives the same split: add an input to an upstream output, and its record fails `MISSING_RECORD_INPUTS`, so it is `missing`, not `stale`. Read literally, the downstream result stays `unchanged` although its upstream's declaration changed. The runner side is defined (`STALE_INPUT`, 1201, needs every read result `unchanged`). The checker side is not.

**Suggested fix.** In 992-993 and the `STALE_RESULT` row, say a result is stale when a result it reads is not `unchanged` (stale or missing). Say that read results are compared by their current record's `content_hash` against the hash in the reader's `inputs`, as 822-823 says for commits. Carry the same clarification into the tidy-up RFC for RFC 0005 §4.

### F041. The spec doesn't say whether a checker judges `unchanged` against the working tree or HEAD

- **Lens:** Implementer · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:983-985`, `docs/specification.md:1183`, `docs/specification.md:92-93`, `docs/specification.md:963-965`, `docs/specification.md:975-976`, `docs/specification.md:1201`, `rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:169`

**What is wrong.** §8 defines `unchanged` only between two commits. It never says which state a checker compares the record's commit with: HEAD, or the working tree. Uncommitted state is the normal case. A runner leaves the results and records it writes uncommitted, and the records go in a later commit. Right after a run, HEAD is the record's own commit, so "at a later commit" doesn't apply at all. On a tree with uncommitted edits, two conforming checkers give different statuses.

**Evidence.** The text:

- `docs/specification.md:984-985`: "At a later commit a result is **unchanged** if, since that commit, nothing has changed in ([`STALE_RESULT`](#check))".
- `docs/specification.md:1183` (`STALE_RESULT`, MUST): "Since the commit in a result's record, nothing has changed in ...". It doesn't say up to what.
- `docs/specification.md:92-93`: "A **checker** reads an analysis's records and results". Line 82 gives the check layer "records and results". Neither line mentions git, HEAD or the working tree.
- `docs/specification.md:975`: "A record names the commit its recipe ran from, so it is committed in a later commit."
- `docs/specification.md:1002-1003`: "How a checker finds this out is the tooling's choice." This leaves the method open. It doesn't say which state is compared.
- An analysis from a commit does get an end point (`:1021`, "through their records at the declared commit"). The analysis being checked gets none.

A minimal repository has input `iris`, and two outputs built once: `clean` reads `iris`, and `fit` reads `clean`. The records are valid under jsonschema and Ajv, and they name commit A.

Scenario 1, right after the run (HEAD is A):

```
$ git status --short --untracked-files=all
?? .astra/records/clean.json
?? .astra/records/fit.json
?? results/clean.csv
?? results/fit.json
$ git cat-file -e HEAD:.astra/records/fit.json
fatal: path '.astra/records/fit.json' exists on disk, but not in 'HEAD'
```

A checker that reads HEAD gives both results `missing`. One that reads the working tree gives `unchanged`.

Scenario 2: the records are committed (B), then `data/iris.csv` is edited and not committed.

```
recorded (clean.json, entry iris): sha256:f35781d3b49e51bb…
on disk:                           sha256:f2bc2c9f25f602d5…
$ git diff --stat <A> HEAD -- data/iris.csv
(no lines)
```

A checker that hashes files on disk makes `clean` and `fit` `stale`. One that diffs A against HEAD leaves them `unchanged`. Scenario 3, an uncommitted edit to `fit`'s command in `astra.yaml`, splits the same way.

**Why it matters.** `STALE_RESULT` is a MUST, yet a local checker and a CI checker give different statuses on every dirty tree. That is exactly when an author runs a check to see what an edit invalidated. The runner depends on the same term. `STALE_INPUT` (`:1201`) builds an output only when every result it reads is `unchanged`. Within one run, `fit` reads `clean`, whose record isn't committed yet. Under a HEAD reading `clean` is `missing`, so a chain can't be built without a commit in between. The gap comes from RFC 0005 §4 (`rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:169`), which has the same wording. Design §12 and §13 don't record it.

**Suggested fix.** Say what a status is judged against. Either the working tree, with uncommitted records, `astra.yaml` files and inputs counted, or HEAD, with a statement of how a runner reads the records it has just written. Carry the clarification into the tidy-up RFC.

### F042. "That commit adds only records and results, so it makes nothing stale" is false when a result is re-made with other bytes

- **Lens:** Implementer · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:975-977`, `docs/specification.md:992-993`, `docs/specification.md:996-998`, `docs/specification.md:1183`, `rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:153`, `rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:190`

**What is wrong.** §8 says a commit of records and results "makes nothing stale". `STALE_RESULT` compares the results a result reads, by their bytes. A commit that adds only a re-made record and its new bytes therefore makes the results that read it stale. RFC 0005 §3, the source, says something narrower: such a commit doesn't make *them* stale, meaning the results whose records it adds. RFC 0005 §4 gives this exact case as an example. Design §11 records no departure here.

**Evidence.** The sentence (`docs/specification.md:975-977`):

> A record names the commit its recipe ran from, so it is committed in a later commit. That commit adds only records and results, so it makes nothing stale.

The rule it contradicts (`docs/specification.md:992-993`, and the §9 row at `:1183`, "or the results it reads"):

> - the results it reads. A result made from a stale one is stale, and once that one is rebuilt, it is unchanged again if the rebuilt bytes are the same.

A code change shows only through new bytes (`:996-998`): "a change to either shows only when the recipe is run again and makes other bytes". The runner may write those bytes uncommitted (`:963-965`, `UNCOMMITTED_CHANGES`), so the commit that adds them holds only records and results.

The source and its own counterexample:

> Since that later commit only adds records and results it doesn't make them stale. (`rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:153`)

> changing a script in `analysis/subanalysis1/src/` leaves every result unchanged as far as a check can tell. If `subanalysis1.output1` is then made again in a universe with other bytes, `subanalysis2.output2`, `subanalysis3.output3_per_universe` and `summary` are stale there (`:190`)

The same case in the analysis of §2:

1. C1: the runner builds `accuracy#forest`, `accuracy#svm` (hash h_s) and `comparison`. `comparison`'s record names C1 and lists `accuracy#svm` with h_s.
2. C2 adds those records and results. Every result is unchanged.
3. C3 edits `src/train.py`. Every result is still unchanged (`:996-998`).
4. The runner re-makes `accuracy#svm` at C3 and gets h_s' ≠ h_s.
5. C4 adds only `.astra/records/svm/accuracy.json` and `results/svm/accuracy.json`.
6. At C4 `comparison` reads h_s' where its record has h_s, so it is `stale`. At C3 it was `unchanged`.

The three records validate against `provenance.schema.json` (Python `jsonschema` and Ajv):

```
comparison-C1.json    VALID   entry accuracy#svm  content_hash sha256:40700a0d...
svm-accuracy-C1.json  VALID   commit 1111...      content_hash sha256:40700a0d...
svm-accuracy-C3.json  VALID   commit 3333...      content_hash sha256:937d007e...
```

**Why it matters.** The gloss contradicts the coded rule a few lines below it and its RFC. An implementer who relies on it writes a checker that carries statuses over records-only commits, or a CI job that auto-commits records on the assumption that they are neutral. Both miss the results made stale by re-made bytes.

**Suggested fix.** Restore the RFC's scope: "so it makes none of the results whose records it adds stale". Optionally add that a re-made result with other bytes makes the results that read it stale.

### F043. A record of a submodule analysis's result names a commit its own repository doesn't have

- **Lens:** Implementer · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:901-903`, `docs/specification.md:977-979`, `docs/specification.md:813-821`, `docs/specification.md:852-854`, `docs/specification.md:887`, `docs/specification.md:639-642`, `src/astra/schema/provenance.yaml:75-77`, `rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:119`, `rfcs/0007-analyses-in-other-repositories.md:115`

**What is wrong.** A declared analysis can be a git submodule (639-640). A result belongs to the analysis whose universe it was built in. A result built once belongs to the analysis whose output it is (852-854). Its record is "tracked in git with the analysis" (887), and RFC 0007:115 says "Records are committed in the repository of the analysis they belong to". So a record of a submodule analysis's result is committed in the submodule's repository.

`commit` is "the git commit the recipe ran from. It pins every file of the code and every `astra.yaml`, an analysis in a submodule through the commit the submodule is pinned at" (901-903). The natural reading is the root's commit, the superproject's. Under that reading the spec contradicts itself:

- "The commit a record names stays in the history of every commit that holds the record" (977-978) is false. No commit of the submodule's repository has the superproject's commit.
- A url declaration of the submodule names a commit `B` "later than the commit their recipes ran from, `A`, which the records name" (818-819). `A` isn't in that repository, so `B` can't be later than it.
- Nobody who has only the submodule's repository can evaluate `STALE_RESULT` ("since the commit in a result's record"). That covers the submodule checked on its own, or declared by url and commit.

The other reading, the submodule's own commit, leaves the 902-903 clause nothing to apply to. It also fails "pins every file" (901-902) for a submodule that declares its parent with `..` (640-642), whose recipes can read files that commit doesn't pin. The text comes word for word from RFC 0005:119, and the same wording is in `provenance.yaml:75-77`. The design doesn't list this (§11-§13), and the tidy-up draft never mentions submodules.

**Evidence.** Two documents, each VALID in the Python and Ajv validators, as is the record:

```yaml
# lib/astra.yaml (its own repository, then a submodule of super)
spec_version: "0.1.0"
outputs:
  base:
    type: data
    format: csv
    recipe:
      command: python make.py --out {output}
# super/astra.yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
analyses:
  lib: {path: lib}
outputs:
  summary:
    type: metric
    format: json
    inputs: [lib.base]
    recipe:
      command: python summarise.py --data {inputs.lib.base} --out {output}
```

A simulated runner at super's HEAD `P` writes `lib/.astra/records/base.json` with `generated_by.commit` = `P` (C locale):

```
S (lib, pinned in super) = 9a8ed9f4063bda5001a59d30c930d9995cfb5087
P (super HEAD)           = a2e51cc89ff778e0c0b3f060a75627b14ed2cb5b
$ git -C super add lib/.astra/records/base.json
fatal: Pathspec 'lib/.astra/records/base.json' is in submodule 'lib'
B (lib commit holding the record) = f735e2dd1f799d95c472d9e25fab5cf0d92a83d6
$ git -C super/lib cat-file -t P
fatal: git cat-file: could not get object info
$ git -C super/lib merge-base --is-ancestor P B
fatal: Not a valid commit name a2e51cc89ff778e0c0b3f060a75627b14ed2cb5b
# consumer: clone of lib, declared by url + commit B
$ git diff --stat P B
fatal: bad object a2e51cc89ff778e0c0b3f060a75627b14ed2cb5b
```

An independent run gives the same result. The record is in lib's tree at the commit that holds it and in none of super's trees. `P` is not a valid commit in lib.

**Why it matters.** Runner authors must pick a commit to write, and two of them will pick differently. With the root's commit, a checker of the submodule's analysis, or of an analysis that declares it by url, can't evaluate staleness. With the submodule's commit, a record can claim to pin files it doesn't. The schemas can't see either case. This is a check- and runner-layer defect.

**Suggested fix.** Say a record names a commit of the repository it is committed in, the submodule's commit for a submodule's analysis, and say what that pins when the submodule declares outside itself. Or keep the root's commit and say how a checker without the root treats it. Either way, this is a tidy-up change to RFC 0005 §2.

### F044. NOT_AT_COMMIT's "exactly its files at that commit" doesn't say which files or which bytes

- **Lens:** Implementer, Edge cases · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:159-160`, `docs/specification.md:352-354`, `docs/specification.md:774-776`, `docs/specification.md:829-832`, `docs/specification.md:901-903`, `docs/specification.md:970-972`, `docs/specification.md:1199`, `rfcs/0007-analyses-in-other-repositories.md:144`

**What is wrong.** NOT_AT_COMMIT is a MUST: a runner drives an analysis "on exactly its files at that commit". The spec never defines a commit's files, and two things are left open:

- **Which files.** The text supports three readings: the analysis's directory, that directory minus the analyses nested in it, or the repository tree at the commit. Nothing says whether submodules are provided at their pinned commits. For a `url` the analysis is at the repository root, so the readings agree. For a `path` + `commit` declaration of a nested analysis they differ.
- **Which bytes.** Nothing says whether the files are the tree's raw blobs or what git checks out. The two differ whenever the commit's `.gitattributes` sets `eol`, `ident` or `working-tree-encoding`, and the reader's config (`core.autocrlf`, filter drivers) adds more variance among checkout-based runners.

The same question is open wherever the spec names a commit's files: `Generation.commit` "pins every file of the code" (L901-902), and an input read by address is got "from its `path` at the declared commit" (L826-827). The wording comes from RFC 0007 §7 (L144), which has the same words, uses only a `url` in its driving example, and doesn't mention `.gitattributes` or filters. It is an ambiguity the RFC left open, not a departure from it.

**Evidence.** The rule names "its files" and nothing more:

- `docs/specification.md:1199`: "A runner drives an analysis from a commit on exactly its files at that commit".
- `docs/specification.md:829-831`: "Its recipes then run on exactly its files at the declared commit ([`NOT_AT_COMMIT`](#runner)). Where the tool puts those files is the tool's choice".

*Which files.* The passages behind each reading:

- Directory, `:159-160`: "An **analysis** is a directory with an `astra.yaml` at its top. Its recipes run in that directory."
- Directory minus nested analyses, `:352-354`: an input file "is inside the analysis's directory and outside every analysis within it".
- Repository tree, `:970-972`: "anything else it uses is pinned by the commit or is part of the environment"; `:901-902`: the commit "pins every file of the code".

A minimal case where the readings differ. Both files validate against the committed schema, with the Python and the JS validators:

```yaml
# monorepo/astra.yaml  (the driver)
analyses:
  stage_then:
    path: stage
    commit: "0123456789abcdef0123456789abcdef01234567"
universes:
  u1:
    decisions:
      stage_then.method: a
```
```yaml
# monorepo/stage/astra.yaml  (driven, read at the commit)
outputs:
  result:
    recipe:
      command: python ../src/x.py --method {decisions.method} --out {output}
```

A runner that provides the repository tree resolves `../src/x.py`; one that provides only `stage/` does not. A driven analysis can also drive analyses it declares by `path` alone (`examples/iris_pipeline/classification/astra.yaml:12,14`: `..` and `../feature_extraction`), which lie outside "its files" under the directory reading.

*Which bytes.* The text points both ways: reads need "no checkout" and git checks them "against the commit id" (L774-776), while "Where the tool puts those files" is the tool's choice (L831), which invites a checkout. git 2.56.0, `GIT_CONFIG_GLOBAL=/dev/null GIT_CONFIG_NOSYSTEM=1`, one commit B with `data/iris.csv` = `a,b\n1,2\n$Id$\n`; values are the first 12 hex digits of each SHA-256:

```
== no attributes
  cat-file blob / show / cat-file --filters / archive / clone   de7dc4727c5e
  -c core.autocrlf=true archive | worktree add                  6555ab5755d7
== .gitattributes in B: data/iris.csv ident
  cat-file blob / show                    de7dc4727c5e
  cat-file --filters / archive / clone    0f70b8956c8f
  -c core.autocrlf=true archive|worktree  e04052dfdcd6
== .gitattributes in B: data/iris.csv text eol=crlf (no reader config)
  cat-file blob / show                              de7dc4727c5e
  cat-file --filters / archive / clone / worktree   6555ab5755d7
```

With `working-tree-encoding=UTF-16LE`, `cat-file blob` gives 8 bytes and `cat-file --filters`, `archive` and a clone give 16. With Git LFS (`filter=lfs`, allowed for inputs at L356-358), `worktree add` gives the data only when git-lfs is installed; otherwise, and through `cat-file blob`, the recipe silently gets the pointer. Git checks only the blob, never what a filter writes:

```
$ git -c filter.crypt.smudge='printf tampered' cat-file --filters $B:data/iris.csv   -> tampered (exit 0)
$ git fsck --full                                                                     -> exit 0
```

That last case needs the runner's own config; the commit can't inject it.

**Why it matters.** Two conforming runners can give a driven recipe different files and different bytes from one declared commit. In a monorepo, recipes of nested analyses that share code outside their directory break under the directory reading and run under the tree reading. A runner that reads objects directly and one that checks out already disagree for an `eol=crlf` file. The records hold the hash of each input read (L362-364, L905-909), so a byte difference is recorded, but nothing checks it against the commit. A runner-layer rule can't be checked by a schema or a validator, so the difference only shows when a result differs or a recipe fails, and runner authors can't tell what satisfies the MUST.

**Suggested fix.** Define a commit's files once, in §7 or §8, and use it for NOT_AT_COMMIT (L830, L1199), inputs read by address and `Generation.commit`: the repository tree at the commit (submodules at their pinned commits), recipes running in the analysis's directory, or else a rule that a recipe uses only its directory; and the bytes as either the raw blob or git's checkout under the commit's own `.gitattributes`, ignoring local eol settings and filter drivers other than recognised stand-in tooling, with stand-ins resolved to the bytes they name and checked against their hash. Put it in the tidy-up RFC.

### F045. `UNCOMMITTED_CHANGES` doesn't say what counts as a change: untracked files, ignored files, submodules, an earlier run's records

- **Lens:** Implementer, Author · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:963-965`, `docs/specification.md:1196`, `docs/specification.md:788-792`, `docs/specification.md:900-902`, `rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:149`, `rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:252`

**What is wrong.** A runner refuses to run "while the repository has changes other than the results and records it writes" (spec 963-965, catalogue row 1196, RFC 0005:149). Nothing says what a change is. Four cases are left open:

- **Untracked files.** "It runs only committed code" (963) suggests an untracked helper module must stop the run. In git's own terms, untracked files aren't "changes".
- **Ignored files.** These are `__pycache__/`, `.venv/` and a recipe's scratch output directory. They aren't mentioned at all.
- **Submodules.** An untracked file inside a submodule shows up in `git status` but not in `git diff HEAD`. Spec 900-902 says the commit pins an analysis in a submodule.
- **"It writes".** This can mean "this run writes", so records and results that an earlier run left uncommitted stop the next run. It can also mean any records and results, so they don't stop it.

The rule row also leaves out a third exemption that the prose states: what a tool keeps of what it read is "none of it ... a change to the declaring analysis's repository that stops a runner from running" (788-792). Until "change" is defined, a tool can't tell whether a cache inside the repository meets this.

`grep -n -i 'untracked\|gitignore\|ignored'` over the spec and RFCs 0005 and 0007 finds nothing. The tidy-up RFC draft and the design doc don't cover the rule either.

**Evidence.** I used a scratch repository (git 2.56.0) with `src/main.py` and a `.gitignore` of `.quarto-out/` and `__pycache__/`, all committed:

```
== untracked helper (echo 'def f(): return 1' > src/helper.py)
$ git status --porcelain --untracked-files=all
?? src/helper.py
$ git diff --quiet HEAD; echo $?
0
== ignored scratch output
$ git status --porcelain
(nothing)
$ git status --porcelain --ignored
!! .quarto-out/
== one Python run, no .gitignore
$ git status --porcelain --untracked-files=all
?? src/__pycache__/helper.cpython-314.pyc
== an earlier run's record and result, uncommitted
$ git status --porcelain --untracked-files=all
?? .astra/records/accuracy.json
?? results/accuracy.json
== untracked file inside a submodule
$ git status --porcelain
 M sub
$ git diff --quiet HEAD; echo $?
0
```

I checked a second repository the same way, with an untracked `src/helper.py` and an ignored `__pycache__/x.pyc`. `git diff-index --quiet HEAD` exits 0, and `git describe --always --dirty` gives `b7275f9` with no `-dirty`.

The sources point both ways. RFC 0006:88 says "an input's file isn't code, so #91's runner, which only runs committed code, doesn't cover it". That is why `INPUT_NOT_TRACKED` exists. If the runner used `git status`, it would already refuse an untracked input file.

**Why it matters.** Two conforming runners disagree on the same checkout:

- **A runner that checks with `git diff`, `diff-index` or `git describe --dirty`.** It runs a recipe that imports the uncommitted `src/helper.py`, and the record's `commit` doesn't pin the code that ran. `UNDECLARED_READ` (970-973) already forbids that recipe, but runners only MAY enforce it.
- **A runner that checks with `git status`.** It refuses after one Python run that leaves `__pycache__/` behind.

Authors can't tell whether they need to gitignore scratch output.

**Suggested fix.** Define "changes" as what `git status --porcelain` reports, with submodules included and ignored files excluded. Exempt every analysis's `.astra/records/` and `results/`, and the places tools keep what they read (788-792). Say that `UNDECLARED_READ` covers an ignored file a recipe uses. Reconcile RFC 0006:88 through the tidy-up RFC.

### F046. A `missing` result whose file is there but not valid has no settled bytes status

- **Lens:** Implementer · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:1007-1010`, `docs/specification.md:1158-1159`, `docs/specification.md:1170-1172`, `docs/specification.md:1180`, `docs/specification.md:1182`, `docs/specification.md:1184`, `rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:46`, `rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:199`

**What is wrong.** The record status is defined by "no valid record". The bytes status is defined by "its record's hash and size". The spec never says whether a record file that is at the result's place, names the result, but breaks `INVALID_RECORD` or `MISSING_RECORD_INPUTS` counts as "its record". Reading A takes that file's `content_hash`, and the bytes can be `present`. Reading B says the result has no valid record, so it has nothing to match against, and the bytes are `absent`. Both readings follow from the text. Rows 1180 and 1182 call the files that break them "a record" ("Every record is valid…", "Every record has exactly one `inputs` item…"), which supports A. RFC 0005:199 calls one "a file breaking a rule of section 1 or 2", which leans towards B without saying so. Line 1158-1159 covers only a result that breaks no rule, and `ABSENT_RESULT` (1184, "recorded bytes") says nothing when no record is valid. RFC 0005 leaves the same gap, and design §§12-13 do not list it.

The simple `MISPLACED_RECORD` case is settled: "A record is the record only of the result its `address` names" (869-870), so the bytes are `absent`. `MISPLACED_RECORD` stays ambiguous only when a record naming the result is kept at another place.

**Evidence.**

spec:1007-1010:
> one for its record, `unchanged`, `stale`, or `missing` when it has no valid record where its address says; and one for its bytes, `present` when the bytes at its default place have its record's hash and size, `absent` otherwise.

spec:1171: "`present` | The bytes at the result's default place hash to the record's `content_hash` and have its `size`".

Take the §2 analysis, where `accuracy` lists `inputs: [iris]`. Write `{"accuracy": 0.97}\n` to `results/svm/accuracy.json` (sha256 `b4e558c0…8b69`, 19 bytes). At `.astra/records/svm/accuracy.json`, put the spec's example record (spec:922-943) with `generated_by.inputs` removed and that hash and size:

```
$ uv run python toolkit/validate.py ProvenanceRecord .../records/svm/accuracy.json
VALID
$ node toolkit/js/validate.mjs <repo> ProvenanceRecord .../records/svm/accuracy.json
VALID
```

`Generation.inputs` is not required (`src/astra/schema/provenance.yaml:78-86`), so the record is schema-valid. It breaks `MISSING_RECORD_INPUTS`, so the result is `missing`. Its bytes match the file's `content_hash` and `size`: `present` under A, `absent` under B. The same split happens under `INVALID_RECORD` when only an unrelated field is bad. Change `finished` to `"2026-10-26T14:00:02Z"` and both validators report a `pattern` error at `/generated_by/finished`, but the file's `content_hash` still matches.

**Why it matters.** A realistic case: an output gains an input after its record is written. One conforming checker reports `missing`/`present`. Another reports `missing`/`absent`, and may report `ABSENT_RESULT` too. Reports and any future check-conformance fixture differ. No gate changes: `STALE_EVIDENCE` (1185) uses only the record status, and `STALE_INPUT` (1201) needs `unchanged` first.

**Suggested fix.** Say at 1010 and 1171 that the bytes status is taken against a valid record only: "A result with no valid record has nothing to match: its bytes are `absent`." Or define `present` against the record at the result's place, valid or not.

### F047. Whether validating the root checks a driven analysis's own, unused universes is not said

- **Lens:** Implementer, Author · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:89-91`, `docs/specification.md:675-677`, `docs/specification.md:705-708`, `docs/specification.md:735-737`, `docs/specification.md:794-795`, `docs/specification.md:1118`, `docs/specification.md:1129`, `docs/specification.md:1135`, `docs/specification.md:1151`, `rfcs/0004-composition.md:149`

**What is wrong.** A validator reads every `astra.yaml` of the stack. The spec doesn't say whether a rule broken only in a driven analysis's own universes makes the root invalid. Those universes "aren't used" (l.705), but §9 states the universe rules for every universe. For an analysis from a commit the answer is explicit: `DECLARED_ANALYSIS_INVALID` requires it to be valid "with everything it declares". A `path` declaration has no such row. `UNSUPPORTED_SPEC_VERSION`'s parenthesis covers only the schema layer. `NO_UNIVERSES`'s "unless it is driven" (l.1151) only makes sense if validator rules run on files other than the root's, so the open question is the narrower one: a driven analysis's own universes. It changes again when a second, undriven declaration of the same directory reads one of them (l.735-737): those universes are used. Are all of them checked then, or only the pinned ones?

**Evidence.** Two minimal stacks. In each, the root's file breaks nothing and the driven file's own universe breaks a validator rule.

```yaml
# a/astra.yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
analyses:
  sub: {path: sub}
outputs:
  report: {type: metric, format: json, inputs: [sub.r]}
universes:
  base:
    decisions: {sub.method: pca, sub.ncomp: two}
# a/sub/astra.yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
decisions:
  method: {options: {pca: {}, ica: {}}}
  ncomp: {when: [method.pca], options: {two: {}, five: {}}}
outputs:
  r: {type: metric, format: json, decisions: [method, ncomp]}
universes:
  own:                  # ncomp is active (method is pca) and unset
    decisions: {method: pca}
```

Stack `b` is the same shape: `inner` has decision `d {a, b}` and its own universe `v1: {decisions: {d: c}}`, which breaks `UNKNOWN_OPTION`.

```
$ uv run python toolkit/validate.py Analysis a/astra.yaml a/sub/astra.yaml b/astra.yaml b/inner/astra.yaml
VALID / VALID / VALID / VALID
$ node toolkit/js/validate.mjs <repo> Analysis (same four files)
VALID / VALID / VALID / VALID
```

Validated as the root, `sub` breaks `MISSING_DECISION`. Validated through its driver, the text reads two ways:

- Check them: l.89-91 "reports every parse, schema and validator rule the analysis breaks"; l.1135 `MISSING_DECISION` "Every universe's effective selection sets every active decision of its analysis and of every analysis it drives"; l.708 "Driving leaves an analysis able to stand alone".
- Ignore them: l.705 "A driven analysis's own universes aren't used."; RFC 0004:149 "universes existing in those other driven analyses are considered to not exist".

Only commits are settled: l.1129 "An analysis from a commit, with everything it declares, is valid at that commit". Design §11-§13 and the tidy-up draft don't mention the question.

**Why it matters.** Validator authors will differ, so a CI check on the root passes or fails depending on the tool. A pipeline stage keeps its own universes so it can stand alone. It gains a decision, the author updates the driver's universes and forgets the stage's own. One tool fails the whole pipeline. Another passes it, and the stage silently stops working on its own.

**Suggested fix.** In §7 next to l.705, plus a §9 row, say which universes of the stack a validator checks: every universe of every analysis, as `DECLARED_ANALYSIS_INVALID` does for commits, or only the root's and those pinned through an undriven declaration. State the contrast with `DECLARED_ANALYSIS_INVALID`.

### F048. `NOT_BUILT_IN_UNIVERSE` doesn't say where a decision consumed in a constraint is used

- **Lens:** Implementer · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:683-688`, `docs/specification.md:720-724`, `docs/specification.md:1142`, `docs/specification.md:435-440`

**What is wrong.** §7 counts a decision named in a constraint (`requires`, `incompatible_with`) as consumed. It then says "whatever uses it can be built only where the decision's analysis is built". The user of a constraint is an option, and an option is never built, so this sentence gives a constraint no universe. The two other statements of the rule disagree with each other. "The universe being built" (720-724) applies to every decision of another analysis, with no qualifier. The §9 row (1142) says the decision "is used only where" its analysis is built, but nothing says where a constraint is used. Three readings follow:

- (a) every universe: the constraint is checked in every universe of the driver;
- (b) only the universes whose effective selection selects the option that carries the constraint (inferred from 435 and 1137-1138, not stated);
- (c) constraints are exempt, and only `MISSING_REQUIRED_OPTION` and `INCOMPATIBLE_OPTIONS` apply. Line 685 argues against this reading.

The text comes unchanged from RFC 0004 (`rfcs/0004-composition.md:410`), and no design decision records this case.

**Evidence.**

> **Consuming** is using an element of another analysis by address: an input or an output in `Output.inputs`, a decision in `Output.decisions`, in a condition or in a constraint, and an output in evidence. A consumed decision has the option selected in the universe being built, so its address never takes a pin, and whatever uses it can be built only where the decision's analysis is built in that universe — `docs/specification.md:683-688`

> So, for every output of another analysis read without a pin, and every decision of another analysis: the analysis it belongs to is built in the universe being built — `docs/specification.md:720-724`

> `NOT_BUILT_IN_UNIVERSE` | MUST | Every output of another analysis read without a pin, and every decision of another analysis, is used only where that analysis is built in the universe being built — `docs/specification.md:1142`

Root A drives `b` but not `b.c`. A sets no `b.c.*` key, so C is not built in `u1` (716-718).

```yaml
# r1/astra.yaml (A)
spec_version: "0.1.0"
analyses: {b: {path: b}}
decisions:
  m:
    options:
      m1: {}
      m2: {requires: [b.c.d.p]}
outputs:
  z: {type: metric, format: json, inputs: [b.y], decisions: [m]}
universes:
  u1: {decisions: {m: m1, b.x: x1}}
# r1/b/astra.yaml (B)
spec_version: "0.1.0"
analyses: {c: {path: c}}
decisions: {x: {options: {x1: {}, x2: {}}}}
outputs: {y: {type: metric, format: json, decisions: [x]}}
# r1/b/c/astra.yaml (C)
spec_version: "0.1.0"
decisions: {d: {options: {p: {}, q: {}}}}
outputs: {w: {type: metric, format: json, decisions: [d]}}
universes: {cv1: {decisions: {d: p}}}
```

`r2` is `r1` with `m2: {requires: [b.c.d.p], excluded: not run yet}`. `r3` is `r1` with `m2: {incompatible_with: [b.c.d.p]}` and `u1: {decisions: {m: m2, b.x: x1}}`. All nine files are VALID in both the jsonschema and the Ajv validator, so only the validator rule decides these stacks.

| | (a) every universe | (b) where the option is selected | (c) constraints exempt |
|---|---|---|---|
| r1 (`m2` never selected) | `NOT_BUILT_IN_UNIVERSE` | valid | valid |
| r2 (`m2` excluded) | `NOT_BUILT_IN_UNIVERSE` | valid | valid |
| r3 (`u1` selects `m2`) | `NOT_BUILT_IN_UNIVERSE` | `NOT_BUILT_IN_UNIVERSE` | valid |

**Why it matters.** Validator authors in astra-tools and astra-typescript give these stacks opposite verdicts. The case is narrow: `PARTIALLY_DRIVEN` (1141) means (a) and (b) differ only for an option that is never selected. That is a realistic case, though. An excluded option exists to "record what it considered and why it ruled it out" (444-446), and an author will record "needs T's GPU backend" as `requires` on an excluded option of an analysis whose sibling T the root doesn't build. Under (a) that stack is invalid.

**Suggested fix.** In §7 "Consuming", say where each use counts: an output's where it is built, a condition's in every universe where it is evaluated, and a constraint's in the universes that select the option carrying it (or exempt constraints). Reword 720-724 to match 1142's "used only where".

### F049. §10's "map the innermost errors" fails on standard output: an untaken `if` or a passing `oneOf`'s failed branch shows up as an innermost error

- **Lens:** Implementer · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:1303-1313`, `src/astra/schema/generated/analysis.schema.json` (`#/$defs/AnalysisDeclaration__identifier_optional`, `#/$defs/Evidence`), `tools/rules.py:410`, `tests/test_rules.py:328`

**What is wrong.** §10 tells a tool using a validator that writes a standard JSON Schema output format to "map the innermost errors". It says each such error has an entry in `rules.json`. That holds for `jsonschema` and Ajv, but not for standard output. A standard format can also list failures that do not decide the result. JSON Schema 2019-09 core §9.2.2.1 says the `if` subschema's outcome "has no direct effect on the overall validation result". §10 of the same document does not keep `if` results out of the output, and its Detailed format requires a node for `"if"/"then"/"else"`. When a declaration or evidence object is invalid for another reason, two 2019-09 validators produce innermost errors that are not failures:

- jschon 0.11.1 (basic, detailed and verbose) lists the untaken `if`'s own failure: `…/if/required` or `…/Evidence/if/not`. `rules.json` has no entry for either location. It has entries only for `…/if` itself.
- @hyperjump/json-schema 1.11.0 (BASIC) lists the failing branch of a `oneOf` that passed. That branch has an entry, so the tool reports the wrong code, and nothing signals the mistake.

The rule that nothing inside an `if` or a `not` is reported is written only in `tools/rules.py:410` and `tests/test_rules.py:328`. Both describe `jsonschema` and Ajv. The specification does not state it.

**Evidence.** The paragraph at `docs/specification.md:1307-1313`:

> Each such error has an entry, including those that say why a `oneOf` failed. A validator that writes one of JSON Schema's standard output formats also lists the applicators above an error (`properties`, `items`, `$ref`, an `additionalProperties` that holds a schema, `then`). Those have no entry; a tool maps the innermost errors.

Two minimal documents, each with `outputs: {result: {type: metric, format: json}}`:

```yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
analyses:
  up: {path: up, commit: "abc123"}        # decl-short-commit
prior_insights:
  p: {claim: A claim., evidence: [{doi: "11.1/x"}]}   # evid-bad-doi (separate file)
```

jschon 0.11.1, `output('basic')`. The innermost rows, each with its `rules.json` code:

```
== decl-short-commit.json | valid: False
   /analyses/up        | …/AnalysisDeclaration__identifier_optional/if/required | code=None | The object is missing required properties ['url']
   /analyses/up/commit | …/AnalysisDeclaration__identifier_optional/properties/commit/pattern | code=INVALID_COMMIT
== evid-bad-doi.json | valid: False
   /prior_insights/p/evidence/0     | …/Evidence/if/not | code=None | The instance must not be valid against the subschema
   /prior_insights/p/evidence/0/doi | …/Evidence/properties/doi/pattern | code=INVALID_VALUE
```

An unknown field, a bad `version`, or a declaration with neither `path` nor `url` gives the same kind of unmapped leaf. Hyperjump 1.11.0, BASIC output:

```
decl-short-commit:     /analyses/up | …/AnalysisDeclaration__identifier_optional/oneOf/1/required | INVALID_DECLARATION_SOURCE
evid-doi-bad-version:  /prior_insights/p/evidence/0 | …/Evidence/oneOf/1/required | INVALID_EVIDENCE_SOURCE
evid-artifact-bad-ref: /prior_insights/p/evidence/0 | …/Evidence/oneOf/0/required | INVALID_EVIDENCE_SOURCE
```

Each of these sources is valid. On the same files, `jsonschema` and Ajv report only `INVALID_COMMIT` and `INVALID_VALUE`. The leak needs the declaration or evidence object itself to be invalid. An error elsewhere in the document does not trigger it. The provenance schemas have no `if` or `oneOf`.

**Why it matters.** It bites tools that follow §10 with a validator other than `jsonschema` or Ajv, such as tools in other languages, editors and CI services. They get errors with no code, which can crash the tool or print "no code". They also get misleading messages ("missing … ['url']" for a declaration by `path`) or a wrong source code. The validators agree on validity, so only the mapping from error to code fails. Two implementers would handle these errors differently, and the tests cover only `jsonschema` and Ajv.

**Suggested fix.** In §10, say that a tool maps only errors that contribute to the result. It drops anything under an `if` subschema, and an error under `oneOf/<i>` unless that `oneOf` itself is reported. Every error that remains has an entry. Alternatively, §10 could stop recommending standard output formats.

### F050. A broken record breaks a schema rule and a check rule at once, and nothing says which code a checker reports

- **Lens:** Implementer · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:87-93`, `docs/specification.md:1058-1059`, `docs/specification.md:1097`, `docs/specification.md:1101`, `docs/specification.md:1170`, `docs/specification.md:1180`, `docs/specification.md:1243-1246`, `src/astra/schema/generated/rules.json`

**What is wrong.** The specification puts records in two layers. As a document, a record is judged by parse and schema rules (:87-88). `MISSING_FIELD` covers "the record's (§8)" fields (:1101), and `INVALID_SYNTAX` requires that "every record is well-formed JSON" (:1097). In those layers "The file fails" and the code is reported as an error (:1058). The same breakage also breaks the check rule `INVALID_RECORD` (:1180), where "Nothing fails" and the result gets `missing` (:1059). No role is told to report a record's parse or schema codes. The validator reads `astra.yaml` files (:89-91), and the checker works "by the check rules (§8)" (:92-93). Only a checker reads the open variant (:1243-1246), yet `rules.json` maps every error the open variant can raise to a schema-layer code and none to `INVALID_RECORD`. So a checker can report `INVALID_RECORD`, or the schema code, or `INVALID_RECORD` with the schema code as its reason. The text allows all three.

**Evidence.** A minimal record with no `content_hash`, run through both validators and mapped by `rules.json`:

```
$ uv run python toolkit/validate.py ProvenanceRecord [--open] no-hash.json
(root) | https://w3id.org/astra/provenance.schema.json#/required | MISSING_FIELD | 'content_hash' is a required property
(root) | https://w3id.org/astra/provenance.open.schema.json#/required | MISSING_FIELD | 'content_hash' is a required property
$ node toolkit/js/validate.mjs <repo> ProvenanceRecord [--open] no-hash.json
(root) | https://w3id.org/astra/provenance.open.schema.json#/required | MISSING_FIELD | must have required property 'content_hash'
```

`rules.json` `errors`: 35 entries under `provenance.open.schema.json` (WRONG_TYPE 19, INVALID_VALUE 8, MISSING_FIELD 5, INVALID_REFERENCE 2, INVALID_COMMIT 1) and 40 under `provenance.schema.json` (the same plus UNKNOWN_FIELD 5), all schema-layer. `INVALID_RECORD` appears in no error, and `grep -rn INVALID_RECORD tests tools examples` finds nothing. The record fixtures name schema codes: `tests/data/invalid/ProvenanceRecord-201-no-content-hash.yaml:1` `# rule: MISSING_FIELD`, `ProvenanceRecord-215-unknown-field.yaml:1` `# rule: UNKNOWN_FIELD`.

Spec :1180: "| `INVALID_RECORD` | MUST | Every record is valid against the record schema of its `spec_version`, or against the open variant when that version is newer than the checker's | `missing` |". Spec :1249-1250: the catalogue exists "so that every tool reports the same code for the same error".

RFC 0005 puts record-schema conformance in the check layer only (`rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:142`, "| check | `missing` |"). The specification also adds records to the parse and schema layers, and design §11 lists no departure for that. That is where the overlap comes from.

**Why it matters.** Checker authors in astra-tools and astra-typescript will choose differently. One reports `INVALID_RECORD`, another `MISSING_FIELD`, a third both. The status is `missing` under every reading, so the contract holds. The reported code still differs, and a shared catalogue exists to prevent exactly that.

**Suggested fix.** Say in §9's check introduction or in §10 that a checker reports `INVALID_RECORD` for a record that fails parsing or its schema, with each underlying code (from `rules.json`, or the loader for parse codes) as its reason. Or take records out of the parse and schema layers.

### F051. Stand-ins are given only as a loose example list, which §5, §7 and §8 read differently

- **Lens:** Implementer, Standards · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:350-351`, `docs/specification.md:356-360`, `docs/specification.md:823-827`, `docs/specification.md:889-891`, `docs/specification.md:1011-1014`, `docs/specification.md:1130`, `docs/specification.md:1173`, `rfcs/0005-results.md:41`, `rfcs/0005-results.md:46`, `rfcs/0006-data-inputs.md:55`, `rfcs/0007-analyses-in-other-repositories.md:130-132`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:619`

**What is wrong.** The spec never defines what a stand-in is. §5 gives "something standing in for it that tooling recognises" and then an example list: "a git-annex link or a Git LFS pointer at the path, or a DVC file beside it". It does not say whether the list is closed, which forms a validator must recognise, or what "beside it" means. Other sections then read the list in different ways:

- **`MISSING_INPUT_FILE` (§5, a MUST).** Git-annex links and LFS pointers sit at the path, so a validator accepts them without recognising them. DVC files do not. DVC tracks a file with `<file>.dvc` beside it, a directory with one `<dir>.dvc` beside the directory, and a pipeline output with no `.dvc` file at all (its hash is in `dvc.lock`). Each reading gives a different verdict on the same repository. The results table makes recognition optional (`MAY`, :1173); the input rule has no such qualifier.
- **Getting an input at a commit (§7).** An input read by address "is got in the same way" as a result, from what stands in for it "there", that is at its `path`. That leaves out the DVC file beside the path that §5 accepts. RFC 0007:132 names no place and points to RFC 0006, which includes the DVC file. Only the specification adds "there".
- **Git-annex's forms (§5, §8).** "git-annex link" names only the symlink git-annex commits for a locked file. An unlocked file is committed as a regular blob holding `/annex/objects/<key>`, which git-annex calls a pointer file. A checker that reads "link" as "symlink" reports `modified` for it where `absent` is correct (:1013, :1173).

The wording comes from RFC 0006:55 and RFC 0005:41, :46. Design §§11–13 and the tidy-up draft do not mention stand-ins.

**Evidence.** The text:

```
docs/specification.md:356: - What is committed is the file itself, or something standing in for it that
docs/specification.md:357:   tooling recognises: a git-annex link or a Git LFS pointer at the path, or a
docs/specification.md:358:   DVC file beside it ([`MISSING_INPUT_FILE`](#validator)).
```
