```
docs/specification.md:1130: | `MISSING_INPUT_FILE` | MUST | An input's `path` names a file, or something standing in for one | 0006 |
docs/specification.md:1173: | `modified` | Reported only by tooling that recognises stand-ins (`MAY`): the stand-in names another hash, or the file is plainly not a stand-in and doesn't match
```

`docs/specification.md:823-827` (§7): "When a recipe reads the result, its bytes come from what git holds at the result's default place at the declared commit, or from the tooling that recognises what stands in for them there, and are checked against the record. An input of the declared analysis read by address is got in the same way, from its `path` at the declared commit." `docs/specification.md:890-891` (§8): "git-annex or Git LFS, for example, keep them in a store and leave a link or a pointer standing in for them."

DVC 3.67.1, committed and cloned fresh. `astra.yaml` has inputs `big: {path: data/big.csv}` (`dvc add data/big.csv`), `shard: {path: raw/shard_01.csv}` (`dvc add raw`) and `derived: {path: prepared/clean.csv}` (a `dvc.yaml` stage output). The Python and Ajv validators both report VALID.

```
$ git ls-files
.dvc/.gitignore  .dvc/config  .dvcignore  .gitignore  astra.yaml  data/.gitignore
data/big.csv.dvc  dvc.lock  dvc.yaml  prepared/.gitignore  raw.dvc
(in the clone)
data/big.csv         exists=no  tracked=no  sibling .dvc=yes
raw/shard_01.csv     exists=no  tracked=no  sibling .dvc=no
prepared/clean.csv   exists=no  tracked=no  sibling .dvc=no
```

- `<path>.dvc` only: `big` passes; `shard` and `derived` fail.
- Also a `<dir>.dvc` beside an ancestor: `shard` passes too.
- Anything DVC recognises: `derived` passes through `dvc.lock`.
- No DVC support: all three fail.

"It names one file" (:350) can't be checked for `shard` from a clone even by DVC-aware tooling: the directory's file list is only in the gitignored `.dvc/cache`, so `raw/shard_01.csv` and a misspelt `raw/shard_99.csv` look the same.

At a commit after `dvc add data/big.csv`, git holds nothing at the path, yet DVC gets the bytes:

```
git ls-tree 2645ce47... -- data/big.csv     : ''
git ls-tree 2645ce47... -- data/big.csv.dvc : '100644 blob 2243012d...	data/big.csv.dvc'
sha256 of original bytes: b9485148...d5a5
dvc get <repo> data/big.csv --rev 2645ce47... -> b9485148...d5a5  big.csv
```

git-annex 10.20260421, `locked.json` added with `git annex add`, `unlocked.json` with `git -c annex.largefiles=anything add`:

```
== git ls-files -s
120000 75795161...	results/u1/locked.json
100644 f7c9ccdb...	results/u1/unlocked.json
== fresh clone (no annex get)
results/u1/unlocked.json | islink False | isfile True | /annex/objects/SHA256E-s19--6e53...8d60.json
results/u1/locked.json   | islink True  | isfile False | ../../.git/annex/objects/.../SHA256E-s19--6b4c...2d75.json
```

A locked file dangles in a fresh clone, so whether it passes `MISSING_INPUT_FILE` also depends on whether the validator recognises annex. git-annex-unlock(1): "Unlocking a file changes how it is stored in the git repository (from a symlink to a pointer file)" and "If you use git add to add a file to the annex, it will be added in unlocked form from the beginning." Unlocked files are also the default on filesystems without symlinks (tips/unlocked_files).

**Why it matters.** The same committed analysis passes one conforming validator and fails another on a MUST, so astra-tools and astra-typescript can disagree on a common DVC layout and an author can't tell which is right. A runner that follows §7 literally can't get a DVC-tracked input from a commit, though one that follows RFC 0007 can. A checker that implements only the named forms reports unlocked annexed results (on Windows, or added with `git add`) as `modified` where they are `absent`.

**Suggested fix.** Define stand-ins once, in §5, as a closed list a validator MUST recognise (e.g. a git-annex symlink or pointer file, or an LFS pointer, at the path; `<path>.dvc`; a `<dir>.dvc` whose directory contains the path; an `outs` entry in `dvc.lock`, or exclude the last two), and have §7 and §8 refer to it. This narrows RFC 0006, so it goes in the tidy-up RFC.

### F052. A missing or malformed `spec_version` breaks UNSUPPORTED_SPEC_VERSION or not, depending on the reader

- **Lens:** Implementer · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:54-61`, `docs/specification.md:89-91`, `docs/specification.md:1118`, `docs/specification.md:1249`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:731-732`

**What is wrong.** The versioning policy is undefined, and the schema checks only the form of `spec_version`. That much is recorded, in spec §1 and in design §12 "For later phases". The record does not cover a file whose `spec_version` is missing or is not a string. No versioning policy would settle that case. Read literally, such a file breaks UNSUPPORTED_SPEC_VERSION as well as the schema rule, because it has no `spec_version` "the validator has the schema for". The validator role says a validator reports every rule the analysis breaks. The rule's parenthetical also makes the choice of schema depend on the stated version, so it leaves no schema to check the rest of the file. One implementer reports only the schema code. A second reports UNSUPPORTED_SPEC_VERSION and skips schema validation. A third reports both. The specification does not choose between them.

**Evidence.** The rule, `docs/specification.md:1118`:

> | `UNSUPPORTED_SPEC_VERSION` | MUST | Every `astra.yaml` in the stack has a `spec_version` the validator has the schema for (the file is then validated against that schema, by the schema layer) | 0007 §6 |

The validator role, `docs/specification.md:89-91`: it "reports every parse, schema and validator rule the analysis breaks."

Minimal documents (a `spec_version` line, then `outputs: {result: {type: metric, format: json}}`). `validate.py` and `validate.mjs` give the same verdicts:

```
missing.yaml (none)    (root) | https://w3id.org/astra/analysis.schema.json#/required | MISSING_FIELD
float.yaml   0.1       /spec_version | ...#/properties/spec_version/type | WRONG_TYPE
short.yaml   "0.1"     /spec_version | ...#/properties/spec_version/pattern | INVALID_VALUE
```

The fixtures for these codes (Analysis-108 MISSING_FIELD, Analysis-133 INVALID_VALUE) pin only the schema-layer code, not the validator-layer outcome. What is recorded, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:731-732`: "whether a v0.1 schema accepts only `0.1.x` files is left to the policy. `spec_version` only checks the format here."

**Why it matters.** Every pre-0.1 `astra.yaml` has `version:` and no `spec_version`, so the first file most users run through a validator hits this case. The file is rejected under every reading. Only the set of codes differs. Spec §10 (`docs/specification.md:1249`) says `rules.json` exists "so that every tool reports the same code for the same error", and here astra-tools and astra-typescript can follow the text and still report different codes.

**Suggested fix.** In the UNSUPPORTED_SPEC_VERSION row or §1, say that the rule applies only to a well-formed `spec_version`, and that the schema layer alone reports a missing or malformed one, against the validator's own (newest) schema. Leave the policy itself to the recorded later phase.

### F053. Nothing reserves `results/` and `.astra/`, so an input or a declaration may point into them

- **Lens:** Implementer · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `src/astra/schema/analysis.yaml:156-163`, `src/astra/schema/analysis.yaml:364-373`, `docs/specification.md:198-199`, `docs/specification.md:344`, `docs/specification.md:350-364`, `docs/specification.md:393-394`, `docs/specification.md:598-599`, `docs/specification.md:633-640`, `docs/specification.md:866-872`, `docs/specification.md:891-892`, `docs/specification.md:908-909`, `docs/specification.md:963-965`, `docs/specification.md:968-977`, `docs/specification.md:983-993`, `docs/specification.md:1124-1125`, `docs/specification.md:1130-1133`, `docs/specification.md:1146-1148`, `docs/specification.md:1183`, `docs/specification.md:1195-1196`, `docs/specification.md:1201`, `rfcs/0004-composition.md:83`, `rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:149`, `rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:153`, `rfcs/0006-data-inputs.md:88`

**What is wrong.** §3 describes `results/` and `.astra/records/` (:198-199) but doesn't reserve them. No rule keeps an `Input.path` or a declaration's `path` out of them, and the `INVALID_PATH` pattern accepts un-normalised spellings (`./.astra//records/summary.json`, `src/../results`). Once the file is committed, the input rules hold (:1130-1132); a declaration has only `DECLARATION_OUTSIDE_REPOSITORY` and `MISSING_DECLARED_ANALYSIS` (:1124-1125). The rules on reading a result (build order, `STALE_INPUT`, `STALE_RESULT`'s "results it reads", `OUTPUT_CYCLE`, the pins) follow references only, so a read by path escapes them all. Four facets:

| Where the path points | What goes wrong |
|---|---|
| An input at its own output's record or default place | A file-system cycle `OUTPUT_CYCLE` doesn't see. With its own record, the result is stale after every run and commit; with its own default place, it is stale until an idempotent recipe settles |
| An input at another output's default place (or, in a driver, a driven result such as `results/u1/sub.output1.csv`) | The result is read outside the result graph: no build order, no `STALE_INPUT`, no pin or universe rule. Two dependency-respecting orders record different inputs at one commit |
| An input at a result's default place, overwritten by the run | `UNCOMMITTED_CHANGES` exempts it as "a result it writes", so the record names a commit at which the input differed. The spec also drops RFC 0005's "itself", so a hand edit to such an input passes the guard too |
| A declaration's `path` at `results` or `results/x` | A root output `astra` (format `yaml`) overwrites the declared analysis's `astra.yaml`; an output matching one of its input files overwrites that input. Under `.astra/records/`, a record can overwrite a declared `.json` input |

Every facet makes `docs/specification.md:975-977` false for a document every rule accepts: "That commit adds only records and results, so it makes nothing stale." The input cases need `results/` tracked in git, which the spec (:976) and RFC 0005:215 (`git annex add results`) expect.

**Evidence.** All these documents are VALID under both the Python `jsonschema` harness and Ajv:

```yaml
# own record (variants: .astra/records/u1/summary.json, results/summary.json, ./.astra//records/summary.json)
inputs: {previous: {path: .astra/records/summary.json}}
outputs:
  summary: {type: data, format: json, inputs: [previous], recipe: {command: python summarise.py {inputs} {output}}}
```

```yaml
# another output's default place
inputs: {raw: {path: results/clean.csv}}
outputs:
  clean: {type: data, format: csv, recipe: {command: python clean.py --out {output}}}
  plot:  {type: figure, format: png, inputs: [raw], recipe: {command: python plot.py {inputs.raw} --out {output}}}
```

```yaml
# declaration into results/ (tree/results/astra.yaml is a valid analysis)
analyses: {nested: {path: results}}
outputs:
  astra: {type: data, format: yaml, recipe: {command: python src/make.py --out {output}}}
```

So are `analyses: {sub: {path: sub}}` with an input at `results/u1/sub.output1.csv`, inputs at `astra.yaml` and `.astra/records/clean.json`, and declarations at `results/x`, `src/../results` and `.astra/records/x`.

Simulations in scratch git repositories, with a runner that refuses a dirty tree, records HEAD and commits only `.astra/records/` and `results/`, and a checker that follows §8 literally:

```
own record:            run 1 from 9d3fa32, commit C1; check at C1: stale (recorded sha256:44136fa355b3 != now sha256:c460decffe45); runs 2, 3: stale
own place, counter:    C1, C2, C3 stale
own place, idempotent: C1 stale; C2, C3 unchanged
other place, order [clean, plot]: recorded raw sha256:12631912c84e -> unchanged
other place, order [plot, clean]: recorded raw sha256:d0604fa5e8d9 -> stale ['content of input raw']
clean's command changes: path form   clean stale, plot unchanged
                         reference form   clean stale, plot stale ['reads stale clean']
overwritten input: guard before plot sees ['.astra/records/clean.json', 'results/clean.csv'], other than what it writes: []
                   plot recorded raw sha256:1989f997fa2f at ef3955ad49; git show C0:results/clean.csv -> sha256:a7c4ffe00aa3
hand edit, not committed: guard sees nothing to refuse; same edit at data/raw.csv: REFUSES (UNCOMMITTED_CHANGES)
```

- `docs/specification.md:963-965`: "while the repository has changes other than the results and records it writes, it refuses to run"; RFC 0005:149 has "the results and records it writes itself".
- `docs/specification.md:891-892`: "Whatever is at a result's default place is its recorded bytes or something standing in for them." No code enforces it, and it lets an implementer read `raw` as "a result it reads".
- `docs/specification.md:599`: "Inputs carry no universe", so a driver's read of u1's result takes no pin (:1146-1147).
- `docs/specification.md:983`: "A record guarantees what its result was made from at the commit it names".
- `rfcs/0004-composition.md:83`: "with the strong requirement that artifact dependencies cannot form cycles."
- `docs/specification.md:1195`: a runner "writes the bytes to their default place" (MUST).

**Why it matters.** Validator authors have no rule to reject these documents, and may disagree on whether `OUTPUT_CYCLE`'s undefined "depends on" covers paths. Checkers must report `stale` after a conforming run and commit, which the spec says can't happen; checkers that compare the record's hash with the file now (:362-364) and those that compare against the record's commit (:983-991) give different statuses for one record. A runner writes records whose commit doesn't reproduce their inputs, the guarantee RFC 0005 exists for, or overwrites a file of the stack it is running. The false sentence at :975-977 comes from RFC 0005:153, so the RFC shares the fault.

**Suggested fix.** Reserve `results/` and `.astra/` in §3 and add a validator rule with its own code: an input's path, and a declaration's path, after `.`, `..` and `//` are resolved, lie outside the `results/` and `.astra/` of every analysis in the stack, and an input is not `astra.yaml`. Say a result is read only by reference, and restore "itself" in `UNCOMMITTED_CHANGES`.

### F054. Nothing says the root is never driven, so in a declaration cycle the root is driven by an analysis in its own stack

- **Lens:** Implementer · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:48`, `docs/specification.md:673-675`, `docs/specification.md:690-695`, `docs/specification.md:705-706`, `docs/specification.md:708`, `docs/specification.md:849-853`, `docs/specification.md:1151`, `examples/iris_pipeline/classification/astra.yaml:10-14`, `examples/iris_pipeline/astra.yaml:100`, `examples/iris_pipeline/astra.yaml:109`, `rfcs/0004-composition.md:63`, `rfcs/0004-composition.md:149`

**What is wrong.** The spec defines driving as a fixed relation over the files, not relative to the root. The table at spec:48 has "an analysis whose universes set another's decisions, and that other". spec:690-695 says the same. "Any analysis can be the root" (673-674), and "A driven analysis's own universes aren't used" (705). No sentence says which universes are built for a given root, or that the root is never driven. In an acyclic stack the question can't come up. With a back-edge (`path: ..`) it does.

Take `examples/iris_pipeline/classification` as the root. It declares `iris: {path: ..}`, and the parent's universes set `classification.classifier`. classification has a decision, per-universe outputs, no universes, and consumes no decision. That leaves two readings:

- **S (driving over the whole stack):** classification is driven. NO_UNIVERSES exempts it, it is built in the parent's universes, and its results belong to the parent (852-853).
- **R (driving only from the root):** classification is undriven. NO_UNIVERSES warns, and nothing is built per universe.

Mutual driving makes reading S self-defeating. Each analysis is driven, so by 705 neither one's universes are used, yet the root's universes call for planned results (849-850). No §9 code rejects mutual driving. "Each universe being built has one driver" (706) is prose with no code.

The spec (708, "Driving leaves an analysis able to stand alone") and RFC 0004 (:26, :63, :305, :363) point to reading R, but nothing normative says it. RFC 0003:148-151 did say it ("Only the root analysis's universes are built"). Design:546 marks that as superseded by 0004, and 0004 doesn't restate it. Design §11-§13 don't record this point.

**Evidence.** A resolver that follows `analyses.*.path` from the root and resolves each flat key to a directory:

```
$ uv run python $W/stack.py examples/iris_pipeline/classification
root: . | stack: ['.', '..', '../feature_extraction']
root decisions: ['classifier'] | root universes: []
  universe ..#baseline sets 'classification.classifier' -> decision 'classifier' of .   <-- the root
  universe ..#mlp_svm sets 'classification.classifier' -> decision 'classifier' of .   <-- the root
```

The flat keys are at examples/iris_pipeline/astra.yaml:100 and :109. feature_extraction consumes `iris.random_seed`, so NO_UNIVERSES exempts it under both readings. classification is the stage where the readings split.

Mutual driving. Both files are `VALID` in validate.py and in validate.mjs (Ajv):

```yaml
# m/astra.yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
analyses: {fe: {path: fe}}
decisions: {seed: {options: {s1: {}, s2: {}}}}
outputs:
  data: {type: data, format: csv, decisions: [seed]}
  report: {type: report, format: md, inputs: [fe.features]}
universes:
  r1: {decisions: {seed: s1, fe.method: pca}}
  r2: {decisions: {seed: s2, fe.method: mlp}}
# m/fe/astra.yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
analyses: {parent: {path: ..}}
decisions: {method: {options: {pca: {}, mlp: {}}}}
outputs:
  features: {type: data, format: npy, inputs: [parent.data], decisions: [method]}
universes:
  f1: {decisions: {method: pca, parent.seed: s1}}
```

```
$ stack.py $W/m
  universe .#r1 sets 'fe.method' -> decision 'method' of fe
  universe fe#f1 sets 'parent.seed' -> decision 'seed' of .   <-- the root
```

- spec:705-706: "A driven analysis's own universes aren't used. Each universe being built has one driver."
- spec:1151: `NO_UNIVERSES` "... unless it is driven or consumes a decision"
- rfcs/0004-composition.md:63: a subanalysis that declares nothing outside its repository "can also be validated and built on its own"

**Why it matters.** Validate the classification stage alone and one validator warns NO_UNIVERSES while another doesn't. Ask a runner to build it alone and it either builds it in the parent's universes, writing into the parent's records, or builds nothing per universe. A checker gets a different set of planned results under each reading. With mutual driving, an implementation that follows the definition literally builds nothing.

**Suggested fix.** In §7, say that the universes being built are the root's, that an analysis is driven only by the flat keys of a universe being built, and that the root is never driven. This restores RFC 0003's "only the root's universes are built" in RFC 0004's terms.

### F055. Through a declaration cycle, §8 gives even the root's own results unboundedly many names and record places

- **Lens:** Implementer · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:852-858`, `docs/specification.md:869`, `docs/specification.md:305-307`, `docs/specification.md:664-668`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:784-789`, `rfcs/0004-composition.md:651-658`, `examples/iris_pipeline/feature_extraction/astra.yaml:10-12`, `examples/iris_pipeline/classification/astra.yaml:10-14`

**What is wrong.** §8 names a result by "the output's id after the ids of the declarations that lead to it" (`docs/specification.md:854-856`), and works out its record place and default place "from the result's address" (869). §7 allows declarations by `path` to form cycles (664-668). Through a cycle, infinitely many chains of declarations lead from an analysis back to itself. So every result of every analysis on the cycle has unboundedly many names, the root's own outputs included, and each name is an address that resolves. Nothing in the specification picks one name.

This is a known open point, described too narrowly. Design §13 records "One result, two names": one analysis "reached through two driven declarations", giving "two record paths" (`design/...-schema-design.md:784-789`). The cycle case is worse in four ways. The extra chains run through undriven back-edges (`classification.iris`, which no universe sets). They reach the root's own outputs, not only a doubly-reached analysis. The number of names is unbounded, not two. And the repository's own example has the cycle.

**Evidence.** In the shipped `examples/iris_pipeline`, the root declares `feature_extraction` and `classification` (`astra.yaml:17-21`). Each stage declares `iris: {path: ..}`, and `classification` also declares `feature_extraction: {path: ../feature_extraction}`. Enumerating chains by §4 resolution, the names of the root's own output `split` with four declarations or fewer are:

```
split
feature_extraction.iris.split
classification.iris.split
classification.feature_extraction.iris.split
feature_extraction.iris.feature_extraction.iris.split
feature_extraction.iris.classification.iris.split
classification.iris.feature_extraction.iris.split
classification.iris.classification.iris.split
```

Counts up to depth 2, 4, 6, 8, 10: 3, 8, 21, 55, 144. `classification.iris.split#baseline` resolves to analysis `.`, output `split`. The root with `summary.inputs` set to `[classification.accuracy, classification.iris.split#baseline, feature_extraction.iris.split]` is VALID in `validate.py` and `validate.mjs`. So are records with §8's example fields and `address` `split#baseline`, `feature_extraction.iris.split#baseline` or `classification.feature_extraction.iris.split#baseline`. By §8's table (864-867), their places are `.astra/records/baseline/split.json`, `.astra/records/baseline/feature_extraction.iris.split.json`, and so on. The schema can't resolve addresses, so this validity is expected. It shows only that nothing below the check layer catches it.

```
$ grep -n -i "fewest\|shortest\|visited\|acyclic\|two names" docs/specification.md
(no output)
```

RFC 0004's layout for this stack puts the result at `baseline/split.json` (`rfcs/0004-composition.md:651-658`), but as an example, not a rule.

**Why it matters.** MISSING_RECORD and MISPLACED_RECORD work from the name. A runner asked for `feature_extraction.iris.split#baseline` writes `baseline/feature_extraction.iris.split.json`. A checker expecting `baseline/split.json` reports the result missing or the record misplaced. An implementation that enumerates "the declarations that lead to it" without cycle detection doesn't terminate on `iris_pipeline`. A later phase working from §13's "two driven declarations" could settle that case and leave the cycle case open.

**Suggested fix.** Widen §13's open point to cover cycles, undriven back-edges and the root's own results. When it is settled, define the name in §8 as the chain through the fewest *driven* declarations from the analysis the result belongs to (none for its own outputs), ties broken by key order. Restricting to driven declarations matters: §7 (732-737) makes a pin through an undriven declaration name the declared analysis's own universe, so an undriven shortcut would name a different result.

### F056. The specification never says that a YAML tag outside the core schema is invalid; the harness rejects one, the `yaml` package warns or builds it

- **Lens:** Author, Standards, Edge cases · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:79`, `docs/specification.md:163-174`, `docs/specification.md:1097`, `tests/conftest.py:136-140`, `tests/test_loader.py:113-128`, `CLAUDE.md:56`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:510-513`

**What is wrong.** §3 says `astra.yaml` is "read strictly, under the core schema only", then lists what that means: the `%YAML` directive, duplicate keys, the core types, timestamps and `<<`. Node tags are not in the list. The INVALID_SYNTAX row asks only for "well-formed YAML 1.2 under the core schema", and a document with a local tag is well-formed. YAML 1.2.2 §10.4 says: "None of the above recommended schemas preclude the use of arbitrary explicit tags." So "core schema only" does not exclude tags by YAML's own definition. The rule that any tag outside the core schema is an error is stated only in non-normative places: the harness docstring, `test_other_tags_are_errors`, CLAUDE.md and the design (§9). No fixture or example uses a tag. The harness also departs from YAML 1.2.2 §6.9.1 on the non-specific tag `!`: it reads `label: ! 2026` as the int 2026, where YAML 1.2.2 (and the `yaml` package) gives the string `"2026"`.

**Evidence.** The spec text:

- `docs/specification.md:79`: "| **parse** | any strict YAML 1.2 loader | one file's text |"
- `docs/specification.md:1097`: "Every `astra.yaml` is well-formed YAML 1.2 under the core schema (with no `%YAML` directive other than 1.2), and every record is well-formed JSON"
- `grep -n -i tag docs/specification.md` matches only the `tags` field, git tags and words like "stage".

Where the rule lives instead, `tests/conftest.py:139-140`: "a tag outside the core schema (`!!set`, `!!binary`, `!!omap`, `!!timestamp`, a local tag) and a `%YAML` directive other than 1.2 are errors." `tests/test_loader.py:113-128` tests `!!set`, `!!binary`, `!!omap`, `!!pairs`, `!!timestamp`, `!!merge` and `!local`.

Each minimal document is `spec_version: "0.1.0"` plus one output carrying the tagged line (the `!not` case adds a decision `outcome_missing` and a universe). Python harness against Ajv with `yaml` 2.9.1 (version 1.2, `schema: 'core'`, `merge: false`):

| tagged line | Python (harness) | Ajv + `yaml` |
|---|---|---|
| `label: !draft Result` | PARSE-ERROR, tag `!draft` | TAG_RESOLVE_FAILED warning, then VALID |
| `when: [!not outcome_missing.multiple_imputation]` | PARSE-ERROR, tag `!not` | warning, then VALID; `when` is `["outcome_missing.multiple_imputation"]`, the negation lost |
| `description: !include docs/result.md` | PARSE-ERROR | warning, then VALID |
| `label: !!bool yes` | PARSE-ERROR, not a core bool | warning, then VALID (`"yes"`) |
| `copy: {!!merge <<: *base, label: A copy}` | PARSE-ERROR, `tag:yaml.org,2002:merge` | VALID, no warning; `copy` is merged with `*base` |
| `analyses: !!omap [- up: {url: not-a-url, commit: nope}]` | PARSE-ERROR | VALID, no warning; a JS Map, contents unchecked |
| `tags: !!set {survey, cleaned}` | PARSE-ERROR | `WRONG_TYPE` |
| `options: !!set {...}` | PARSE-ERROR | `EMPTY_COLLECTION` |
| `label: ! 2026` | `WRONG_TYPE`, 2026 is not a string | VALID (`"2026"`) |
| `spec_version: !!str 0.1.0` (control) | VALID | VALID |

The `yaml` package does this by default: `dist/doc/Document.js:272` sets `opt = { resolveKnownTags: true, schema: 'core' }` for version 1.2, and `dist/schema/tags.js:47-54` puts binary, merge, omap, pairs, set and timestamp in `coreKnownTags`. ruamel's stock `YAML(typ='safe')` gives a third reading: it builds `!!omap`, `!!set` and `!!timestamp`.

**Why it matters.** Validator authors in JS (astra-typescript, editor plugins) who follow "any strict YAML 1.2 loader" with their library's defaults accept files that astra-tools rejects. In two cases the result is worse than a disagreement. A `when` written with `!not` is accepted with its meaning reversed. `analyses: !!omap` passes with nothing inside it checked. `!!merge` performs a merge that §3 says can't happen. An author used to `!include` or `!env` from other configs gets a parse error from one tool and a valid analysis from another.

**Suggested fix.** In §3 and the INVALID_SYNTAX row, state that no tag is allowed but the core schema's (`!!str`, `!!int`, `!!float`, `!!bool`, `!!null`, `!!seq`, `!!map`), each only on its core form, and settle the non-specific `!`. Add `# fails at: parse` fixtures for a local tag and for `!!set` or `!!omap`. §10 could tell JS loaders to set `resolveKnownTags: false` and check the remaining tags themselves. A TAG_RESOLVE_FAILED warning is not enough on its own, since `yaml` also warns on `!!float 1`, which the harness accepts.

### F057. `FragmentSelector.value` is defined as an RFC 3778 fragment, but no pattern or rule checks it, so any string is valid

- **Lens:** Author, Standards · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `src/astra/schema/insight.yaml:47-58`, `docs/specification.md:513-517`, `docs/specification.md:1112`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:277`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:750-751`

**What is wrong.** The LinkML description and the specification define `location.value` as a PDF fragment identifier (RFC 3778) without the leading `#`. That is a precise, checkable form, like `doi`, which has a pattern under `INVALID_VALUE`. But `value` has no pattern and no `rule`, and no rule in §9 covers its form at any layer. The contract accepts values the normative description excludes: a space, a leading `#`, prose, a newline. The design says "the description fixes the syntax" (`:277`), but it fixes it only in prose. §12 records only that `value` accepts `""`. That is one case of a wider gap: any string is accepted.

**Evidence.** `src/astra/schema/insight.yaml:56-58`: "The PDF fragment identifier (RFC 3778), without the leading `#`, such as `page=6`." There is no `pattern`. Compare `doi` at `:68-71`, which has `pattern: '^10\.[0-9]{4,}/[^\x00-\x20\x7f]+$(?!\n)'` and `rule: INVALID_VALUE`. In `analysis.schema.json`, `$defs/FragmentSelector/properties/value` is `{"type": "string", "description": ...}`. In `rules.json:48-51`, the only codes for it are `UNKNOWN_FIELD`, `WRONG_TYPE` and `MISSING_FIELD`. The `INVALID_VALUE` row (`docs/specification.md:1112`) lists `Evidence.doi` but not `FragmentSelector.value`, which appears in §9 only under `MISSING_FIELD` (`:1101`).

```yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
outputs:
  result: {type: metric, format: json}
prior_insights:
  p:
    claim: C
    evidence:
      - {doi: 10.1234/x, location: {value: "page 6"}}
      - {doi: 10.1234/x, location: {value: "#page=6"}}
      - {doi: 10.1234/x, location: {value: "Table 2, column 6"}}
      - {doi: 10.1234/x, location: {value: "p. 6, second paragraph"}}
      - {doi: 10.1234/x, location: {value: "line\nbreak"}}
```
```
$ uv run python toolkit/validate.py Analysis fragment-values.yaml
VALID
$ node toolkit/js/validate.mjs <repo> Analysis fragment-values.yaml
VALID
```
Both validators agree. Checked against RFC 3987 `ifragment` with `rfc3987.match(v, rule='ifragment')`, `page=6` matches and every other value above fails. Web Annotation §4.2.1 defines `value` as "The contents of the fragment component of an IRI that describes the Segment." A migrated `value: "6"`, and `value: "row=5"` on evidence citing a csv artifact, are also valid.

**Why it matters.** The tidy-up RFC (`NNNN-schema-tidy-up.md:75`) says "Tooling that read `page` reads the page from `value`." astra-tools, astra-typescript and editors then have to parse free text that the contract let through. One rejects `page 6`, another reads it leniently, and no code exists for either to report. Authors get no feedback on a location no viewer can open. `docs/specification.md:22` says "Section 9 states every rule that a tool checks or keeps", so as written, tools check nothing here.

**Suggested fix.** Give `value` a pattern under `INVALID_VALUE` and add it to the `:1112` row. At minimum, the IRI-fragment character set (no space, no control characters, no `#`, `%` only before two hex digits). A stricter pattern adds a `key=value` shape with RFC 3778's six keys (`nameddest`, `page`, `zoom`, `view`, `viewrect`, `highlight`), matched case-insensitively. RFC 3778 allows `#` as a separator, but an IRI fragment does not, so the spec should say which reading it takes. RFC 3778 is obsoleted by RFC 8118. Alternatively, make the definition advisory. Either way, widen the §12 entry. Tom decides.

### F058. "Checked by the same rules" gives cited results of an analysis from a commit a bytes status, which needs bytes §7 says are never read

- **Lens:** Author · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:776`, `docs/specification.md:784-786`, `docs/specification.md:822-826`, `docs/specification.md:1019-1021`, `docs/specification.md:1184`, `docs/specification.md:1187-1189`, `rfcs/0007-analyses-in-other-repositories.md:206-207`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:437-439`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:638`

**What is wrong.** §8 and §9 say that the results of an analysis from a commit that are read *or cited* are checked "by the same rules". Those rules include `ABSENT_RESULT`, which is about bytes (`present`, `modified`, `absent`). A result that is only cited, with no recipe reading it, can get a bytes status only if its bytes are fetched. §7 says those bytes are never read. RFC 0007 gives the bytes status only to results a recipe reads. The spec's evidence rules (l.1016, l.1163) say what the *evidence* gets. They don't say whether the cited result itself gets a bytes status. Design §11 records no departure here, and the design carries the same wording.

**Evidence.**

§7, what is read from a commit (`docs/specification.md:776`, `:784-786`):

```
Only these are read:
- which files there are at a commit, and which differ between two commits,
  without their contents;
- the bytes of each result or input file that a recipe reads.
```

`docs/specification.md:823-826`: "When a recipe reads the result, its bytes come from what git holds at the result's default place at the declared commit, [...] and are checked against the record."

§8 (`docs/specification.md:1019-1021`) and §9 (`:1187-1189`, citing "0007 §4–5"):

```
The results of an analysis from a commit that are read or cited are checked
by the same rules, through their records at the declared commit
```

The same rules include (`:1171`, `:1184`):

```
| `present` | The bytes at the result's default place hash to the record's `content_hash` and have its `size` |
| `ABSENT_RESULT` | SHOULD | Every result's recorded bytes are at its default place | `absent`, or `modified` | 0005 §1 |
```

RFC 0007's "What's checked" splits the two (`rfcs/0007-analyses-in-other-repositories.md:206-207`):

```
| A result of an analysis from a commit that is read or cited is unchanged at the declared commit | check | `unchanged`, `stale` or `missing` |
| The bytes of a result of an analysis from a commit that a recipe reads are the ones its record names | check | `present`, `modified` or `absent` |
```

A file listing can't give `present`. `content_hash` is a plain SHA-256 of the content (`src/astra/schema/types.yaml:107-109`). A git tree gives only the blob id and the size:

```
$ git ls-tree -r -l HEAD
100644 blob cfa20f81071245f292f0b52b37beb7adf9259a26       8	results/u1/summary.csv
$ git cat-file -p HEAD:results/u1/summary.csv | sha256sum
492d5ea496056f1a6a6592241032fab764c321596317930b4fa0e1e8bc3b7470  -
```

The design has the same sentence (`design/...:437-439`). Its traceability row (`:638`) maps "Bytes from what git holds at the commit, checked against the record" to "`ABSENT_RESULT`, at the declared commit", without "that a recipe reads".

**Why it matters.** Checker authors will resolve this differently. A checker that follows §9 fetches the bytes of every cited result to report `present` or `absent`. That can be gigabytes, for example a finding that cites a 4.8 GB archive no recipe reads. A checker that follows §7 reports only the record status. The same citation then gets different statuses in different tools. No error verdict changes: `ABSENT_RESULT` is a SHOULD, `absent` "is not an error" (l.1011), and `STALE_EVIDENCE` uses only the record status.

**Suggested fix.** In l.1019-1021 and l.1187-1189, and in the design at :437-439 and :638, say that read or cited results get their record's status by the same rules, and that `ABSENT_RESULT` applies only to those a recipe reads (RFC 0007 l.206-207).

### F059. `test_every_map_key_is_an_id` skips `propertyNames/type`, and its nine entries are `WRONG_TYPE`

- **Lens:** Internal consistency · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `tests/test_rules.py:498-512`, `src/astra/schema/generated/rules.json:23,144,158,163,168,172,178,182,191`, `tools/rules.py:61-62`, `docs/specification.md:1099`, `docs/specification.md:179-182`

**What is wrong.** The test's docstring says a map key that doesn't match its key's type is `INVALID_ID`. The test filters both the catalogue's error map and the expected keyword set to locations ending in `/propertyNames` or `/propertyNames/pattern` (lines 503 and 509). The nine `/propertyNames/type` keywords in `analysis.schema.json` fall outside both sets. `rules.json` gives them `WRONG_TYPE`, the default for `type` (`tools/rules.py:61-62`). The test passes, but what its docstring claims does not hold for a key that isn't a string. The test and the specification both skip the keyword on which jsonschema and Ajv disagree. This is a symptom of the separate map-key finding and depends on how that finding is settled.

**Evidence.** The filter (`tests/test_rules.py`):

```
499     """A map key that doesn't match its key's type is `INVALID_ID`, whatever that type (an `address` included)."""
503         if location.endswith(("/propertyNames", "/propertyNames/pattern"))
509         if pointer.endswith(("/propertyNames", "/propertyNames/pattern"))
512     assert {code for code in keys.values()} == {"INVALID_ID"}
```

The committed `rules.json`, with the same filter:

```
checked by the test: 18 {'INVALID_ID'}
skipped by the test: 9 {'WRONG_TYPE'}
```

The nine entries are for options, `Universe.decisions`, analyses, decisions, findings, inputs, outputs, prior_insights and universes. For example, `rules.json:178`: `"https://w3id.org/astra/analysis.schema.json#/properties/outputs/propertyNames/type": "WRONG_TYPE"`. If the filter is widened to `"/propertyNames" in location` / `in pointer`, the test fails:

```
E       AssertionError: assert {'INVALID_ID', 'WRONG_TYPE'} == {'INVALID_ID'}
```

For `outputs: {1: {type: metric, format: json}}`, jsonschema reports `…/outputs/propertyNames/type | WRONG_TYPE`. Ajv reports `…/propertyNames/pattern | INVALID_ID`. With `true:` as the key, jsonschema says `WRONG_TYPE` and Ajv says VALID. No fixture under `tests/data/` reaches a `/propertyNames/type` keyword.

The specification gives two answers. `docs/specification.md:1099`: "`INVALID_ID` | schema | MUST | Every map key matches its key's type". `docs/specification.md:179-182`, which follows `"true": {}`: "A value of another type than its field takes fails as [`WRONG_TYPE`]".

**Why it matters.** The guard on map-key codes is green while its claim is false in Python for a realistic file, such as a decision with options `true:` and `false:`. Authors of astra-tools and astra-typescript who trust the test or §9 expect `INVALID_ID`. Python reports `WRONG_TYPE`.

**Suggested fix.** Settle the code for a key that isn't a string together with the map-key finding. Then widen the test to every location under `/propertyNames`, or make §9 and the docstring say `WRONG_TYPE`. Either way, add an invalid fixture with an unquoted boolean or integer key.

### F060. A parse fixture headed `INVALID_SYNTAX` passes when its only fault is a duplicate key

- **Lens:** Internal consistency, Standards · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `tests/conftest.py:27-31`, `tests/test_fixtures.py:52-56`, `tests/test_specification.py:73`, `docs/specification.md:167-169`, `docs/specification.md:1097-1098`, `CLAUDE.md:65`

**What is wrong.** `PARSE_ERRORS` maps `INVALID_SYNTAX` to ruamel's `YAMLError`, the base class of every loader error. The parse branch of `test_invalid_fixture` checks only `pytest.raises(PARSE_ERRORS[rule])`. `DuplicateKeyError` is a subclass of `YAMLError`, so a fixture headed `# rule: INVALID_SYNTAX` passes when its only fault is a repeated key. The specification gives a repeated key its own code, `DUPLICATE_KEY`. The coverage check `test_every_schema_rule_has_a_fixture` then counts that fixture as `INVALID_SYNTAX` coverage. The check is exact in the other direction: a fixture headed `DUPLICATE_KEY` that fails some other way fails the test. The schema branch of the same test requires the exact code (`assert codes == {rule}`, `tests/test_fixtures.py:76`). The defect is latent: the four committed parse fixtures are labelled correctly (182 raises `ParserError`, 183 `ScannerError`, 101 and 146 `DuplicateKeyError`).

A tag outside the core schema (`!!set`, `!local`) raises `ConstructorError`, also a `YAMLError`. That is `INVALID_SYNTAX`'s own case ("read strictly, under the core schema only", `docs/specification.md:163-165`; "under the core schema", `:1097`), so it is not a wrong reason.

**Evidence.** `tests/conftest.py:27-31`:

```python
PARSE_ERRORS: dict[str, type[Exception]] = {
    "INVALID_SYNTAX": YAMLError,
    "DUPLICATE_KEY": DuplicateKeyError,
}
"""Each parse-layer code, with the loader error a fixture breaking it raises (any loader error, for `INVALID_SYNTAX`)."""
```

`docs/specification.md:167-169`: "a `%YAML` directive other than `%YAML 1.2` makes the file invalid ([`INVALID_SYNTAX`]), and so does a key written twice in one map ([`DUPLICATE_KEY`])". `CLAUDE.md:65`: the test asserts "that the fixture fails for the right reason".

In a clone, with a scratch fixture under `tests/data/invalid/`:

```yaml
# rule: INVALID_SYNTAX
# fails at: parse
spec_version: "0.1.0"
spec_version: "0.1.0"
```

```
DuplicateKeyError MRO: ['DuplicateKeyError', 'MarkedYAMLError', 'YAMLError', 'Exception', ...]
PASSED ...test_invalid_fixture[Analysis-901-duplicate-key-headed-syntax]
FAILED ...test_invalid_fixture[Analysis-904-malformed-headed-duplicate]
```

With Analysis-182 and Analysis-183 set aside, so that 901 is the only fixture headed `INVALID_SYNTAX`:

```
$ uv run python -m pytest -q tests/test_fixtures.py tests/test_specification.py -rA
PASSED tests/test_fixtures.py::test_invalid_fixture[Analysis-901-duplicate-key-headed-syntax]
PASSED tests/test_specification.py::test_harness_checks_every_parse_and_schema_code
PASSED tests/test_specification.py::test_every_schema_rule_has_a_fixture
116 passed
```

**Why it matters.** A mislabelled parse fixture goes unnoticed, and the suite reports `INVALID_SYNTAX` as covered when it isn't. This breaks Ruling R24, "Failing for the right reason". astra-tools and astra-typescript port these fixtures to check their loaders, so a mislabelled one would teach them the wrong code for a duplicate key.

**Suggested fix.** Make the parse branch name the code, as the schema branch does: map `DuplicateKeyError` to `DUPLICATE_KEY` and any other `YAMLError` (including `ConstructorError`) to `INVALID_SYNTAX`, and assert it equals the fixture's rule. Reword the docstring at `conftest.py:31` to say "any loader error but a duplicate key".

### F061. `Environment.iri`'s pattern accepts strings that aren't IRIs

- **Lens:** Internal consistency, Standards, Edge cases · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `src/astra/schema/provenance.yaml:145`, `src/astra/schema/provenance.yaml:149`, `src/astra/schema/generated/provenance.schema.json:10`, `src/astra/schema/generated/provenance.open.schema.json:10`, `docs/specification.md:910-911`, `docs/specification.md:1112`, `docs/specification.md:1180`, `docs/specification.md:1345-1347`, `rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:142`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:230`, `tests/data/invalid/ProvenanceRecord-209-iri-without-scheme.yaml:14`

**What is wrong.** The field's description, §8 and RFC 0005 all ask for an IRI. The pattern checks less than that: a scheme, a colon, then any run of characters that are not ASCII controls or space. So it accepts characters that RFC 3987 §3.1 forbids in an IRI (`<`, `>`, `"`, `{`, `}`, `|`, `\`, `^` and the backtick), a `%` not followed by two hex digits, a second `#`, and C1 controls such as U+0085. No rule covers the gap: `INVALID_VALUE` is "matches its pattern", and `INVALID_RECORD` is "valid against the record schema". The design records the same pattern (with `\S+`) as "an absolute IRI" and admits no gap (design:230). It is in neither §12 nor §13. The only invalid fixture for the field has no scheme (`iri: env/1`).

**Evidence.** The schema and the text that sets the requirement:

- `provenance.yaml:145`: `pattern: '^[A-Za-z][A-Za-z0-9+.-]*:[^\x00-\x20\x7f]+$(?!\n)'`. The same pattern is in both generated record schemas at line 10.
- `provenance.yaml:149`: "An absolute IRI naming exactly that environment".
- `specification.md:910-911`: "whose `iri` is an absolute IRI naming exactly the environment the recipe ran in".
- RFC 0005:142 puts "an IRI for the environment" among the things the record schema checks.
- `specification.md:1347`: "the environment's IRI is its `@id`".

Each test record is ProvenanceRecord-209 with only `environment.iri` changed. jsonschema and Ajv both report VALID for every value below, against the closed and the open schema. `rfc3987.match(v, rule='IRI')` (rfc3987 1.3.8) returns None for every one:

```
"urn:x<y>"  'urn:a"b'  'urn:x:{"py":"3.12"}'  "urn:x:a|b"  "urn:x:a\\b"  "urn:x:a^b"
"urn:x:a`b"  "conda:numpy>=1.26"  "urn:x:100%"  "urn:x:%zz"  "urn:x:a#b#c"  "urn:x:a\u0085b"
```

`rfc3987.match` accepts the fixture's `urn:example-runner:env:…`, `https://example.org/env/1#x`, `urn:x:café` and `urn:x:a%20b`.

The same records converted to RDF through the published `provenance.context.jsonld`:

```
rdflib 7.6.0, g.serialize(format="nt"):
  Exception: "urn:x<y>" does not look like a valid URI, I cannot serialize this as N3/Turtle.
  (likewise " { } | \ ^ ` and conda:numpy>=1.26; %zz, 100%, a#b#c, U+0085 serialise silently)
jsonld.js 8.3.3:  _:b1 <http://www.w3.org/ns/prov#used> <urn:x<y>> .
rdflib nquads parse of that line: ParserError Invalid line (Failed to eat <...> at <urn:x<y>> .)
pyld 3.3.0: writes <urn:x<y>>, <urn:x:%zz>, ...; drops the prov:used triple only for U+0085
```

JSON-LD 1.1 API §8.1 says: "An IRI is well-formed if it matches the ABNF for IRI as described in [RFC3987]". §8.2.2 then says: "If item is a node object and the value of its @id entry is not well-formed, return null."

**Why it matters.** Runner authors get no warning from the schema for a plausible value such as `conda:numpy>=1.26`. Checkers can disagree on the same result. One applies `INVALID_RECORD` as written and accepts `urn:x<y>`. Another honours "absolute IRI" and rejects it, so for that checker the result is `missing`. §10's claim that a record is PROV-O fails for these records, and RDF processors handle them differently: rdflib refuses to serialise, jsonld.js and pyld write invalid N-Quads, and a conforming JSON-LD processor drops the environment. "Absolute IRI" is also ambiguous. RFC 3987's `absolute-IRI` forbids a fragment, but the pattern accepts `urn:a#b`.

**Suggested fix.** Tighten the pattern within the shared regex subset. The following was checked with jsonschema and Ajv, and the repo's pattern guard passes it: `^[A-Za-z][A-Za-z0-9+.-]*:([^\x00-\x20\x7f-\x9f<>"{}|\\^`%#]|%[0-9A-Fa-f]{2})+(#([^\x00-\x20\x7f-\x9f<>"{}|\\^`%#]|%[0-9A-Fa-f]{2})*)?$(?!\n)`. Add an invalid fixture with `iri: "urn:x<y>"` (INVALID_VALUE, at `/generated_by/environment/iri`). Say whether a fragment is allowed. Otherwise, reword "absolute IRI" to describe the form the pattern actually checks.

### F062. The ancestor-combinator exemption lets an invalid fixture break a second, unrelated exactly-one-of rule and still pass

- **Lens:** Internal consistency · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `tests/test_fixtures.py:61-67`, `tests/conftest.py:33-34`, `CLAUDE.md:58`, `CLAUDE.md:65`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:498-499`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:504-507`

**What is wrong.** `test_invalid_fixture` ignores every `oneOf`/`anyOf` error at an ancestor of `fails at`, whatever its cause. `conftest.py:34` gives the reason: "they report what fails inside them". That reason never holds in this contract. The only combinators in the three generated schemas are `$defs/AnalysisDeclaration__identifier_optional/oneOf` (`path` or `url`) and `$defs/Evidence/oneOf` (`doi` or `artifact`). Every branch only checks that a key is present (`{"properties": {"path": {}}, "required": ["path"]}`), so a failure below them cannot make them fail. Every ancestor error the exemption hides is a second, independent violation: INVALID_DECLARATION_SOURCE or INVALID_EVIDENCE_SOURCE.

This conflicts with "a minimal valid document with one thing broken" (`CLAUDE.md:58`, design:498-499). The harness does what `CLAUDE.md:65` and R24 (design:504-507) say. Those passages grant the exemption without limit, and that is what the "one thing broken" rule contradicts. The known item (design:763-765) records only that "`Analysis-159` still reports a second code (its ancestor `oneOf`)". It does not record that the harness accepts any such second violation.

The gap covers only the "valid under more than one branch" form. When neither key is present, the `oneOf` brings `required` errors at the same pointer, they count as `elsewhere`, and the test fails.

**Evidence.** A scratch fixture in a clone:

```yaml
# rule: INVALID_COMMIT
# fails at: /analyses/up/commit
spec_version: "0.1.0"
outputs:
  result:
    type: metric
    format: json
analyses:
  up:
    path: up
    url: https://x/y
    commit: "abc1234"
```

```
PASSED tests/test_fixtures.py::test_invalid_fixture[Analysis-192-short-commit-and-two-sources]
PASSED tests/test_fixtures.py::test_invalid_fixture[Analysis-193-bad-artifact-and-doi]
FAILED tests/test_fixtures.py::test_invalid_fixture[Analysis-194-short-commit-no-source]
```

jsonschema and Ajv 8.20 both report two codes for 192:

```
/analyses/up        | …/AnalysisDeclaration__identifier_optional/oneOf | INVALID_DECLARATION_SOURCE
/analyses/up/commit | …/properties/commit/pattern                      | INVALID_COMMIT
```

Fixture 193 (`doi` and a bad `artifact` together, `# rule: INVALID_REFERENCE`) passes the same way and also reports INVALID_EVIDENCE_SOURCE. A scan of the 95 committed schema-layer invalid fixtures finds only one error away from `fails at`, in `Analysis-159`, the case already recorded.

**Why it matters.** This is a test that can pass for the wrong reason. The defect is latent: no committed fixture other than 159 relies on it. But a future fixture with two independent breaks will pass. astra-tools and astra-typescript, which check the same fixtures through `rules.json`, will then see two codes where the header names one.

**Suggested fix.** Allow an ancestor combinator error only where the fixture declares it, for example with an optional third header line naming its pointer and code, which only Analysis-159 needs. Count every other one as `elsewhere`. Correct the docstring at `conftest.py:34`, and limit the exemption in `CLAUDE.md:65` and R24 to match.

### F063. The wheel ships a stray `astra/schema/README.md`, and the "nothing else" test can't see it

- **Lens:** Internal consistency · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `src/astra/schema/README.md:1-3`, `tests/test_package.py:1`, `tests/test_package.py:35`, `pyproject.toml:84-85`, `CLAUDE.md:52`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:61`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:162`

**What is wrong.** D7 says the package ships only artefacts. `CLAUDE.md:52` says it ships "the LinkML sources and `generated/` ... (plus `astra/docs/llms.txt`)". The built wheel also holds `astra/schema/README.md`, a leftover from the project template (`9149e19 Initialise git with minimal project`). It ships because `pyproject.toml:84-85` packs all of `src/astra`, and nothing excludes it. The file itself is harmless. The problem is the guard. `tests/test_package.py:1` claims "The built wheel ships the schema artefacts and nothing else (design D7)". Line 35 accepts any name that starts with `astra/schema/`. `test_wheel_ships_the_contract` pins the exact set under `generated/`, and `test_wheel_ships_the_sources` only checks that the 4 modules are present. Nothing pins the top level of `astra/schema/` or a new subdirectory there. Non-Python files there ship and pass. Hatch also packs untracked files that git doesn't ignore, so a stray local file ships from a developer's tree.

**Evidence.** A wheel built from a clone at `96c902a`:

```
$ uv build --wheel --out-dir ../wheel
astra/schema/README.md
astra/schema/{analysis,insight,provenance,types}.yaml
astra/schema/generated/{analysis.schema.json,provenance.context.jsonld,provenance.open.schema.json,provenance.schema.json,rules.json}
astra/docs/llms.txt
```

`src/astra/schema/README.md:3`: "This folder contains the LinkML schema yaml files." It leaves out `generated/`.

`tests/test_package.py:35`:

```python
if not (n.startswith("astra/schema/") or n == "astra/docs/llms.txt")
```

In the clone, an untracked `src/astra/schema/universe.yaml` (`id: stray`) and `src/astra/schema/generated_notes.txt` both shipped, and the test still passed:

```
$ uv run pytest -q tests/test_package.py
4 passed in 3.08s
```

`universe.yaml` is the module this phase deleted (design §3 "Removed"). Had the deletion been missed, the test would still pass. As a control, a planted `src/astra/schema/generated/extra.json` fails `test_wheel_ships_the_contract`, and `.py` files are rejected by line 38.

**Why it matters.** The test passes while the invariant it names is false. A maintainer who relies on it to keep D7 gets no warning when a leftover module, a scratch file or a note lands in `astra/schema/` and goes out to PyPI. The package contents and the D7 wording already disagree by one file.

**Suggested fix.** Delete `src/astra/schema/README.md`, or exclude it in `[tool.hatch.build.targets.wheel]`. Make `test_wheel_ships_only_schema_artefacts` compare the non-dist-info names to the exact set: the 4 sources, the 5 generated files and `astra/docs/llms.txt`.

### F064. Four of the §9 extractor's checks have no test, and removing any one leaves the suite green

- **Lens:** Internal consistency · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `tests/test_rules.py:3`, `tests/test_rules.py:158-240`, `tools/rules.py:273`, `tools/rules.py:284`, `tools/rules.py:298`, `tools/rules.py:149`, `tools/rules.py:144-147`, `docs/specification.md:1070-1088`

**What is wrong.** `tests/test_rules.py:3` says the extractor "is tested on small specifications, one per way a §9 can be malformed". `MALFORMED` (lines 158-240) has no case for four of the extractor's checks:

- subsections out of order;
- two rule tables in one subsection;
- a repeated column;
- a cell whose rebuilt Markdown parses differently, which is §9's own `\*` example.

§9's "The catalogue as data" requires three of these: the order at 1070-1071, exactly one rule table per subsection at 1072-1073, and the rebuild rule at 1084-1088. The fourth, "no repeated column", appears only in the extractor's error message. §9 1075-1077 says only "and no others". A one-line edit to `tools/rules.py` disables any one of the four checks. The weakened extractor then accepts a malformed §9, and all 361 tests still pass.

**Evidence.** These mutations are applied one at a time to a clone's `tools/rules.py`:

```
order            273: if headings != list(COLUMNS):     ->  if sorted(headings) != sorted(COLUMNS):
two-tables       284: if len(rule_tables) != 1:         ->  if len(rule_tables) < 1:
repeated-column  298: ... or header.count(column) > 1   ->  (dropped)
reparse          149: if _shape(...) != _shape(kept):   ->  if False:
```

Each one is fed a malformed copy of the real §9:

```
# original extractor
order:           REJECTED: §9's subsections must be parse and schema, validator, check, runner, in order
two-tables:      REJECTED: line 1191, ### runner: has 2 rule tables, not one
repeated-column: REJECTED: line 1193, ### runner: the rule table's column 'Source' is unknown or repeated
reparse:         REJECTED: line 1149, ### validator, row `REDUNDANT_PIN`: its Rule cell can't be written back as Markdown without its links

# each mutant; then pytest tests/
order:           ACCEPTED, layers ['parse', 'schema', 'validator', 'runner', 'check']      -> 361 passed
two-tables:      ACCEPTED, 65 rules; EXTRA_RULE in rules: False                           -> 361 passed
repeated-column: ACCEPTED; STALE_INPUT "source": "(second source)"                        -> 361 passed
reparse:         ACCEPTED; REDUNDANT_PIN "No reference to an *output* built once has a pin" -> 361 passed
```

The refuter got the same results in a separate clone, using `tests/test_rules.py`'s own `VALID` spec.

End to end, each mutant gets its malformed §9 and a refreshed `rules.json`. Three still pass. Order alone is caught, by `tests/test_specification.py:50-51` (`test_catalogue_has_every_layer_in_order`). The drift check can't catch any of the four, because it regenerates with the same extractor.

The disallowed-token branch (`tools/rules.py:144-147`, "holds a {t.type}") is also untested. If it is removed, the reparse check still rejects an image, but with a different message.

**Why it matters.** A refactor of `tools/rules.py` can drop the checks that keep `rules.json` faithful to §9, and `just test` stays green. A §9 edit then ships a `rules.json` that drops a rule (a second table) or rewords one (a duplicated column, a lost escape). astra-tools and astra-typescript consume that file. No rule in today's `rules.json` is wrong, so this is the low end of Important. The order check alone would be Minor.

**Suggested fix.** Add `MALFORMED` cases for:

- subsections out of order;
- two rule tables in one subsection;
- a repeated column;
- `\*x\*` in a Rule cell;
- an image in a Rule cell, matched on "holds a image".

Optionally, state in §9 that each column appears once.

### F065. The `%YAML` rule goes beyond YAML 1.2, but §2 credits it to "any strict YAML 1.2 loader"

- **Lens:** Standards · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:79`, `docs/specification.md:163-168`, `docs/specification.md:1097`, `docs/specification.md:1262`, `tests/data/invalid/Analysis-183-yaml-1-1-directive.yaml:3`, `tests/conftest.py:50-60`

**What is wrong.** §3 and §9 make any `%YAML` directive other than 1.2 an `INVALID_SYNTAX` error. As a profile restriction, that is fine. But YAML 1.2.2 §6.8.1 requires a 1.2 processor to accept `%YAML 1.1`. It says a higher minor version gets only a warning. So a conforming loader can't enforce the rule. §2 still says "any strict YAML 1.2 loader" enforces the parse layer. §10 says the parse-layer codes are left out of `rules.json` "because loaders report them". The specification never says this check has to be added on top of the loader, and it never says what "strict" means.

**Evidence.** YAML 1.2.2 §6.8.1 (https://yaml.org/spec/1.2.2/#681-yaml-directives): "A version 1.2 YAML processor must also accept documents with an explicit “%YAML 1.1” directive". Also: "Documents with a “YAML” directive specifying a higher minor version (e.g. “%YAML 1.3”) should be processed with an appropriate warning."

- `docs/specification.md:79`: "| **parse** | any strict YAML 1.2 loader | one file's text |"
- `docs/specification.md:167-168`: "a `%YAML` directive other than `%YAML 1.2` makes the file invalid ([`INVALID_SYNTAX`](#parse-and-schema))"
- `docs/specification.md:1262`: "The parse-layer codes have no entry, because loaders report them, not schemas"

These are the review toolkits, run on the repository's own invalid fixture and on copies of the minimal document under `%YAML 1.3` and `%YAML 2.0`:

```
Python (harness loader)    Analysis-183  PARSE-ERROR ScannerError: found %YAML 1.1: only 1.2 is read
Ajv 8.20 + yaml 2.9.1      Analysis-183  VALID
  {version:'1.2', schema:'core', uniqueKeys, merge:false}
                           y13           PARSE-WARNING BAD_DIRECTIVE: Unsupported YAML version 1.3 / VALID
                           y20           PARSE-WARNING BAD_DIRECTIVE: Unsupported YAML version 2.0 / VALID
```

The yaml package reports nothing for `%YAML 1.1`. With its default options it reads a different analysis. On `%YAML 1.1` followed by `decisions: {d: {options: {yes: {}, no: {}}}}`:

```
toolkit opts:     errors=0 warnings=0 directives.yaml={"explicit":true,"version":"1.1"} options keys=["yes","no"]
package defaults: errors=0 warnings=0 directives.yaml={"explicit":true,"version":"1.1"} options keys=["true","false"]
```

Stock ruamel.yaml 0.19.1 also accepts the file, and it reads the keys as `[True, False]`. The harness gets the rule only from a custom scanner. `tests/conftest.py:50` has `class _CoreSchemaScanner(Scanner):`, `:55` has `if token.name == "YAML" and token.value != (1, 2):`, and `:129` installs it.

**Why it matters.** Implementers who take §2 at its word build on a stock loader. The TypeScript validator then accepts Analysis-183, the fixture the Python harness rejects. With the yaml package's defaults it also renames option ids. A careful reader of §9:1097 will add the check. §2 and §10 still tell them the loader already does it.

**Suggested fix.** In §3, say that the strict reading goes beyond YAML 1.2.2 §6.8.1. A tool must check the `%YAML` version itself (e.g. the yaml package's `doc.directives.yaml`) and keep the loader to the core schema. In §2 ("a YAML 1.2 loader plus the checks of §3") and §10:1262, credit the parse layer to the loader plus these checks.

### F066. The harness loader rejects tabs used as separation, which YAML 1.2 allows and the `yaml` package accepts

- **Lens:** Standards, Edge cases · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `tests/conftest.py:126-132`, `tests/conftest.py:27-30`, `tests/conftest.py:135-142`, `docs/specification.md:79`, `docs/specification.md:163-165`, `docs/specification.md:318-320`, `docs/specification.md:1097`, `CLAUDE.md:56`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:302`

**What is wrong.** YAML 1.2.2 counts the tab as white space. It forbids tabs only in indentation. A tab before a comment, a trailing tab, a tab after `:`, a blank line that holds only a tab, and a tab inside a plain scalar are all well-formed. The harness loader (ruamel.yaml 0.19.1, pure safe) rejects every one of them with `ScannerError`. `PARSE_ERRORS` maps any `YAMLError` to `INVALID_SYNTAX`. Stock ruamel (safe pure, safe C, round-trip) and PyYAML reject them the same way. The `yaml` package (2.9.1) accepts them, and still rejects a tab used as indentation. §3 says the file is "read strictly", and §2 asks for "any strict YAML 1.2 loader", but neither names a conformance target. §3's list of restrictions says nothing about tabs. So a conforming document gets opposite verdicts from the two validation paths the brief prescribes. Nothing tests tabs: no tracked fixture, example or spec file contains one.

§4 (spec:319) is incomplete as well. It says "After a space it starts a comment", but a tab before `#` starts a comment too. The harness can't load the PIN_LIKE_COMMENT case `- result<TAB>#svm` at all.

**Evidence.** Each document is `Analysis-001-minimal.yaml` with one tab added (lines shown with `cat -A`):

```
t1  "    type: metric^I# the F1 score$"
t2  "    type:^Imetric$"
t3  "    type: metric^I$"
t4  "^I$"                      (tab-only line between type and format)
t6  "      - result^I#svm$"    (pin-like comment)

$ uv run python $T/validate.py Analysis t0-control.yaml t1..t6
t0, t5 (space before #)  VALID
t1-t4, t6                PARSE-ERROR ScannerError: while scanning for the next token
                         (found character '\t' that cannot start any token ... line 4, column 17)
$ node $T/js/validate.mjs . Analysis <same files>      # Ajv + yaml 2.9.1
t0-t6                    VALID
```

YAML 1.2.2: [33] `s-white ::= s-space | s-tab`; [66] `s-separate-in-line ::= s-white+ | <start-of-line>`; [77] `s-b-comment ::= ( s-separate-in-line c-nb-comment-text? )? b-comment`; §6.1 "tab characters must not be used in indentation". Example 5.12 is `block:→|`.

yaml-test-suite (`data-2022-01-17`): the harness's `load_yaml` rejects 18 valid tab cases that yaml 2.9 accepts. They include DC7X "Various trailing tabs", DK95/04 (`foo: 1\n\t\nbar: 2`, json `{"foo":1,"bar":2}`) and J3BT (Example 5.12).

`tests/conftest.py:127`: `"""ruamel.yaml's pure safe loader, YAML 1.2, restricted to the core schema."""`
`docs/specification.md:1097`: "Every `astra.yaml` is well-formed YAML 1.2 under the core schema (with no `%YAML` directive other than 1.2) ..."

**Why it matters.** Trailing tabs, tab-aligned comments and tab-only blank lines are common in hand-edited files. A Python validator built like the harness reports `INVALID_SYNTAX` for such a file. A TypeScript validator on `yaml` passes the same file. The repository's tests can't catch the difference, because they use the deviating loader. A future `INVALID_SYNTAX` fixture that happened to contain a tab would pass whatever it meant to break, since any loader error counts. The `PIN_LIKE_COMMENT` warning is never raised when the separator is a tab. The generated contract itself is not wrong.

**Suggested fix.** Name the parse layer's target in §3. Either forbid tabs outside quoted and block-scalar content as an ASTRA restriction under `INVALID_SYNTAX`, or require tab separation to be accepted and give the harness a loader or pre-scan that accepts it, with valid fixtures for each tab case. Correct the YAML 1.2 claims in `conftest.py` and `CLAUDE.md:56`. In §4, write "after white space (a space or a tab)".

### F067. The harness loader treats NEL, LS and PS as line breaks, as YAML 1.1 does

- **Lens:** Standards, Edge cases · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `tests/conftest.py:124-130`, `tests/conftest.py:133-147`, `tests/test_loader.py:1`, `CLAUDE.md:56`, `docs/specification.md:79`, `docs/specification.md:163-165`, `docs/specification.md:1097`

**What is wrong.** YAML 1.2 made NEL (U+0085), LS (U+2028) and PS (U+2029) ordinary characters. The harness loader is ruamel.yaml 0.19.1's pure safe loader, and its scanner still lexes all three as line breaks (`ruamel/yaml/scanner.py:43`, `:1456`, `:1720`). It folds a NEL to a space in plain, single-quoted and double-quoted scalars. It drops an LS or PS that ends a plain scalar. It fails to parse a block scalar that contains any of the three. Its reader also refuses DEL, C1 controls other than NEL, and U+FFFE anywhere in the stream, quoted scalars included (`reader.py:187-188`). YAML 1.2.2 requires that all of these be allowed in quoted scalars. The yaml 2.9.1 package keeps every one of them as content. So the same file gets different values, or different verdicts, in the Python and TypeScript stacks. Three places call the harness loader YAML 1.2: `conftest.py:125` and `:136`, `test_loader.py:1`, and `CLAUDE.md:56`. No loader test, fixture or example contains any of these characters.

The reverse deviation also exists. yaml 2.9.1 accepts raw C1 and DEL in plain scalars, which YAML 1.2.2 forbids (`ns-char` is built on `c-printable`). So "any strict YAML 1.2 loader" (spec:79) is met by neither stack here.

**Evidence.** YAML 1.2.2 §5.4: "YAML treats them as non-break characters as of version 1.2". §5.1: "YAML processors must allow all non-C0 characters inside quoted scalars."

Base document: `spec_version: "0.1.0"` plus `outputs: {result: {type: metric, format: json}}`, with one field changed. The characters are raw UTF-8. Python means jsonschema with the harness loader; JS means Ajv 8.20 with yaml 2.9.1 (`{version:'1.2', schema:'core', uniqueKeys:true, merge:false}`).

| Change | Python | JS |
|---|---|---|
| `format: json<NEL>` | VALID (reads `'json'`) | `/outputs/result/format` INVALID_VALUE |
| `type: metric<LS>` | VALID (reads `'metric'`) | `/outputs/result/type` INVALID_VALUE |
| `description: \|` with `First paragraph.<NEL>Second paragraph.` | PARSE-ERROR `ParserError: while parsing a block mapping` | VALID |
| the same with `<LS>` | PARSE-ERROR `ParserError` | VALID |
| `url: "https://example.org/a<NEL>b"` | `/analyses/up/url` INVALID_VALUE (`'https://example.org/a b'`) | VALID |
| `label: "F1<U+0096>score"` | PARSE-ERROR `ReaderError: unacceptable character #x0096` | VALID |
| `path: data/a<NEL>b.csv` | VALID, reads `'data/a b.csv'` | VALID, reads `"data/a\u0085b.csv"` |

The repository's own test functions, run on two fixtures kept outside the repository, both pass. `Analysis-900-ls-in-block-scalar.yaml`, headed `# rule: INVALID_SYNTAX` / `# fails at: parse`, passes, though the file is well-formed YAML 1.2. That contradicts spec:1097: "Every `astra.yaml` is well-formed YAML 1.2 under the core schema". `Analysis-901-ls-after-type.yaml` passes as a valid fixture, though under YAML 1.2 the contract rejects it with INVALID_VALUE.

**Why it matters.** The parse layer is meant to be loader-independent. Text pasted from web pages or Word, or mis-decoded from cp1252 (where NEL is "…"), carries these characters. A Python validator and a TS validator then disagree on validity, or both accept a path and look for different files under MISSING_INPUT_FILE. The harness can pass a wrong fixture for the wrong reason, and anyone who reuses its loader in astra-tools inherits YAML 1.1 lexing. The JSON Schemas themselves are unaffected.

**Suggested fix.** Have §3 forbid raw NEL, LS, PS, C1 and DEL in `astra.yaml` under INVALID_SYNTAX (authors write `\N`, `\L`, `\P` in double quotes; both loaders agree on these), so the rule binds both stacks. Then pre-scan for them in the harness, and add loader tests and a parse fixture.

### F068. Anchors and aliases are allowed but unbounded: common loaders disagree, and a refusal has no code

- **Lens:** Standards, Edge cases · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:79`, `docs/specification.md:89-90`, `docs/specification.md:163-182`, `docs/specification.md:1097`, `tests/conftest.py:126-147`, `tests/data/invalid/Analysis-181-merge-key.yaml:5,9`

**What is wrong.** §3 never mentions anchors or aliases. They are ordinary YAML 1.2 syntax, so a file that uses them is well-formed and conforms, and the suite already relies on them: Analysis-181 aliases `&base` and expects `UNKNOWN_FIELD`, not a parse error. The parse layer is "any strict YAML 1.2 loader" (spec:79), but loaders differ on aliases. The `yaml` package, by default, throws on the 100th alias to one anchor. ruamel has no limit. The specification does not say whether a validator may refuse on resource grounds, and no code covers such a refusal. `INVALID_SYNTAX` (spec:1097) cannot apply, because the file is well-formed. The other direction has no bound either. A validator that reports every error, as spec:89-90 requires, can be stalled by a file of a few hundred bytes.

**Evidence.** `grep -n -i -E 'anchor|alias' docs/specification.md` finds only "anchored pattern" (line 245, the regex sense). spec:163-165: "**`astra.yaml`** is YAML 1.2 …, read strictly, under the core schema only". The bullets at 167-182 cover `%YAML`, duplicate keys, the core types and merge keys, and nothing else.

`dec1-100.yaml` declares decisions `model` and `seed`. Output `o0` has `decisions: &ds [model]`, and `o1`…`o100` have `decisions: *ds`.

```
$ uv run python toolkit/validate.py Analysis dec1-100.yaml       # harness loader + jsonschema
VALID
$ node toolkit/js/validate.mjs <repo> Analysis dec1-100.yaml       # yaml 2.9.1 + Ajv
yaml/dist/nodes/Alias.js:67  throw new ReferenceError(msg);
ReferenceError: Excessive alias count indicates a resource exhaustion attack   (exit 1, no code)
```

99 aliases load in both. With `toJS({ maxAliasCount: -1 })` Ajv reports dec1-100 VALID, so the loader's default alone causes the split. yaml 2.9.1 `dist/doc/Document.js:300`: `maxAliasCount: typeof maxAliasCount === 'number' ? maxAliasCount : 100`. The anchor counts as 1, and `Alias.js:65` throws when `data.count * data.aliasCount > maxAliasCount`.

The unbounded side: `bomb-7.yaml` (363 bytes) holds anchored lists of 10 aliases each, nested 7 levels, under one output's `tags`.

```
$ time -f '%U s user, %e s elapsed, %M KB max RSS' uv run python toolkit/validate.py Analysis bomb-7.yaml
/outputs/r/tags/0 … | …/properties/tags/items/type | WRONG_TYPE | …
/outputs/r/tags     | …/properties/tags/uniqueItems | DUPLICATE_ENTRY | …
23.49 s user, 25.18 s elapsed, 1763308 KB max RSS
```

Time grows tenfold per level (bomb-6: 2.2 s to validate). ruamel shares aliased nodes, so loading takes 0.003 s and `is_valid` returns at once. The cost is jsonschema's messages, which `repr` the whole expanded value (`_keywords.py:212`, `:287`).

**Why it matters.** A large multiverse analysis that reuses one `decisions` or `tags` list through an alias in 100 or more outputs validates in astra-tools and crashes a TypeScript validator or editor on `yaml`'s defaults, with no ASTRA code. A Python validator in CI, an LSP or an upload service can be stalled by a tiny hostile file.

**Suggested fix.** In §3, say that aliases are allowed and resolved before the schema layer. Then either set a floor every validator must accept, with a named parse code for refusing beyond it, or forbid aliases under `INVALID_SYNTAX` and rewrite Analysis-181 (`<<: {type: metric}` still gives `UNKNOWN_FIELD`). Add a fixture at the boundary.

### F069. No rule says `astra.yaml` is exactly one YAML document, so a file with a second document (even a trailing `---`) has two readings

- **Lens:** Standards · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:79`, `docs/specification.md:163-165`, `docs/specification.md:185-186`, `docs/specification.md:1097`, `tests/conftest.py:27-30`

**What is wrong.** A YAML 1.2 stream holds zero or more documents. A valid analysis followed by a line `---` is a well-formed stream of two documents, the second one empty. The `INVALID_SYNTAX` rule asks only that `astra.yaml` be "well-formed YAML 1.2 under the core schema", and this file is. §3 lists the `%YAML` directive and duplicate keys, but not extra documents. The only hint is the passing clause at :185-186, "A record is read as one document, as `astra.yaml` is". No earlier sentence says this, and the clause can also be read as "read the first document". No rule says a second document makes the file invalid, or gives it a code. No fixture covers the case.

**Evidence.** The spec's text (docs/specification.md):

- :79: "| **parse** | any strict YAML 1.2 loader | one file's text |"
- :1097: "Every `astra.yaml` is well-formed YAML 1.2 under the core schema (with no `%YAML` directive other than 1.2), and every record is well-formed JSON"
- :185-186: "A record is read as one document, as `astra.yaml` is, by the same parse and schema rules."

YAML 1.2.2 §9.2: "A YAML stream consists of zero or more documents." Production [208], `l-explicit-document ::= c-directives-end ( l-bare-document | ( e-node s-l-comments ) )`, makes a lone `---` an empty document.

`a0` is a minimal valid analysis: `spec_version: "0.1.0"` and `outputs: {result: {type: metric, format: json}}`, in block style. `a1` is `a0` followed by a line `---`. `a4` is the same content, then `---`, then the content again.

```
$ uv run python stream_py.py a1 a4      # multi-document APIs
a1-trailing-docstart.yaml: harness load_all -> 2 document(s) ['dict', 'NoneType']
$ node stream_js.mjs a1 a4              # yaml@2.9.1, 1.2, core
a1-trailing-docstart.yaml: parseAllDocuments -> 2 document(s), errors []

$ uv run python toolkit/validate.py Analysis a1 a4
PARSE-ERROR ComposerError: expected a single document in the stream
$ node toolkit/js/validate.mjs <repo> Analysis a1 a4
PARSE-ERROR MULTIPLE_DOCS: Source contains multiple documents

$ go run .          # gopkg.in/yaml.v3 v3.0.1, yaml.Unmarshal
a1-trailing-docstart.yaml: err=<nil> keys=2
a4-two-docs.yaml: err=<nil> keys=2
$ ruby stream_rb.rb # Psych 5.2.2 YAML.safe_load
a4-two-docs.yaml: ok -> {"spec_version" => "0.1.0", ...}
```

The harness counts any loader error as `INVALID_SYNTAX` (tests/conftest.py:28: `"INVALID_SYNTAX": YAMLError,`), so the Python and JavaScript validators agree. Go's and Ruby's usual APIs validate the first document and drop the rest without a word. (Psych reads YAML 1.1, and yaml.v3 is not a strict 1.2 core-schema loader, but the spec does not say that "strict" includes "exactly one document".)

**Why it matters.** Two implementers can read the text two ways. One rejects the file. The other validates its first document and ignores the rest, which the literal `INVALID_SYNTAX` rule allows. A validator author has to guess the rule and its code. An author whose file ends in `---` gets a parse error that the specification does not explain.

**Suggested fix.** Reword `INVALID_SYNTAX` at :1097 to "Every `astra.yaml` is a YAML 1.2 stream of exactly one document, well-formed under the core schema ...", and add the same as a bullet in §3 at :163. Add a parse fixture (a valid analysis followed by `---`), excluded in `.pre-commit-config.yaml` and `.yamllint.yaml`.

### F070. An environment IRI whose scheme is a context prefix (`astra:`, `prov:`, `schema:`, `xsd:`, `linkml:`) becomes a different IRI in RDF

- **Lens:** Standards, Edge cases · **Severity:** Important · **Where:** `src/astra/schema/provenance.yaml:13-17`, `src/astra/schema/provenance.yaml:141-152`, `src/astra/schema/generated/provenance.context.jsonld:7-13`, `docs/specification.md:910-913`, `docs/specification.md:1339-1347`

**What is wrong.** The description and §8 say `Environment.iri` is "an absolute IRI", and allow "a scheme of the runner's own". The pattern accepts any scheme. But the slot's range is `uriorcurie` ("a URI or a CURIE" in LinkML), and the record's context defines `astra`, `linkml`, `prov`, `schema` and `xsd` as simple terms ending in `/` or `#`, so JSON-LD 1.1 gives them the prefix flag. An `iri` whose scheme is one of those names is read as a compact IRI and expanded. The record says one IRI and its RDF says another. §10's "the environment's IRI is its `@id`" does not hold for these schemes, and nothing in the specification warns against them.

**Evidence.** The schema and context:

```yaml
# provenance.yaml:141-145
iri:
  identifier: true
  range: uriorcurie
  pattern: '^[A-Za-z][A-Za-z0-9+.-]*:[^\x00-\x20\x7f]+$(?!\n)'
```

```json
// provenance.context.jsonld:7-13
"xsd": "http://www.w3.org/2001/XMLSchema#",
"astra": "https://w3id.org/astra/",
"linkml": "https://w3id.org/linkml/",
"prov": "http://www.w3.org/ns/prov#",
"schema": "http://schema.org/",
"@vocab": "https://w3id.org/astra/",
"iri": "@id",
```

Records are §8's example with only `generated_by.environment.iri` changed. All are VALID against `provenance.schema.json` in the Python and the Ajv validators. Their `prov:used` object, through three RDF routes (rdflib 7.6.0 JSON-LD with the committed context; jsonld.js 8.3.3 `toRDF`; `linkml-convert -C ProvenanceRecord -t ttl`), is the same in each:

```
astra:env:sha256:abc   -> https://w3id.org/astra/env:sha256:abc
linkml:env:sha256:abc  -> https://w3id.org/linkml/env:sha256:abc
prov:env:sha256:abc    -> http://www.w3.org/ns/prov#env:sha256:abc
schema:env:sha256:abc  -> http://schema.org/env:sha256:abc
xsd:env:sha256:abc     -> http://www.w3.org/2001/XMLSchema#env:sha256:abc
```

On the JSON-LD routes, `urn:example-runner:env:sha256:abc` and `commit:env:sha256:abc` are kept (`commit` is an expanded term definition, with no prefix flag).
