- 290–295: no row of the resolution table resolves to an option.
- 311: "A `reference` can end in `#` and a universe id, a **pin**"
- 328–330: "Inputs, outputs, decisions, universes, prior insights, findings and declared analyses are addressable." Options are not on the list.
- 1018: "address, pinned when the output is built per universe"

RFC 0004 (`rfcs/0004-composition.md:107`) and RFC 0003 (`rfcs/0003-cross-universe-artifact-references.md:166-168`, `artifact-id [ "#" universe-id ]`) agree with §4. Design §11 records no departure.

Option ids are unique only within their decision, so no address could pick out one option. Two decisions that share the option `none` are valid:

```yaml
decisions:
  scaling:  {options: {none: {}, standard: {}}}
  outliers: {options: {none: {}, clip: {}}}
```

An option written as an address, `decisions: [model.svm]` with decision `model` and option `svm`, passes the schema in Python and Ajv. §4 reads it as declaration `model` and element `svm`, so it fails only at the validator layer (`UNRESOLVED_ADDRESS`).

Read literally, row 45 would allow a pin after a reference that already ends in one. The grammar and both validators reject that:

```
inputs: [a#u#v]
Python: /outputs/result/inputs/0 | …/Output__identifier_optional/properties/inputs/items/pattern | INVALID_REFERENCE
Ajv:    /outputs/result/inputs/0 | …/Output__identifier_optional/properties/inputs/items/pattern | INVALID_REFERENCE
(control: inputs: [a#u] -> VALID in both)
```

`tests/test_types.py:29` lists `"a#u#v"` among the values `reference` rejects.

**Why it matters.** Readers look definitions up in the glossary. Someone writing a report bridge or a validator who takes row 40 at its word reads `random_seed.seed_42` as an option's address, while §4 resolves `random_seed` as a declaration. Row 45 calls the part before the pin a reference, where the grammar every validator implements calls it an address. Each row points to §4, where the right answer is, which is why this is Minor.

**Suggested fix.** Row 40: "the name of an element other than an option, through the declarations that lead to it; an option is named by an option reference". Row 45: "`#` and a universe id ending a reference, after its address". At line 1018, write "reference" for "address".

### F090. §8 gives the driver every result of a driven analysis, then gives built-once results to their own analysis

- **Lens:** Implementer · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:852-854`, `docs/specification.md:871-874`

**What is wrong.** This is a wording issue. §8 states a general rule and then an exception to it, with nothing to mark the second sentence as an exception. The first sentence's "so" clause covers every result of a driven analysis, built-once outputs included. Its premise ("the analysis whose universe it was built in") does not reach those outputs, because an output built once has no universe (`docs/specification.md:602`: "An output built once has no universe of its own."). The next sentence gives built-once results to their own analysis. Lines 871-874 repeat the broad wording for the bytes. Elsewhere the specification states the precise rule. §7:754 says "What is built in a driver's universes belongs to the driver (§8)." `REBUILT_FROM_COMMIT` (834-836) reads a driven commit analysis's built-once outputs through their records. Those records are read "in the analysis the result belongs to (§8), in that analysis's repository" (813-815), which only works if the driven analysis owns the result. RFC 0005:31 and RFC 0007:172,181 agree. The broad wording comes from RFC 0004:361, and that RFC gives the exception four lines later, at :365.

**Evidence.**

> `docs/specification.md:852-854`: "A result belongs to the analysis whose universe it was built in, so the results of a driven analysis belong to its driver. The result of an output built once belongs to the analysis whose output it is."

> `docs/specification.md:871-874`: "...and tells the recipe where to write through `{output}`, so a driven analysis's recipes write into the driver's results."

A root drives `stage.d`. In `stage`, `prep` reads only `root.raw`, so it is built once (596-600):

```yaml
# ex/stage/astra.yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
analyses:
  root: {path: ..}
decisions:
  d: {options: {a: {}, b: {}}}
outputs:
  prep: {type: data, format: parquet, inputs: [root.raw]}
  fit: {type: metric, format: json, inputs: [prep], decisions: [d]}
```

The first sentence puts the record of `prep` in the root, at `.astra/records/stage.prep.json` with `address: stage.prep`. The second puts it in `stage`, at `stage/.astra/records/prep.json` with `address: prep`. Both analyses and both records validate under jsonschema and Ajv. That is expected, because record placement is a check-layer matter (`MISSING_RECORD`, `MISPLACED_RECORD`, §9:1179).

**Why it matters.** A runner or checker author who reads 852-853 on its own writes, or expects, built-once records and bytes in the driver. Their `MISSING_RECORD` and `MISPLACED_RECORD` checks then disagree with tools that follow 853-854 and §7. A careful reader settles it, since the specific sentence follows the general one and §7 agrees with it.

**Suggested fix.** Reuse §7's wording. At 852-853: "so what a driven analysis builds in its driver's universes belongs to the driver". At 873-874: "so a driven analysis's recipes write what they build in the driver's universes into the driver's results".

### F091. The codes `MISSING_DECISION` and the other selection rules report are not fixed: "every analysis it drives" reads two ways, and a universe with no effective selection is left open

- **Lens:** Implementer · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:89-91`, `docs/specification.md:531-532`, `docs/specification.md:539`, `docs/specification.md:543-546`, `docs/specification.md:552-557`, `docs/specification.md:564-565`, `docs/specification.md:697-698`, `docs/specification.md:716-718`, `docs/specification.md:1133-1135`, `src/astra/schema/analysis.yaml:292-293`, `src/astra/schema/analysis.yaml:326-327`

**What is wrong.** A validator "reports every parse, schema and validator rule the analysis breaks" (spec:89-91), so the set of codes is normative. Two gaps let validators report different sets for the same file:

1. **The pronoun.** Every statement of `MISSING_DECISION` ends in "every analysis it drives" (spec:564-565, 697-698, 1135; analysis.yaml:292-293). In the specification an analysis drives, and so does a universe, so "it" can be either. The intended reading is the driver: the glossary (spec:48) and RFC 0004:407 define driving between analyses. But "an analysis the universe drives" (spec:539), "that universe is pushed through every analysis it drives" (spec:717) and the `Universe` and `decisions` descriptions (analysis.yaml:292-293, 326-327) point the other way.
2. **No effective selection.** The effective selection is defined only through the universe `derived_from` names (spec:552-557). Nothing says which selection rules apply when it can't be built:
   - `derived_from` names no universe (`UNKNOWN_DERIVED_FROM`). One validator skips `MISSING_DECISION` for that universe; another drops the bad `derived_from`, applies step 1 and reports it.
   - The universe is derived from a member of a cycle it is not part of (`from_loop -> loop_a <-> loop_b`). `from_loop` is never "derived from itself" (spec:1134), so it doesn't break `DERIVED_FROM_CYCLE` as worded, its resolution never ends, and nothing says which rules apply to it. RFC 0003:228 words the rule more broadly ("chains do not loop").

The file is invalid in every case; only the reported codes differ. Neither §9 nor `rules.json` gives an order or dependency between rules: an entry carries only `code`, `layer`, `level`, `statement`, `source` and, for check rules, `status`.

**Evidence.** The wordings:

```
docs/specification.md:48     | driver, driven | an analysis whose universes set another's decisions, and that other | §7 |
docs/specification.md:539    of the analysis or, for a decision of an analysis the universe drives, its
docs/specification.md:697    - Every universe of the driver sets every active decision of every analysis
docs/specification.md:698      it drives ([`MISSING_DECISION`](#validator)).
docs/specification.md:717    universes, that universe is pushed through every analysis it drives: it is
docs/specification.md:1135   | `MISSING_DECISION` | MUST | Every universe's effective selection sets every active decision of its analysis and of every analysis it drives | 0003, 0004 |
rfcs/0004-composition.md:407 | A driven analysis has each of the active decisions it defines set in every one of the driver's universes | error |
```

`rules.json:508` carries the §9 statement word for word.

Three documents, schema-valid in both the Python and the Ajv validators, so they reach the validator layer:

```yaml
# case1/astra.yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
decisions:
  model:   {options: {rf: {}, svm: {}}}
  scaling: {options: {none: {}, std: {}}}
outputs:
  score: {type: metric, format: json, decisions: [model, scaling]}
universes:
  full:   {decisions: {model: rf, scaling: none}}
  orphan: {derived_from: nowhere, decisions: {model: svm}}
# case2: the same, with
#   loop_a:    {derived_from: loop_b, decisions: {model: rf}}
#   loop_b:    {derived_from: loop_a, decisions: {model: svm}}
#   from_loop: {derived_from: loop_a, decisions: {scaling: none}}
# case3: analyses: {sub: {path: sub}}; sub defines decision `method`
#   u1: {decisions: {model: rf, sub.method: a}}
#   u2: {decisions: {model: svm}}     # every root decision set, no sub.* key
```

A toy resolver applying §6 steps 1-2 and both readings of "it drives":

```
== case1
  orphan: effective selection undefined: derived_from names no universe ('nowhere')
== case2
  from_loop: effective selection undefined: chain loops at 'loop_a'
== case3
  u2: effective selection {'model': 'svm'}
    'it' = the universe: MISSING_DECISION -; PARTIALLY_DRIVEN ['sub']
    'it' = the driver:   MISSING_DECISION ['sub.method']; PARTIALLY_DRIVEN ['sub']
```

In case 3 RFC 0004:407 gives the driver's result. `grep -n -i "depend\|skip\|not checked\|no effective" docs/specification.md` finds no statement that a rule is skipped when one it depends on is broken. The repository has no validator-layer implementation to run.

**Why it matters.** `rules.json` exists "so that every tool reports the same code for the same error" (spec:1249-1250). Implementers of astra-tools and astra-typescript working from §9, `rules.json` or the `Universe` description have only the pronoun to go on, and must invent a behaviour for cases 1 and 2; a naive recursive resolver doesn't terminate on `from_loop`. Nothing breaks today. It will matter when a conformance suite writes the expected codes for its fixtures.

**Suggested fix.** Write "every analysis its driver drives" in `MISSING_DECISION` (spec:532, 564-565, 697-698, 1135; analysis.yaml:293), and optionally "an analysis its analysis drives" at spec:539, 717 and analysis.yaml:327. After the effective-selection list in §6, add: "A universe whose `derived_from` doesn't resolve, or whose chain loops, has no effective selection; the rules that need one aren't checked for it." Optionally align `DERIVED_FROM_CYCLE` with RFC 0003's "chains do not loop". Tom decides.

### F092. §8 doesn't say that a redundant pin on an output built once still names its one result

- **Lens:** Implementer · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:1016-1018`, `docs/specification.md:602-605`, `docs/specification.md:521-522`, `docs/specification.md:852-858`, `docs/specification.md:864-870`, `docs/specification.md:1149`

**What is wrong.** A reference to an output built once can carry a pin, and the file is valid: `REDUNDANT_PIN` is only a SHOULD. The spec does answer what such a reference resolves to, but the answer is spread over §4, §5, §6 and §8, and the one sentence that maps evidence to a record doesn't give it. Read as an answer, spec:598-599 ("it has one result, the same in every universe"), spec:311-312 ("A pin reads the output in that universe") and "redundant" (521-522) together mean that the pin changes nothing. Spec:1016-1018 instead says evidence takes its status "through the record its address names: the address, pinned when the output is built per universe, names exactly one result". If "its address" is read as the evidence's `artifact` reference, `comparison#forest` gives `.astra/records/forest/comparison.json`, a record that never exists. RFC 0003's phrase "selects nothing" (604) can also be read as "selects no result". This is a wording gap, not a contradiction. The text comes from RFC 0003:207-209 and, word for word, from RFC 0005:203.

**Evidence.** In this document `comparison` is built once. `report` reads it pinned, and a finding cites it pinned:

```yaml
outputs:
  comparison:
    type: table
    format: csv
    inputs: [accuracy#forest, accuracy#svm]
    recipe: {command: python compare.py --a {inputs.accuracy#forest} --b {inputs.accuracy#svm} --out {output}}
  report:
    type: table
    format: csv
    inputs: [comparison#forest]
    recipe: {command: python report.py --in {inputs.comparison#forest} --out {output}}
findings:
  svm_scores_higher:
    claim: The SVM scores higher than the random forest.
    evidence:
      - artifact: comparison#forest
```
```
$ uv run python toolkit/validate.py Analysis astra.yaml     -> VALID
$ node toolkit/js/validate.mjs <repo> Analysis astra.yaml   -> VALID
```

No validator-layer MUST rejects it (spec:1149: `| REDUNDANT_PIN | SHOULD | No reference to an output built once has a pin | 0003 |`). The result's address has `#` only "for a result built in a universe" (spec:856-858). The record and bytes places "are worked out from the result's address" (spec:869). So `comparison`'s only record is `.astra/records/comparison.json`, and in the §4 grammar the pin is not part of an address (spec:212: `reference = address [ "#" id ] ; "#" pins a universe`). The text never says in one place that a reference first resolves to a result, and that the record comes from that result's address.

**Why it matters.** A checker that applies 1016-1018 mechanically gives the evidence `missing` where the intended status is the status of `comparison.json`, for example `unchanged`. The same question arises for `{inputs.comparison#forest}` (`results/comparison.csv`, or `results/forest/comparison.csv`), and for a pin through a driven declaration on a built-once output of the driven analysis (e.g. `inner.summary#u1`).

**Suggested fix.** At spec:1016-1018, say that evidence takes the status of the record of the result its `artifact` names, worked out from that result's address. At spec:604, say "a pin on it changes nothing: the reference names its one result".

### F093. `PIN_LIKE_COMMENT` doesn't say what "looks like a pin" means

- **Lens:** Implementer · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:318-320`, `docs/specification.md:1152`, `src/astra/schema/generated/rules.json:623-627`, `rfcs/0003-cross-universe-artifact-references.md:175-176`, `rfcs/0003-cross-universe-artifact-references.md:242`

**What is wrong.** The rule warns on an `inputs` entry whose line comment "looks like a pin". Nothing in the specification defines the term. The one example, `- f1_score #svm`, settles the obvious case: `#`, then the id of a universe. It doesn't settle a space after `#` (`# svm`), an id that names no universe (`#rbf`), an id followed by prose (`#svm_is_best, see notes`), or a comment on its own line after the entry. RFC 0003 has the same wording and is just as vague, so the specification follows its source. Design §12 and §13 don't record the gap.

The rule also covers only `inputs`. `Evidence.artifact` is "a reference written as in `Output.inputs`" (`docs/specification.md:505`) and loses a pin to a stray space the same way. This costs little: if the output is built per universe, the unpinned evidence already draws `UNPINNED_EVIDENCE` (`:519-520`); if it is built once, the pin was redundant (`REDUNDANT_PIN`, `:521-522`). The diagnostic is less precise, but nothing is lost silently.

**Evidence.** `docs/specification.md:1152`:

> | `PIN_LIKE_COMMENT` | SHOULD | No `inputs` entry has a line comment that looks like a pin (needs the source text) | 0003 |

`grep -n -i "looks like\|line comment\|source text" docs/specification.md` finds only `:1152` and an unrelated line. The RFC, `rfcs/0003-cross-universe-artifact-references.md:176`: "validators should flag an input whose line-comment looks like a pin."

A document with an output `f1_score` built per universe (`svm`, `rf`) and these block entries:

```yaml
      - f1_score #svm                      # a
      - f1_score # svm                     # b
      - f1_score #svm_is_best, see notes   # c
      - f1_score #rbf                      # d (no universe rbf)
      - f1_score  # TODO: check this       # e
  ...
      evidence:
        - artifact: f1_score #svm
```

```
$ uv run python dump.py astra.yaml      # load_yaml from tests/conftest.py
{"a": ["f1_score"], "b": ["f1_score"], "c": ["f1_score"], "d": ["f1_score"], "e": ["f1_score"]} ... "evidence": [{"artifact": "f1_score"}]
$ uv run python toolkit/validate.py Analysis astra.yaml     -> VALID
$ node toolkit/js/validate.mjs <repo> Analysis astra.yaml   -> VALID
```

Single-line flow forms (`inputs: [f1_score #svm]`) are parse errors in both loaders. A flow collection broken across lines (`inputs: [f1_score #svm` then `]` on the next line) loads to `["f1_score"]` and validates, so the pin is lost there too.

**Why it matters.** Entry `a` should clearly warn and `e` clearly shouldn't. For `b`, `c` and `d`, two validators will give different warnings for the same document. Validity is never affected, and the rule needs the source text, so it is best-effort anyway.

**Suggested fix.** Define the term in §9 (and the tidy-up RFC), for example "a comment on the same line as the entry, with `#` immediately followed by an id". Optionally say the same check may run on `Evidence.artifact`.

### F094. rules.json maps three empty `required: []` keywords, which can never fail

- **Lens:** Implementer, Internal consistency, Standards · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:1258-1264`, `src/astra/schema/generated/analysis.schema.json:50`, `src/astra/schema/generated/analysis.schema.json:323`, `src/astra/schema/generated/analysis.schema.json:519`, `src/astra/schema/generated/rules.json:15`, `src/astra/schema/generated/rules.json:94`, `src/astra/schema/generated/rules.json:153`, `tools/rules.py:397-398`, `tools/jsonschema_generator.py:79-80`, `tests/test_rules.py:321`, `tests/test_rules.py:331`, `tests/test_rules.py:369-373`

**What is wrong.** §10 defines `errors` as "the code of every keyword of the three JSON Schemas that can report an error". On that ground it leaves out the open variant's `additionalProperties`, "which fails on nothing". The generator removes the key from `required` in `AnalysisDeclaration__identifier_optional`, `Option__identifier_optional` and `Universe__identifier_optional` but keeps the empty array. JSON Schema 2019-09 Validation §6.5.3 says "Omitting this keyword has the same behavior as an empty array", so these three keywords also fail on nothing. `rules.json` still gives each one `MISSING_FIELD`. `tools/rules.py` adds an entry for every `required` keyword and never checks whether it is empty. The test passes because it counts the same way: `REPORTING` includes `"required"` unconditionally, so `reporting_keywords` yields empty arrays too.

**Evidence.**

```
$ grep -n '"required": \[\]' src/astra/schema/generated/*.json
src/astra/schema/generated/analysis.schema.json:50:      "required": [],
src/astra/schema/generated/analysis.schema.json:323:      "required": [],
src/astra/schema/generated/analysis.schema.json:519:      "required": [],
$ grep -n 'identifier_optional/required' src/astra/schema/generated/rules.json
15:  …#/$defs/AnalysisDeclaration__identifier_optional/required": "MISSING_FIELD",
94:  …#/$defs/Option__identifier_optional/required": "MISSING_FIELD",
153: …#/$defs/Universe__identifier_optional/required": "MISSING_FIELD",
```

These are the only empty `required` arrays in the three schemas. The cause is at `tools/rules.py:397-398`:

```python
            elif keyword == "required":
                self.add(here, "MISSING_FIELD")
```

`Draft201909Validator({"required": []})` reports nothing on `{}` or `{"x": 1}`. A document with `options: {a: {}}`, `universes: {u: {}}` and `analyses: {up: {}}` makes both validators (Python and Ajv) report only `/analyses/up` at `…/AnalysisDeclaration__identifier_optional/oneOf` (INVALID_DECLARATION_SOURCE). Neither reports anything at the three `/required` keywords. The test's own walker agrees with the map: 266 entries in all, equal to the 266 that `reporting_keywords` yields. So three map entries can never be reached.

The nine `…/propertyNames/type` entries are not part of this defect. A Python consumer reports them as WRONG_TYPE for unquoted `true:` or `1:` keys (see §3).

**Why it matters.** No verdict or code lookup changes. A conformance suite, or a self-test in astra-tools or astra-typescript that requires a fixture for every `errors` entry, will find three entries no input can reach. The shipped artefact is also a slight superset of what §10, `CLAUDE.md:33`, `tools/rules.py:8` and `:633` and `tests/test_rules.py:371` say it holds.

**Suggested fix.** Have `tools/jsonschema_generator.py:79-80` drop an emptied `required`, or skip empty `required` both in `tools/rules.py:397` and in `reporting_keywords`. Changing only one of the last two makes `test_every_keyword_that_can_fail_has_one_code` fail.

### F095. The spec never says a result is one file, so a directory result (Zarr, a multi-file set) has no defined hash or size

- **Lens:** Implementer, Author · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:350-351`, `docs/specification.md:378-381`, `docs/specification.md:414`, `docs/specification.md:864-867`, `docs/specification.md:896-898`, `docs/specification.md:1171-1173`, `docs/specification.md:1195`, `src/astra/schema/provenance.yaml:52`, `src/astra/schema/provenance.yaml:57`, `src/astra/schema/analysis.yaml:191-194`, `rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:25`

**What is wrong.** §5 says an input "names one file" and gives multi-file data a route. §8 says nothing of the kind about a result. It defines `content_hash` and `size` over "the result's bytes" only, and calls `{output}` "the path the result is written to". The `format` pattern accepts `zarr`, and no rule or code covers a recipe that writes a directory at `{output}`. RFC 0005 says "The result's file", and the spec drops the word.

The answer can be pieced together. SHA-256 is defined over one byte string, and the status table makes a directory at the default place `absent` ("Anything else is at the default place", 1172). So the contract isn't wrong. But an author has to infer that from the hash definition, the status table and the RFC.

**Evidence.**

- spec:350-351: "It names one file ([`MISSING_INPUT_FILE`](#validator)). Data in many files is many inputs." RFC 0006:53 adds "or an output's recipe brings it in (section 3)", which sends multi-file data to an output.
- spec:380-381: "Any token of that form is a format. It is the extension of the result's default place (§8)."
- spec:896-898: "the `content_hash` and `size` (in bytes) of the result's bytes".
- provenance.yaml:52: "The SHA-256 digest of the result's bytes." provenance.yaml:57: "The size of the result, in bytes."
- rfcs/0005:25: "The result's file is written to a default location worked out the same way".
- RFC 0006:132 defers "a format that is a folder such as Zarr", but only for inputs.
- `grep -n -i directory docs/specification.md` finds nothing about results.

```yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
outputs:
  field:
    type: data
    format: zarr
    recipe:
      command: python regrid.py {output}
```

```
python jsonschema  Analysis  -> VALID
ajv                Analysis  -> VALID
```

`RecordedInput.content_hash` and `size` (provenance.yaml:127-134) have the same gap when the result a recipe reads is a directory.

**Why it matters.** Zarr stores, partitioned Parquet, PLINK filesets and per-block HDF5 sets are directories or multi-file sets. An author with one can't tell from §5 or §8 whether `{output}` may be a directory, and must guess (one reviewer bundled a panel as `format: tar.gz`). A runner author may record a tar or manifest hash, and any checker would then report its results as `absent`.

**Suggested fix.** In §8 (or §5 Outputs), mirror the input sentence: "A result is one file, written at `{output}`. An artefact of many files is an archive or several outputs." Carry "file" into the `Output.format` and `content_hash`/`size` descriptions, and list folder formats as future work, as RFC 0006 does for inputs.

### F096. MISSING_FIELD sends readers to §8 for the record's required fields, but §8 doesn't say which they are

- **Lens:** Implementer, Author, Internal consistency · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:1101`, `docs/specification.md:894-917`, `docs/specification.md:335-337`, `src/astra/schema/generated/provenance.schema.json:55-59`, `src/astra/schema/generated/rules.json` (MISSING_FIELD `statement`)

**What is wrong.** MISSING_FIELD names the required fields of `astra.yaml` one by one, then ends "and the record's (§8)". `rules.json` copies that statement, so tools print it with every missing record field. §8's "What a record holds" lists `spec_version`, `address`, `content_hash`, `size`, `generated_by` and Generation's `commit`, `inputs`, `environment`, `runner`, `started` and `finished` all the same way. It never says which are required. The contract makes `inputs`, `started` and `finished` optional. The contract is right: it matches RFC 0005 §2, and the check rule MISSING_RECORD_INPUTS covers `inputs` when the output lists entries. The defect is in the text only. §5 says it for the analysis ("everything else is optional", 335-337). §8 has no such sentence. The design's catalogue row has the same wording, with "(§6)" (`design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:351`), so the spec does not depart from the design here.

**Evidence.** `docs/specification.md:1101`:

> Every required field is present: `spec_version`, `outputs`, … `TextQuoteSelector.exact`, `FragmentSelector.value`, and the record's (§8)

`docs/specification.md:916-917`, with no "optional" mark:

> - `started` and `finished`: when the recipe started and finished, in UTC to the millisecond, `YYYY-MM-DDThh:mm:ss.sssZ`.

```
$ sed -n 844,1022p docs/specification.md | grep -n -i 'optional\|required\|omit\|left out'
(no output)
```

`provenance.schema.json:55-59` (Generation): `"required": ["commit", "environment", "runner"]`. A record with none of `inputs`, `started` or `finished`:

```json
{"spec_version": "0.1.0", "address": "output1",
 "content_hash": "sha256:7af13cf14f96106b729279ec96430c5b115437053c29aeca4ff70b95770fb83e",
 "size": 732,
 "generated_by": {"commit": "a7d9ea93c409ab6da0d919308f91f51de7c7ec6f",
   "environment": {"iri": "urn:example-runner:env:1"},
   "runner": {"id": "example-runner", "version": "1.2.0"}}}
```

```
rec-minimal.json    VALID   (jsonschema and Ajv)
rec-no-runner.json  /generated_by | .../$defs/Generation/required | MISSING_FIELD   (control)
```

The answer is in the field reference. §8 links to `elements/ProvenanceRecord.md` and `elements/Generation.md`, and the generated Generation page gives `inputs` as `*` and `started` and `finished` as `0..1`. Spec 29-32 defers fields to that reference (D8). The catalogue row points at §8's prose, though, not at the reference.

**Why it matters.** Runner and checker authors follow the catalogue to §8. There they find `started` and `finished` listed like `commit`, and may make them mandatory. A checker built that way rejects records the contract accepts. A runner that writes every field §8 lists still writes valid records.

**Suggested fix.** Name the record's required fields in the MISSING_FIELD cell, as the row already does for `astra.yaml`'s. Or point the row at the record's field reference rather than §8. Tom decides.

### F097. The check layer's levels have no stated effect, and the `MAY` on `modified` sits outside any rule row

- **Lens:** Implementer, Standards · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:22-27`, `docs/specification.md:1010-1014`, `docs/specification.md:1058-1059`, `docs/specification.md:1072-1074`, `docs/specification.md:1173`, `docs/specification.md:1184`, `docs/specification.md:1203-1206`, `src/astra/schema/generated/rules.json:675`, `rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:87`

**What is wrong.** §9 says what a level means in every layer but the check layer, and the one level the check layer's statuses add sits in a table no one reads as rules. Two facets:

- **Levels.** For parse, schema and validator rules the level decides between error and warning (1058). For check rules "Nothing fails" (1059), and nothing says what `MUST` and `SHOULD` mean there. So `MISSING_RECORD` (MUST) and `ABSENT_RESULT` (SHOULD) differ only in their status. Yet §8 says "`absent` is not an error" (1011), which suggests `stale`, `missing` and `modified` are. §9 never says whether they are.
- **The `MAY`.** The statuses table gives `modified` a level: "Reported only by tooling that recognises stand-ins (`MAY`)" (1173), taken from RFC 0005 (87). The table has no Code column, so the extractor skips it (1074) and the permission is in no rule row and has no level in `rules.json`. §1 accounts for every codeless keyworded statement (two recommendations in §7 and §8, two runner duties in §9), and this one is not on the list. Set in a code span, the way the spec elsewhere names a level, it is unclear whether 1173 states a permission at all.

**Evidence.**

`docs/specification.md:1058-1059`:
```
| parse, schema, validator | The file fails: the code is reported as an **error** for a `MUST` and as a **warning** for a `SHOULD` |
| check | Nothing fails. The code is reported and **the result gets a status**, given in the check table's extra column, *Status when broken* |
```

`docs/specification.md:1010-1012`: "Since the bytes can be kept elsewhere, `absent` is not an error. Only tooling that recognises stand-ins can tell that the bytes have been `modified`".

```
$ python3 -c "import json;r=json.load(open('src/astra/schema/generated/rules.json'));print({x['code']:(x['level'],x.get('status')) for x in r['rules'] if x['layer']=='check'})"
{'MISSING_RECORD': ('MUST', '`missing`'), 'INVALID_RECORD': ('MUST', '`missing`'), 'MISPLACED_RECORD': ('MUST', '`missing`'), 'MISSING_RECORD_INPUTS': ('MUST', '`missing`'), 'STALE_RESULT': ('MUST', '`stale`'), 'ABSENT_RESULT': ('SHOULD', '`absent`, or `modified`'), 'STALE_EVIDENCE': ('MUST', "the cited result's status")}
```

No rule in any layer has level `MAY`, and no rule has a severity field.

`docs/specification.md:1173`: "| `modified` | Reported only by tooling that recognises stand-ins (`MAY`): the stand-in names another hash, or the file is plainly not a stand-in and doesn't match |"

§1, `docs/specification.md:24-27`: "The two recommendations no tool can check are stated where they apply (§7, §8), and the two duties of a runner that no tool can check after the runner's rules (§9)." A scan for capitalised BCP 14 keywords outside rule rows and code fences finds 798 (§7), 978 (§8) and 1204-1205 (the runner duties), all named by §1, and 1173, which is not. The runner duties get a codeless paragraph of their own (1203-1206); the `modified` permission gets nothing like it.

**Why it matters.** Statuses are fixed, so two conforming checkers give the same statuses. They can still differ on whether a `stale` or `modified` result is reported as an error, because §8 implies a split that §9 doesn't define. A tool that reads only `rules.json` can't tell that `modified` comes only from tooling that recognises stand-ins. A reader who trusts §1 to account for every codeless keyword misses this one.

**Suggested fix.** Add a clause to the check row at 1059 saying what a check-layer `SHOULD` means (its status, like `absent`, can be legitimate). Then either state the `modified` permission as a plain sentence after the check table and add it to §1's list, or drop "(`MAY`)" at 1173 and word `ABSENT_RESULT`'s status as "`absent` (or `modified`, for tooling that recognises stand-ins)".

### F098. `INVALID_RECORD` depends on "newer", which nothing defines, and is silent on an older record version

- **Lens:** Implementer · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:54-61`, `docs/specification.md:92-93`, `docs/specification.md:948-952`, `docs/specification.md:1180`, `docs/specification.md:1242-1246`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:731-732`

**What is wrong.** `INVALID_RECORD` is a MUST rule, and it chooses the schema by comparing versions: the closed record schema of the record's `spec_version`, or the open variant "when that version is newer than the checker's". The specification never says how versions are ordered. §2 also gives a checker no version, so "the checker's" version is undefined too. §1 says the specification "fixes only the form of a version". The format fixes three integers, so comparing them as integers is the obvious reading, but the text never says so. This is mainly a wording gap.

There is a second gap. The rule and both passages that explain it cover two cases: the record's own version, and a newer one. They say nothing about an older record version the checker has no schema for. `astra.yaml` has a rule for this case (`UNSUPPORTED_SPEC_VERSION`). Records have none. Design §12 defers "which versions a schema accepts" to the versioning policy, and spec §1 points there too. That policy is not yet written. `docs/rfc-process.md:111` links it to `docs/about.md`, which contains no policy text.

**Evidence.**

```
docs/specification.md:1180: | `INVALID_RECORD` | MUST | Every record is valid against the record schema of its `spec_version`, or against the open variant when that version is newer than the checker's | `missing` | 0005 §2, §8 |
docs/specification.md:59-61: This specification fixes only the form of a version. What one version promises about another, and which versions a schema accepts, is left to ASTRA's versioning policy.
docs/specification.md:1118: | `UNSUPPORTED_SPEC_VERSION` | MUST | Every `astra.yaml` in the stack has a `spec_version` the validator has the schema for (...) | 0007 §6 |
design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:731-732: Versioning policy: whether a v0.1 schema accepts only `0.1.x` files is left to the policy. `spec_version` only checks the format here.
```

RFC 0005:136 and tidy-up change 21 (`NNNN-schema-tidy-up.md:171`) also speak only of a "later" version. A naive string comparison orders versions wrongly:

```
$ python3 -c 'print("0.10.0" > "0.9.0", (0,10,0) > (0,9,0))'
False True
```

The shipped closed schema accepts any well-formed `spec_version`, so choosing a schema is left entirely to the checker. `ProvenanceRecord-002` with `spec_version` set to `0.0.1` or `99.0.0` is VALID against both the closed and the open schema, in both jsonschema and Ajv. Adding an unknown field makes it fail the closed schema only (`#/additionalProperties`, `UNKNOWN_FIELD`).

**Why it matters.** At v0.1 there is only one record version, and no tool writes records yet, so no real file triggers this today. Once a second record version ships, checkers will behave differently on an older record they have no schema for. One validates it against its own closed schema, one calls it `missing`, and one uses the open variant. A checker that compares versions as strings will also pick the wrong schema for `0.10.0` and later.

**Suggested fix.** In §1, say that versions are ordered by MAJOR, then MINOR, then PATCH, compared as integers. Define a checker's version as the latest `spec_version` it has the record schema for. When the versioning policy is written, give the check layer a counterpart to `UNSUPPORTED_SPEC_VERSION` for records.

### F099. "What is read" says an analysis from a commit runs none of its code, without the RFC's "Reading it" scope

- **Lens:** Implementer · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:774-786`, `docs/specification.md:804`, `docs/specification.md:829-832`, `rfcs/0007-analyses-in-other-repositories.md:84`, `rfcs/0007-analyses-in-other-repositories.md:144`

**What is wrong.** "What is read" says "None of its code runs, and no checkout is needed" with no condition. Its "Only these are read" list has no entry for the analysis's own files. "Driving" then says the recipes of a driven analysis from a commit run on its files at the commit. The substance is answered in the text: the paragraph sits under "What is read", "Use" opens with "Unless it is driven", and :841-842 sets "read" against "driven". But taken alone, the sentence and the list are false for a driven analysis. The reader has to piece the scope together from three places. RFC 0007 §2 scopes the sentence ("Reading it runs none of its code"). The spec drops "Reading it", and design §11 does not record the change. This is a wording finding.

**Evidence.**

```
docs/specification.md:775-776
  None of its code runs,
  and no checkout is needed. Only these are read:
docs/specification.md:804
  **Use.** Unless it is driven, none of the recipes of an analysis from a
docs/specification.md:829-832
  **Driving.** A driver's universes can drive an analysis from a commit, as any
  declared analysis. Its recipes then run on exactly its files at the declared
  commit ([`NOT_AT_COMMIT`](#runner)). Where the tool puts those files is the
  tool's choice, and they aren't part of the declaring analysis's code.
rfcs/0007-analyses-in-other-repositories.md:84
  ... Reading it runs none of its code. Only these are read
rfcs/0007-analyses-in-other-repositories.md:144
  ... Driving it runs its recipes so the tool needs its files at the declared
  commit. ... they aren't part of the declaring analysis's code and aren't a
  change that stops a runner from running.
```

There is a second, related gap. Spec :832 keeps only the first half of RFC 0007:144 and drops "and aren't a change that stops a runner from running". The `UNCOMMITTED_CHANGES` exemption at :788-792 covers only "none of it", the items in the "Only these are read" list, and the driven files are not among them.

**Why it matters.** A tool author who reads "What is read" on its own sees a blobs-only fetch with no checkout and no code. The Driving paragraph and `NOT_AT_COMMIT` correct that, but only later in the text. A runner author who follows the text exactly could raise `UNCOMMITTED_CHANGES` when the tool puts the driven files inside the declaring repository's working tree. RFC 0007 §7 allows that placement.

**Suggested fix.** At :775, restore the RFC's scope: "Reading it runs none of its code and needs no checkout." At :832, add "and aren't a change that stops a runner from running ([`UNCOMMITTED_CHANGES`](#runner))".

### F100. The `missing` row omits a record that breaks a parse rule

- **Lens:** Implementer · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:184-186`, `docs/specification.md:1058-1059`, `docs/specification.md:1097-1098`, `docs/specification.md:1170`, `docs/specification.md:1180`, `rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:199`

**What is wrong.** A careful reading gives the answer, but the answer is spread over four places. §3 reads records "by the same parse and schema rules" as `astra.yaml` (184-186). So a truncated record breaks `INVALID_SYNTAX` (1097), a record with a repeated key breaks `DUPLICATE_KEY` (1098), and breaking a parse rule means "The file fails" (1058). Such a record is not a valid record, so its result is `missing`. RFC 0005 says this directly. The `missing` row of §9, though, explains the status with a colon and four check codes only (1170). It reads as a complete list, and a parse failure is not on it. The spec also doesn't say whether a checker reports `INVALID_RECORD` (1180, "valid against the record schema") as well as the parse code.

**Evidence.** Quotes:

- 184-186: "A record is read as one document, as `astra.yaml` is, by the same parse and schema rules."
- 1170: "| `missing` | The result has no valid record where its address says: `MISSING_RECORD`, `INVALID_RECORD`, `MISPLACED_RECORD` or `MISSING_RECORD_INPUTS` doesn't hold |"
- RFC 0005:199: "`missing` (no record, or a file breaking a rule of section 1 or 2)".

A record from `tests/data/valid/ProvenanceRecord-002-built-once.yaml`, written as JSON, with `"size": 999,` before `"size": 732,`:

```
$ uv run python toolkit/validate.py ProvenanceRecord dup-key.json
dup-key.json   PARSE-ERROR DuplicateKeyError: while constructing a mapping
$ node toolkit/js/validate.mjs <repo> ProvenanceRecord dup-key.json
dup-key.json   PARSE-ERROR DUPLICATE_KEY: Map keys must be unique
```

A checker that reads records with `json.load` or `JSON.parse`, then validates against `provenance.schema.json`:

```
dup-key.json   size = 732 | schema errors: []
dup-key.json   size = 732 | ajv valid: true null
```

RFC 8259 §4 makes unique names only a SHOULD, so this record is well-formed JSON. Only `DUPLICATE_KEY`, applied through 186, catches it. A checker that skips 186 is non-conforming, since the parse layer is enforced by a strict YAML 1.2 loader (79). But nothing at 1170 or 1098 tells it so. `DUPLICATE_KEY` speaks of "a map" and lists `astra.yaml` ids.

**Why it matters.** A checker author who works from the status table can miss parse failures. One checker gives a duplicate-key record `unchanged`, and another gives it `missing`. A truncated record can be reported as `INVALID_SYNTAX` or as `INVALID_RECORD`. This goes against §10's aim "that every tool reports the same code for the same error" (1248-1250). No ProvenanceRecord fixture fails at parse, so the tests don't settle the question.

**Suggested fix.** Add "or the record breaks a parse rule (§3)" to the `missing` row at 1170, and say whether `INVALID_RECORD` is reported as well. Do not give the same error two codes. Tom decides.

### F101. Three record rules cover "every record", but only planned results get a status, so records at no planned place are left undefined

- **Lens:** Implementer · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:1180-1182`, `docs/specification.md:1059`, `docs/specification.md:1007`, `docs/specification.md:1161-1162`, `docs/specification.md:1170`, `docs/specification.md:92-93`

**What is wrong.** A checker gives statuses only to planned results. Three of the four record rules, `INVALID_RECORD`, `MISPLACED_RECORD` and `MISSING_RECORD_INPUTS`, quantify over "Every record", and only `MISSING_RECORD` is scoped to planned results. A record can sit at a place that no planned result has. The specification does not say whether a checker evaluates and reports such a record or ignores it. If it reports one, the layer table's "the result gets a status" can't hold, because there is no result to give the status to. Statuses don't diverge: a stray file changes no planned result's status. The codes do.

This is a step away from RFC 0005, which evaluates the record rules through each planned result. The step is not listed in design §11, and §12 and §13 don't mention it.

**Evidence.** From `docs/specification.md`:

- 92-93: "A **checker** reads an analysis's records and results, and gives each planned result its statuses by the check rules (§8)."
- 1059: "| check | Nothing fails. The code is reported and **the result gets a status** ..."
- 1161: "A checker gives every planned result two statuses ..."
- 1179: "`MISSING_RECORD` | MUST | Every planned result has a record at ..."
- 1180: "`INVALID_RECORD` | MUST | Every record is valid against the record schema of its `spec_version` ..."
- 1181: "`MISPLACED_RECORD` | MUST | Every record's `address` names the result whose record is kept where it is"
- 1182: "`MISSING_RECORD_INPUTS` | MUST | Every record has exactly one `inputs` item for each entry its output lists ..."

`rules.json` carries the same statements. Compare `rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:199`: "Each planned result is unchanged | check | `stale` ... or `missing` (no record, or a file breaking a rule of section 1 or 2)".

Reproduction: take the §2 analysis, delete universe `svm` and point `comparison` at `accuracy#forest`. Add a record for an output that no longer exists, plus a stray record with one unknown field:

```
$ validate.py Analysis repo/astra.yaml        -> VALID
$ validate.py ProvenanceRecord .astra/records/scores.json orphan-invalid.json
.astra/records/scores.json   VALID
orphan-invalid.json          (root) | ...provenance.schema.json#/additionalProperties | UNKNOWN_FIELD
```

Ajv gives the same verdicts. The planned results are now `accuracy#forest` and `comparison`. A checker that walks `.astra/records/**` reports `INVALID_RECORD` on `orphan-invalid.json`, with no status attached. A checker that walks planned results never opens the file. The same split happens when an output is renamed: the old records' addresses name no current result, so a file-walking checker can read `MISPLACED_RECORD` as broken. It also happens when an output switches from built once to per universe, which leaves `comparison.json` with an unpinned address. A record left behind by deleting a universe (`svm/accuracy.json`, address `accuracy#svm`) breaks no rule, so it causes no split. `INVALID_SYNTAX`'s "every record is well-formed JSON" (1097) uses the same quantifier.

**Why it matters.** Two conforming checkers emit different codes for the same repository. One stays silent while broken or stale record files pile up. A conformance suite can't test either behaviour, because neither is specified.

**Suggested fix.** In `INVALID_RECORD`, `MISPLACED_RECORD`, `MISSING_RECORD_INPUTS` and the record clause of `INVALID_SYNTAX`, change "Every record" to "the record at each planned result's place". Then either state that a checker ignores records at no planned place, or add a check-layer SHOULD with its own code for them.

### F102. §2 says the check layer sees records and results, but four of the seven check rules need `astra.yaml`, the stack and git

- **Lens:** Implementer · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:82`, `docs/specification.md:92-93`, `docs/specification.md:780-781`, `docs/specification.md:788-789`, `docs/specification.md:945-947`, `docs/specification.md:1179`, `docs/specification.md:1182-1185`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:291-292`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:305`

**What is wrong.** §2 lists the layers "set apart by what each one sees" (75). For the check layer it gives only "records and results" (82), and the checker role says it "reads an analysis's records and results" (92-93). The rules in §9 need more than that:

- `MISSING_RECORD` (1179) covers "every planned result". Planned results are what "an analysis's outputs and universes call for" (849-850), and "the results of a driven analysis belong to its driver" (852-853). So the checker needs `astra.yaml`, the stack and the effective selections.
- `MISSING_RECORD_INPUTS` (1182) needs "each entry its output lists". That list is in `astra.yaml`.
- `STALE_RESULT` (1183) compares the declaration "since the commit in a result's record". A record holds none of it: "the output's declaration, the options of its decisions and its format are in the `astra.yaml` files at that commit" (945-947). §7 lists this read itself: "the `astra.yaml` of a result's analysis at the commit the result's record names" (780-781). That needs git history.
- `STALE_EVIDENCE` (1185) covers "the evidence of the analysis it is run on" (1019). Evidence is in the insights of `astra.yaml`.

The results of an analysis from a commit are checked "through their records at the declared commit" (1187-1189), in that analysis's repository (813-815). The design states the dependency at design:291-292 ("That depends on `astra.yaml`, so it's a check rule"), yet repeats the same table row at design:305. The row doesn't say "only", unlike the validator row (81), but as an overview it understates. §7 and §8 make the real needs clear to a careful reader.

**Evidence.** The quoted lines above. The contract confirms that a record carries no declaration:

```
$ jq -c '{record:(.properties|keys), defs:(."$defs"|with_entries(.value=(.value.properties//{}|keys)))}' src/astra/schema/generated/provenance.schema.json
{"record":["address","content_hash","generated_by","size","spec_version"],"defs":{"Environment":["iri"],"Generation":["commit","environment","finished","inputs","runner","started"],"RecordedInput":["content_hash","entry","size"],"Runner":["id","version"]}}
```

A valid record with `generated_by.declaration` added is rejected by jsonschema and by Ajv (`UNKNOWN_FIELD` at `/generated_by`, `.../$defs/Generation/additionalProperties`).

**Why it matters.** An implementer who designs a checker from §2 builds one that runs on a results artefact alone, for example in CI without the git history. That checker can't apply `MISSING_RECORD`, `MISSING_RECORD_INPUTS`, `STALE_RESULT` or `STALE_EVIDENCE`. The design's done criterion is that the specification is enough to build a checker from (design:42-43). Also, 788-789 says "validators and runners can work offline from what they kept" but leaves out checkers, which read 780-781 too.

**Suggested fix.** Make the check row "every `astra.yaml` of the stack, now and at each record's commit, and git (its history and remotes); records and results", and the role at 92-93 to match. Change design:305 the same way, and add checkers to 788-789.

### F103. An input is "committed" in the prose but "tracked in git" in `INPUT_NOT_TRACKED`, and the spec never says which git state the validator reads

- **Lens:** Implementer · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:344`, `docs/specification.md:356-359`, `docs/specification.md:1131`, `docs/specification.md:81`, `src/astra/schema/analysis.yaml:141`

**What is wrong.** The concept and the field definition call an input a *committed* file. The rule that checks it says the file is *tracked* in git. In git these are two states. A staged, uncommitted file is tracked (it is in the index) but is not in HEAD. The reverse also exists: a file in HEAD that was then removed with `git rm --cached` is committed, but `git ls-files` and `git status` treat it as untracked. §2 says only that the validator "sees ... git", without saying whether that means the index or HEAD. The spec copies this split unchanged from RFC 0006 (lines 24 and 55 say "committed", the rule at line 86 says "tracked"), so it is not a departure from the RFC. Design §11, §12 and §13 do not mention it.

This is a wording defect. §9 is "the only place this specification states a rule that a tool checks or keeps" (`spec:1025-1026`), and its rule uses git's own term, "tracked". So a careful reader ends up checking the index. A reader has to work this out, though, because the bullet at 356-359 puts both words side by side.

**Evidence.**

- `docs/specification.md:344`: "An [Input](elements/Input.md) is a file committed with the analysis, named by"
- `docs/specification.md:356-359`: "What is committed is the file itself, or something standing in for it ... The file, or what stands in for it, is tracked in git ([`INPUT_NOT_TRACKED`](#validator))."
- `docs/specification.md:1131`: "| `INPUT_NOT_TRACKED` | MUST | An input's file, or what stands in for it, is tracked in git | 0006 |"
- `src/astra/schema/analysis.yaml:141` (`Input`): "A file the analysis reads, committed with it and named by its path."
- `docs/specification.md:81`: "| **validator** | a semantic validator | every `astra.yaml` of the analysis stack, and git; no results |"
- Pro Git §2.2: "Tracked files are files that were in the last snapshot, as well as any newly staged files".

Tested in a scratch repository with git 2.56.0:

```sh
printf 'y\n2\n' > data/new.csv && git add data/new.csv      # staged, not committed
git ls-files --error-unmatch data/new.csv; echo "ls-files exit=$?"
git cat-file -e HEAD:data/new.csv; echo "cat-file HEAD exit=$?"
```

```
data/new.csv
ls-files exit=0
fatal: path 'data/new.csv' exists on disk, but not in 'HEAD'
cat-file HEAD exit=128
```

**Why it matters.** It bites validator implementers. One that checks HEAD (the prose's "committed") reports `INPUT_NOT_TRACKED` for a newly staged input. One that checks the index (the rule's "tracked") accepts it. A HEAD check would also reject every new input when run from a pre-commit hook. The harm stops at the validator's verdict on a working tree that is mid-edit. `UNCOMMITTED_CHANGES` (`spec:1196`) keeps a runner from running while the staged file is pending, so no result is ever made from an uncommitted input.

**Suggested fix.** Pick one git state and use one word for it everywhere. For example, make the rule say "is tracked in git (in its index; it needn't be committed yet)" and use "tracked" or "kept in git" at 344, 356 and `analysis.yaml:141`. Or say "committed (in HEAD)" everywhere, rule included. Record the choice in the tidy-up RFC.

### F104. The Terms table glosses "built per universe" as one result per universe, but an output with a `when` has none where it is inactive

- **Lens:** Implementer · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:44`, `docs/specification.md:482-485`, `docs/specification.md:596-598`, `docs/specification.md:849-850`

**What is wrong.** The Terms table says an output built per universe has "one result per universe". Read literally, that is a result in every universe. §6 also counts an output with a `when` as built per universe, and §5 says an inactive output isn't built in that universe. So an output can be built per universe and still have no result in some universes. RFC 0003 says the same thing: with a `when`, "whether it exists depends on the universe". The normative text in §5 and §6 is right. Only the one-line gloss is wrong, and it is the one place in the specification that states a count. This is a wording defect.

**Evidence.** Quotes at commit 96c902a:

- `docs/specification.md:44`: "| built per universe, built once | whether an output has one result per universe or one in all | §6 |"
- `docs/specification.md:596-598` (§6): "An output is **built per universe** when it lists `decisions`, has a `when`, or has an unpinned entry of `inputs` that names an output built per universe. Any other output is **built once**: it has one result, the same in every universe."
- `docs/specification.md:482-485` (§5): "An inactive output isn't built in that universe: nothing reads it there without a pin ([`INPUT_NOT_BUILT`]), and no pin names it there ([`PIN_INACTIVE`])."
- `rfcs/0003-cross-universe-artifact-references.md:195-197`: "An output is *built per universe* if it declares `decisions`, has a `when` (whether it exists depends on the universe), ..."
- `docs/specification.md:849-850` (§8): "The results an analysis's outputs and universes call for are its **planned** results." This doesn't mention activation.

A minimal document that both validators accept:

```yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
decisions:
  correction:
    options: {none: {}, calibrated: {}}
outputs:
  calibrated_table:
    type: table
    format: csv
    when: [correction.calibrated]
universes:
  u_none:
    decisions: {correction: none}
  u_cal:
    decisions: {correction: calibrated}
```

```
$ uv run python $TOOLKIT/validate.py Analysis when-output.yaml
VALID
$ node $TOOLKIT/js/validate.mjs <repo> Analysis when-output.yaml
VALID
```

`calibrated_table` has a `when`, so §6 makes it built per universe. It is inactive in `u_none`, so §5 says it isn't built there. It has one result, in `u_cal`, out of two universes. The gloss gives it a result in both.

**Why it matters.** It affects a checker author who takes the planned set from the summary. §8's definition of planned results doesn't leave out the universes where an output is inactive. A checker that applies `MISSING_RECORD` (`docs/specification.md:1179`, "Every planned result has a record") gets the right set only by also reading §5. One that reads the gloss first expects a record for `calibrated_table` in `u_none`, and reports `MISSING_RECORD` there.

**Suggested fix.** Line 44: "whether an output has one result in each universe where it is active, or one in all". Optionally, line 850 could also say that an output plans no result in a universe where it is inactive.

### F105. §10's "innermost errors" sentence treats jsonschema and Ajv as one, but they report different errors

- **Lens:** Implementer · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:1303-1313`, `docs/specification.md:1323-1326`, `tools/rules.py:410`

**What is wrong.** This is a wording problem. Both validators reach the same codes, but §10 describes them in one sentence that fits neither of them exactly:

> `jsonschema` and Ajv report the innermost errors: each names the keyword that failed, either an assertion (such as `pattern`, `required` or `additionalProperties: false`) or a `oneOf`, `not`, `if` or `propertyNames` that fails as a whole. Each such error has an entry, including those that say why a `oneOf` failed. (`docs/specification.md:1305-1309`)

Read strictly, "innermost" is wrong for both validators:

- jsonschema 4.26 never reports an `if` error or a `propertyNames` error. It reports the errors inside the `then` and inside the `propertyNames` subschema. For a failing `oneOf` it reports the `oneOf` error only, and the branch errors that "say why a `oneOf` failed" sit in `error.context`. The jsonschema bullet (1323-1326) never mentions `.context`.
- Ajv reports both levels: the inner error together with the `if`, `propertyNames` or `oneOf` above it.

The repository already records the first point: `tools/rules.py:410` says the `if` error is reported only "by Ajv, when its `then` fails". The spec does not say this.

**Evidence.** jsonschema 4.26.0, `.venv/lib/python3.12/site-packages/jsonschema/_keywords.py`:

```
28: def propertyNames(validator, propertyNames, instance, schema):
33:         yield from validator.descend(instance=property, schema=propertyNames)
361:         yield ValidationError(
363:             context=all_errors,
382: def if_(validator, if_schema, instance, schema):
386:             yield from validator.descend(instance, then, schema_path="then")
```

Both validators were run on every invalid fixture:

```
Analysis-102/103/140/141/177: jsonschema 1         | Ajv 2  (pattern + propertyNames)
Analysis-148/164:             jsonschema 1 (+2 ctx) | Ajv 3  (oneOf + 2 required)
Analysis-149:                 jsonschema 1         | Ajv 2  (then/required + if)
Analysis-165:                 jsonschema 1         | Ajv 2  (then/not + if)
fixtures compared=95; different count=9; (pointer, code) sets differ in: none
```

A document with one capitalised key in each of the nine id-keyed maps gives 9 errors in jsonschema and 18 in Ajv. Every one of them is `INVALID_ID`. Two bad keys in one map give 2 errors in jsonschema and 4 in Ajv. Of the `errors` entries in `rules.json`, the 2 `…/if` entries and the 9 `…/propertyNames` entries are reached only through Ajv.

**Why it matters.** The mapping works. On all 95 schema-layer fixtures, both validators give the same set of (instance location, code) pairs. But a Python implementer who reads §10 will wait for `if` and `propertyNames` errors that jsonschema never reports. They will also not learn that a `oneOf`'s branch errors are in `.context`. Python-based and JS-based tools, and their tests, also report different numbers of diagnostics for the same file: 1 against 2 or 3, or n against 2n for n bad keys in one map.

**Suggested fix.** Describe each validator in its own sentence. jsonschema reports the inner errors of a `then` and of a `propertyNames` subschema, and reports a `oneOf` error with its branch errors in `.context`. Ajv reports both levels. Optionally, add that both validators give the same set of (instance location, code) pairs.

### F106. §10 gives "leaves out the `if` of a failing `then`" as a reason the schema path is not the location, but it is not one

- **Lens:** Implementer · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:1323-1326`, `tests/conftest.py:200-201`

**What is wrong.** §10's note on Python's `jsonschema` says:

> An error's `absolute_schema_path` runs through each `$ref` without naming it (and leaves out the `if` of a failing `then`), so it is not the location. (`docs/specification.md:1323-1325`)

The "so" makes both clauses reasons why the path differs from the absolute keyword location. Only the first one is. `jsonschema` 4.x does leave `if` out of the path (`validators.py:391-392`: `if k not in {"if", "$ref"}: error.schema_path.appendleft(k)`). But `then` is a sibling of `if`, so `if` never appears in the JSON Pointer to `…/then/<keyword>`. Leaving the `if` out is what makes the `then` part of the path *match* the location. Only the unnamed `$ref` makes them differ.

The sentence also misses the real `if`-related difference between validators. For a failing `then`, Ajv reports a second error at `…/if` ("must match \"then\" schema"). `jsonschema` reports no `if` error at all. `rules.json` gives both locations the same code, so a tool's verdict does not change.

The harness docstring repeats the claim: "(and leaves out an `if` before its `then`), so it isn't that place" (`tests/conftest.py:200-201`).

**Evidence.** `jsonschema` 4.26.0, `absolute_schema_path` printed next to the harness's `absolute_keyword_location`:

```
[minimal]  absolute_schema_path: then/required
           location: https://example.org/s.json#/then/required
[via $ref] absolute_schema_path: properties/a/then/required
           location: https://example.org/s2.json#/$defs/D/then/required
[analysis] pointer: /analyses/other
           absolute_schema_path: properties/analyses/additionalProperties/then/required
           location: https://w3id.org/astra/analysis.schema.json#/$defs/AnalysisDeclaration__identifier_optional/then/required
```

The minimal schema is `{"$id": "https://example.org/s.json", "if": {"required": ["url"]}, "then": {"required": ["commit"]}}`. The `[analysis]` case is an `analyses` entry with a `url` and no `commit` (as in `tests/data/invalid/Analysis-149-url-without-commit.yaml`). With no `$ref` the path is the location. With a `$ref` the two differ only by the `$ref` (`properties/analyses/additionalProperties` holds `{"$ref": "#/$defs/AnalysisDeclaration__identifier_optional"}`).

On the same document, Ajv reports both `…/then/required` and `…/if`, each `URL_WITHOUT_COMMIT`. `jsonschema` reports only `…/then/required`.

**Why it matters.** An implementer of a Python validator who builds locations from `absolute_schema_path` may "correct" then-paths by inserting `if`. Those locations have no entry in `rules.json`. The method §10 prescribes (find `error.schema` by identity, append `error.validator`) is correct, and the repository's harness uses it, so the contract is unaffected. Only the explanation misleads.

**Suggested fix.** Drop the parenthetical, so that only the `$ref` is the reason. Optionally state the real difference: "`jsonschema` reports a failing `then` only by its inner keyword; Ajv also reports the `if`, which has its own entry." Make the same change in the `tests/conftest.py` docstring.

### F107. A missing commit also breaks MISSING_DECLARED_ANALYSIS, and the spec doesn't say whether a validator reports both

- **Lens:** Implementer · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:89-90`, `docs/specification.md:653-657`, `docs/specification.md:1119`, `docs/specification.md:1125`, `docs/specification.md:1126`, `docs/specification.md:1128`

**What is wrong.** MISSING_DECLARED_ANALYSIS is worded so that it is false whenever the commit can't be read. A declaration whose `commit` the repository lacks breaks COMMIT_NOT_FOUND. A `url` whose remote lacks the commit breaks REMOTE_UNREACHABLE. In both cases the declaration also breaks MISSING_DECLARED_ANALYSIS. §2 says a validator reports every rule broken, so read literally a conforming validator reports both codes. The prose at 653-657, however, lists REMOTE_UNREACHABLE, MISSING_DECLARED_ANALYSIS and DECLARED_ANALYSIS_INVALID as if they were stages of one question. That reading suggests one code per fault. The spec never says whether a rule about something that can't be read is broken or doesn't apply. The cascade goes further: every address through such a declaration also breaks UNRESOLVED_ADDRESS (1119), and DECLARED_ANALYSIS_INVALID (1129) arguably counts as broken too.

**Evidence.** The rows, `docs/specification.md`:

- 1125: `MISSING_DECLARED_ANALYSIS`, MUST: "A declaration's `path` (at its `commit`, if it has one), or the top level of its `url`'s repository at its `commit`, holds an `astra.yaml`"
- 1126: `COMMIT_NOT_FOUND`, MUST: "A declaration with a `path` and a `commit` names a commit the repository has"
- 1128: `REMOTE_UNREACHABLE`, MUST: "A declaration's `url` can be fetched and has its `commit`"
- 89-90: a validator "reports every parse, schema and validator rule the analysis breaks."

A minimal document. It is schema-valid (`VALID` under both the Python and the Ajv validators). `sub/astra.yaml` is committed at HEAD, and `upstream/` holds a committed `astra.yaml`:

```yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
analyses:
  nf: {path: sub, commit: "0000000000000000000000000000000000000000"}
  rm: {url: "file://$W/upstream", commit: "1111111111111111111111111111111111111111"}
outputs:
  result: {type: metric, format: json, inputs: [nf.x, rm.x]}
```

```
$ git cat-file -e 0000000000000000000000000000000000000000^{commit}
fatal: Not a valid object name 0000…^{commit}                          # COMMIT_NOT_FOUND
$ git cat-file -e 0000000000000000000000000000000000000000:sub/astra.yaml
fatal: path 'sub/astra.yaml' exists on disk, but not in '0000…'       # 1125 false too
$ git fetch --depth=1 file://$W/upstream 1111111111111111111111111111111111111111
fatal: git upload-pack: not our ref 1111…                              # REMOTE_UNREACHABLE
```

The overlap comes from the RFCs: RFC 0004:402 ("holds an `astra.yaml`") together with RFC 0007:195, 198 and 200 ("Every other rule of RFC 0004 applies to analyses from commits as it is"). Neither design §12 nor §13 records it.

**Why it matters.** Every reading fails the file with a MUST error, so no invalid analysis passes. The set of codes reported for one fault differs, though. A validator that follows the literal text reports COMMIT_NOT_FOUND, MISSING_DECLARED_ANALYSIS and an UNRESOLVED_ADDRESS per address. A careful one treats the later rules as undetermined and reports only COMMIT_NOT_FOUND. The future validator conformance suite, keyed on codes, will hit this first. The same gap shows elsewhere in §9, for example MISSING_INPUT_FILE and INPUT_NOT_TRACKED (1130-1131).

**Suggested fix.** Add one sentence to §2 or §9 that settles it. Either a validator doesn't report a rule whose subject another broken rule leaves undetermined, or every such code is reported. Then scope 1125 (and UNRESOLVED_ADDRESS through that declaration) to match.

### F108. `INPUT_NOT_BUILT` doesn't say that only active outputs read

- **Lens:** Implementer · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:1144`, `docs/specification.md:729-730`, `docs/specification.md:482-485`, `docs/specification.md:615`, `rfcs/0004-composition.md:412`

**What is wrong.** The §9 row says "Every output read without a pin is active (its `when` holds) in the universe being built". It doesn't say who does the reading. A validator can check the rule in two ways. It can check every unpinned entry of every output in every universe (the static reading). Or it can check only the outputs that are built in that universe (the build-time reading). Two outputs that share a `when`, one reading the other, tell the two apart. This is a wording gap, not a contradiction: the intended reading can be pieced together from §5 and §6, but the row and §7, which an implementer works from, don't state it. The spec copies the gap from RFC 0004:412.

**Evidence.** A minimal document:

```yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
label: Shared condition
decisions:
  correction:
    options: {none: {}, calibrated: {}}
outputs:
  curve:
    type: data
    format: csv
    when: [correction.calibrated]
  calibrated_table:
    type: table
    format: csv
    when: [correction.calibrated]
    inputs: [curve]
universes:
  cal:
    decisions: {correction: calibrated}
  raw:
    decisions: {correction: none}
```

It passes the parse and schema layers in both toolkit validators (`VALID`). `INPUT_NOT_BUILT` is a validator-layer rule, so the question is how to read the text. In `raw`, `curve` and `calibrated_table` are both inactive, and `calibrated_table` has the unpinned entry `curve`.

- The static reading follows from the row (spec:1144), from §7 (spec:729-730: "every output read without a pin, of the analysis itself or of another, is active in the universe being built") and from §6 (spec:615: "An unpinned entry of `inputs` reads its output in the universe being built."). Under it, the document fails with `INPUT_NOT_BUILT`.
- The build-time reading follows from §5 (spec:482-484: "An inactive output isn't built in that universe: nothing reads it there without a pin"). `calibrated_table` isn't built in `raw`, so it reads nothing there, and the document is valid.

The neighbouring rows do say which elements count: `MISSING_DECISION` says "every active decision" (spec:1135). No spec example, file under `examples/` or valid fixture has a conditional output reading a conditional output without a pin. The only conditional output with inputs, `bias` in `tests/data/valid/Analysis-002-core.yaml`, reads through a pin.

**Why it matters.** A validator that loops over universes and unpinned entries takes the static reading. It then rejects every chain of outputs that share a `when`, in every universe where the `when` fails. Extending the §5 example's `calibrated_table` with an upstream conditional output does exactly that.

**Suggested fix.** Reword spec:1144 and spec:729-730 (and, to match, the §5 sentence): "Every output read without a pin by an output active in the universe being built is active there." Record the clarification against RFC 0004:412 in the tidy-up RFC.

### F109. §8 names a result by "the declarations that lead to it" without saying driven, so an undriven second declaration gives a second name

- **Lens:** Implementer · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:855-858`, `docs/specification.md:40`, `docs/specification.md:664-665`, `docs/specification.md:732-737`, `docs/specification.md:305-306`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:784-789`

**What is wrong.** §8 defines a result's name as "the output's id after the ids of the declarations that lead to it". It does not say the declarations must be driven. Under spec:664, a driven declaration and an undriven one of the same directory lead to the same analysis. §7 recommends exactly that pattern for reading a driven analysis's own universes (spec:735-737). Read alone, §8 then gives a result built in the driver's universe two names and two record paths.

The specification does answer the question when its passages are read together. A record's `address` is "its result as the analysis the result belongs to addresses it" (spec:305-306). A pin through an undriven declaration names that analysis's own universe (spec:732-735). So only the chain of driven declarations addresses the result. The answer is spread over §4, §7 and §8, though, and the defining sentence and the glossary row (spec:40) leave it out. This is a wording problem.

**Evidence.** spec:855-856:

> "the result's **name** is the output's id after the ids of the declarations that lead to it (`subanalysis1.output1`)"

spec:664: "The same directory is the same analysis, whichever analysis declares it and by whatever id". spec:732-734: "A pin `#u` names, when its reference goes through a driven declaration, the universe `u` of the driver, and otherwise the universe `u` of the analysis whose output it is".

A minimal stack. The root declares `prep: {path: prep}` undriven and `fit: {path: fit}`. `fit` declares `pre: {path: ../prep}`. The root's `u1` sets `fit.method: ols, fit.pre.scaling: standard`. `prep` has universes `v1` and `v2` only. The root's `report` reads `fit.model`, which reads `pre.clean`, so prep's `clean` is planned in `u1`. By §8 alone it has two names, `prep.clean` and `fit.pre.clean`. Both candidate records validate:

```
$ uv run python toolkit/validate.py ProvenanceRecord .astra/records/u1/prep.clean.json .astra/records/u1/fit.pre.clean.json   -> VALID x2
$ node toolkit/js/validate.mjs <repo> ProvenanceRecord (same 2 records)                                                      -> VALID x2
```

Read by spec:732-734, `prep.clean#u1` names prep's own `u1`, which does not exist. Only `fit.pre.clean#u1` names the root's result. §7's own example has the same shape (`inner` and `inner_undriven`, spec:741-742).

The design's open point "One result, two names" (design:784-789) covers only "two driven declarations". Its tentative tie-break, "the name through the fewest declarations, then the first in key order", picks the undriven `prep.clean` here if it is applied over all declarations.

**Why it matters.** `MISSING_RECORD` (spec:1179) and `MISPLACED_RECORD` (spec:1181) work from the name. A runner or checker author who reads §8 alone, or applies the design's tie-break to every declaration, writes or looks for `u1/prep.clean.json`. One who follows §4 and §7 uses `u1/fit.pre.clean.json`. Each then reports the other's record as missing or misplaced. The schemas can't catch this, because both records are valid.

**Suggested fix.** At spec:855-856 (and the glossary row at spec:40), say "the ids of the driven declarations that lead to it", and add that an undriven declaration of the same directory names only that analysis's own universes (cite spec:732). When the open point at design:784-789 is settled, limit its tie-break to driven declarations.

### F110. "Editing a finding leaves a result unchanged" is false when an input names the analysis's own `astra.yaml`

- **Lens:** Implementer · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:995-996`, `docs/specification.md:985-994`, `docs/specification.md:1183`, `docs/specification.md:1130-1132`, `docs/specification.md:901-903`, `rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:184`

**What is wrong.** An input may name the analysis's own `astra.yaml`. No schema or validator rule excludes it. The normative rule, `STALE_RESULT`, says a result is stale when its inputs' content changes. An edit to a finding, a description or another output changes the bytes of `astra.yaml`, so the result goes stale. The summary sentence after the §8 list says the opposite, without qualification. The rule decides the case and checkers that follow it agree. The defect is a summary sentence that says too much. The spec copies the sentence word for word from RFC 0005 §4.

**Evidence.** A minimal document, VALID under both `validate.py` and `validate.mjs` (Ajv):

```yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
inputs:
  analysis_doc:
    path: astra.yaml
outputs:
  paper:
    type: report
    format: pdf
    inputs: [analysis_doc]
    recipe:
      command: render {inputs.analysis_doc} -o {output}
findings:
  f1:
    claim: The paper renders the analysis.
    evidence:
      - artifact: paper
```

In a scratch git repo with the file committed, `MISSING_INPUT_FILE`, `INPUT_NOT_TRACKED` and `INPUT_IN_ANOTHER_ANALYSIS` ("An input's file is outside every other analysis's directory", `docs/specification.md:1132`) all hold. Committing it as C1, then changing only `f1.claim` in C2:

```
C1 hash(astra.yaml)=sha256:4b0d3ee676aaefd0b3bfb0d6d194aad7b545c171cf678c56db2fb26ef1ec9055
-    claim: The paper renders the analysis.
+    claim: The paper renders the whole analysis.
C2 hash(astra.yaml)=sha256:a022661ddc8c8d4a957b78409932b8a46a7f405f46c57af96605087ebc8a995a
```

A record for `paper` with `commit: C1` and that input's C1 hash is VALID. At C2 the input's content differs. The two passages of §8:

> "- the content of its inputs;" (`docs/specification.md:991`; catalogue row `:1183`: "its inputs' content")

> "Otherwise it is **stale**. Nothing else counts: editing a finding, a description or another output leaves a result unchanged." (`docs/specification.md:995-996`)

`rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:184` has the same sentence. The design (§11-13) does not mention the case. Reading `astra.yaml` without declaring it is allowed, since the commit "pins every file of the code and every `astra.yaml`" (`docs/specification.md:901-903`). That read leaves the result unchanged, so declaring the read is a choice the author makes, and the rules then give a stale result.

**Why it matters.** Authors are told that editing a finding never stales a result. A report that renders the analysis's own findings is a realistic output, and an author who declares `astra.yaml` as its input sees it go stale on every edit to a finding. A checker author who trusts the sentence over the list could add a special case for findings and break a MUST rule.

**Suggested fix.** Qualify `docs/specification.md:995-996`, for example: "editing a finding, a description or another output counts only where it changes something listed above (an `astra.yaml` named as an input, a result the output reads)". Forbidding `astra.yaml` as an input is the other option. The RFCs do not forbid it.

### F111. A key or address that loops back through declarations to the analysis's own decision is not among the listed forms

- **Lens:** Implementer · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:538-541`, `docs/specification.md:664-667`, `docs/specification.md:683`, `docs/specification.md:690-706`, `docs/specification.md:720-727`, `docs/specification.md:1140-1143`, `docs/specification.md:1151`, `src/astra/schema/analysis.yaml:325-328`

**What is wrong.** Declarations by `path` alone may form cycles (spec:666-667). A file can therefore reach its own elements through declarations: `self.model` with `self: {path: .}`, or `feature_extraction.iris.random_seed` through iris_pipeline's back-edge `iris: {path: ..}`. These addresses resolve by §4 (spec:281-288), so `UNRESOLVED_ADDRESS` does not fire. The key rule lists two forms only: the analysis's own decision id, or a flat key for "an analysis the universe drives". A loop-back key fits neither form. The "either spelling" permission (spec:701-702) covers only "an analysis reached through two driven declarations", so it does not reach the root's own decisions. The answer exists: spec:664-665 makes the same directory the same analysis, so a loop-back names the root's own element and is neither driving nor consuming. `CONFLICTING_SELECTIONS` (spec:1141) applies to every spelling. But "another analysis" in Driving, Consuming, `UNDRIVEN_DECLARATION` and the `NO_UNIVERSES` exemption is never glossed as "a different directory". A reader has to piece the answer together from 664, §4, the catalogue and RFC 0004:221.

**Evidence.** Both validators accept every file. The question is for the validator layer.

```yaml
# self2/astra.yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
analyses: {self: {path: .}}
decisions: {model: {options: {svm: {}, rf: {}}}}
outputs: {accuracy: {type: metric, format: json, decisions: [model]}}
universes:
  u1: {decisions: {model: svm}}
  u2: {decisions: {self.model: rf}}
```

