```
self.model                          -> dir '.', element 'model'; LOOPS BACK to the file
feature_extraction.iris.random_seed -> dir '.', element 'random_seed'; LOOPS BACK to the file
b.c.a.m                             -> dir '.', element 'm'; LOOPS BACK to the file
```

When the key form is read as closed, `self2` and an iris_pipeline universe that sets `random_seed` only as `feature_extraction.iris.random_seed` lose that selection and give `MISSING_DECISION`. When the key is read as a spelling, both are valid. When the key is read per declaration, `self` or `iris` counts as driven, and the result is `PARTIALLY_DRIVEN`. The unmodified iris_pipeline is valid under every reading.

- spec:538-540: "A key is the id of a decision of the analysis or, for a decision of an analysis the universe drives, its address, `<declaration>.<decision>`, a flat key (§7)."
- analysis.yaml:325-327: "Each key is the id of a decision of the analysis or, for a decision of an analysis it drives, a flat key"
- spec:664-665: "The same directory is the same analysis, whichever analysis declares it and by whatever id"
- spec:690: "**Driving** is setting another analysis's decisions from one's own universes"
- RFC 0004:221: "Whether the decision is defined via the whole address or a top-level declaration doesn't matter since the same directory is always the same analysis"

**Why it matters.** Validator authors in astra-tools and astra-typescript have to rebuild the answer before they can tell whether a loop-back spelling of a root decision is allowed, ignored or an error. Authors of stacks with back-edges, the layout iris_pipeline uses, face the same question. The case is an edge case, and a careful reading lands on "accept as a spelling".

**Suggested fix.** After spec:664, add one sentence: an address reaching the analysis's own directory through declarations by `path` alone names its own element, and is neither driving nor consuming. In spec:538-540 and analysis.yaml:325-328, add "or its address through declarations that lead back to it".

### F112. §4 says an output lists each input, decision and condition once, but two spellings of one element pass

- **Lens:** Implementer · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:269-271`, `docs/specification.md:1111`, `docs/specification.md:664-667`, `docs/specification.md:701-703`, `docs/specification.md:413`, `docs/specification.md:1182`

**What is wrong.** §4 draws a conclusion from `DUPLICATE_ENTRY` that the rule does not support:

> No list of strings holds the same entry twice ([`DUPLICATE_ENTRY`](#parse-and-schema)), so an output lists each of its inputs, decisions and conditions once. (`docs/specification.md:270-271`)

`DUPLICATE_ENTRY` is `uniqueItems`, and it compares strings (§9, `:1111`). The spec itself gives one element several addresses: "The same directory is the same analysis, whichever analysis declares it and by whatever id" (`:664-665`), and a decision "can have a decision set under either spelling (`stage.inner.d` or `inner.d`)" (`:701-702`). No validator rule in §9 rejects two entries of one `inputs`, `decisions` or `when` list that resolve to the same element. Read as elements, the sentence is false. Read as strings, it only restates the rule. The §9 row is worded correctly.

**Evidence.** Two declarations of one directory are enough. No cycle is needed (`c/stage/astra.yaml` has one output, `features`):

```yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
analyses:
  a: {path: stage}
  b: {path: stage}
outputs:
  report:
    type: metric
    format: json
    inputs: [a.features, b.features]
```

A self-declaration does the same for decisions and conditions:

```yaml
analyses:
  self: {path: .}
decisions:
  model: {options: {a: {}, b: {}}}
outputs:
  accuracy: {type: metric, format: json, decisions: [model]}
  twice:
    type: metric
    format: json
    inputs: [accuracy, self.accuracy, self.self.accuracy]
    decisions: [model, self.model]
    when: [model.a, self.model.a]
```

The shipped `examples/iris_pipeline`, with only classification's `predictions` changed, also passes:

```
>     inputs: [feature_extraction.features, iris.feature_extraction.features, iris.split, feature_extraction.iris.split]
>     decisions: [classifier, iris.classification.classifier]
```

The Python (`jsonschema`) and Ajv validators accept all of these. The control `inputs: [accuracy, accuracy]` is rejected with `DUPLICATE_ENTRY` at `/outputs/twice/inputs`. A path resolver resolves each pair to one element, for example `iris.split` and `feature_extraction.iris.split` both to `'..'`, `split`.

**Why it matters.** A validator author who reads §4 assumes duplicates are handled, so no tool checks for aliases. A recipe then gets one file twice through `{inputs}`, which passes "the paths of all the output's inputs, in the order of `inputs`" (`:413`). `MISSING_RECORD_INPUTS` (`:1182`) then requires the record to list that one result twice. This is the harm given as the motivation for tidy-up change 24: "A recipe would get the input twice and RFC-0005's record would have to list it twice" (`astra-rfcs/.../NNNN-settling-the-schema-for-v0.1.md:211`). No defined behaviour depends on the element reading, since placeholders, `{inputs}` and records all work on entries as written. So the defect is in the wording. Design §13's "Agreeing spellings" covers only `Universe.decisions`, not these lists.

**Suggested fix.** Reword to "so no output lists the same entry twice; two spellings of one element are two entries." A validator rule or warning for aliased entries would be a separate design addition.

### F113. A declaration is defined as "another analysis", but a declaration of the analysis itself (`path: .`) is valid

- **Lens:** Implementer · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `src/astra/schema/analysis.yaml:353-356`, `src/astra/schema/analysis.yaml:464-466`, `docs/specification.md:626`, `docs/specification.md:664-668`, `docs/specification.md:1124-1125`, `rfcs/0007-analyses-in-other-repositories.md:68-76`

**What is wrong.** This is a wording problem. The normative descriptions say a declaration names *another* analysis. A declaration by `path` alone of the declaring analysis's own directory (`path: .`, `path: sub/..`) names the analysis itself. The RFCs allow such a declaration, the contract accepts it, and no rule in §9 rejects it. Its meaning follows from §7's general sentence "The same directory is the same analysis", so `self.x` names the analysis's own `x`. But a reader has to combine that sentence with §9's "only place this specification states a rule" (`docs/specification.md:1025`) to see that the "another" wording is loose rather than a rule.

**Evidence.** A minimal document:

```yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
analyses:
  self:
    path: .
outputs:
  accuracy: {type: metric, format: json}
  report:
    type: report
    format: pdf
    inputs: [self.accuracy]
```

```
$ uv run python .../toolkit/validate.py Analysis a/astra.yaml b/astra.yaml   # b: path: sub/..
VALID
$ node .../toolkit/js/validate.mjs <repo> Analysis a/astra.yaml b/astra.yaml
VALID
```

The only constraint the schema puts on `path` is the pattern `^(?![/~])[^\x00-\x1f\x7f\\]+$(?!\n)`, and `.` matches it.

- `analysis.yaml:354`: "Another analysis that this analysis uses, found by exactly one of `path` and `url`."
- `analysis.yaml:465-466`: "The other analyses this analysis uses, keyed by the id it addresses them by."
- `specification.md:626`: "An analysis uses another by declaring it in `analyses`".
- `specification.md:664-667`: "The same directory is the same analysis, whichever analysis declares it and by whatever id ... Declarations by `path` alone can form cycles".
- `specification.md:1124-1125`: `DECLARATION_OUTSIDE_REPOSITORY` and `MISSING_DECLARED_ANALYSIS` both hold for `path: .`.
- RFC 0004 allows cycles ("There is no problem in making cyclic declarations", `rfcs/0004-composition.md:75`). RFC 0007 uses `path: .`, but only with a `commit` (`rfcs/0007:68-76`).

**Why it matters.** A validator author who reads the field descriptions may reject `self: {path: .}`, but with no code to report. An author who reads §7 accepts it. Authors and agents get no signal that the declaration adds nothing. The loop-back addressing it allows already arises through the `..` cycles that RFC 0004 sanctions, for example in `examples/iris_pipeline/feature_extraction/astra.yaml:12`, so this finding adds no defect beyond the wording.

**Suggested fix.** In §7, after "The same directory is the same analysis…", say that a declaration by `path` alone of the declaring analysis's own directory names the analysis itself. Or soften "Another analysis" in the `AnalysisDeclaration` description to "An analysis".

### F114. `Output.decisions` says "depends on" but not in which universe, and doesn't say whether decisions reached through inputs belong in it

- **Lens:** Author · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:387-389`, `src/astra/schema/analysis.yaml:204-206`, `docs/specification.md:596-602`, `examples/iris/astra.yaml:41-42`, `examples/iris/astra.yaml:83-89`

**What is wrong.** The field is defined as "the decisions the output depends on: a different option for any of them can give a different result". Read literally, this takes in the decision that a pinned comparison compares. §8 says `comparison`'s result changes with `model`'s option (`:1000-1001`), yet §2 lists no decision for it (`:131-138`). Listing the decision is valid, and by §6 it makes the comparison built per universe. The intended reading is "the option selected in the universe being built" (`:412`, `:685-686`). That can be pieced together from §2, §4 and §6, but the definition doesn't say so. The definition also doesn't say whether to list decisions that reach an output only through its unpinned inputs, and the examples answer this both ways.

**Evidence.** The §2 example cut down, with `comparison` given `decisions: [model]` and cited without a pin:

```yaml
  comparison:
    type: table
    format: csv
    inputs: [accuracy#forest, accuracy#svm]
    decisions: [model]
    ...
findings:
  svm_scores_higher:
    claim: The SVM scores higher than the random forest.
    evidence:
      - artifact: comparison
```

```
$ uv run python toolkit/validate.py Analysis comparison-lists-decision.yaml
VALID
$ node toolkit/js/validate.mjs <repo> Analysis comparison-lists-decision.yaml
VALID
```

By `docs/specification.md:596` ("built per universe when it lists `decisions`"), `comparison` now has two results, `comparison#forest` and `comparison#svm`, at `results/{forest,svm}/comparison.csv`, from the same pinned inputs. The unpinned citation now draws `UNPINNED_EVIDENCE` (`:519-521`, `:1150`).

The transitive case. In `examples/iris/astra.yaml`, `accuracy` and `f1_score` read `inputs: [trained_output]` and list `decisions: [scaling, model, test_size, random_seed]`, which their recipes never use. `conclusion` (`:83-89`) reads the same input and lists none. `examples/iris_pipeline/classification/astra.yaml:51-52` lists `classifier` for `accuracy` but not the other decisions that reach it through `predictions`.

**Why it matters.** It bites authors of comparisons between universes, the main use of pins. The definition doesn't tell them the compared decision stays out. If they list it, the comparison gets one result per universe, its addresses and layout change, and an unpinned finding that cites it gets a warning. For the unpinned case the two choices give the same validity, the same build multiplicity and the same staleness. That part is a clarity gap, but the repository's examples disagree on it.

**Suggested fix.** Define the field as the decisions whose option, in the universe being built, can change the result, and note that a pinned input fixes its own universe (§6). Say that listing a decision reached only through an unpinned input is allowed and changes nothing, or make the examples consistent.

### F115. A bad map key is reported at the map, and §10 does not say where a tool finds the key

- **Lens:** Author · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:1303-1313`, `docs/specification.md:1323-1335`, `tests/data/invalid/Analysis-103-option-key-with-space.yaml:2`

**What is wrong.** A map key that breaks the id grammar fails the map's `propertyNames`. JSON Pointer cannot address an object key, so both validators report the error at the map's pointer (`/decisions/gcm/options`), not at the key. The fixtures record this as intended (`# fails at: /decisions/d/options`). §10 explains how to turn an error into its code, and gives per-validator advice for `jsonschema` and Ajv. It does not say where the offending key is. In `jsonschema` it is `error.instance`, and the message names it. In Ajv it is the inner `pattern` error's `propertyName` (and its `data` under `verbose: true`), or `params.propertyName` of the `propertyNames` error. Ajv's default text names no key. This applies to all 9 `propertyNames` maps in `analysis.schema.json`.

**Evidence.** A minimal document with one bad option id among four:

```yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
outputs:
  result: {type: metric, format: json}
decisions:
  gcm:
    options:
      access_cm2: {}
      MPI-ESM1-2-HR: {}
      ukesm1: {}
      cesm2: {}
```

```
jsonschema: /decisions/gcm/options | .../propertyNames/pattern | INVALID_ID
            | 'MPI-ESM1-2-HR' does not match '^(?:^[a-z][a-z0-9_]*$(?!\\n))$'
            instance: 'MPI-ESM1-2-HR'
Ajv:        /decisions/gcm/options | .../propertyNames/pattern | INVALID_ID | must match pattern "..."
            /decisions/gcm/options | .../propertyNames         | INVALID_ID | property name must be valid
            {"keyword":"pattern",...,"propertyName":"MPI-ESM1-2-HR","data":"MPI-ESM1-2-HR"}
            {"keyword":"propertyNames","params":{"propertyName":"MPI-ESM1-2-HR"},...}
errorsText: data/decisions/gcm/options must match pattern "^(?:^[a-z][a-z0-9_]*$(?!\n))$",
            data/decisions/gcm/options property name must be valid
```

With two bad keys (`MPI-ESM1-2-HR`, `UKESM1-0-LL`), Ajv's `errorsText` prints the same pair of lines twice and names neither key.

§10's Ajv bullet (`docs/specification.md:1334-1335`) says only: "With `verbose: true`, an error's `parentSchema` is the subschema, and its `keyword` the keyword." Neither bullet mentions `instance` or `propertyName`.

**Why it matters.** astra-typescript and editor integrations built on Ajv's defaults report a key error as "property name must be valid" at the map. An author with four options learns only that some option id is wrong. Hyphenated or capitalised ids copied from domain names (CMIP6 source ids, SSP names) are a likely mistake. Both validators agree on the location and the code, so this is guidance to tools, not a contract defect.

**Suggested fix.** Add a sentence to §10 after the Ajv bullet: an error from a map's `propertyNames` is reported at the map's pointer; the key is `jsonschema`'s `error.instance`, or Ajv's `error.propertyName` (`params.propertyName` on the `propertyNames` error), and a tool shows it with the code.

### F116. No example in the specification shows a driving universe: the flat key is only in prose

- **Lens:** Author · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:536-540`, `docs/specification.md:690-693`, `docs/specification.md:739-752`

**What is wrong.** Driving is done by flat keys, `<declaration>.<decision>: <option>` in `Universe.decisions`. The specification states this form only in prose. None of its YAML examples has a flat key. The only example with `analyses:` (§7, lines 739-752) shows the declarations and the pinned reads, but no `universes:` block. It points at the driving universes only in comments, and its `inner.result#u1` names a root universe `u1` that the example never declares. The source RFCs have worked driving examples. The specification does not carry them over and does not link to `examples/`.

**Evidence.** The YAML fences of the specification are at lines 100, 465, 577 and 739. Parsed, their universes have only local keys (`model`, `scaling`; `mode`, `backend`, `gpu_optimization`). `grep -nE '[a-z_0-9]+\.[a-z_0-9]+(\.[a-z_0-9]+)*: ' docs/specification.md` finds nothing. The §7 block:

```
docs/specification.md:741:  inner: {path: stage/inner}            # the root's universes set inner.d
docs/specification.md:747:    inputs: [inner.result#u1]           # the root's universe u1
```

The form in prose, at lines 538-540 and 690-693:

> `<decision>: <option>`, such as `model: svm`. A key is the id of a decision of the analysis or, for a decision of an analysis the universe drives, its address, `<declaration>.<decision>`, a flat key (§7).

> universes, by flat keys: the decision's address, `<declaration>.<decision>`, through as many declarations as lead to it (`classification.classifier`, `stage.inner.d`).

The likely wrong guess, a nested map, gets the generic type error from both validators:

```yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
analyses:
  inner: {path: stage/inner}
outputs:
  result: {type: metric, format: json, inputs: [inner.result]}
universes:
  u1:
    decisions:
      inner: {d: d1}      # flat form: inner.d: d1
```

```
jsonschema: /universes/u1/decisions/inner | ...#/$defs/Universe__identifier_optional/properties/decisions/additionalProperties/type | WRONG_TYPE | {'d': 'd1'} is not of type 'string'
Ajv:        /universes/u1/decisions/inner | (same keyword) | WRONG_TYPE | must be string
flat form:  VALID under both
```

The §7 block with `spec_version: "0.1.0"` and `universes: {u1: {decisions: {inner.d: d1}}}` added validates under both.

The impact is limited. The prose is explicit, and lines 266-267 say the entries of `Universe.decisions` are not objects. Worked examples exist outside the specification: `examples/iris_pipeline/astra.yaml:98-109`, `tests/data/valid/Analysis-004-universes.yaml:30`, and RFC 0004.

**Why it matters.** Pipeline authors, the main users of driving, have no worked example of the one construct that drives. The error for the likeliest wrong guess is correct but says nothing about flat keys, so it does not lead them to the right form.

**Suggested fix.** Add the driving universe to the §7 example, for example `universes: {u1: {decisions: {inner.d: d1}}}`. This also declares the `u1` that `inner.result#u1` already names.

### F117. §7 uses the defined term "built once" in a second sense ("built once per universe")

- **Lens:** Author · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:664-665`, `docs/specification.md:44`, `docs/specification.md:596-599`, `docs/specification.md:834-836`, `rfcs/0004-composition.md:73`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:571`

**What is wrong.** §1 and §6 define "built once" for an output: it has one result in all universes, the opposite of "built per universe". §7 says that an analysis declared more than once "is read once, and built once per universe". The intended point is that the analysis is not built again for each declaration. But the phrase joins the defined term "built once" to "per universe", which is half of its opposite. Read literally, the sentence is also wrong. The analysis's outputs built once have one result in total, not one per universe. For an analysis from a commit, they aren't built at all.

**Evidence.** The term, in §1's Terms table and in §6:

- `docs/specification.md:44`: "| built per universe, built once | whether an output has one result per universe or one in all | §6 |"
- `docs/specification.md:597-599`: "Any other output is **built once**: it has one result, the same in every universe."

The other sense, in §7:

- `docs/specification.md:664-665`: "The same directory is the same analysis, whichever analysis declares it and by whatever id: it is read once, and built once per universe."

What happens to an analysis from a commit:

- `docs/specification.md:834-836`: "Its outputs built once aren't built: like the rest of its results, they are read through their records at the declared commit ([`REBUILT_FROM_COMMIT`](#runner))."

The phrase is copied from RFC 0004 (`rfcs/0004-composition.md:73`: "The subanalysis would be read once and built once per universe rather than for every time it is declared."). The design's traceability table carries it over (`design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:571`). RFC 0003 defines the term against this very phrase. `rfcs/0003-cross-universe-artifact-references.md:201`: "*built once*: materialised once per project, not once per universe". The RFC uses the phrase in its plain sense. The clash arises because the spec makes "built once" a defined term. Design §11 to §13 record nothing about it.

**Why it matters.** The spec uses "built once" and "built per universe" as defined terms. A reader or implementer who has learnt them gets two opposed terms in one clause, in the sentence that says how often a declared analysis is built. The intended point still comes through, and no rule depends on the sentence, so two implementers would not build differently because of it.

**Suggested fix.** Avoid both terms and use "result", for example: "it is read once, and each of its results is made at most once, however many declarations reach it." Update the table line at `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:571` to match. The reporter's wording ("in each universe, built once") still implies that outputs built once are built in every universe.

### F118. The fixture-header regex rejects the digits that §9's code grammar allows

- **Lens:** Internal consistency · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `tests/conftest.py:24`, `tests/conftest.py:245-254`, `docs/specification.md:1079-1080`, `tools/rules.py:156-172`, `CLAUDE.md:50`, `CLAUDE.md:61`

**What is wrong.** The code grammar is stated in three places, and they disagree. §9 and the extractor allow digits in a code. The fixture harness does not. A schema-layer code with a digit can go into §9 and `rules.json`, but its invalid fixture can't be read. The defect is latent: none of the 65 codes in `rules.json` has a digit today.

**Evidence.** `docs/specification.md:1079-1080`, in "The catalogue as data":

> Its Code is one code span holding a capital letter, then capitals, digits
> and underscores (`[A-Z][A-Z0-9_]*`), and no other row has it.

`tools/rules.py:164-168` (`_code`) enforces that grammar: an ASCII capital first, then ASCII capitals, digits or `_`. The harness, `tests/conftest.py:24`, has no digits:

```python
_RULE_LINE = re.compile(r"# rule: ([A-Z][A-Z_]*)")
```

`fixture_header` (`tests/conftest.py:245-254`) applies it with `fullmatch`. Calling the harness and the extractor directly:

```
codes in rules.json: 65 | with a digit: []
spec grammar [A-Z][A-Z0-9_]* fullmatches SHA256_MISMATCH: True
_RULE_LINE fullmatch '# rule: SHA256_MISMATCH': None
Analysis-999-digit-code.yaml -> ValueError: Analysis-999-digit-code.yaml: lines 1-2 must be '# rule: <CODE>' and '# fails at: <location>'
rules._code accepts SHA256_MISMATCH -> SHA256_MISMATCH
```

End to end, in a scratch clone, with `Analysis-101-duplicate-output.yaml` copied to `Analysis-999-digit-code.yaml` and line 1 changed to `# rule: SHA256_MISMATCH`:

```
FAILED tests/test_fixtures.py::test_invalid_fixture[Analysis-999-digit-code]
FAILED tests/test_rules.py::test_every_fixture_rule_is_a_code_in_rules_json
FAILED tests/test_specification.py::test_every_fixture_rule_is_in_the_catalogue
FAILED tests/test_specification.py::test_every_schema_rule_has_a_fixture - Va...
```

All four fail with the same `ValueError` from `tests/conftest.py:251`. With a digit-free code that is not in the catalogue (`# rule: SHA_MISMATCH`), the tests give the clear errors instead: `SHA_MISMATCH is not a parse-layer code`.

The §9 grammar and `rules.py` were tightened together in 765624b. `_RULE_LINE`, from 8063b8c, was not updated with them.

**Why it matters.** It bites whoever adds the first code with a digit. CLAUDE.md:50 says "A new schema-layer code also needs an invalid fixture", and CLAUDE.md:61 says its header takes "a code from the specification's rule catalogue (§9)". `just gen-schema` accepts the code. Once a correct fixture header is written, four tests fail with a message that blames the header's layout, not the code's characters. The contract is not affected: `rules.json` and the JSON Schemas carry such a code correctly, and nothing passes for the wrong reason.

**Suggested fix.** Change `tests/conftest.py:24` to `re.compile(r"# rule: ([A-Z][A-Z0-9_]*)")`. A unit test that passes a code with a digit to `fixture_header` would keep the three statements of the grammar in step.

### F119. Untyped presence checks add extra codes for null and non-object values, more widely than the Analysis-159 note records

- **Lens:** Internal consistency · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `src/astra/schema/generated/analysis.schema.json:7-32`, `src/astra/schema/generated/analysis.schema.json:51-58`, `src/astra/schema/generated/analysis.schema.json:113-140`, `src/astra/schema/generated/analysis.schema.json:166-175`, `src/astra/schema/analysis.yaml:395-417`, `src/astra/schema/insight.yaml:98-120`, `tests/conftest.py:33-34`, `tests/test_fixtures.py:61-73`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:763-765`

**What is wrong.** The `AnalysisDeclaration` and `Evidence` constraints test only whether a key is present, not what type its value has. They come from `exactly_one_of` with `required: true` and from rules with `value_presence: PRESENT/ABSENT`. A null or wrongly typed value therefore counts as present, and a non-object entry passes every `required`. Design §12 records one consequence only: `Analysis-159` also gets its ancestor `oneOf`'s code. The design records this; what is worse is that the effect reaches further than that note says:

- **Class rules fire too.** If a declaration has `path` and also `url: null` (or `url: 5`), the declaration gets `URL_WITHOUT_COMMIT` as well as `INVALID_DECLARATION_SOURCE` and `WRONG_TYPE`. `version: null` beside `artifact` gets `INVALID_EVIDENCE_SOURCE` from `then/not`.
- **Non-object entries get the `oneOf` code at their own pointer.** A string or null declaration, or a string evidence item, passes both `oneOf` branches. It gets the `oneOf` code at the same pointer as `WRONG_TYPE`, not at an ancestor.

None of these cases can be written as a fixture. The harness lets only a `oneOf`/`anyOf` error at an ancestor go by, and requires exactly one code at the fails-at pointer.

**Evidence.** Every document starts with `spec_version: "0.1.0"` and one output, so these are its only errors. Both toolkit validators report the same codes. `AD` is `…/$defs/AnalysisDeclaration__identifier_optional`.

```
analyses: {stage: {path: stage, url: null}}
/analyses/stage     | AD/then/required        | URL_WITHOUT_COMMIT
/analyses/stage     | AD/oneOf                | INVALID_DECLARATION_SOURCE
/analyses/stage/url | AD/properties/url/type  | WRONG_TYPE
(Ajv adds /analyses/stage | AD/if | URL_WITHOUT_COMMIT)

analyses: {stage: ../stage}        (likewise [stage], and a null `stage:`)
/analyses/stage | AD/oneOf | INVALID_DECLARATION_SOURCE
/analyses/stage | AD/type  | WRONG_TYPE

evidence: ["10.5555/12345678"]
/findings/f/evidence/0 | Evidence/oneOf | INVALID_EVIDENCE_SOURCE
/findings/f/evidence/0 | Evidence/type  | WRONG_TYPE

evidence: [{artifact: result, version: null}]
/findings/f/evidence/0         | Evidence/then/not               | INVALID_EVIDENCE_SOURCE
/findings/f/evidence/0/version | Evidence/properties/version/type | WRONG_TYPE
```

For the string declaration, jsonschema's message is "'../stage' is valid under each of …". That reads as if the declaration had both fields. The cause is in the generated schema. `if: {"properties": {"url": {}}, "required": ["url"]}` (analysis.schema.json:7-13) and `then: {"properties": {"commit": {}}, "required": ["commit"]}` (:51-58) are untyped. The `oneOf` branches (:15-32) are untyped, and so are the Evidence `if`/`oneOf`/`then` (:113-140, :166-175).

The repository's own `test_invalid_fixture`, run on headed fixtures:

```
FAIL WRONG_TYPE @ /analyses/stage (string)                 test_fixtures.py:73  assert codes == {rule}
FAIL WRONG_TYPE @ /findings/f/evidence/0 (string)          test_fixtures.py:73  assert codes == {rule}
FAIL WRONG_TYPE @ /analyses/stage/url (url: null)          test_fixtures.py:67  assert elsewhere == set()
FAIL WRONG_TYPE @ /findings/f/evidence/0/version (null)    test_fixtures.py:67  assert elsewhere == set()
PASS WRONG_TYPE @ /findings/f/evidence/0/doi (doi: null)   (the recorded Analysis-159 shape)
```

The spec says a null is not a value: "An optional field without a value is left out: no field takes `null`." (`docs/specification.md:190-191`). The note says (`design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:763-765`): "`Analysis-159` still reports a second code (its ancestor `oneOf`) because LinkML 1.12.1 can't type an `exactly_one_of` branch."

**Why it matters.** Every one of these documents is invalid, and both validators agree on the codes, so no verdict is wrong. But an author who writes `stage: ../stage` gets two or three codes for one mistake, and a tool can't map that file to a single code. The fixture discipline can't cover these shapes. The note also blames only the `exactly_one_of` branch, so a fix that types the branches would leave the class rules and the non-object case as they are. With typed branches, `"../stage"` still gets the `oneOf` error.

**Suggested fix.** Widen the §12 note to cover the class rules and non-object entries. To remove the extra codes, have the generator put `type: object` on the `if` and the branches, and give the presence conditions their slot's type (e.g. `properties: {url: {type: string}}`). Alternatively, §10 could tell tools to drop combinator and class-rule codes where a `WRONG_TYPE` error sits at or below the same pointer.

### F120. Ajv's `uniqueItems` differs from jsonschema: it misses a repeated `__proto__` and skips items of the wrong type

- **Lens:** Internal consistency, Standards, Edge cases · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `src/astra/schema/generated/analysis.schema.json:613-620`, `src/astra/schema/generated/rules.json:188`, `docs/specification.md:1111`, `docs/specification.md:1232-1235`, `docs/specification.md:1327`

**What is wrong.** All 14 `uniqueItems` lists in `analysis.schema.json` have `items: {type: string}`. Ajv 8.20 handles that shape with a fast path (`lib/vocabularies/validation/uniqueItems.ts:42-61`) that differs from jsonschema 4.26 in two ways.

1. It indexes items in a plain object literal (l.49) and tests `typeof indices[item] == "number"` (l.55). `({})["__proto__"]` is `Object.prototype`, never a number, so Ajv misses a repeated `__proto__`. Python rejects `tags: [__proto__, __proto__]` with DUPLICATE_ENTRY. Ajv accepts it. That is a split verdict. It occurs on the six `tags` lists and the root `tags`. On the seven lists with an item pattern, the pattern rejects `__proto__` in both validators, so only the code set differs.
2. It skips items of the wrong type (l.48, l.52: `gen.if(wrongType, _\`continue\`)`). For `tags: [2024, 2024]`, Python reports WRONG_TYPE twice and DUPLICATE_ENTRY. Ajv reports only the two WRONG_TYPE errors. The verdict is the same, but the code set is not. This holds on all 14 lists.

Both behaviours break JSON Schema 2019-09 Validation §6.4.3: "the instance validates successfully if all of its elements are unique". Python is right: 1 and 1.0 are equal JSON values. §10 names Ajv as a supported validator (`docs/specification.md:1327`) and does not mention either difference. Design §12 and §13 do not record it.

**Evidence.** Minimal documents. Each has `spec_version: "0.1.0"`, `tags: <list>` and `outputs: {r: {type: metric, format: json}}`.

```
tags: [__proto__, __proto__]
  python: /tags | analysis.schema.json#/properties/tags/uniqueItems | DUPLICATE_ENTRY
  ajv:    VALID
tags: [2024, 2024]
  python: /tags/0 …/tags/items/type WRONG_TYPE; /tags/1 … WRONG_TYPE;
          /tags | …#/properties/tags/uniqueItems | DUPLICATE_ENTRY
  ajv:    /tags/0 … WRONG_TYPE; /tags/1 … WRONG_TYPE   (nothing else)
```

`outputs: {r: {…, tags: [__proto__, __proto__]}}` splits the same way, at `/outputs/r/tags`. `[1, 1.0]`, `[null, ~]`, `[[], []]`, `[true, true]` and `[{a: 1, b: 2}, {b: 2, a: 1}]` split like `[2024, 2024]`. The controls `[a, a]`, `[constructor, constructor]` and `[a, 1, a, 1]` agree. Standalone, on `{type: array, items: {type: string}, uniqueItems: true}`:

```
ajv:        ["__proto__","__proto__"] []   ["a","a"] [' uniqueItems']   [1,1] ['/0 type','/1 type']
jsonschema: ['__proto__','__proto__'] [('', 'uniqueItems')]   [1,1] [('0','type'),('1','type'),('','uniqueItems')]
```

**Why it matters.** It affects astra-tools and astra-typescript. A conformance fixture `tags: [__proto__, __proto__]` (`fails at: /tags`, `rule: DUPLICATE_ENTRY`) passes the Python harness, but an Ajv-based tool finds the document valid. A cross-language comparison of code sets also differs on any list with duplicates of the wrong type. The trigger values are unrealistic, and the spec's contract holds for a conformant validator. Only the Ajv guidance is incomplete.

**Suggested fix.** Add a note to §10's Ajv bullet: Ajv's `uniqueItems` skips items that fail `items.type` and misses a repeated `__proto__`. A code-set comparison ignores DUPLICATE_ENTRY when the list also has a WRONG_TYPE. Report the `__proto__` case to Ajv upstream; a search found no existing report.

### F121. `.inf`, `-.inf`, `.nan` and numbers beyond double range get different codes and verdicts in Python and Ajv

- **Lens:** Internal consistency, Edge cases · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:170-171`, `docs/specification.md:181-182`, `docs/specification.md:1233-1240`, `docs/specification.md:1327`, `src/astra/schema/generated/analysis.schema.json:160-164`, `src/astra/schema/generated/provenance.schema.json:77-81`, `src/astra/schema/generated/provenance.schema.json:131-135`, `tests/conftest.py:110-112`

**What is wrong.** §3 admits the YAML core schema's floats, which include `.inf`, `-.inf` and `.nan`. A JSON Schema 2019-09 instance has no such number. Neither §3 nor §10 says what these values are to the contract, and nothing says what happens to numbers outside IEEE-754 double range (about 1.8e308). Two conforming validators then disagree. Python's `jsonschema` counts ±inf and nan as numbers, so `minimum` runs on `-inf`, and reads a 400-digit integer as an integer. Ajv (`strictNumbers`, on by default) rejects non-finite values as numbers before `minimum` runs, and JS reads a 400-digit integer as `Infinity`. The reference harness builds the non-finite floats (`tests/conftest.py:110-112`). No fixture, design entry or RFC mentions them.

In scope, this touches the three integer fields `Evidence.version`, `size` and `RecordedInput.size`.

**Evidence.** Each document is Analysis-001 plus `prior_insights: {p: {claim: A claim., evidence: [{doi: 10.5555/12345678, version: <v>}]}}`.

```
<v>          Python jsonschema                        Ajv (defaults)
-.inf        version/minimum INVALID_VALUE            version/type WRONG_TYPE
             + version/type WRONG_TYPE
.inf, .nan   version/type WRONG_TYPE                  version/type WRONG_TYPE
1 then 399 zeros   VALID                              version/type WRONG_TYPE
```

Records split in the same way on `size` and `generated_by/inputs/0/size`. Real JSON gives the same results without YAML. `"size": -1e400` gives INVALID_VALUE plus WRONG_TYPE under `json.loads` and WRONG_TYPE alone under `JSON.parse`. A 400-digit `"size"` is VALID in Python and WRONG_TYPE in Ajv. The split starts at 2^1024: `10^308` is VALID in both, and `10^309` is not. With Ajv `{strict: false}` or `{strictNumbers: false}`, `version: .inf` is VALID. Ajv 8.20.0 `lib/core.ts:250` has `strictNumbers: o.strictNumbers ?? s ?? true`.

The spec says the contract holds "exactly when a JSON Schema draft 2019-09 validator reports no error" (`specification.md:1233-1235`). It claims cross-language agreement only for patterns (`:1238-1240`). It tells Ajv users only "Compile with the options `allErrors: true` and `verbose: true`" (`:1327`).

A 400-digit integer is still a valid JSON number. RFC 8259's production has no range limit, and 2019-09 numbers are "arbitrary-precision", so Python's VALID there conforms. The line is double range, not what JSON can carry.

**Why it matters.** The verdict differs only for adversarial values or for an Ajv built non-strict. Under §10's options, Python and TypeScript tools agree on the verdict for the non-finite values but report different code sets for `-.inf`. Beyond double range, `Evidence.version` and `size` get opposite verdicts. One sentence decides each case.

**Suggested fix.** In §3, say that `.inf`, `-.inf`, `.nan` and numbers outside double range take no integer or number field (WRONG_TYPE), and make `construct_core_float` reject non-finite values. In §10, tell Ajv users to keep `strictNumbers` on (the default). Don't recommend `strict: true`: with it, Ajv refuses to compile `analysis.schema.json` (strictRequired).

### F122. "Every anchored pattern ends in `$(?!\n)`" is false of 23 patterns in the contract, which LinkML wraps as `^(?:…)$`

- **Lens:** Internal consistency, Standards, Edge cases · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:245-246`, `CLAUDE.md:19`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:210`, `tests/test_contract.py:168-170`, `src/astra/schema/types.yaml:40-42`, `src/astra/schema/types.yaml:52-54`, `src/astra/schema/types.yaml:64-66`, `src/astra/schema/types.yaml:77-79`, `src/astra/schema/types.yaml:89-91`, `src/astra/schema/generated/analysis.schema.json:77`, `src/astra/schema/generated/provenance.schema.json:74`, `src/astra/schema/generated/provenance.schema.json:119`

**What is wrong.** §4 says of the schema, which is the generated JSON Schema (§2, `docs/specification.md:80`), that every anchored pattern ends in `$(?!\n)`. The five structured patterns (`id`, `address`, `reference`, `option_reference`, `condition`) are already anchored in `types.yaml`, and none sets `partial_match`. LinkML 1.12.1 therefore wraps each one as `^(?:<syntax>)$`, so the contract holds them with doubled anchors and a final bare `$`. The patterns still behave the same: the inner `$(?!\n)` already pins the end. The guard test accepts the second ending, so nothing flags the wrapper. No design section, RFC or commit message records it. Every hand-written pattern in the sources does end in `$(?!\n)`.

**Evidence.** The statement, at `docs/specification.md:245-246`: "Every anchored pattern ends in `$(?!\n)`". `CLAUDE.md:19` and `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:210` say the same.

A walk of every `pattern` keyword in the committed schemas:

```
analysis.schema.json:        patterns=27 anchored=26 end $(?!\n)=7 end $(?!\n))$=19
provenance.schema.json:      patterns=9  anchored=9  end $(?!\n)=7 end $(?!\n))$=2
provenance.open.schema.json: patterns=9  anchored=9  end $(?!\n)=7 end $(?!\n))$=2
```

That is 23 of the 44 anchored occurrences, from 5 distinct patterns. For example, `analysis.schema.json:77`: `"pattern": "^(?:^[a-z][a-z0-9_]*$(?!\\n))$"`.

The cause is `linkml_runtime/utils/schemaview.py:1815-1816` (and again at 2568-2569):

```python
        if not structured_pattern.partial_match:
            pattern = f"^(?:{pattern})$"
```

The guard, at `tests/test_contract.py:169`: `if not pattern.endswith((r"$(?!\n)", r"$(?!\n))$")):`. If that line is narrowed to `$(?!\n)` alone, the test fails on all three schemas.

The patterns behave the same. Over 51,190 strings (including `""`, `"a\n"`, `"\na"`, `"a\n\n"` and `"a\r\n"`), the wrapped and unwrapped forms give 0 differences in Python, and 0 between Python and JS `RegExp` with `u`. Error messages quote the doubled anchor: `'result\n' does not match '^(?:^[a-z][a-z0-9_]*$(?!\\n))$'`.

**Why it matters.** Someone who checks the contract against §4 finds 23 patterns that break the stated rule, each ending in the bare `$` the same paragraph warns against. That someone could be an astra-typescript author porting the guard, or a pattern linter written from the spec. They then have to work out for themselves that the inner lookahead makes it harmless. No document gets a different verdict.

**Suggested fix.** Add `partial_match: true` to the five structured patterns in `types.yaml`, with a comment saying the syntax is already anchored, then narrow `test_contract.py:169` back to `$(?!\n)`. Verified in a clone: only the 23 pattern lines change, `rules.json` and the context don't, and you get 361 passed with lint clean. The other option is to reword §4, `CLAUDE.md` and the design to allow LinkML's `^(?:…)$` wrapper.

### F123. `quote` and `location` are defined only for a "cited work", though evidence citing an output can carry them

- **Lens:** Internal consistency · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `src/astra/schema/insight.yaml:5-7`, `src/astra/schema/insight.yaml:32`, `src/astra/schema/insight.yaml:39`, `src/astra/schema/insight.yaml:73-95`, `docs/specification.md:508-513`

**What is wrong.** Evidence cites either a published work by `doi` or an output as an `artifact`. Inside `Evidence`, "cited work" means the DOI branch: `doi` and `version` (valid only with a `doi`) say "the cited work", and `artifact` says "the cited output". The selector definitions use the DOI wording only. `quote`, `location`, `TextQuoteSelector` and its `exact` all speak of "the cited work", and §5 ties `exact` to "the work". Read literally, the selectors are defined only for `doi` evidence. But the module description and §5's "It locates the passage" apply them to all evidence, and the schema accepts them on `artifact` evidence. The answer (selectors apply to both kinds) exists, but the reader has to work it out from the module header, the lack of any rule, and the contrast with `version`. This is a wording slip. No rule is missing and no document is wrongly accepted or rejected.

**Evidence.** The field descriptions (`src/astra/schema/insight.yaml`), copied unchanged into `generated/analysis.schema.json`:

```
 5-7  ...evidence, which cites a published work by its DOI or an output by reference,
      and locates the passage it relies on with W3C Web Annotation selectors.
  32  A passage of the cited work, quoted exactly, ...           (TextQuoteSelector)
  39  The passage, exactly as the cited work writes it.          (exact)
  73  The DOI of the cited work, ...                             (doi)
  77  A reference to the cited output, ...                       (artifact)
  85  The version of the cited work, counted from 1, ...         (version, doi only)
  90  The passage of the cited work the claim rests on.          (quote)
  95  The place in the cited work the claim rests on, such as `page=6`.  (location)
```

`docs/specification.md:509-513`: "It locates the passage the claim rests on with W3C Web Annotation selectors: `quote`, ... whose `exact` is the passage as the work writes it". In §5 "the work" refers back to the `doi` bullet at :501-504.

The schema accepts both selectors on evidence that cites an output:

```yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
outputs:
  report:
    type: report
    format: pdf
findings:
  forest_wins:
    claim: The forest scores highest.
    evidence:
      - artifact: report
        quote:
          exact: "the forest scores 0.93"
        location: {value: "page=3"}
```

```
$ uv run python validate.py Analysis artifact-quote.yaml     # jsonschema
VALID
$ node js/validate.mjs $REPO Analysis artifact-quote.yaml    # Ajv
VALID
```

On `main`, `location` covered outputs explicitly: "Location hint (page number for PDFs/reports)". v0.1 narrowed the wording, and no RFC or design entry records an intent to limit selectors to `doi` evidence.

**Why it matters.** The field reference is generated from these descriptions, and it is the normative definition of the fields. An author who quotes a passage from a PDF report output reads that `quote` is a passage "of the cited work" and cannot tell whether an output counts. Tool authors don't diverge, because §9 has no rule here. But the normative text says one thing in the module header and §5 and another in the field definitions.

**Suggested fix.** Say "the cited work or output" (or "the cited source") at `insight.yaml:32`, `:39`, `:90` and `:95`, and "as the work or output writes it" at `specification.md:512`. Tom decides.

### F124. WRONG_TYPE's list of JSON types leaves out `number`, the type `Resources.cpus` takes

- **Lens:** Internal consistency · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:1110`, `src/astra/schema/generated/rules.json:368`, `src/astra/schema/generated/analysis.schema.json:431-435`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:360`

**What is wrong.** The WRONG_TYPE row in §9 reads like a complete list of the JSON types that fields take: "a string, an integer, a list or a map". One field takes a JSON number. `Resources.cpus` is `range: float` in `analysis.yaml:66-67` and `"type": "number"` in the generated schema. A non-number value for it fails with WRONG_TYPE, and that rule's statement doesn't name the type the field expects. §9 itself names the field two rows further down: INVALID_VALUE (spec:1112) lists "`Resources.cpus` ≥ 0". The contract is right. Only the wording of the statement is incomplete.

**Evidence.**

```
$ sed -n 1110p docs/specification.md
| `WRONG_TYPE` | schema | MUST | Every value has the JSON type its field takes: a string, an integer, a list or a map (so `excluded: true`, or a list where a map is expected, fails) | §4, §10 |
```

`analysis.schema.json:431-435`:

```
        "cpus": {
          "description": "CPU cores requested. Fractional values are allowed (e.g., 0.5) for runners that support CPU shares.",
          "minimum": 0,
          "type": "number"
        },
```

The JSON types across the three generated schemas show that `number` appears only here, and that no field is boolean. That makes `boolean`'s absence from the list correct:

```
analysis.schema.json:         15 array, 2 integer, 1 number, 22 object, 61 string
provenance.schema.json:       1 array, 2 integer, 5 object, 11 string
provenance.open.schema.json:  1 array, 2 integer, 5 object, 11 string
```

`rules.json:126` maps `…/analysis.schema.json#/$defs/Resources/properties/cpus/type` to `WRONG_TYPE`. `rules.json:368` repeats the incomplete statement word for word.

The minimal documents are `spec_version: "0.1.0"` with one output, `{type: metric, format: json, recipe: {command: run, resources: {cpus: X}}}`:

```
$ uv run python toolkit/validate.py Analysis cpus-number.yaml cpus-string.yaml cpus-bool.yaml
cpus-number.yaml: VALID
cpus-string.yaml: /outputs/b/recipe/resources/cpus | …#/$defs/Resources/properties/cpus/type | WRONG_TYPE | 'two' is not of type 'number'
cpus-bool.yaml:   /outputs/b/recipe/resources/cpus | …#/$defs/Resources/properties/cpus/type | WRONG_TYPE | True is not of type 'number'
```

Ajv (`toolkit/js/validate.mjs`) gives the same pointers, locations and code ("must be number").

**Why it matters.** Tools print the statement from `rules.json` to explain an error. A user who writes `cpus: "2"` is told that every value is "a string, an integer, a list or a map", and the type the field takes isn't in that list. A reader who checks §9 against the schema also finds a type the rule leaves out. `Resources` is otherwise out of scope (brief:88, design:29). Only the §9 wording, which v0.1 wrote new, needs to change.

**Suggested fix.** Write "a string, a number, an integer, a list or a map", or drop the list ("the JSON type its field takes"), which stays correct if field types change. Then run `just gen-schema` to regenerate `rules.json`, and update design:360 to match.

### F125. Several §9 rows break the Rule column's own convention, and `UNDRIVEN_DECLARATION` has no antecedent on its own

- **Lens:** Internal consistency, Standards · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:1031-1034`, `docs/specification.md:1098`, `docs/specification.md:1107`, `docs/specification.md:1110`, `docs/specification.md:1118`, `docs/specification.md:1142-1143`, `docs/specification.md:1152`, `src/astra/schema/generated/rules.json:284`, `src/astra/schema/generated/rules.json:347`, `src/astra/schema/generated/rules.json:368`, `src/astra/schema/generated/rules.json:564`, `src/astra/schema/generated/rules.json:627`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:313-316`

**What is wrong.** §9 says the Rule cell is "one sentence saying what holds" and "never says what happens when it is broken". Three rows break this outright. `DUPLICATE_KEY` and `INVALID_PATH` have two sentences each. `WRONG_TYPE` ends with "fails". A fourth row, `UNDRIVEN_DECLARATION`, says "every such read". "Such" refers to the row above it, `NOT_BUILT_IN_UNIVERSE`, so the row means nothing on its own. "Read" also leaves out decisions, which that antecedent includes and which are consumed, not read. Two rows add tooling notes rather than something that holds: `PIN_LIKE_COMMENT` ("needs the source text") and `UNSUPPORTED_SPEC_VERSION` ("the file is then validated against that schema"). These two are editorial, since the convention does not ban notes explicitly. `rules.json` copies every cell word for word as a standalone `statement`. The extractor (`tools/rules.py:302-333`) counts no sentences and checks no wording, so all of these pass `just gen-schema`. The design's catalogue has the same rows (design:348, 357, 360, 393, 402).

**Evidence.** The convention, `docs/specification.md:1031-1034`:

> **Rule:** one sentence saying what holds in a conforming file, record or run. […] It never says what happens when it is broken.

The rows:

- :1098 `DUPLICATE_KEY`: "Every key of a map is distinct. (This makes the ids of inputs, outputs, decisions, options, insights, universes and declarations unique.)"
- :1107 `INVALID_PATH`: "… with no backslash and no ASCII control character. An input's `path` is the same, and also has no `..` segment and doesn't end in `/`"
- :1110 `WRONG_TYPE`: "… a string, an integer, a list or a map (so `excluded: true`, or a list where a map is expected, fails)"
- :1118 `UNSUPPORTED_SPEC_VERSION`: "… (the file is then validated against that schema, by the schema layer)"
- :1142 `NOT_BUILT_IN_UNIVERSE`: "Every output of another analysis read without a pin, and every decision of another analysis, is used only where …"
- :1143 `UNDRIVEN_DECLARATION`: "In the file of the analysis whose universe is being built, every such read goes through a declaration that analysis drives"
- :1152 `PIN_LIKE_COMMENT`: "No `inputs` entry has a line comment that looks like a pin (needs the source text)"

```
$ python3 -c "import json,re; r=json.load(open('src/astra/schema/generated/rules.json')); pat=re.compile(r'\.\s|\bfails?\b|\bsuch\b|\bneeds?\b|\bthen\b'); [print(x['layer'],x['code'],'::',x['statement']) for x in r['rules'] if pat.search(x['statement'])]"
parse DUPLICATE_KEY :: Every key of a map is distinct. (This makes the ids of inputs, ... unique.)
schema INVALID_PATH :: A declaration's `path` is relative (...), ... control character. An input's `path` is the same, ...
schema WRONG_TYPE :: Every value has the JSON type its field takes: ... (so `excluded: true`, or a list where a map is expected, fails)
validator UNSUPPORTED_SPEC_VERSION :: Every `astra.yaml` in the stack has a `spec_version` ... (the file is then validated against that schema, by the schema layer)
validator UNDRIVEN_DECLARATION :: In the file of the analysis whose universe is being built, every such read goes through a declaration that analysis drives
validator PIN_LIKE_COMMENT :: No `inputs` entry has a line comment that looks like a pin (needs the source text)
```

All 65 statements were scanned. The check and runner tables have no such rows. §6 (`docs/specification.md:719-727`) words the `UNDRIVEN_DECLARATION` subject in full: "for every output of another analysis read without a pin, and every decision of another analysis: … it is reached through a declaration that analysis drives".

**Why it matters.** §10 (`docs/specification.md:1248-1250`) says `rules.json` exists so that every tool "words each rule the same way". A tool that prints `UNDRIVEN_DECLARATION`'s `statement` shows a sentence that refers to nothing. The other rows weaken the convention for whoever adds the next rule. The meaning is not in doubt: the table and §6 settle it, so nothing in the contract changes.

**Suggested fix.** Reword `UNDRIVEN_DECLARATION` from §6: "Every output of another analysis read without a pin, and every decision of another analysis, is reached, in the file of the analysis whose universe is being built, through a declaration that analysis drives". Make each other row one sentence and move the parentheticals into §3-§5. Make the same changes in the design's catalogue.

### F126. INVALID_REFERENCE's catalogue statement also covers the keys of `Universe.decisions`, which INVALID_ID claims

- **Lens:** Internal consistency, Standards · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:1100`, `docs/specification.md:1099`, `docs/specification.md:226`, `docs/specification.md:235-237`, `docs/specification.md:313-316`, `src/astra/schema/analysis.yaml:336-341`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:349-350`

**What is wrong.** The keys of `Universe.decisions` are addresses. A malformed key breaks two MUST rules of §9 as written. INVALID_ID names the case: "Every map key matches its key's type (an `id`, or an `address` in `Universe.decisions`)". INVALID_REFERENCE covers it too: "Every address, reference, option reference and condition matches its grammar". §4 and §5 settle the overlap in favour of INVALID_ID, and the contract follows them. The catalogue row lacks the "other" that §4 has. §9 calls itself "the only place this specification states a rule" (`docs/specification.md:1025-1026`), and `rules.json` ships both statements word for word as `statement`. The design's catalogue has the same two rows (design:349-350).

The overlap is narrow. `DecisionSelection.decision` is the only key slot whose range isn't `id`, so it covers only the keys of `Universe.decisions`.

**Evidence.**

```
docs/specification.md:226   | [`address`](elements/Address.md) | `address` | `Output.decisions`, the keys of `Universe.decisions` |
docs/specification.md:1099  | `INVALID_ID` | schema | MUST | Every map key matches its key's type (an `id`, or an `address` in `Universe.decisions`), and every `id`-typed value matches the `id` grammar | §4 |
docs/specification.md:1100  | `INVALID_REFERENCE` | schema | MUST | Every address, reference, option reference and condition matches its grammar | 0003, 0004 |
docs/specification.md:235-237  "a map key or an `id` as INVALID_ID, any other address, reference, option reference or condition as INVALID_REFERENCE"
docs/specification.md:314-316  "the entries of `Output.decisions` are addresses (INVALID_REFERENCE), and so are the keys of `Universe.decisions` (INVALID_ID)."
```

The schema source picks INVALID_ID on purpose (`src/astra/schema/analysis.yaml:336-341`):

```yaml
        key: true
        range: address
        # A map key that doesn't match its key's type is a bad id, whatever
        # that type (§9), so this names its code over `address`'s.
        annotations:
          rule: INVALID_ID
```

A minimal document puts a pinned address in a selection key. A control puts the same string in `Output.decisions` instead (`decisions: ["model#u"]`):

```yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
outputs:
  result: {type: metric, format: json}
decisions:
  model: {options: {rf: {}}}
universes:
  u:
    decisions:
      "model#u": rf
```

```
key-pin.yaml    /universes/u/decisions | ...#/$defs/Universe__identifier_optional/properties/decisions/propertyNames/pattern | INVALID_ID
value-pin.yaml  /outputs/result/decisions/0 | ...#/$defs/Output__identifier_optional/properties/decisions/items/pattern | INVALID_REFERENCE
Analysis-141    /universes/u/decisions | ...propertyNames/pattern | INVALID_ID
```

Python `jsonschema` and Ajv agree; Ajv also reports the `propertyNames` keyword, which `rules.json` maps to INVALID_ID too. The same string fails the same `address` pattern in both places. Fixture `tests/data/invalid/Analysis-141-selection-key-not-an-address.yaml` (`Model: rf`, line 16) expects INVALID_ID.

**Why it matters.** No tool that maps errors as §10 says gets this wrong: the check is a JSON Schema `propertyNames/pattern`, and the `errors` map gives it INVALID_ID. The overlap bites anyone who works from the catalogue's statements alone, such as a conformance-suite author or a validator written without `rules.json`. For a bad selection key, they could report INVALID_REFERENCE. The issue is in the wording only.

**Suggested fix.** Reword spec:1100 and design:350 as "Every address, reference, option reference and condition, other than a key of `Universe.decisions`, matches its grammar", then regenerate `rules.json`.

### F127. §9 says a file with a broken `SHOULD` fails; §2 and RFC 0003 say it stays valid

- **Lens:** Internal consistency, Standards · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:1058`, `docs/specification.md:1062-1064`, `docs/specification.md:89-91`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:326`

**What is wrong.** §9's table of what a broken rule leads to says that, in the parse, schema and validator layers, "The file fails". The level only changes how the code is reported: as an error for a `MUST`, as a warning for a `SHOULD`. The paragraph under the table confirms this reading. It sets the check layer apart because breaking a check rule changes "what a result is, not whether a file is valid", so a rule in any other layer decides whether the file is valid. §2 says the opposite: an analysis is valid when it breaks none of the `MUST`s. RFC 0003 agrees with §2. Every parse and schema rule is a `MUST`, so the conflict only affects the five validator-layer `SHOULD` rules: `COMMIT_NOT_ANCESTOR`, `REDUNDANT_PIN`, `UNPINNED_EVIDENCE`, `NO_UNIVERSES` and `PIN_LIKE_COMMENT`. The design has the same row and lists no departure for it in §11.

**Evidence.**

`docs/specification.md:1058` and `:1062-1064`:

> | parse, schema, validator | The file fails: the code is reported as an **error** for a `MUST` and as a **warning** for a `SHOULD` |
>
> The check layer is the only one laid out differently, with that extra column, because breaking one of its rules changes what a result is, not whether a file is valid.

`docs/specification.md:89-91` (§2):

> A **validator** reads an analysis, every `astra.yaml` of its stack and git, and reports every parse, schema and validator rule the analysis breaks. An analysis is valid when it breaks none of their `MUST`s.

`rfcs/0003-cross-universe-artifact-references.md:67-69`, which describes the case `NO_UNIVERSES` (a `SHOULD`) covers:

> An analysis that declares decisions but no universes is valid, but nothing that depends on those decisions can be built; validators should warn about it.

```
$ python3 -c "import json;d=json.load(open('src/astra/schema/generated/rules.json'));[print(r['code'],r['layer'],r['level']) for r in d['rules'] if r['level']!='MUST']"
COMMIT_NOT_ANCESTOR validator SHOULD
REDUNDANT_PIN validator SHOULD
UNPINNED_EVIDENCE validator SHOULD
NO_UNIVERSES validator SHOULD
PIN_LIKE_COMMENT validator SHOULD
ABSENT_RESULT check SHOULD
```

Elsewhere the spec uses "fails" to mean invalid (`:181`), and the check row's "Nothing fails" is set against the first row. "The file fails" therefore can't be read as only "breaks the rule".

**Why it matters.** `rules.json` carries a `level` but no severity, so the authors of astra-tools and astra-typescript work out errors and warnings from this row. An author who reads §9 alone would make warnings fail validation and exit non-zero. §2 settles the question, and a validator author has to read §2, so this is a wrong sentence next to a correct definition.

**Suggested fix.** Change the row to "A broken `MUST` is reported as an error and makes the file invalid; a broken `SHOULD` is reported as a warning and the file stays valid". Reword `:1062-1064` to match, and make the same change to the row at design `:326`.

### F128. The OutputType descriptions don't follow the schema's description style

- **Lens:** Internal consistency · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `src/astra/schema/analysis.yaml:31-43`, `src/astra/schema/generated/analysis.schema.json:328`, `CLAUDE.md:12`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:29`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:261`

**What is wrong.** The `OutputType` enum and its five values still have their descriptions from `main`, unchanged. They are fragments with no full stop, and "visualization" is a US spelling. Every other in-scope description in the four modules ends in a full stop and uses British spelling. The design exempts only `Recipe`, `Resources` and the `container`s. `OutputType` is not on that list, so it is in scope.

**Evidence.**

```
$ sed -n 31,43p src/astra/schema/analysis.yaml
  OutputType:
    description: Type of analysis output
    permissible_values:
      metric:
        description: A metric or measurement
      figure:
        description: A figure or visualization
      table:
        description: A table of data
      data:
        description: A data file or dataset
      report:
        description: A report or document
```

`git show main:src/astra/schema/analysis.yaml` has the same six strings at lines 86-98. Two independent scans read every `description` in the four modules with ruamel's safe loader. Both flag only these six strings and two recipe strings (`Resources.gpus`, `Recipe.resources`), which are out of scope. A grep for `-ize`/`-ization` finds "visualization" (`analysis.yaml:38`) as the only in-scope hit. Elsewhere the text writes "artefact" (`analysis.yaml:8, 167, 184, 424`) and "recognises" (`docs/specification.md:357, 825, 1012, 1173`).

- `CLAUDE.md:12`: "Write each description in one or two sentences, in the present tense, saying what the field is and what it takes."
- `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:29`: "recipes: `Recipe`, `Resources` and both `container`s are untouched". Line 261 gives OutputType its own row with no such note: "| **OutputType** | `metric`, `figure`, `table`, `data`, `report` | – |".
- The slot that uses the enum already follows the style. `analysis.yaml:184`, `Output.type`: "The kind of artefact the output is."
- `generated/analysis.schema.json:328`: `"description": "Type of analysis output",`. Only the enum's own description reaches the JSON Schema. The value descriptions appear only in the generated field reference.

Two qualifications. `CLAUDE.md:12` is written about fields, and an enum and its values are not fields. So the rule reaches them only through "one or two sentences". And British spelling is a convention the text follows, not a written rule.

**Why it matters.** It is cosmetic and does not change validation. The field reference shows six descriptions that read differently from all the others. Editor hovers that read the JSON Schema show "Type of analysis output", which says less than `Output.type` does.

**Suggested fix.** Use the wording of `Output.type` for the enum ("The kind of artefact an output is."). End each value's description with a full stop, and write "A figure or visualisation." Tom decides.

### F129. `AnalysisDeclaration.url`'s description has three sentences, against CLAUDE.md's one-or-two rule

- **Lens:** Internal consistency · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `src/astra/schema/analysis.yaml:378-386`, `CLAUDE.md:12`, `docs/specification.md:647-652`

**What is wrong.** CLAUDE.md sets a style rule for the LinkML descriptions: one or two sentences each. The description of `AnalysisDeclaration.url` has three. Of the in-scope descriptions in the four modules, it is the only one over two. Its second and third sentences repeat what §7 already says. No design decision or ruling exempts it.

**Evidence.** The rule, at `CLAUDE.md:12`:

> Write each description in one or two sentences, in the present tense, saying what the field is and what it takes.

The description, at `src/astra/schema/analysis.yaml:378-386`:

```yaml
        description: >-
          Where git fetches another repository from, as a remote:
          `scheme://…`, such as `https://github.com/owner/analysis`, or
          `[user@]host:path`, such as `git@github.com:owner/repo`. The
          declared analysis is the one at the top level of that repository,
          at `commit`. An analysis inside the root's repository is declared
          by `path`, and another repository on this machine by an explicit
          `file://` URL, such as `file:///srv/repo`; a bare word or a local
          path, such as `../repo` or `C:/repo`, is not a `url`.
```

A sentence count over every `description` in `types.yaml`, `analysis.yaml`, `insight.yaml` and `provenance.yaml` treats code spans as opaque. A strict splitter and a loose splitter give the same result:

```
analysis: classes.Recipe: 5                       # out of scope (recipes)
analysis: classes.AnalysisDeclaration.attributes.url: 3
descriptions by sentence count: {1: 102, 2: 30, 3: 1, 5: 1}
```

§7 already says sentences 2 and 3, at `docs/specification.md:647-652`:

> The declared analysis is the one at the top level of that repository, at the declaration's `commit`. A bare word or a local path (`a`, `owner/repo`, `../repo`, `C:/repo`) is not a `url` ([`INVALID_VALUE`](#parse-and-schema)): an analysis inside the root's repository is declared by `path`, and another repository on this machine by an explicit `file://` URL.

`git log -L` shows the description has had three sentences since 050ee55 ("Add universes and declarations"). 1888aae and c7c3e17 later made the third sentence longer. This is old drift, not a recent slip.

**Why it matters.** This is polish. The field reference in `docs/elements/` is generated from these descriptions, so this one entry reads longer than the others and restates guidance that is in §7. Nothing in it is false. Removing the third sentence loses nothing normative: the `url` pattern enforces it (`INVALID_VALUE`) and §7 states it in full.

**Suggested fix.** Drop the third sentence, which §7 carries, or fold the second sentence into the first. Either way the description has two sentences.

### F130. Nothing says that a timestamp's day isn't checked against its month, so 30 February is valid and is an ill-typed `xsd:dateTime` in RDF

- **Lens:** Internal consistency · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `src/astra/schema/types.yaml:123-133`, `src/astra/schema/provenance.yaml:99-110`, `docs/specification.md:233`, `docs/specification.md:916-917`, `docs/specification.md:1341-1346`, `src/astra/schema/generated/provenance.context.jsonld:21-24`, `src/astra/schema/generated/provenance.context.jsonld:33-36`

**What is wrong.** The `timestamp` pattern checks that each field is in its range. It does not check that the day exists in its month. The tidy-up RFC intends this and says so (change 27). The specification and the type's description don't say it. The type carries `uri: xsd:dateTime`, and §10 says a converted record "is PROV-O". So a valid record can convert to ill-typed `xsd:dateTime` literals. This is a gap in the documentation, not in the contract: the schema does what the RFC says.

**Evidence.** Copy `tests/data/valid/ProvenanceRecord-001-full.yaml` and change only the times:

```
feb30.yaml:          started: "2026-02-30T14:00:00.000Z"   finished: "2026-04-31T14:00:02.000Z"
feb29_nonleap.yaml:  started: "2025-02-29T14:00:00.000Z"
month13.yaml:        started: "2026-13-30T14:00:00.000Z"   (control)
```

```
$ validate.py ProvenanceRecord feb30.yaml feb29_nonleap.yaml month13.yaml
feb30.yaml          VALID
feb29_nonleap.yaml  VALID
month13.yaml        /generated_by/started | ...#/$defs/Generation/properties/started/pattern | INVALID_VALUE
```

Ajv gives the same results against the closed and the open schema. Neither provenance schema has a `format` keyword.

Through rdflib 7.6.0 with the committed context, and through `linkml-convert -t ttl`, `feb30.yaml` gives:

```
prov:startedAtTime '2026-02-30T14:00:00.000Z' xsd#dateTime ill_typed= True   (ValueError: day is out of range for month)
prov:endedAtTime   '2026-04-31T14:00:02.000Z' xsd#dateTime ill_typed= True
```

XSD 1.1 Part 2, §3.3.7.1, "Day-of-month Values": the day "must be no more than 30 if month is one of 4, 6, 9, or 11; no more than 28 if month is 2" (29 in leap years).

- `types.yaml:125`: `uri: xsd:dateTime`. `provenance.context.jsonld:21-24` and `:33-36` type `finished` and `started` as `xsd:dateTime`.
- Tidy-up RFC, `NNNN-schema-tidy-up.md:195`: "Converted to RDF they are `xsd:dateTime`, as PROV-O expects. Whether a day exists in its month (31 February) isn't checked."
- `types.yaml:127-130`: "A date and time in UTC to the millisecond, written `YYYY-MM-DDThh:mm:ss.sssZ` with each field in its range (month 01–12, day 01–31, ...)". There is no caveat.
- `specification.md:233` says "a UTC time to the millisecond". `:916-917` says "in UTC to the millisecond, `YYYY-MM-DDThh:mm:ss.sssZ`". Neither gives the ranges or the caveat.

**Why it matters.** The rule is in the draft RFC but not in the spec. A checker author who reads only the spec and parses times natively gets different answers from different parsers. Python's `datetime.fromisoformat` rejects `2026-02-30T14:00:00.000Z`. Node's `new Date` rolls it over to `2026-03-02T14:00:00.000Z` and records another instant. A consumer of the RDF gets ill-typed PROV-O. The impact is low, since runners write clock times.

**Suggested fix.** Add the RFC's sentence to the `timestamp` description and to §8 at `:916-917`: "whether the day exists in its month, such as 30 February, isn't checked". Optionally, say in §10 that such a time is an ill-typed `xsd:dateTime`.

### F131. The specification-example test never validates fragments, so a broken YAML example passes

- **Lens:** Internal consistency · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `tests/test_specification.py:108`, `tests/test_specification.py:126`, `docs/specification.md:465`, `docs/specification.md:577`, `docs/specification.md:739`, `docs/specification.md:1275`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-changes.md:87`

**What is wrong.** `WHOLE_EXAMPLES` keeps only fenced blocks with a top-level `spec_version`. The other blocks are parsed and never validated. That is 2 of the 6 yaml/json blocks in the specification. The YAML blocks at 465, 577 and 739 are illustrative pieces of `astra.yaml`, and the test drops them whatever they contain. A bad id, a duplicate entry, a bad pin, a bad enum value or an unknown field in any of them still passes. Nothing is broken today: each fragment is schema-valid once `spec_version` is added, plus a stub output for 577. The test keeps its stated scope ("every whole document among them is valid", `tests/test_specification.py:5-6`; `CLAUDE.md:65`). The design companion lists "spec examples" among the "Guards so the contract can't loosen quietly" (`design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-changes.md:87`), and for fragments they aren't.

**Evidence.** The filter, `tests/test_specification.py:108-113`:

```python
WHOLE_EXAMPLES = [
    (language, doc)
    for language, doc in examples()
    if isinstance(doc, dict) and "spec_version" in doc
]
"""The examples that are whole documents, with a top-level `spec_version`; the others are fragments."""
```

The blocks, sorted with the test's own `_FENCE` and loader:

```
100  yaml whole    ['decisions', 'findings', 'inputs', 'label', 'outputs', 'spec_version', 'universes']
465  yaml fragment ['decisions', 'outputs']
577  yaml fragment ['universes']
739  yaml fragment ['analyses', 'outputs']
922  json whole    ['address', 'content_hash', 'generated_by', 'size', 'spec_version']
1275 json fragment ['$comment', 'errors', 'rules']
```

In a clone, all three YAML fragments broken:

```diff
-  calibrated_table:
-    type: table
+  Calibrated-Table:
+    type: tabel
-    when: [correction.calibrated]
+    when: [correction.calibrated, correction.calibrated]
-    decisions: {mode: advanced, backend: gpu, gpu_optimization: tensor_cores}
+    decisions: {Mode: advanced, backend: gpu, gpu_optimization: tensor_cores}
+    bogus_field: 1
-    inputs: [inner_undriven.result#v1]  # inner's own universe v1
+    inputs: [inner_undriven.result#V1, inner_undriven.result#V1]
```

```
$ .venv/bin/python -m pytest -q tests/test_specification.py -p no:cacheprovider
9 passed in 0.72s
```

The same broken blocks with `spec_version: "0.1.0"` added fail under both jsonschema and Ajv:

```
465: /outputs                          propertyNames/pattern  INVALID_ID
     /outputs/Calibrated-Table/type    OutputType/enum        INVALID_VALUE
     /outputs/Calibrated-Table/when    uniqueItems            DUPLICATE_ENTRY
577: /universes/gpu_advanced           additionalProperties   UNKNOWN_FIELD
     /universes/gpu_advanced/decisions propertyNames/pattern  INVALID_ID
740: /outputs/in_inner_v1/inputs/0, /1 items/pattern          INVALID_REFERENCE
     /outputs/in_inner_v1/inputs       uniqueItems            DUPLICATE_ENTRY
```

The abridged `rules.json` at 1275 is also unchecked. Today its entries match `src/astra/schema/generated/rules.json`.

**Why it matters.** Spec authors rely on the test to keep the examples honest. A rename or a grammar change that breaks a fragment is published unnoticed, and analysis authors copy it. A future whole example that drops or misspells `spec_version` is quietly treated as a fragment too. `test_specification_has_whole_examples` (line 118) catches that only while it leaves a language with no whole example.

**Suggested fix.** Mark each fragment explicitly, for example with an HTML comment naming a stub base (with `spec_version`, plus a stub output when `outputs` is absent), and validate the merged document. Fail on any yaml/json block that is neither whole nor marked, and check the abridged `rules.json` as a subset of the generated one, ignoring `$comment`.

### F132. §9 calls `#73` an issue, but it is a pull request

- **Lens:** Internal consistency · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:1050`, `docs/specification.md:1112`, `src/astra/schema/generated/rules.json:381`

**What is wrong.** The Source-column convention in §9 says that `#73` is "an issue of the specification's repository". On GitHub, #73 is the merged pull request "Add recommended Output.format, required from 0.1.0". `#73` is the only `#n` source in the specification and in `rules.json`, and only the `INVALID_VALUE` row uses it. So the convention misdescribes the one source of this kind that it covers.

**Evidence.** `docs/specification.md:1049-1051`:

```
`tidy-up` is the draft schema tidy-up RFC, `main` a rule of the
specification before version 0.1, `#73` an issue of the specification's
repository, and a bare `§n`, with no RFC number before it, a section of this
```

The `INVALID_VALUE` row at `docs/specification.md:1112` ends `| 0005, 0007, #73 |`. `src/astra/schema/generated/rules.json:381` copies it: `"source": "0005, 0007, #73",`.

```
$ git log --oneline --all | grep '#73'
580b5dd Add recommended Output.format, required from 0.1.0 (#73)

$ curl -s -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code} %{redirect_url}\n' https://github.com/LightconeResearch/astra-spec/issues/73
302 https://github.com/LightconeResearch/astra-spec/pull/73

$ curl -s https://api.github.com/repos/LightconeResearch/astra-spec/pulls/73   # number, state, merged, merge_commit_sha
73 closed True 580b5ddcc4d74bf4edbe382dde76dec88f15a2a9
```

The merge commit 580b5dd adds `Output.format` and its token pattern, which is the part of `INVALID_VALUE` that #73 is cited for. The design says the same at `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:672`: "`Output.format`'s token pattern (#73, `main`) | `INVALID_VALUE`". PR #73 says it "Partially addresses #72", but #72 is still open and is about where outputs are stored, not the format token. So #73 is the correct source. Only the word "issue" is wrong.

**Why it matters.** A reader who follows the source finds a pull request where the specification promises an issue. A tool that builds links from the convention would build `/issues/73`. GitHub redirects that URL to `/pull/73`, and the REST API counts every pull request as an issue, so the source can still be reached. The defect is in the wording only. No rule or validator behaviour changes.

**Suggested fix.** At line 1050, write "`#73` a pull request of the specification's repository", or make the source a link to `https://github.com/LightconeResearch/astra-spec/pull/73`. The extractor reads the Source cell verbatim, so `rules.json` does not change. The design's line 672 could be reworded to match.

### F133. The examples address through `root`, a declaration the specification never shows and the name of a defined term

- **Lens:** Internal consistency · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:46`, `docs/specification.md:273`, `docs/specification.md:283-285`, `docs/specification.md:300`, `docs/specification.md:355`, `docs/specification.md:460`, `docs/specification.md:932`, `src/astra/schema/provenance.yaml:123`

**What is wrong.** §1 defines **root** as "the analysis being validated or built". Three examples then use `root` as the first segment of an address: `root.data` (§5), `root.a.a1` (§8, conditions) and `"entry": "root.data"` in the record example. By §4, the first segment of an address is the id of an entry of `analyses`, so `root` here is a declaration id. No such declaration appears anywhere on the page. The only parent declaration the specification shows, at :300, is `iris: {path: ..}`, which matches `examples/iris_pipeline/`. These addresses come from RFC 0004's running example, which declares the parent as `{ id: root, path: .. }`. The specification kept the addresses and dropped the declaration. The same `root.data` appears in the normative description of `RecordedInput.entry`, so it also reaches the generated field reference.

**Evidence.**

- `docs/specification.md:46`: "| root | the analysis being validated or built | §7 |"
- `docs/specification.md:273`: "Every id has the one grammar `id`, and no id is reserved"
- `docs/specification.md:283-285`: "Every segment of an address but the last is the id of a declaration (§7): the first names an entry of that analysis's `analyses`"
- `docs/specification.md:300`: "`feature_extraction/astra.yaml`, which declares `iris: {path: ..}`"
- `docs/specification.md:354-355`: "Another analysis reads it by address (`root.data`), not by a path."
- `docs/specification.md:459-460`: "`d` can be a decision of a declared analysis, by its address (`root.a.a1`)."
- `docs/specification.md:925` and `:932`, the record example: `"address": "subanalysis1.output1#u1"` and `"entry": "root.data",`
- `src/astra/schema/provenance.yaml:121-123`: "The entry exactly as the output's `inputs` lists it in the `astra.yaml`, such as `root.data`."

```
$ grep -n 'path: \.\.' docs/specification.md
300:`feature_extraction/astra.yaml`, which declares `iris: {path: ..}`,
```

The source, `rfcs/0004-composition.md:78-80`:

```yaml
# analysis/subanalysis1/astra.yaml
analyses:
  - { id: root, path: .. }
```

The repository's examples use `iris`. `examples/iris_pipeline/classification/astra.yaml:11-12` declares `iris:` / `path: ..`, and `examples/README.md:20` says "Each stage declares the root as `iris` (`path: ..`)".

**Why it matters.** A careful implementer who applies :273 and :283-285 resolves `root.data` correctly, as the declaration `root`. The contract is unaffected, because `entry` is a plain reference string. The confusion falls on readers and authors. The examples look as if `root.` were a prefix that names the stack's root, and the page gives no declaration to correct that reading.
