In the record example the reader gets the right answer only by coincidence: `address` is relative to the driver, `entry` is relative to `subanalysis1`'s file, and that file's parent happens to be the root. The page is also inconsistent with itself: §4 names the parent `iris` and §5 and §8 name it `root`.

**Suggested fix.** Write the parent as `iris` in :355, :460 and :932 (`iris.data`, `iris.a.a1`), matching :300 and the examples, and change `provenance.yaml:123` the same way. Alternatively, show the declaration once ("where `subanalysis1` declares its parent as `root: {path: ..}`").

### F134. `tests/data/README.md` is stale against CLAUDE.md and the test harness

- **Lens:** Internal consistency · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `tests/data/README.md:4-5`, `tests/data/README.md:20-21`, `tests/data/README.md:33-34`, `tests/data/README.md:36-37`, `tests/data/README.md:47-49`, `CLAUDE.md:56`, `CLAUDE.md:61`, `CLAUDE.md:65`, `tests/test_fixtures.py:50-73`

**What is wrong.** The fixture README was last changed in 0aa0188. That is before the harness rewrite (8063b8c) and before the rules.json commits (98e8155, 765624b). It now disagrees with CLAUDE.md's Tests section and with the code in five places:

1. **Loader.** It describes ruamel's stock safe loader. The harness uses a restricted core-schema loader (`tests/conftest.py:126-132`). The two loaders read timestamps and merge keys differently.
2. **Numbering.** It says invalid fixtures are numbered "from `101`". The invalid ProvenanceRecord fixtures run from 201 to 215, as the plan sets out (`design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-plan.md:792`).
3. **Source of codes.** It points to "the rule catalogue (design §7)". CLAUDE.md:61 and D13 name the specification's §9, and the test reads codes from rules.json, which is built from §9. Design §7 still holds a copy with the same 65 codes, so the pointer gives no wrong codes today. But it names the non-normative copy, which can drift.
4. **What the test asserts.** It says the validator "reports an error there". The test also asserts that no error appears anywhere else and that rules.json gives the errors the fixture's code.
5. **Parse fixtures.** It describes them as "(a duplicate key)". Two of the four parse fixtures are something else: malformed YAML and a `%YAML 1.1` directive.

**Evidence.**

`tests/data/README.md`:
- :4-5: "read as YAML 1.2 (ruamel.yaml's safe loader, which keeps `yes`, `no` and `on` as strings and rejects duplicate keys)"
- :20-21: "`<nnn>` numbers the fixture: from `001` for valid fixtures, from `101` for invalid ones."
- :33-34: "`<CODE>` is the code of the rule it breaks, from the rule catalogue (design §7)."
- :37: "The test asserts that the validator reports an error there."
- :47: "A fixture that fails at `parse` (a duplicate key) is not valid YAML"

CLAUDE.md says otherwise:
- :56: "ruamel.yaml's safe loader restricted in `tests/conftest.py` (no timestamps or merge keys, …)"
- :61: "# rule: <CODE>          a code from the specification's rule catalogue (§9)"
- :65: "the validator reports errors at that location and nowhere else … and `rules.json` gives those errors the fixture's rule"
- :65: "Fixtures that fail at parse (a duplicate key, malformed YAML, a `%YAML` directive other than 1.2)"

The two loaders disagree on the same text:
```
# text: "a: 2026-01-01\nb: yes\nbase: &b {x: 1}\nc:\n  <<: *b\n  y: 2\n"
stock ruamel safe (pure): a=datetime.date(2026, 1, 1) (date), b='yes', c={'x': 1, 'y': 2}
harness load_yaml_text:   a='2026-01-01' (str), b='yes', c={'<<': {'x': 1}, 'y': 2}
```

The test's assertions (`tests/test_fixtures.py`):
```python
    """It fails at `fails_at` and nowhere else (a combinator at an ancestor aside), by an error `rules.json` gives its rule."""   # :50
    assert elsewhere == set()                                                                                                       # :67
    assert codes == {rule}                                                                                                          # :73
```

The parse fixtures:
```
$ grep -l "fails at: parse" tests/data/invalid/*
Analysis-101-duplicate-output.yaml      # rule: DUPLICATE_KEY
Analysis-146-duplicate-universe.yaml    # rule: DUPLICATE_KEY
Analysis-182-malformed-yaml.yaml        # rule: INVALID_SYNTAX
Analysis-183-yaml-1-1-directive.yaml    # rule: INVALID_SYNTAX (%YAML 1.1)
```
`.yamllint.yaml:9-12` and `.pre-commit-config.yaml:11` exclude all four.

**Why it matters.** Someone adding a fixture reads this file first. From it they get the wrong numbering, a pointer to the non-normative catalogue, a loader that resolves dates and merge keys when the harness does not, and a weaker idea of what the test checks. None of this makes a test pass for the wrong reason, and none of it changes the contract.

**Suggested fix.** Cut the README down to a pointer to CLAUDE.md's Tests section, or rewrite these five places to match it.

### F135. §9 doesn't say which Markdown its cells are judged in: the extractor reads CommonMark, the site renders Python-Markdown with Zensical's extensions

- **Lens:** Internal consistency · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:1066-1068`, `docs/specification.md:1084-1091`, `docs/specification.md:1254-1255`, `tools/rules.py:101-102`, `zensical.toml:1-77`, `uv.lock:2565-2566`, `CLAUDE.md:39`

**What is wrong.** "The catalogue as data" lists what a cell may hold, and says a table that breaks it is an error. It does not name a Markdown dialect. §10 calls `statement` "inline Markdown" without naming one either. `tools/rules.py` parses CommonMark with GFM tables. The published page is built by Zensical 0.0.40 with Python-Markdown and Zensical's default extensions, because `zensical.toml` sets no `markdown_extensions`. The same cell can therefore read one way in `rules.json` and another way on the site. A rule table can even exist for the extractor and not on the site. Nothing diverges today, so the defect is latent.

**Evidence.** `tools/rules.py:102` is `return MarkdownIt("commonmark").enable("table")`. Spec quotes:

- `docs/specification.md:1084`: "**Cells** hold only text, code spans, emphasis, links and inline HTML."
- `docs/specification.md:1254-1255`: "its `statement`, the Rule cell as inline Markdown with each link reduced to its text".

`grep -i -E 'commonmark|gfm|python-markdown|dialect'` finds nothing in the spec. Zensical 0.0.40's `config.py:448-487` defaults include `footnotes`, `attr_list`, `pymdownx.arithmatex`, `caret`, `emoji`, `mark`, `smartsymbols` and `tilde`. `config.py:899` adds `toc` and `tables`. The reproducer mutated one Rule cell per case, ran the real `extract_rules`, then ran `zensical build` on a clone:

```
cell                     rules.json statement        site renders
~~always~~               ~~always~~                  <del>always</del>
==others== / ^^now^^     ==others== / ^^now^^        <mark>others</mark> / <ins>now</ins>
:warning:                :warning:                   <img alt="⚠" class="twemoji" ...>
*pin*{: .x }             *pin*{: .x }                <em class="x">pin</em>
--> 1/2 =/= (c)          --> 1/2 =/= (c)             &rarr; &frac12; &ne; &copy;
$x$                      $x$                         <span class="arithmatex">\(x\)</span>
(a \< b)                 (a < b)                     (a \&lt; b)
`a\|b`                   `a|b`                       <code>a\|b</code>
`a|b`                    REJECTED: 5 cells, 4 columns   a normal 4-cell row
pin[^n] + "[^n]: Note"   pin^n                       footnote marker <sup>…1</sup>
table right under a paragraph line
                         extracted (65 rules)        <p>These rules bind a runner. | Code | ...</p>
```

Footnotes diverge only when a definition is present. The pipe problem is limited to backtick code spans: inline HTML `<code>a&#124;b</code>` reads the same on both sides. Today's tree is clean: comparing the 65 committed statements with the built page gives "rules compared: 65, text differences: 0".

**Why it matters.** Spec editors are told the extractor enforces the cell format (`CLAUDE.md:39`). It enforces it only in a dialect the published page doesn't use. A future edit to §9 can pass `just gen-schema` while the normative page and `rules.json` word a rule differently. astra-tools, astra-typescript and editors would then show `~~always~~` or `:warning:` where the spec shows something else. Smart symbols can change a rule's text with no visible extension syntax. Consumers of `rules.json` are not told how to render `statement` either.

**Suggested fix.** Say in §9 and §10 that cells are CommonMark with GFM tables. Then either pin Zensical's `markdown_extensions` so §9 renders the same way, or add a test that renders each Rule cell with the site's configuration and compares it with `rules.json`.

### F136. §10 says `status` is the status a result gets, but `rules.json` holds the cell's Markdown, and two values aren't a status

- **Lens:** Internal consistency · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:1252-1257`, `docs/specification.md:1157-1158`, `docs/specification.md:1084-1088`, `src/astra/schema/generated/rules.json:635-683`, `tools/rules.py:261-263`

**What is wrong.** §10 lists the fields of a rule's entry in `rules.json`. It calls only `statement` inline Markdown. It gives `source` no form, and it describes `status` by meaning: "the status a result gets when it is broken". In fact all three fields are the cells' inline Markdown, as §9's "Cells" bullet and `tools/rules.py` say. So every `status` is wrapped in backticks. `ABSENT_RESULT`'s `status` is a disjunction. `STALE_EVIDENCE`'s `status` is prose that names no status, and evidence gets it, not a result. The form is written down in §9, and §10's own example shows it. The fault is that §10 singles out `statement` as Markdown, which invites the contrary reading, and its gloss doesn't fit `STALE_EVIDENCE`. This is a wording defect.

**Evidence.** §10 (`docs/specification.md:1254-1257`):

> its `statement`, the Rule cell as inline Markdown with each link reduced to its text; its `source`; and, for a rule of the check layer, its `status`, the status a result gets when it is broken.

§9's "Cells" bullet (`docs/specification.md:1084-1085`): "`rules.json` records a cell as Markdown rebuilt from what it parses to". The extractor says the same for all three fields (`tools/rules.py:261-263`): "the last three are the cells' inline Markdown".

```
$ grep -n '"status"' src/astra/schema/generated/rules.json
635:      "status": "`missing`"
...
667:      "status": "`stale`"
675:      "status": "`absent`, or `modified`"
683:      "status": "the cited result's status"
$ grep -n '"source"' src/astra/schema/generated/rules.json | grep '`'
318:      "source": "0004, `main`",
409:      "source": "`main`",
535:      "source": "`main`, tidy-up",
```

None of the seven `status` values equals one of the six status tokens (`docs/specification.md:1166-1173`). §9 says the column "is what the result gets when the rule doesn't hold" (`:1157-1158`), but for `STALE_EVIDENCE` it is evidence: "Evidence citing a result gets that result's first status" (`:1163-1164`).

**Why it matters.** A checker author (astra-tools, astra-typescript) reading §10 alone may expect `status` to be a status token and compare it directly. That fails on all seven rules. The field is display wording, so reading it as text works; §10 just doesn't say so. No test would catch either reading: `tests/test_rules.py` checks only that the key is present.

**Suggested fix.** In §10: "its `source` and, for the check layer, its `status` (the Status when broken cell), both inline Markdown like `statement`". Optionally change "a result gets" to "a result, or evidence citing it, gets", here and at §9 `:1157`.

### F137. The `minItems`/`minProperties` default ignores the keyword's value, so a minimum of 2 or more is silently coded `EMPTY_COLLECTION`

- **Lens:** Internal consistency · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `tools/rules.py:61-70`, `tools/rules.py:432-433`, `docs/specification.md:1103`, `docs/specification.md:1268-1273`, `CLAUDE.md:48`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:119-120`

**What is wrong.** Spec §10, CLAUDE.md and D13 say that `minProperties` and `minItems` take the default code `EMPTY_COLLECTION` because their code "never varies". That holds only for a value of 1. `EMPTY_COLLECTION` says a collection has "at least one entry". The error map keys the default on the keyword alone and never reads its value. If a schema change raises `minimum_cardinality` above 1, `just gen-schema` still passes, and the new constraint is coded `EMPTY_COLLECTION`. A one-item list is then reported as empty. The build rejects the neighbouring keywords it can't code (`maxItems`, `maximum`, `const`), but it never flags this case. A `rule` annotation can't fix the code either: the build rejects it. The only way to give such a minimum its own code is to edit `tools/rules.py`.

The defect is latent. All three minimums today are 1 (`analysis.yaml:247`, `analysis.yaml:451`, `insight.yaml:146`), so the committed `rules.json` is correct.

**Evidence.** `tools/rules.py:61-64` and `:432-433`:

```python
DEFAULTS = {
    "type": "WRONG_TYPE",
    "minProperties": "EMPTY_COLLECTION",
    "minItems": "EMPTY_COLLECTION",
...
            if keyword in DEFAULTS:
                self.add(here, DEFAULTS[keyword])
```

`docs/specification.md:1103`:

> | `EMPTY_COLLECTION` | schema | MUST | `outputs`, `Decision.options` and `Insight.evidence` each have at least one entry | 0004, `main` |

Repro in a local clone of 96c902a. The baseline `just gen-schema` leaves `git status` clean. Then set `outputs` to `minimum_cardinality: 3` and `Insight.evidence` to `minimum_cardinality: 2`:

```
gen-schema exit: 0           (rules.json unchanged)
".../$defs/Insight__identifier_optional/properties/evidence/minItems": "EMPTY_COLLECTION",
".../properties/outputs/minProperties": "EMPTY_COLLECTION",
```

Validate this document against the mutated contract. It is `VALID` against the real repository:

```yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
outputs:
  result: {type: metric, format: json}
  table: {type: table, format: csv}
findings:
  f:
    claim: The result is 0.93.
    evidence:
      - artifact: result
```

```
jsonschema: /findings/f/evidence | ...evidence/minItems | EMPTY_COLLECTION | [{'artifact': 'result'}] is too short
Ajv:        /outputs | ...outputs/minProperties | EMPTY_COLLECTION | must NOT have fewer than 3 properties
```

For contrast, the build fails on the keywords it can't code, and it fails on a `rule` annotation added to override the default:

```
maximum_cardinality: 5 on Insight.evidence -> ValueError: .../evidence/maxItems: no code for this keyword
minimum_cardinality: 2 + rule: INVALID_VALUE -> ValueError: no keyword of the JSON Schemas takes its code from the 'rule' annotation of Insight.evidence
```

**Why it matters.** Whoever raises a minimum gets a wrong code with no error. astra-tools and astra-typescript read codes from `rules.json`, so they would then tell authors that a non-empty list is empty. The tests catch this only if the new invalid fixture names a code other than `EMPTY_COLLECTION`. A fixture tagged `EMPTY_COLLECTION` passes.

**Suggested fix.** In `_ErrorMap.value`, map `minItems`/`minProperties` to `EMPTY_COLLECTION` only when the value is 1, and raise `unknown()` for any other value, as for `maxItems`. Word the default as "`minItems`/`minProperties` of 1" in §10, CLAUDE.md and D13.

### F138. The extractor counts a row's cells on the raw source line, so a rule table in a blockquote fails with a false cell count

- **Lens:** Internal consistency · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `tools/rules.py:248-255`, `tools/rules.py:306-310`, `docs/specification.md:1072-1078`

**What is wrong.** `_source_cell_count` splits the raw source line of each row, `lines[line - 1].strip()`. `.strip()` removes indentation but not a blockquote's `>` marker. markdown-it parses a quoted table as a table, with one cell per column in every row. In the raw line, though, the marker becomes an extra first cell, since only an empty leading cell is dropped. The extractor then rejects a well-formed table and reports one cell more than the row has. §9's "catalogue as data" paragraph does not forbid a quoted rule table, so the extractor is stricter than the paragraph it enforces. Only blockquote markers trigger this: a table inside a list item, or indented up to 3 spaces, is accepted.

**Evidence.** The count (`tools/rules.py:248-255`):

```python
def _source_cell_count(lines: list[str], line: int) -> int:
    cells = escapedSplit(lines[line - 1].strip())
    if cells and cells[0] == "":
        cells.pop(0)
    ...
```

It is compared with the header at `tools/rules.py:306-310` (`f"it has {written} cells, but the table has {len(header)} columns"`). A probe rewrites the §9 runner table (`docs/specification.md:1193-1201`) in memory and calls `extract_rules`:

```
== baseline (unchanged)
  markdown-it cells per row: [4, 4, 4, 4, 4, 4, 4, 4]
  ACCEPTED, 65 rules
== runner table inside a blockquote ('> ' on every line)
  table_open ... level 1 after [..., 'blockquote_open'] | markdown-it cells per row: [4, 4, 4, 4, 4, 4, 4, 4]
  REJECTED: line 1195, ### runner, row `UNRECORDED_RESULT`: it has 5 cells, but the table has 4 columns
== blockquote without the space ('>' on every line)
  REJECTED: ... it has 5 cells, but the table has 4 columns
== runner table inside a list item
  ACCEPTED, 65 rules
== 3-space indented table
  ACCEPTED, 65 rules
```

With the raw-line count bypassed for those rows, the output equals the baseline exactly. The count is the only check that rejects the table. The spec defines a rule table only by its header: "A rule table is a table whose header has a Code column" (`docs/specification.md:1072`), and "Each row is one rule, with one cell per column and none empty" (`:1078`). Every quoted row meets that.

**Why it matters.** A spec editor who quotes a rule table gets an error that names the wrong problem. The failure is closed: a quoted row missing a cell or carrying an extra one is still rejected. §9 has no quoted tables today, so `rules.json` is unaffected.

**Suggested fix.** Strip the blockquote prefix (`^(\s{0,3}>\s?)+`, or slice the row as markdown-it's block state does) before `escapedSplit`. Do not count from the tokens instead: markdown-it pads short rows and truncates long ones, which would hide the extra cell that `tests/test_rules.py:170` ("extra cell") catches. Alternatively, §9 can say rule tables are not quoted, and the extractor can reject a table under `blockquote_open` with that message.

### F139. §10 says "strict validators (such as Ajv) compile" the contract, but `analysis.schema.json` throws under Ajv's `strict: true`

- **Lens:** Standards · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:1229-1230`, `src/astra/schema/generated/analysis.schema.json:113-122`, `src/astra/schema/generated/analysis.schema.json:166-175`, `src/astra/schema/insight.yaml:109-120`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:105-107`, `tests/test_contract.py:307-310`

**What is wrong.** This is a wording point. §10 says the contract's top level "holds only standard JSON Schema keywords, so that strict validators (such as Ajv) compile it". That holds for Ajv's default mode, which Ajv's documentation calls strict mode and in which `strictRequired` is off. It does not hold for `strict: true` or `strictRequired: true`. Under either option `analysis.schema.json` fails to compile. The cause is the Evidence class rule. Stock LinkML generates it with each `properties` beside its `not`, not inside it (`jsonschemagen.py:289-295`). Ajv's `strictRequired` rejects a `required` whose property isn't defined in the same or a parent subschema. The `if/not` gets through only because Ajv compiles the `oneOf` branch that defines `doi` first. The provenance schemas compile under every option.

The design records the limitation, but only in a companion note, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-no-python-models.md:70-72`: "the latter compiling it in its default strict mode (Ajv's extra `strictRequired` option rejects LinkML's encoding of one Evidence rule)". It is not in §12 or §13 of the schema design. The spec and `schema-design.md:105-107` say "strict validators" without that qualification.

**Evidence.** Ajv 8.20.0 (`ajv/dist/2019`), HEAD 96c902a, each schema compiled with `allErrors: true, verbose: true` plus the option shown:

```
analysis.schema.json {}                      compiles
analysis.schema.json {"strict":true}         THROWS strict mode: required property "version" is not defined at "https://w3id.org/astra/analysis.schema.json#/then/not" (strictRequired)
analysis.schema.json {"strictRequired":true} THROWS (same)
analysis.schema.json {"strict":"log"}        compiles, with the same message as a warning
provenance.schema.json, provenance.open.schema.json: compile under every option
```

`strict: true` switches the check on: Ajv `dist/core.js:77`, `strictRequired: o.strictRequired ?? s ?? false`.

The generated shape (`analysis.schema.json:113-122`, `:166-175`):

```json
"if":   { "not": { "required": ["doi"] },     "properties": { "doi": {} } },
"then": { "not": { "required": ["version"] }, "properties": { "version": {} } },
```

Scratch variants under `strict: true`. Moving the `then`'s `properties` inside its `not` compiles. Also deleting the `oneOf` then throws at `#/if/not` for `doi`. Fixing both, with or without the `oneOf`, compiles. The fixed schema gives every `Analysis-*` fixture the same verdict and the same errors (instance path, schema path, keyword) as the original, and the `rules.json` keys `…#/$defs/Evidence/if` and `…/then/not` stay valid.

`tests/test_contract.py:309` explains the test with "so strict validators (Ajv) compile the contract", but the test checks only the top-level keyword names. No test or CI step runs Ajv.

**Why it matters.** A tool following §10's own Ajv method (`docs/specification.md:1327`, "`allErrors: true` and `verbose: true`") works. But an implementer of astra-typescript, an editor or ajv-cli who reads "strict validators" and turns on `strict: true` cannot load `analysis.schema.json` at all. The failure is a compile-time exception, not a different verdict. Any future `value_presence: ABSENT` rule gets the same shape.

**Suggested fix.** In `tools/jsonschema_generator.py`, put each presence condition's `properties` inside its `not` (`not: {properties: {version: {}}, required: [version]}`, and the same for `doi`), and add a check that compiles all three schemas with Ajv `strict: true`. Or narrow §10, the design and the docstring to "Ajv's default (strict) mode".

### F140. §3 never says how YAML integers and floats become JSON values, so `version: 2.0` reads as a type error but passes

- **Lens:** Standards · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:170-182`, `docs/specification.md:188-190`, `docs/specification.md:1110`, `docs/specification.md:1233-1235`, `src/astra/schema/insight.yaml:81-86`

**What is wrong.** This is a wording defect. The contract is right. The §3 YAML bullet lists the core schema's types as "strings, nulls, booleans, integers and floats". Seven lines later it says "A value of another type than its field takes fails as `WRONG_TYPE`". Read in place, that sentence types values by YAML tag. On that reading, a YAML float such as `2.0` in an integer field is "another type" and fails. The specification answers this elsewhere. §3 at 188-190 and §9 at 1110 say "the JSON type the field takes", and §10 at 1233-1235 makes the 2019-09 validator's verdict the contract. JSON Schema counts any number with a zero fractional part as an integer, so `2.0` is valid. The specification never states this mapping from YAML int and float to JSON number. It also never says what the core floats `.inf`, `-.inf` and `.nan` become, and JSON has no value for them. `grep -n '\.inf\|\.nan\|NaN\|infinit\|fractional\|JSON number\|data model' docs/specification.md` finds nothing.

**Evidence.** Minimal document (in scope: `Evidence.version`, `range: integer`, `minimum_value: 1`, `insight.yaml:81-83`):

```yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
outputs:
  result:
    type: metric
    format: json
prior_insights:
  noise:
    claim: Forests resist noise.
    evidence:
      - doi: 10.5555/12345678
        version: 2.0        # also tried: 3e0, 2., 2.5
```

Both validators, on the same files:

```
version   jsonschema (Python)   Ajv 8.20 (default)
2.0       VALID                 VALID
3e0       VALID                 VALID
2.        VALID                 VALID
2.5       WRONG_TYPE            WRONG_TYPE
.inf      WRONG_TYPE            WRONG_TYPE
.nan      WRONG_TYPE            WRONG_TYPE
1e400     WRONG_TYPE            WRONG_TYPE
```

The loader keeps `2.0` as a Python float, and jsonschema counts it as an integer. This follows JSON Schema 2019-09, Validation §6.1.1: "\"integer\" which matches any number with a zero fractional part". Core §4.2.1 says "the data model makes no distinction between integers and other numbers".

The non-finite floats are where the validators part. On every in-scope field they agree under Ajv's default options. The only field where they disagree under defaults is `Resources.cpus` (`type: number`, `minimum: 0`). Recipes are out of scope for this review, so this is a symptom only: `.inf` and `.nan` are VALID in jsonschema (NaN passes `minimum`) and `WRONG_TYPE` in Ajv, whose `strictNumbers` default requires `isFinite`. `-.inf` fails in both, as `INVALID_VALUE` in jsonschema and `WRONG_TYPE` in Ajv.

**Why it matters.** An implementer who type-checks YAML nodes, as 177-182 describe, rejects `version: 2.0`, and the contract accepts it. A checker reporting codes gives `.inf` and `.nan` different verdicts or codes depending on its validator. The specification gives no rule to decide between them.

**Suggested fix.** In §3, say that YAML integers and floats are JSON numbers and are typed as JSON Schema types them, so `2.0` is an integer. Also say that `.inf`, `-.inf` and `.nan` have no JSON value and fail, and name the code.

### F141. The specification leaves astra.yaml's encoding and character set to YAML 1.2.2 without saying so, and the two loaders read the same bytes differently

- **Lens:** Standards, Edge cases · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:79`, `docs/specification.md:163-165`, `docs/specification.md:1097`, `tests/conftest.py:135-142`, `tests/conftest.py:245-247`

**What is wrong.** §3 says only that `astra.yaml` is YAML 1.2 and links YAML 1.2.2. Nothing in the specification uses the words "encoding" or "printable". So the encoding rules come only by normative reference. YAML 1.2.2 §5.2 requires a processor to read UTF-8, UTF-16 and UTF-32, told apart by the BOM or the pattern of null bytes. §5.1 forbids C0 controls other than TAB, LF and CR anywhere, even inside quoted scalars, but requires processors to allow every non-C0 character inside quoted scalars. Under that reading every case has one answer: UTF-16 and UTF-32 are valid, while a Latin-1 byte or a raw ESC or NUL is `INVALID_SYNTAX`. Neither common loader follows these rules in full, and the specification gives implementers no pointer to them. §2 calls the parse layer's input "one file's text" (line 79), which hides the decoding step. §10 gives detailed guidance for Ajv but none on decoding.

The specification's own harness falls short too. ruamel 0.19.1 (`ruamel/yaml/reader.py:172-189`) detects only the UTF-16 BOMs. It has no UTF-32 support and no null-pattern detection. It also rejects DEL and C1 inside quoted scalars, which YAML 1.2.2 says must be accepted. On the JS side, `yaml` 2.9.1 takes only a string, so the caller picks the decoding. Reading the file with `'utf8'`, the natural default, garbles UTF-16 and turns invalid bytes into U+FFFD. `yaml` also doesn't check the printable set. The JSON Schema contract is not involved: on identical data, Ajv and `jsonschema` agree.

**Evidence.** The same minimal analysis, with `label: Iris é classification` and `path: données/iris.csv`, saved in each encoding:

| file | Python harness (ruamel) | Ajv + yaml 2.9, read as `'utf8'` |
|---|---|---|
| UTF-8, with or without BOM | VALID | VALID |
| UTF-16LE + BOM (`ff fe`), as PowerShell 5.1's `>` writes | VALID | `(root) \| …analysis.schema.json#/type \| WRONG_TYPE \| must be object` |
| UTF-16LE, no BOM | ReaderError `#x00e9` | WRONG_TYPE at (root) |
| UTF-32LE + BOM | ReaderError: unacceptable character #x0000 | WRONG_TYPE at (root) |
| Latin-1 (`é` = `e9`) | ReaderError: unacceptable character #x00e9: invalid continuation byte | VALID |
| raw ESC in `description: "…"` | ReaderError: unacceptable character #x001b | VALID |
| raw DEL / U+0080 in `"…"` | ReaderError `#x007f` / `#x0080` | VALID |

In JS, the UTF-16 file loads as one scalar string (`"��s\u0000p\u0000e\u0000c…"`), and the Latin-1 file gives `path="donn�es/iris.csv"`. In Python, the UTF-8 and UTF-16 files load to the same data. A decoder written to §5.2 (BOM or null pattern, `TextDecoder` with `fatal: true`) and followed by `yaml` 2.9 reads every UTF-16 and UTF-32 file as the UTF-8 one, and rejects the Latin-1 file. All tracked fixtures are BOM-less UTF-8. `fixture_header` reads fixtures as UTF-8 (`conftest.py:247`), so as things stand a non-UTF-8 parse fixture can't be added.

**Why it matters.** Windows authors produce UTF-16 or cp1252 files. One validator accepts such a file and the other rejects it, or reads a different label or path. Where the JS side rejects a UTF-16 file, it reports a misleading schema-layer `WRONG_TYPE` rather than `INVALID_SYNTAX`. Validator authors have to find the rules in YAML 1.2.2 for themselves. Three reviewers missed them.

**Suggested fix.** In §3, add a sentence: the encoding and characters are YAML 1.2.2 §5.1-5.2's, anything outside them is `INVALID_SYNTAX`, and a validator decodes the bytes itself, fatally, before it calls a string-only loader. Or require UTF-8 only, which narrows YAML and is a tidy-up change. Either way, note where the harness departs.

### F142. The harness loader resolves a `!`-tagged scalar as if it were plain, where YAML 1.2.2 makes it a string

- **Lens:** Standards · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `tests/conftest.py:66-79`, `tests/conftest.py:126-132`, `tests/conftest.py:136-141`, `tests/test_loader.py:87-128`, `docs/specification.md:176-178`

**What is wrong.** YAML 1.2.2 gives a node with the `!` non-specific tag the type str, seq or map, by its kind. It says so in §6.9.1, where Example 6.28 reads `- ! 12` as `"12"`, and in §10.3.2 for the core schema. The spec reads `astra.yaml` as "YAML 1.2 ... under the core schema only" (`docs/specification.md:163-165`). The harness loader instead runs a `!`-tagged scalar through the core resolvers (`_CoreSchemaResolver`, `tests/conftest.py:66-79`). So `! 12` loads as the int 12. The same applies to quoted scalars: `! "12"` loads as 12 too, `! true` loads as True, `! 1.5` as 1.5, and an empty `!` as None where YAML gives `""`. It also accepts `!<!> 12`, which Example 6.25 calls invalid. The yaml package (2.9.1, core, 1.2) follows the spec on every one of these cases.

The cause is upstream, in ruamel.yaml 0.19.1. Its parser marks a `!`-tagged scalar implicit (`parser.py:441`: `if (token.plain and tag is None) or str(tag) == '!':`), and its composer then resolves it (`composer.py:152`: `if tag is None or str(tag) == '!':`). The stock `YAML(typ="safe", pure=True)` behaves the same way. The harness overrides other ruamel defaults (timestamps, merge keys, `%YAML`) but not this one. Its docstring (`tests/conftest.py:136-141`) says nothing about `!`, and `tests/test_loader.py:87-128` has no `!` case.

**Evidence.** The same inputs go to the harness loader (`load_yaml_text`) and to yaml 2.9.1 with the toolkit's options:

```
'a: ! 12'     harness {'a': 12}    yaml {"a":"12"}
'a: ! true'   harness {'a': True}  yaml {"a":"true"}
'a: ! 1.5'    harness {'a': 1.5}   yaml {"a":"1.5"}
"a: ! '12'"   harness {'a': 12}    yaml {"a":"12"}
'a: !'        harness {'a': None}  yaml {"a":""}
'a: !!str 12' harness {'a': '12'}  (control)
```

`tests/data/valid/Analysis-001-minimal.yaml` with one line added:

```yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
outputs:
  result:
    type: metric
    format: json
    label: ! 2024
```

```
Python: /outputs/result/label | ...#/$defs/Output__identifier_optional/properties/label/type | WRONG_TYPE | 2024 is not of type 'string'
Ajv:    VALID
```

The same split (WRONG_TYPE in Python, VALID in Ajv) holds for `label: ! "2024"`, `label: !`, `tags: [! 2024, ! true]` (two errors) and `commit: ! 1234567890123456789012345678901234567890` on an analysis declaration. The controls agree: `label: "2024"` is VALID in both, and `label: 2024` is WRONG_TYPE in both. No fixture, example or spec example uses `!`, so no current test passes for the wrong reason.

**Why it matters.** The harness is the repository's reference consumer loader ("Everything is validated as a consumer would", CLAUDE.md). On these files it disagrees with YAML 1.2.2 and with the JavaScript loader, so the two validators give different verdicts on a document YAML defines without ambiguity. `!` is YAML's documented way to force a string without quotes, for example on an all-digit commit id. The contract and the spec's text are unaffected.

**Suggested fix.** In `_core_schema_yaml`, install a Composer (or Parser) override that gives a `!`-tagged scalar `tag:yaml.org,2002:str`, and reject `!<!>`. Add `! 12`, `! '12'`, `!` and `!<!> x` to `tests/test_loader.py`. Optionally, write "an untagged plain scalar" in spec §3 (`docs/specification.md:176-178`).

### F143. The specification doesn't say when two keys are "the same" for `DUPLICATE_KEY`, and the harness compares them by Python equality

- **Lens:** Standards · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:168-169`, `docs/specification.md:1098`, `tests/conftest.py:29`, `tests/conftest.py:135-136`

**What is wrong.** §3 says a file is invalid if it has "a key written twice in one map". The catalogue says "Every key of a map is distinct". Neither says what makes two keys equal. §3 does make YAML 1.2.2 normative ("read strictly, under the core schema only", `docs/specification.md:163-165`), and YAML defines key equality as node equality: the same tag and the same canonical form. So an answer exists, but only by reference, and "written twice" points the other way, towards comparing the text. This is a wording issue. No rule is missing. D2 (design:56) says only that "duplicate keys are invalid YAML 1.2", and §12 and §13 say nothing about how keys are compared.

The harness doesn't follow YAML's rule. ruamel.yaml checks `if key in mapping:` (`ruamel/yaml/constructor.py:260`), which is Python equality. The `yaml` package, which the toolkit uses for Ajv, follows a third rule. On string keys all three agree, whatever the spelling: `a`, `'a'`, `"\x61"` and a block scalar `a` are one key in every loader. They differ only on non-string keys.

**Evidence.** YAML 1.2.2 §3.2.1.3: "Two scalars are equal only when their tags and canonical forms are equal character-by-character." Run through `conftest.load_yaml_text` and through yaml 2.9.1 (`{version:'1.2', schema:'core', uniqueKeys:true, merge:false}`):

| map keys | YAML 1.2.2 | harness (ruamel) | yaml 2.9.1 |
|---|---|---|---|
| `a`, `"a"`, `'a'`, `"\x61"` | duplicate | DuplicateKeyError | DUPLICATE_KEY |
| `1`, `1.0` | distinct | DuplicateKeyError | DUPLICATE_KEY |
| `1`, `true` | distinct | DuplicateKeyError | `{"1":"x","true":"y"}` |
| `0`, `false` | distinct | DuplicateKeyError | `{"0":"x","false":"y"}` |
| `.nan`, `.nan` | duplicate | `{nan: 'x', nan: 'y'}` | `{"NaN":"y"}` |

In an analysis with `decisions: {d: {options: {<k1>: {}, <k2>: {}}}}`:

```
(1, true):    py: PARSE-ERROR DuplicateKeyError     js: .../options/propertyNames/pattern | INVALID_ID
(1, 1.0):     py: PARSE-ERROR DuplicateKeyError     js: PARSE-ERROR DUPLICATE_KEY
(.nan, .nan): py: .../propertyNames/type | WRONG_TYPE   js: .../propertyNames/pattern | INVALID_ID
```

**Why it matters.** The harm is small. Valid ids are strings, and every map in `analysis.schema.json` has `propertyNames` or `additionalProperties: false`. So every file above is invalid whatever the loader; the loader only decides whether it gets `DUPLICATE_KEY` at parse or `INVALID_ID`/`WRONG_TYPE` at schema. The `DUPLICATE_KEY` fixtures use plain string keys, so they don't depend on this. But a validator author reading §3 has no definition to implement, and "written twice" invites a textual comparison, which would wrongly accept `seed_42` against `"seed_42"`.

**Suggested fix.** In §3 and the `DUPLICATE_KEY` row, say two keys are the same when they are equal YAML nodes (same tag and canonical form), so `a`, `'a'` and `"\x61"` are one key and `1` and `"1"` are two. Optionally, note in `conftest.load_yaml` that ruamel uses Python equality, which differs only on non-string keys.

### F144. §3 paraphrases the core schema loosely: it leaves out maps and sequences, and decides numbers by what "looks like a number"

- **Lens:** Standards, Edge cases · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:170-171`, `docs/specification.md:175-180`, `docs/specification.md:79`, `tests/conftest.py:39-46`

**What is wrong.** The normative rule is right: `astra.yaml` is YAML 1.2 "read strictly, under the core schema only" (`docs/specification.md:163-165`). The bullets after it paraphrase that rule loosely in two places. First, lines 170-171 say "the core schema's only types are strings, nulls, booleans, integers and floats". YAML 1.2.2 §10.3.1 says "The core schema uses the same tags as the JSON schema", and that schema adds to the failsafe schema's map, seq and str (§10.2.1, §10.1.1). Read in context, the sentence means scalar types, so it misleads no one, but as written it is false. Second, lines 176-178 say "a plain scalar that looks like a number is a number". The core schema doesn't go by what looks like a number. It goes by a fixed regex table (§10.3.2), and anything that matches none of the rows is a string. Several scalars that look like numbers are strings under that table, and the commit-id warning covers only the all-digit case. The contract and the harness follow the table (`tests/conftest.py:39-46`, `_CORE_SCALARS`). Only the prose is wrong. The design already names one of these traps ("`1_000` is a string", `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:511`), but the specification doesn't.

**Evidence.** Each scalar loaded as `a: <scalar>`:

```
scalar        | harness  | yaml 2.9.1 core | ruamel 0.19.1 YAML(typ='safe') | js-yaml 4.1.0 CORE_SCHEMA | PyYAML 6.0.3
1_000         | '1_000'  | '1_000'         | 1000     | 1000     | 1000
0b101         | '0b101'  | '0b101'         | 5        | 5        | 5
-0x1          | '-0x1'   | '-0x1'          | -1       | -1       | -1
80:20         | '80:20'  | '80:20'         | '80:20'  | '80:20'  | 4820
010           | 10       | 10              | 10       | 10       | 8
1e3           | 1000.0   | 1000            | 1000.0   | 1000     | '1e3'
+.nan         | '+.nan'  | '+.nan'         | '+.nan'  | '+.nan'  | '+.nan'
0e1234…5678   | 0.0      | 0               | 0.0      | 0        | '0e1234…'
```

Minimal Analysis documents, validated against the committed schema (Python and Ajv agree):

```
label: 1_000                                    VALID
label: 0b101                                    VALID
label: 80:20                                    VALID
label: .inf                                     /outputs/result/label  WRONG_TYPE  inf is not of type 'string'
label: 0x1F                                     /outputs/result/label  WRONG_TYPE  31 is not of type 'string'
commit: 0e12345678901234567890123456789012345678
                                                /analyses/upstream/commit  WRONG_TYPE  0.0 is not of type 'string'
```

Loaded with ruamel's stock safe loader instead of the harness, `label: 1_000` and `label: 0b101` fail with WRONG_TYPE, though the contract accepts them.

**Why it matters.** Authors can't tell from §3 which unquoted values are numbers. Implementers need the exact table, not an impression of it, especially if they start from a YAML 1.1 library. Two loaders that call themselves YAML 1.2 (ruamel's stock safe loader, js-yaml's CORE_SCHEMA) depart from the table. An implementer who reads line 79's "any strict YAML 1.2 loader" can pick one of them and reject labels the contract accepts. A hex commit id of digits and one `e` is a float. §3's "all-digit" warning misses this case, though its advice to quote commit ids covers it. The values involved are rare in real files.

**Suggested fix.** Write "the core schema's only scalar types are …". Replace "looks like a number" with a citation of YAML 1.2.2 §10.3.2's table, with examples on each side: `1e3`, `.inf` and `0x1F` are numbers, `1_000`, `0b101`, `12:30` and `+.nan` are strings, and `010` is 10. Optionally, say that some loaders sold as YAML 1.2 depart from the table.

### F145. The pattern guard checks only how a pattern ends, so a bare `$` in an earlier branch passes

- **Lens:** Standards · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `tests/test_contract.py:165-170`, `tests/test_contract.py:286-296`, `CLAUDE.md:19`, `docs/specification.md:245-248`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:210-215`

**What is wrong.** `pattern_problems` accepts an anchored pattern if it ends in `$(?!\n)` or, for LinkML's `^(?:…)$` wrapper, in `$(?!\n))$`. Only the last branch has to carry the lookahead. A pattern with a top-level alternation can therefore end correctly and still have a bare `$` in an earlier branch. That `$` matches before a final newline in Python and doesn't in ECMA-262. The guard passes it. This needs no wrapper: a slot's own `^a$|^b$(?!\n)` passes too. The guard is faithful to the rule as written ("Every anchored pattern ends in `$(?!\n)`"), but the rule is a condition on the ending only, and that isn't enough. The defect is latent: the five structured syntaxes (`types.yaml:41,53,65,78,90`) have no top-level `|`, and every committed pattern is sound today.

This is not the deferred item at design `:753-754` ("the pattern guard as a whitelist (inline flags, `\p{…}` and similar slip through a blacklist)"). `|`, `$`, `(?:` and `(?!` are all legitimate constructs, so a construct whitelist would let this through as well.

**Evidence.** The ending check (`tests/test_contract.py:168-170`):

```python
if pattern.startswith("^"):
    if not pattern.endswith((r"$(?!\n)", r"$(?!\n))$")):
        problems.append(r"anchored, but doesn't end in $(?!\n)")
```

The repository's `pattern_problems`, then Python `re.search(p, "a\n")` and Node `new RegExp(p).test("a\n")`:

```
'^(?:a|b$(?!\n))$'            guard: [] | python: True  | node: false
'^(?:[a-z]+|[0-9]+$(?!\n))$'  guard: [] | python: True  | node: false
'^a$|^b$(?!\n)'               guard: [] | python: True  | node: false
control '^(?:a|b)$(?!\n)'     guard: [] | python: False | node: false
```

End to end in a clone at 96c902a: `reference` at `types.yaml:65` is rewritten as an alternation describing the same language, `'^{id}(\.{id})*|{id}(\.{id})*#{id}$(?!\n)'`. LinkML emits `^(?:^[a-z][a-z0-9_]*(\.[a-z][a-z0-9_]*)*|…#[a-z][a-z0-9_]*$(?!\n))$`, and `pytest tests/test_contract.py -k pattern` gives `3 passed`. An output with `inputs: ["data\n"]` is then `VALID` under jsonschema, and Ajv 8.20 reports `/outputs/result/inputs/0 … INVALID_REFERENCE`. Under the committed contract both report `INVALID_REFERENCE`.

**Why it matters.** The guard exists to stop Python validators (astra-tools) and Ajv (astra-typescript, editors) from disagreeing on a value. A future pattern with an alternation, written in a slot or as a structured syntax, would ship such a disagreement while the guard stays green.

**Suggested fix.** Strip a leading `^(?:` and trailing `)$` wrapper, then require every `$` outside a lookahead to be followed immediately by `(?!\n)`. The lookahead exemption keeps `Input.path`'s `(?:/|$)` legal. Alternatively, check behaviourally that each anchored pattern rejects a matching value with `"\n"` appended. `partial_match: true` alone does not close the gap.

### F146. The PDF fragment syntax is cited as RFC 3778, which RFC 8118 obsoletes

- **Lens:** Standards · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:515-517`, `src/astra/schema/insight.yaml:49-50`, `src/astra/schema/insight.yaml:57-58`, `src/astra/schema/generated/analysis.schema.json:181,184`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:274`, `astra-rfcs/tidy-up/astra-spec/rfcs/NNNN-schema-tidy-up.md:75`

**What is wrong.** The normative definition of `FragmentSelector.value` cites RFC 3778 for the PDF fragment identifier syntax. RFC 8118 obsoleted RFC 3778 in March 2017. Its §3 says the syntax now lives in ISO 32000-2, and that ISO 32000-2 wins any disagreement. RFC 8118 and ISO 32000 appear nowhere in the repository. The spec follows its sources here: the draft tidy-up RFC (line 75, "an RFC 3778 fragment such as `page=6`") and the design (line 274, "described as an RFC 3778 PDF fragment") use the same citation, and design §11 records no departure on it.

**Evidence.** The repository's citations:

```
docs/specification.md:515-517
  whose `value` is a PDF fragment identifier
  ([RFC 3778](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc3778)) without the `#`, such
  as `page=6`.
src/astra/schema/insight.yaml:49    A place in a PDF, given as a PDF fragment identifier (RFC 3778), ...
src/astra/schema/insight.yaml:57    The PDF fragment identifier (RFC 3778), without the leading `#`, ...
analysis.schema.json:181,184        (the same two descriptions, generated)
```

RFC 8118 (https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8118.txt):

```
9:    Obsoletes: 3778
26:   media type registration of "application/pdf".  It obsoletes RFC 3778.
175:  The specification of fragment identifiers for PDF appeared originally
      in [RFC3778] and is now included in ISO 32000-2 [ISOPDF2]. ...  Any
      disagreements between [ISOPDF2] and this document should be resolved
      in favor of the ISO 32000-2 definition.
197:  page=<pageNum>
         Identifies a specified (physical) page; the first page in the
         document has a pageNum value of 1.
```

RFC 3778 line 180 says only "page=<pagenum> Open the specified (physical) page". It does not say where page numbers start. Its parameter list (`highlight`, `nameddest`, `page`, `view`, `viewrect`, `zoom`) lacks RFC 8118's `comment`, `ef`, `fdf`, `search` and `structelem`.

The citation is the only definition of the syntax. Design :277 says "`FragmentSelector` has no `conformsTo`: the description fixes the syntax", and in the contract `value` is a bare `"type": "string"` (analysis.schema.json:183-186). The W3C Web Annotation model (§4.2.1) still lists `http://tools.ietf.org/rfc/rfc3778` as the PDF fragment specification IRI.

**Why it matters.** A tool author who follows the spec's link to parse `value` lands on an obsolete document. That document has an outdated parameter list and does not say whether `page=6` counts from 0 or 1. The ASTRA spec does not say either. No document validates differently: `value` takes any string and §9 has no rule on its syntax. So this is a wording issue in the normative text.

**Suggested fix.** In spec §5, both `insight.yaml` descriptions, the tidy-up draft and design :274, cite RFC 8118 §3 (which defers to ISO 32000-2) and say that `page=` counts physical pages from 1. Optionally note that Web Annotation identifies this syntax by the RFC 3778 IRI. Then regenerate.

### F147. Nothing says how Evidence's `quote` and `location` combine

- **Lens:** Standards · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:509-517`, `docs/specification.md:1347-1350`, `src/astra/schema/insight.yaml:31-33`, `src/astra/schema/insight.yaml:87-95`, `tests/data/valid/Analysis-006-insights.yaml:26-30`

**What is wrong.** Evidence carries two W3C Web Annotation selectors side by side: `quote`, a TextQuoteSelector, and `location`, a FragmentSelector. The specification gives both the same role: each "locates the passage the claim rests on". Web Annotation allows two readings of a pair like this. In the first, the selectors are alternative descriptions of one segment (§4.2). In the second, the quote refines the fragment and is sought only inside it (§4.2.9, `refinedBy`). Neither the specification, nor the LinkML descriptions, nor the design, nor the tidy-up RFC says which reading ASTRA means. The schema's wording leans toward alternatives: each selector selects from "the cited work". On `main` the reading was stated: the quote was "The authoritative anchor for verification" and the location a "Location hint". Commit 0a6d046 removed both phrases and recorded no replacement.

**Evidence.** `docs/specification.md:509-517`:

> It locates the passage the claim rests on with W3C Web Annotation selectors: `quote`, a TextQuoteSelector (`oa:TextQuoteSelector`), whose `exact` is the passage as the work writes it, with the `prefix` and `suffix` around it; and `location`, a FragmentSelector (`oa:FragmentSelector`), whose `value` is a PDF fragment identifier ... such as `page=6`.

`src/astra/schema/insight.yaml`:

```
31-33  A passage of the cited work, quoted exactly, with the text just before
       and after it to tell it apart where the same words occur more than once.
90     description: The passage of the cited work the claim rests on.
95     The place in the cited work the claim rests on, such as `page=6`.
```

The slots have no `slot_uri`, so they map to `astra:quote` and `astra:location`, not to `oa:hasSelector` or `oa:refinedBy`. The specification says "No RDF reading of `astra.yaml` is published yet" (`docs/specification.md:1347-1348`). `git grep -n -i "hasSelector\|refinedBy\|within the page\|on that page"` finds nothing.

Web Annotation Data Model (https://www.w3.org/TR/annotation-model/):

- §4.2: "Multiple Selectors SHOULD select the same content, however some Selectors will not have the same precision as others. Consuming user agents MUST pick one of the described segments, if they are different."
- §4.2.4: with several matches, "the selection SHOULD be treated as matching all of the matches."
- §4.2.9: a TextQuoteSelector "that further modifies a FragmentSelector"; `refinedBy` is the selector that "SHOULD be applied to the results of the first."

The fixture (`Analysis-006-insights.yaml:26-30`, `page=6` with a one-sentence quote) fits either reading, because its two selectors agree. Both validators accept every combination, so the contract is unaffected.

**Why it matters.** Take `quote: {exact: "the forest"}` with `location: {value: "page=6"}`, where "the forest" also occurs on page 9. A tool that reads refinement searches page 6 only. A tool that reads alternatives may pick either segment, and one that reads only the quote highlights every match. Editors and citation tools would disagree. No §9 rule reads the selectors' meaning, so validators, checkers and runners are not affected.

**Suggested fix.** Add one sentence to §5 (after line 517) and to `Evidence.location`'s description: "When both are given, the quote is sought within the location (a FragmentSelector `oa:refinedBy` the TextQuoteSelector)." Or state the alternatives reading, or restore `main`'s (the quote is authoritative, the location a hint). Tom decides.

### F148. §10 calls the record's root node "the record", but in PROV it stands for the result, and it also carries the record's `spec_version`

- **Lens:** Standards · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:1341-1343`, `docs/specification.md:961`, `src/astra/schema/provenance.yaml:30-33`, `src/astra/schema/provenance.yaml:35-40`, `src/astra/schema/provenance.yaml:58-63`

**What is wrong.** Both RDF readings of a record produce one root node. That node has `prov:wasGeneratedBy` the run of the recipe, and it carries the result's `address`, `content_hash` and `size`. As PROV, it is the result. §10 and the normative class description call it the record. The runner writes the record, and the recipe's run does not make it. The same node also carries `astra:spec_version`, which describes the record document and not the result. Whichever way the node is read, one of its properties belongs to the other thing.

**Evidence.** LinkML's conversion, the one §10 names (`-P urn=urn:` is needed for the fixture's `urn:` environment IRI):

```
$ uv run linkml-convert -s src/astra/schema/provenance.yaml -C ProvenanceRecord \
    -t ttl -P urn=urn: tests/data/valid/ProvenanceRecord-001-full.yaml
_:c14n0 a prov:Entity ;
	prov:wasGeneratedBy _:c14n1 ;
	astra:address "subanalysis1.output1#u1" ;
	astra:content_hash "sha256:7af13cf1...b83e" ;
	astra:size 732 ;
	astra:spec_version "0.1.0" .
```

rdflib reads the §8 example with the committed `provenance.context.jsonld` and gives an untyped root node with the same five properties.

- `docs/specification.md:1341-1342`: "a record is PROV-O: the record is a `prov:Entity` that `prov:wasGeneratedBy` its `generated_by`".
- `docs/specification.md:961`: "A runner writes a record for every result it makes".
- `provenance.yaml:30-33`: `class_uri: prov:Entity`, "The provenance of one result, which the runner that made it writes as JSON".
- `provenance.yaml:62-63`: `slot_uri: prov:wasGeneratedBy`, "The run of the recipe that made the result."
- `provenance.yaml:39`: `spec_version` is "The version of the ASTRA specification the record follows".
- PROV-O §4.1, `prov:wasGeneratedBy`: "Generation is the completion of production of a new entity by an activity. This entity did not exist before generation and becomes available for usage after this generation."
- PROV-O §4.2, `prov:Bundle`: "A bundle is a named set of provenance descriptions, and is itself an Entity".

The record holds the run's `finished` time, so it is written after the run ends. It cannot be the entity that run generated. The same §10 sentence treats the `RecordedInput` nodes, which also carry `content_hash` and `size`, as the things the run used. By that pattern the root node is the result. Neither RFC 0005 (it says only "a record is PROV-O", `rfcs/0005:132`) nor the design says what the root node denotes.

**Why it matters.** A PROV consumer that reads §10 literally attributes the record file's creation to the recipe's run, and the specification version to the result. The data is right only if the node is read as the result. Validation and the rule catalogue are unaffected, and the root is a blank node, so no implementer has to give it an identity.

**Suggested fix.** In §10 and in the `ProvenanceRecord` description, say that the root node stands for the result, a `prov:Entity` that `prov:wasGeneratedBy` its `generated_by`. Note that `spec_version` describes the record, or move it off that node in a later RDF reading (for example a `prov:Bundle`).

### F149. `MAY` is an allowed rule level, but §9 gives a broken `MAY` row no outcome, and the runner row makes it non-conformance

- **Lens:** Standards · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:1035`, `docs/specification.md:1036-1037`, `docs/specification.md:1056-1060`, `docs/specification.md:1081`, `tools/rules.py:45`, `tools/rules.py:322-324`, `tests/test_rules.py:61`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:63`

**What is wrong.** §9 allows `MUST`, `SHOULD` or `MAY` as a row's level. It also says that every row's code "names what is wrong when the rule is broken". The spec adopts BCP 14 (:11-16), and under RFC 2119 §5 a `MAY` item is "truly optional", so nothing can be wrong when it is left out. The consequence table (:1056-1060) gives no outcome for a `MAY` row. Its parse, schema and validator row covers only `MUST` (error) and `SHOULD` (warning). Its runner row ignores the level: "The runner doesn't conform." Read against a `MAY` row, it makes a runner that leaves out an optional item non-conforming, which contradicts BCP 14. The extractor accepts `MAY` in all four tables, including the schema table, where the JSON Schema keyword behind the row can only fail the file. No row uses `MAY` today, so the gap is latent.

**Evidence.** The definitions, in `docs/specification.md`:

- :1035 "**Level:** `MUST`, `SHOULD` or `MAY` (RFC 2119), applied to that sentence."
- :1036-1037 "**Code:** names what is wrong when the rule is broken. It is what a tool reports."
- :1058 "| parse, schema, validator | The file fails: the code is reported as an **error** for a `MUST` and as a **warning** for a `SHOULD` |"
- :1060 "| runner | The runner doesn't conform. Where a rule says the runner refuses, it reports the code |"

`tools/rules.py:45` has `LEVELS = {"MUST", "SHOULD", "MAY"}`. With one row's level changed to `MAY` in memory, `rules.extract_rules` accepts it in every table:

```
validator MAY -> PIN_LIKE_COMMENT validator MAY | No `inputs` entry has a line comment that looks like a pin (needs the source text)
schema MAY -> INVALID_COMMIT schema MAY | Every `commit` is a full commit id: 40 or 64 lowercase hexadecimal digits
check MAY -> ABSENT_RESULT check MAY | Every result's recorded bytes are at its default place
runner MAY -> NOT_AT_COMMIT runner MAY | A runner drives an analysis from a commit on exactly its files at that commit
control LOWER -> REJECTED: line 1199, ### runner, row `NOT_AT_COMMIT`: unknown level 'MIGHT': a level is MUST, SHOULD or MAY
```

No row in `rules.json` is `MAY`:

```
$ python3 -c "import json,collections; r=json.load(open('src/astra/schema/generated/rules.json')); print(len(r['rules']), collections.Counter(x['level'] for x in r['rules']))"
65 Counter({'MUST': 59, 'SHOULD': 6})
```

The spec already keeps permissions out of the rule rows. RFC 0005's two `MAY` items go to the statuses table (:1173, `modified`) and to prose (:1203-1205): "Two duties that are not rules of their own, because nothing can check them: a runner SHOULD let someone see what an environment it recorded holds, and MAY enforce `UNDECLARED_READ` (with a sandbox, say)." The extractor's test does the opposite. `tests/test_rules.py:61` models the sandbox duty as a row, "| `NO_SANDBOX` | MAY | A runner runs each recipe in a sandbox | `main` |", and expects `"level": "MAY"`.

The level set is settled. D9 (`design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:63`) reads "a level (`MUST`/`SHOULD`/`MAY`)", and tidy-up change 28 (`NNNN-schema-tidy-up.md:199`) repeats it. Design §7 (:317) has the same consequence table, with the same gap.

**Why it matters.** Nobody is affected in v0.1. The first `MAY` row anyone adds leaves validators without a defined outcome. Read literally, it also makes runners that leave out the option non-conforming. Two implementers would then resolve it differently. A `MAY` row in the schema layer could not be honoured at all, because its keyword always fails the file.

**Suggested fix.** Keeping D9 as it is: add `MAY` to the consequence table, for example "a `MAY` row names an optional behaviour of a tool; nothing fails, and the code is what a tool that implements it reports", and reject `MAY` in the parse and schema tables. The alternative, dropping `MAY` from the levels, would need D9 and change 28 amended too. Tom decides.

### F150. The extractor's keyword/level check misses a keyword split across Markdown tokens

- **Lens:** Standards · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `tools/rules.py:110`, `tools/rules.py:177`, `tools/rules.py:326-328`, `tools/rules.py:7`, `CLAUDE.md:39`, `docs/specification.md:1081-1083`

**What is wrong.** §9 says a rule's Rule cell "uses no RFC 2119 keyword in capitals, in its text or in a code span, of another level" (`docs/specification.md:1081-1083`). `tools/rules.py:7` and `CLAUDE.md:39` present the check as a guarantee: a contradicting keyword fails `just gen-schema`. The check does not look at what the cell renders to. `_text` joins the text and code-span tokens with a space and drops inline HTML (`tools/rules.py:110`). `_keywords` splits words on any character that is not alphanumeric or `_` (`tools/rules.py:177`). So a keyword split across two tokens, or broken by an invisible character, is never seen, though it renders as one capitalised word. Intraword emphasis, a link on part of the word, an empty HTML element, a code span plus text, a soft hyphen and a zero-width space all do it. With a link, `rules.json` then publishes a whole-word `MUST` in the statement of a SHOULD rule.

The space join is deliberate (commit 765624b): it catches `` see`SHOULD` `` and `[the](#a)SHOULD`, and `tests/test_rules.py:218-227` relies on it. No test covers a keyword split across tokens. The current catalogue is clean: all 65 extracted rules match the committed `rules.json`, and none has a keyword split this way.

**Evidence.** The Rule cell of `REDUNDANT_PIN` (`docs/specification.md:1149`, level SHOULD) is rewritten in memory, then `rules.extract_rules` runs on the spec:

```
control: plain MUST          REJECTED: line 1149, ### validator, row `REDUNDANT_PIN`: its rule says MUST, which contradicts its level, SHOULD
control: *MUST*, <b>MUST</b>, M&#85;ST, `MUST`   REJECTED
intraword emphasis MU*ST*    ACCEPTED  level=SHOULD   (renders as '... built once MUST have a pin')
empty inline HTML            ACCEPTED  statement='... MU<span></span>ST have a pin'
link on part [MU](#x)ST      ACCEPTED  statement='No reference to an output built once MUST have a pin'
code span + text `MU`ST      ACCEPTED  statement='... `MU`ST have a pin'
soft hyphen MU&shy;ST        ACCEPTED  statement='... MU\xadST have a pin'
zero-width space U+200B      ACCEPTED
```

The link case yields the entry `{"code": "REDUNDANT_PIN", "level": "SHOULD", "statement": "No reference to an output built once MUST have a pin"}`. The other splits leave markup or invisible characters in `statement`, and contradict the level only once rendered. The `` `MU`ST `` case is borderline, since §9 names text and code spans separately.

**Why it matters.** The check is documented as strict, and it is the only guard that keeps a rule's wording consistent with its level in `rules.json`, which other implementations consume. A careless edit (a link on part of a word, a stray HTML tag) can publish a statement whose keyword contradicts its level, and the build passes.

**Suggested fix.** Check the union of the keywords found in the space-joined token text (as now) and in the rendered plain text of the cell (inline HTML removed, U+00AD and U+200B dropped). This keeps the existing tests and catches the split cases. Add a test for each split.

### F151. Each schema's `$id` is the same in every version, and §10 doesn't say which version it names

- **Lens:** Standards · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `src/astra/schema/generated/analysis.schema.json:524`, `src/astra/schema/generated/provenance.schema.json:112`, `src/astra/schema/generated/provenance.open.schema.json:112`, `docs/specification.md:1217-1231`, `docs/specification.md:1118`, `docs/specification.md:1180`, `justfile:35`

**What is wrong.** Each JSON Schema's `$id` is `https://w3id.org/astra/` and its file name (`justfile:35`). A release changes only `$comment`, so every version of `analysis.schema.json` uses the same `$id`. The rules still need schemas from more than one version. `INVALID_RECORD` checks each record "against the record schema of its `spec_version`", so a checker keeps the closed record schemas of older versions. JSON Schema 2019-09 says one URI identifies one schema. §10 gives each file one URI and never says whether that URI names one version's schema or the latest one. The `errors` keys in `rules.json` are built from the `$id`, so they carry no version either.

**Evidence.** The ids and versions in the committed files:

```
analysis.schema.json:2:          "$comment": "analysis schema version 0.0.14",
analysis.schema.json:524:        "$id": "https://w3id.org/astra/analysis.schema.json",
provenance.schema.json:112:      "$id": "https://w3id.org/astra/provenance.schema.json",
provenance.open.schema.json:112: "$id": "https://w3id.org/astra/provenance.open.schema.json",
```

`docs/specification.md:1228-1231`: "Each JSON Schema's `$id` is its URI, so the closed and open record schemas are distinct resources. [...] the version of the schema it was generated from is in its `$comment`."

`docs/specification.md:1180` (`INVALID_RECORD`): "Every record is valid against the record schema of its `spec_version`, or against the open variant when that version is newer than the checker's".

JSON Schema 2019-09 core §8.2.4.5: "there is no way for a URI to identify more than one schema. When multiple schemas try to identify as the same URI, validators SHOULD raise an error condition."

The reproducer took each schema, made a copy whose only change was `$comment` set to 0.2.0, and loaded both into one validator. The two libraries behave differently.

```
Ajv 8.20, one instance:
  analysis.schema.json: v2 THROWS: schema with key or id "https://w3id.org/astra/analysis.schema.json" already exists
  (same for provenance.schema.json and provenance.open.schema.json; separate instances: both compile)

Python referencing, one Registry, v2 = v1 + required: label:
  registry with two resources at https://w3id.org/astra/analysis.schema.json -> no error; holds: analysis schema version 0.2.0
  validating a 0.1.0 doc through $ref ... in that registry: ["'label' is a required property"]
  validating directly against v1: []
```

There are 266 `errors` keys in `rules.json`, and every one begins with an unversioned `$id`.

**Why it matters.** Nothing breaks while only one version exists. No tag up to v0.0.14 ships generated JSON Schemas. The generated schemas have no external `$ref`, so a tool that builds one validator or one Ajv instance per version works in both languages. The cost comes from the gap in §10. Once 0.2 ships, a tool in astra-tools or astra-typescript that puts both versions into one Ajv instance throws. A tool that puts both into one `referencing` registry raises no error and checks old records against the new schema. A schema fetched from `astra-spec.org/X.Y.Z/schema/` still declares the unversioned w3id `$id`, and an error's keyword location doesn't say which version's `rules.json` to look it up in. The design's own reason for per-file ids applies across versions too: "R15: distinct resources need distinct ids" (`design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-changes.md:33`). §12 of the design defers nearby versioning questions but not this one.

**Suggested fix.** One option is to put the version in each `$id` (for example `https://w3id.org/astra/0.1/analysis.schema.json`), so the `rules.json` keys carry it too. The other is to add one sentence to §10: the URI names the latest version, and a tool keeps one validator and one `rules.json` per version.

### F152. §4 Addresses puts a condition's leading `~` inside its address

- **Lens:** Standards · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:281-283`, `docs/specification.md:214`

**What is wrong.** This is a wording slip. The parenthetical in §4 Addresses treats a condition like an option reference: "the address is everything before the last segment". Read literally, that makes the address of the condition `~correction.none` the string `~correction`, which is not an `address`. The grammar puts the `~` outside the option reference (`condition = [ "~" ] option-reference`), so the address is `correction`. Every other normative text gets this right. The grammar does (line 214), the `condition` type description does ("An option reference, optionally preceded by `~`", `src/astra/schema/types.yaml:86`), and so does §5 ("`~d.o` holds when it isn't; `d` can be a decision of a declared analysis, by its address", lines 458-460). The design copies the grammar but has nothing that matches the parenthetical, so this is a slip in the prose and not a recorded choice. The contract is not affected: `^~?{id}(\.{id})+$` keeps the `~` outside the id segments.

**Evidence.**

```
docs/specification.md:211: address          = id *( "." id )               ; [declaration "."]... element
docs/specification.md:214: condition        = [ "~" ] option-reference     ; "~": the option is not selected
docs/specification.md:281: An address is read in the analysis whose `astra.yaml` it is written in. (In
docs/specification.md:282: an option reference or a condition, the address is everything before the last
docs/specification.md:283: segment, which is the option.) Every segment of an address but the last is
```

Parsing values with the ABNF block from the specification (`abnf` 2.9.1) gives:

```
condition '~correction.none': address node(s) = ['correction']; text before last '.' = '~correction', matches address: False
condition '~iris.random_seed.seed_42': address node(s) = ['iris.random_seed']; text before last '.' = '~iris.random_seed', matches address: False
```

Pinned references are not part of this defect. The paragraph talks about addresses, and an address never holds `#` (`reference = address [ "#" id ]`, line 212). §4 Pins (lines 311-313) says where the pin goes.

**Why it matters.** Implementers of `UNRESOLVED_ADDRESS`, and report bridges that resolve conditions, may follow the prose instead of the grammar. If they do, they will look up `~correction` and report a valid condition as unresolved. The surrounding text makes that unlikely, but the parenthetical is the one place that says otherwise.

**Suggested fix.** "(In an option reference, the address is the segments before the last, which is the option; a condition's leading `~` and a reference's `#` pin are not part of the address.)" Or drop the parenthetical and point to the grammar's `address`.

### F153. Nothing checks §4's ABNF against the schema's id grammar, so the two can drift while `just test` passes

- **Lens:** Standards · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:208-215`, `src/astra/schema/types.yaml:24`, `src/astra/schema/types.yaml:30-31`, `design/2026-11-03-v0.1-schema-design.md:62`, `tests/test_specification.py:30`, `tests/test_types.py:18`

**What is wrong.** The grammar of `id`, `address`, `reference`, `option-reference` and `condition` is written by hand twice. §4 of the specification gives it as normative ABNF. `types.yaml` gives it as `settings.id` plus five `structured_pattern`s. D8 puts grammars in the specification on purpose, and gives as its reason "One statement of each fact, so the two can't drift" (design:62). Nothing reads the ABNF block, so nothing ties the two statements together. They agree today. A change to the ABNF is not caught at all. Some changes to `settings.id` are caught, by the hand-written value table in `tests/test_types.py` and by fixtures, but neither of these reads the ABNF.

**Evidence.** The two statements, `docs/specification.md:209-214` and `src/astra/schema/types.yaml:31`:

```abnf
id               = lower *( lower / DIGIT / "_" )
lower            = %x61-7A                      ; a-z
address          = id *( "." id )               ; [declaration "."]... element
reference        = address [ "#" id ]           ; "#" pins a universe
option-reference = address "." id              ; a decision's address, then an option id
condition        = [ "~" ] option-reference     ; "~": the option is not selected
```

```yaml
settings:
  id: "[a-z][a-z0-9_]*"
```

Nothing reads the block. The only test that reads specification fences takes yaml and json (`tests/test_specification.py:30`: `` _FENCE = re.compile(r"^```(yaml|json)\n(.*?)^```$", ...) ``):

```
$ grep -rn -i 'abnf' tests tools justfile pyproject.toml .github .pre-commit-config.yaml .yamllint.yaml; echo "grep exit=$?"
grep exit=1
```

They agree today. A mechanical ABNF-to-regex translation, compared with the patterns in `analysis.schema.json` on all 579,195 strings of length 0 to 5 over `a z 0 9 _ . # ~ A - \n space é !`, finds no difference for any of the five rules.

Drift in the ABNF, in a clone:

```
-id               = lower *( lower / DIGIT / "_" )
+id               = ALPHA *( ALPHA / DIGIT / "_" / "-" / "." )
-reference        = address [ "#" id ]           ; "#" pins a universe
+reference        = address                      ; no pin
-condition        = [ "~" ] option-reference     ; "~": the option is not selected
+condition        = [ "!" ] option-reference     ; "!": the option is not selected
$ just test
============================= 361 passed in 10.25s =============================
✓ No problems found
```

Drift in the schema, in a clone, with `id: "[a-z][a-zA-Z0-9_]*"`, after `just gen-schema`:

```
$ just test
============================= 361 passed in 9.87s ==============================
✓ No problems found
```

After this change an `outputs` key `fScore` validates. The committed contract rejects it (`INVALID_ID`, `'fScore' does not match '^(?:^[a-z][a-z0-9_]*$(?!\\n))$'`), and so does §4's `lower = %x61-7A`. A change that admits `-` is caught: 3 tests fail (`test_types.py` `id-invalid-'rf-standard'`, fixtures Analysis-140 and Analysis-144).

**Why it matters.** It bites spec editors and reviewers. After an edit to either side, the normative grammar and the contract can disagree with no failing test. Then an implementer who follows §4 and one who follows the JSON Schema accept different ids, and §9's `INVALID_ID` ("matches the id grammar") contradicts the §10 contract. The repository already ties its other hand-written normative block, the §9 tables, to the contract through `rules.json`. The grammar has no such link.

**Suggested fix.** Add a dev-only test that reads the §4 `abnf` fence, translates each rule (with the `abnf` package, or a small translator for this regular subset), and compares it with its named type's generated pattern over enumerated strings and mutants. A cheaper option is to assert that the fence's text equals a copy kept next to `settings` in `types.yaml`.

### F154. `modified`'s "the stand-in names another hash" doesn't say the other hash must be a SHA-256

- **Lens:** Standards · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `docs/specification.md:1011-1014`, `docs/specification.md:1172-1173`, `docs/specification.md:1184`, `docs/specification.md:888-892`, `docs/specification.md:919-920`, `rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:41-42`, `rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:217`, `rfcs/0005-result-provenance-and-layout.md:244`

**What is wrong.** This is a wording gap. The bytes status `modified` holds when "the stand-in names another hash" (:1173; :1012-1013 says "a stand-in naming another hash"). RFC 0005 wrote the same test, but with SHA-256 stand-ins in mind: the git-annex key `SHA256E-s<size>--<SHA-256 hash>` (:41) and the LFS pointer's `oid sha256:` (:42). The RFC says "Both carry the content hash and size in its record" (:217). The specification keeps the test and drops that scope. It names git-annex and Git LFS only "for example" (:888-892) and names no key backend. A git-annex key from any backend other than SHA256 names a digest of the recorded bytes under another algorithm. Read literally, that is "another hash", and a checker reports `modified` for the right bytes. The careful reading still holds up: a digest under another algorithm doesn't show the bytes changed, so the result falls to `absent` (:1172), and `modified` is only a `MAY`. But a reader has to piece that together from :919, :1011-1012 and :1172.

**Evidence.** These are the same 19 bytes, `{"accuracy": 0.93}\n`, added under each backend (git-annex 10.20260421):

```
bytes: 74fc5701c3d6a4f2096ff0bf4000af5b919ef8c2aa96f51d3894e4f3cff15ba0 size 19
results/u1/md5e.json -> MD5E-s19--ca23f635938d012b746f98d8917b5b12.json
results/u1/sha1e.json -> SHA1E-s19--cb00a4490dfe4314305f417f38c723a7e1292539.json
results/u1/sha256e.json -> SHA256E-s19--74fc5701c3d6a4f2096ff0bf4000af5b919ef8c2aa96f51d3894e4f3cff15ba0.json
results/u1/sha512e.json -> SHA512E-s19--c5f35ca5...6490.json
results/u1/blake2b256e.json -> BLAKE2B256E-s19--cf5628a9...1593.json
```

A record for these bytes (`content_hash: sha256:74fc57...ba0`, `size: 19`) validates. DataLad 1.3.4 writes `* annex.backend=MD5E` to a new dataset's `.gitattributes`, and the key it makes for these bytes is `MD5E-s19--ca23f635938d012b746f98d8917b5b12.json`. A Git LFS pointer made with an extension (git-lfs 3.8.1, a gzip clean filter) names the record's hash in `ext-0-gz sha256:74fc57...ba0`, but its `oid` and `size` (`sha256:01759ba5...`, `39`) describe the gzipped object. That is the same gap. Keys that name no hash (WORM, VURL) are not affected: they fall to `absent`.

**Why it matters.** It bites research datasets managed with DataLad, which use MD5E by default. If a checker compares hash strings across algorithms, it tells the author that correct results are `modified`, i.e. corrupt. Two conforming checkers can report `modified` and `absent` for the same clone.

**Suggested fix.** At :1012-1013 and :1173, write "a stand-in naming a SHA-256 hash or a size other than the record's". A stand-in that names neither gives `absent`.

### F155. The url pattern accepts a bare `https://`, `file://` or `ssh://` through its scp-like branch

- **Lens:** Edge cases · **Severity:** Minor · **Where:** `src/astra/schema/analysis.yaml:375`, `src/astra/schema/generated/analysis.schema.json:46`, `docs/specification.md:644-647`

**What is wrong.** The `url` pattern has two branches. The `scheme://…` branch requires at least one character after `://`. The scp-like branch also matches every `scheme://…` value, reading it as host `<scheme>` and path `//…`. The two branches overlap completely, so the scheme branch's `+` never rejects anything. The schema therefore accepts exactly the bare `[A-Za-z][A-Za-z0-9+.-]+://`: any scheme of two or more letters with nothing after it. One-letter schemes (`a://`) are rejected, but only because of the drive-letter lookahead. git reads these values as the scheme form with no host or path, not as scp-like, and fails. So they fit neither of "git's two forms" that the specification allows.

**Evidence.** The pattern (`analysis.yaml:375`; the same string in `analysis.schema.json:46`):

```
'^(?:[A-Za-z][A-Za-z0-9+.-]*://[^\x00-\x20\x7f]+|(?![A-Za-z]:)(?:[^\x00-\x20\x7f/@:]+@)?[^\x00-\x20\x7f/@:]+:[^\x00-\x20\x7f]+)$(?!\n)'
```

A minimal document:

```yaml
spec_version: "0.1.0"
outputs:
  result:
    type: metric
    format: json
analyses:
  up:
    url: "https://"
    commit: "d1570f85f45af286e965bf70c451031763c5deee"
```

The Python `jsonschema` validator and Ajv both accept it, and they accept the same document with `file://`, `ssh://` and `git+ssh://`. Both reject the controls `a`, `a://` and `https:` with INVALID_VALUE at `/analyses/up/url`. Split at the top-level `|`:

```
'https://'   whole=True  scheme-branch=False  scp-branch=True
'file://'    whole=True  scheme-branch=False  scp-branch=True
```

git 2.56.0:

```
$ LC_ALL=C git ls-remote file://    -> fatal: no path specified; see 'git help pull' for valid url syntax
$ LC_ALL=C git ls-remote ssh://     -> fatal: no path specified; see 'git help pull' for valid url syntax
$ LC_ALL=C git ls-remote https://   -> fatal: unable to access 'https://': Could not resolve host: info   (ssh not called)
$ LC_ALL=C git ls-remote https:/    -> ssh called with: https git-upload-pack '/'
```

The specification (`docs/specification.md:644-647`) says a `url` is "in one of git's two forms: `scheme://…` … or scp-like `[user@]host:path`". No invalid fixture covers this case.

**Why it matters.** A truncated or empty URL, such as a copy-paste slip, passes the schema layer. The author learns of it only from REMOTE_UNREACHABLE, after a network attempt. That undercuts the purpose of tidy-up change 24, which added the pattern so that a typo is not taken for a remote.
